- My notes: [[How to ADHD (2024)]] 
00:00-00:08
Penguin Random House Audio presents How to ADHD, an insider's guide to working with your brain, not against it.


00:08-00:10
This is the author, Jessica McCabe.


00:10-00:14
Introduction.


00:14-00:19
You don't write because you want to say something.


00:19-00:22
You write because you've got something to say.


00:22-00:23
F. Scott Fitzgerald.


00:23-00:25
Hello, brains.


00:25-00:27
You found my book.


00:27-00:28
A book?


00:28-00:29
How did that happen?


00:29-00:31
Well, first I had to write one.


00:31-00:37
So why did I, someone with ADHD, do such a long and forever taking thing?


00:37-00:43
Because I lose and forget things, and what I have learned over the last seven years is too important to do that with.


00:43-00:55
As I'll explain in chapter one, my intention in starting the YouTube channel, How to ADHD, was to put everything I learned about ADHD in one place so that I could actually find it again when I needed it.


00:55-00:57
Well, it's years later.


00:57-01:08
Over the course of building the channel, I've developed a deep and detailed understanding of the invisible obstacles those with ADHD tend to run into, as well as our options for dealing with them.


01:08-01:16
The almost weekly videos in which I've shared what I learned every step along my journey have helped me and millions of others learn to work with our brains, not against them.


01:16-01:24
In fact, my team and I have made so many videos that even I'm a little overwhelmed by the amount of information we've put together.


01:24-01:30
Sometimes I wish I could just flip to a table of contents or control F my channel or my brain.


01:30-01:38
Until that is possible, I'm putting the most important information I learned, what's been the most helpful for me and for my community in a book.


01:38-01:51
More importantly though, I wanted to write a book that would give people the experience they'd have if they saw my TEDx talk, binge watched my videos, hung out in the comments section and went to coffee with me.


01:51-02:08
I wanted to give others, if I could, what I had found on my own journey, a deep understanding of how our brains work, a sense of solidarity and a toolbox full of strategies tailored to the specific challenges we face in achieving what it is we set out to achieve.


02:08-02:13
I wanted to make our invisible obstacles visible for as many people as possible.


02:13-02:19
So we stopped blaming ourselves for tripping over them and understand how to navigate them.


02:19-02:21
And I wanted to do it all in one book.


02:21-02:26
It was an ambitious project and I wasn't sure how to make it happen.


02:26-02:31
I was pretty sure it wasn't even possible many, many times while creating it.


02:31-02:42
But I used the book I was writing to push me over the finish line, tapping into the tools, reading the ending anecdotes for each chapter when I felt discouraged and well, here it is.


02:42-02:48
What you are listening to right now is the book I needed and didn't have.


02:48-02:50
Maybe it's the book you needed and didn't have.


02:51-02:58
Think of this as a user's guide to ADHD full of insights, research, strategies and validation.


02:58-03:02
One that both explains and embraces how our brains work.


03:02-03:12
You won't find definitive solutions for managing ADHD, but a menu of tools I've collected from the community, ADHD experts, lived experience and research.


03:12-03:16
You'll also find deep dives into why we even need these strategies.


03:16-03:19
So you can choose the ones that fit your unique life and brain.


03:20-03:32
The information and strategies I share in these chapters are meant to empower those with ADHD, enlighten and support the efforts of those who love someone with ADHD and be helpful and insightful for anyone who is simply human.


03:32-03:36
How to book.


03:36-03:40
This book was written by and for ADHD brains.


03:40-03:44
That said, ADHD brains often struggle to read books.


03:44-03:52
We tend to get distracted or bored, forget what we just read, lose our place or stare at the same wall of text for five minutes unable to actually read it.


03:52-03:57
For this reason, I was determined to write this book in a way that is ADHD friendly.


03:57-03:59
The paragraphs are short.


03:59-04:01
I include many reading shortcuts throughout the book.


04:01-04:08
I worked closely with my very patient editor to make the text as ADHD friendly as possible for a book this jam packed with information.


04:08-04:12
I've included quotes from brains within the How to ADHD community.


04:12-04:22
They share their personal experiences, including how they find ways to navigate around obstacles and how they work with their brains in case you need ideas for how to apply any of these tools.


04:22-04:25
Almost every chapter is divided into four sections.


04:25-04:28
One, the experience of.


04:28-04:37
At the beginning of every chapter, I describe my lived experience of the topic of the chapter, which tends to be relatable to those with ADHD.


04:37-04:40
Sometimes I get a little literary in these sections.


04:40-04:47
I may use metaphors, hyperbole or jokes to make them more fun to listen to and let's be honest, to read.


04:47-04:55
If you don't enjoy this kind of thing, or if you prefer literal language to figurative language and just wanna get to the facts, just fast forward through or skip the chapter introductions.


04:55-05:04
These sections sometimes deal with emotionally heavy topics, especially the ones that begin the how to feel, how to heart and how to people chapters.


05:04-05:12
It was important to me to be honest about my experiences here because the struggles we face in these areas can be profoundly painful.


05:12-05:15
But feel free to step away or skip ahead if you need to.


05:15-05:17
Two, what I learned.


05:17-05:25
In this section, I share the information that helped me the most on my own journey, as well as the information that resonated most with my community.


05:25-05:31
If you like the easy to understand science communication in my lecture style videos, this part of the chapter is for you.


05:31-05:42
I've compiled facts and insights from credible sources, such as peer reviewed studies, books by ADHD coaches, doctors and researchers, and conversations with experts in the field.


05:42-05:48
That being said, there's so, so, so much more to know about each of these topics than I could possibly include in a single book.


05:48-05:51
And there's new research being published all the time.


05:51-06:00
If you're extra curious, please consider this information as a jumping off point for more learning and check out howtoadhdbook.com for more.


06:00-06:02
Three, the toolbox.


06:02-06:09
In each toolbox section, you'll find strategies that work with your ADHD brain rather than against it.


06:09-06:18
The strategies I've included are research backed, commonly recommended by ADHD coaches and or ones that have been helpful to my community and me.


06:18-06:25
Each toolbox section has four or five main evidence-based strategies, tools, as well as a few ways each one can be used.


06:25-06:32
It is important to note that none of these tools are magic wands that will painlessly eliminate the obstacles that those with ADHD face.


06:32-06:38
Everyone has go-to tools that work more often for them and a couple of tools they only use occasionally.


06:38-06:41
And we all have days when nothing seems to work.


06:41-06:46
My hope for you is that by the end of this audio book, you'll have a personal toolbox full of options.


06:46-06:57
This, in my experience, is better than a toolbox with nothing but a sticky note that says, "Try harder." Even if you never use some of these tools, they're here for you when you need them.


06:57-06:59
Four, the ending anecdote.


06:59-07:05
After spending months and years studying a particular topic, my perspective on it often changes.


07:05-07:09
The final section in each chapter shares this perspective shift.


07:09-07:17
I include these stories to remind you and myself that there is no single way to look at any topic and that we can always learn something new.


07:17-07:20
Not just new information, but new ways to view it.


07:20-07:22
It's one of the awesome things about having a brain.


07:22-07:25
Is book, is book.


07:25-07:37
So, here it is, "How to ADHD The Book." This whole journey started as a personal project, an attempt to understand how to work with my brain more effectively.


07:37-07:39
It quickly became a group project.


07:39-07:43
My community jumped in to help me before I even thought to ask.


07:43-07:49
I learned a lot of what I've written here from talking with brains, hearts, ADHD experts, and researchers.


07:49-07:54
I call my viewers brains, "Hello, brains," because it's their brains that brought them to my channel.


07:54-07:57
Plus, they're here to learn about their brain.


07:57-08:07
When people find their way to my channel because they love someone with ADHD and want to understand them better, I call those people hearts, "Hello, hearts," because their hearts are what led them there.


08:07-08:11
In addition to what you may learn throughout this audiobook, I wrote a whole chapter for hearts.


08:11-08:35
Over the years, we've shared long, deep discussions about what it means to have ADHD, about how being normal isn't a realistic goal, but being functional is, about how sometimes, paradoxically, being functional means behaving in less neurotypical ways so we can be more mentally healthy, happy, and generous humans for ourselves and for those we love.


08:35-08:39
I offer all that I've learned back to you, the listener.


08:39-08:42
I'm really proud of how this audiobook has turned out.


08:42-08:44
Thank you for listening.


08:44-08:47
Allow your brain to explore this audiobook however it wants.


08:47-08:51
This book was written for your brain, and I hope your brain enjoys it.


08:51-08:56
And if you are finding us for the first time here, hello, brain, welcome to the community.


08:56-09:02
A note on language.


09:02-09:07
What I prioritize when it comes to language is accessibility.


09:07-09:19
So many of us have run into issues of access, access to information about how our brains work, access to the supports we need, access to one another, and access even to ourselves and our own voices.


09:19-09:29
There is language that decreases or denies access, which I do not use or allow in my community, language that attacks, shames, or silences.


09:29-09:32
There is language that creates access.


09:32-09:38
For example, disability gives us access to legal protections and accommodations.


09:38-09:42
Impairments explains what we might need and where we might struggle.


09:42-09:57
Research-based terms such as response inhibition, divergent thinking, and working memory give us and our healthcare providers access to information about our deficits and strengths relative to neurotypical brains, so we can access treatment tailored to our needs.


09:57-10:06
Colloquial language like doom spirals and brain smoothie makes discussing our challenges more accessible to those of us who did not go to grad school and helps us build community.


10:06-10:12
There's language that people use to identify themselves or their loved ones.


10:12-10:18
Identity-first language, person-first language, or terms such as neurodivergent or neurotypical.


10:18-10:27
People hotly debate which type of language is appropriate and some communities ban the use of one term or another, including in how people refer to themselves.


10:27-10:29
I do not.


10:29-10:38
While the language we use matters, rigidly enforcing the use of specific language can deny access to those who need it most.


10:38-10:47
Those with the least cognitive flexibility or the least ability to remember and get it right, or those living in places that have the least understanding of ADHD.


10:47-10:58
It can alienate or isolate those who are more comfortable using different terminology for themselves or are at a different place in their self-identity and self-acceptance journey.


10:58-11:11
What's more, some people may maliciously wield the correct language in a way that actively stigmatizes our community, while others might use the incorrect language in an accepting, supportive context.


11:11-11:16
My policy is to use the language that an individual person prefers.


11:16-11:19
Brain holders' choice, I call it.


11:19-11:25
Because of this, I also use the language a particular community tends to use when there is a clear preference.


11:25-11:28
In our community, people often use different terms to refer to the same thing.


11:28-11:32
In these cases, I use these different terms interchangeably.


11:32-11:49
Ultimately, my hope is to combat the underlying stigma surrounding ADHD through education and understanding and normalize the experience of having ADHD enough that the meaning can eventually be assumed to be respectful, regardless of the particular language used.


11:49-12:00
Just as I can be characterized as a girl with green eyes or a green-eyed girl without anyone, including myself, thinking less of me, I'm hoping my use of language in this audiobook will help advance this goal.


12:00-12:07
In terms of how I refer to myself, I use person-first and identity-first language, depending on context.


12:07-12:14
I also enjoy casual community-created terms like "neuro-spicy," especially when speaking about my anxiety or trauma.


12:14-12:18
It took some time for me to adjust to some of the words I now use.


12:18-12:22
I shied away from the term "disability" for a long time.


12:22-12:26
Ableism is deeply entrenched in our society, and I internalized a lot of it.


12:27-12:36
But the bigger issue for me was that I didn't feel I was disabled enough to claim that identity and the protections and accommodations that come with it.


12:36-12:42
It took me a long time to understand that it was my internalized ableism telling me I should be able to do without them.


12:42-12:55
My hope is that using the word "disability" helps me let go of my internalized ableism, and that it helps others accept themselves and access the supports and protections they need.


12:55-13:00
For what it's worth, many things can be considered a disability, even pregnancy.


13:00-13:14
According to the Americans with Disabilities Act, ADA, disability means you have a mental or physical impairment that substantially limits one or more major life activities, which can include focusing, working, or communicating.


13:14-13:16
It was helpful for me to learn and embrace that.


13:16-13:24
As for the stigma around disabilities and the willingness to speak openly about mine, I had a head start on that one.


13:25-13:28
Being disabled is an identity I share with my mother.


13:28-13:38
She was born with one leg shorter than the other, and after several unsuccessful surgeries, she moved through this world using a special shoe, crutches, and/or a wheelchair.


13:38-13:46
She was also a talented special education preschool and kindergarten teacher, and the strongest, most capable woman I have ever known.


13:46-13:51
I can still hear the baffled tone in her voice when she was told she'd be excluded from something she cared about.


13:51-13:54
What do you mean I can't teach in that room because I'm on crutches?


13:54-13:56
Put some carpet down.


13:56-13:57
And they did.


13:57-14:01
My mother spoke openly about her disability to anyone who was curious.


14:01-14:08
Children would gawk and stare at her in the grocery store when they asked questions like, what's wrong with your leg?


14:08-14:11
Their parents would turn red, rush their child away, and begin scolding them.


14:11-14:15
My mom would respond, no, let them ask.


14:15-14:24
She'd then patiently explain her scars, show them how to touch them, see, it's fine, and help them to understand how her mobility aids worked.


14:24-14:35
When you encounter someone with a disability, it's a good idea to ask about their preferences, if it's okay to ask questions, if you can touch their service dog or their mobility aids, if there are types of support they'd like you to provide.


14:35-14:38
Everyone has different needs and preferences.


14:38-14:39
It's important not to assume.


14:39-14:48
She knew that these explanations would normalize her experience for them and help them understand that there are differences in how bodies work.


14:48-14:54
She knew that this exposure combats the stigma that results in biases about people who are different.


14:54-14:59
She encouraged every curious person to participate in those conversations, however awkwardly they could.


14:59-15:07
This was because she knew that while people may always communicate differently, language evolves through conversation.


15:07-15:25
As a trained speech and language pathologist, my mom's job was to help students with disabilities learn to communicate, and she encouraged augmentative and alternative communication systems to meet each student where they were, giving them a path to having their voices heard and their needs met as they learned to speak.


15:25-15:35
She knew that contrary to popular opinion and concerns from others, these different ways of communicating would not make it harder for her students to learn how to speak.


15:35-15:37
They would be a path toward it.


15:37-15:45
I volunteered in her classroom every summer, starting when I was five, and her classroom rules are ones that guide my own work.


15:45-15:46
We are here to learn.


15:46-15:51
We make space for differences, and we allow all voices a chance to be heard.


15:51-15:57
My mother modeled language for her students and let them speak back how they could.


15:57-16:06
This increased their sense of safety and efficacy, which made it easier for them to stay in the conversation and learn how to communicate in more nuanced ways over time.


16:06-16:15
This is what my mom, Mrs. McCabe, or Abeka to many of her students, modeled for me in her classroom and outside of it.


16:15-16:18
In my mom's memory, it's what I still do now.


16:18-16:20
In this audio book and beyond.


16:20-16:27
Chapter one, how to fail at everything.


16:27-16:29
Be yourself.


16:29-16:31
No, not like that.


16:31-16:32
Society.


16:32-16:35
Potential.


16:35-16:40
My whole life, I felt like I was failing to be the person I was supposed to be.


16:40-16:50
When I was little, my mom would drop me off at school, my hair braided, jacket clean and warm from tumbling in the dryer, quietly reading a new book.


16:50-16:58
At the end of the day, my dad would pick me up, dirty, disheveled, backpack left unzipped and messy, anxiously shivering because I'd forgotten my jacket.


16:58-17:02
I went to school looking like the person I was supposed to be.


17:02-17:05
I went home looking like me.


17:05-17:07
And I wasn't what anyone expected.


17:07-17:14
When you were eight, people expect you to be able to dress yourself, keep your shoes tied and keep your backpack zipped.


17:14-17:15
The basics.


17:15-17:23
When you were 30, people expect you to show up to work on time, pay your bills and put gas in your car before it runs out.


17:23-17:26
I've never been great at meeting basic expectations.


17:26-17:28
I could, however, exceed them.


17:28-17:31
In school, I took standardized tests every year.


17:31-17:35
These tests measured my performance in each subject by grade level.


17:35-17:39
In third grade, my reading comprehension came back PHS.


17:39-17:41
I asked my teacher what it meant.


17:41-17:43
She didn't know, so she asked the principal.


17:43-17:46
He told us post high school.


17:46-17:47
I really liked to read.


17:47-17:50
In high school, I was assigned an essay to write.


17:50-18:03
I do not remember what the topic was, but I decided that to research it properly, I needed to go to a duck farm, buy some eggs, incubate and hatch those eggs, raise the ducklings, and then teach them to swim in my bathtub.


18:03-18:06
This wasn't for a science fair project, by the way.


18:06-18:09
I did this for English class.


18:09-18:17
I'm not sure why I felt I had to go to those lengths, but the day I presented my paper to the class, I was the only student walking around campus with three ducklings in tow.


18:17-18:24
In college, I signed up for multiple classes on the music business because I wanted to support my then boyfriend, who was a musician.


18:24-18:30
I had no plans to be a composer, but I took a composition class where I learned how to write music using math.


18:30-18:32
I was pretty good at it, too.


18:32-18:36
The teacher gave me the same feedback that I heard throughout my entire life.


18:36-18:37
You have so much potential.


18:37-18:40
Expectations.


18:40-18:48
The fact that I could sometimes exceed expectations made it even more frustrating for me and everyone around me when I failed to meet the basic ones.


18:48-18:51
Be a good daughter.


18:51-19:02
As a daughter, I was supposed to make my parents proud, but I struggled to meet most of my parents' expectations for me, clean your bedroom, do your homework, and behave at the dinner table.


19:02-19:04
So I tried to earn their respect in other ways.


19:04-19:10
When I was in middle school, my mom survived a car accident that killed two of her friends.


19:10-19:14
The accident left her with a broken back that never healed completely.


19:14-19:24
With both drivers uninsured and my mom, a special education teacher, suddenly unable to work, our family went from financially comfortable to broke.


19:24-19:27
Mom needed to go back to work sooner than she probably should have.


19:27-19:37
I started my acting career at 15 because as a kid growing up in Los Angeles, I knew it was a path where someone my age could make enough money to support their parents.


19:37-19:39
I didn't want my mom to have to work anymore.


19:39-19:42
It was clear how much pain she was in.


19:42-19:46
I couldn't take away her pain, but I could try to make her life easier.


19:46-19:49
When my parents struggled with their marriage, I tried playing therapist.


19:49-19:55
When my little brother experienced significant mental health issues, I tried to mediate between him and my parents.


19:55-19:57
Sometimes I parented him myself.


19:57-20:12
After quite a bit of therapy, I now understand that this was not healthy, but I so desperately wanted to be a good daughter and make my disabled mom's life easier that I did whatever I could, especially because I felt like such a difficult child.


20:12-20:15
Pay attention in class.


20:15-20:18
As a student, I was supposed to know what was happening in class.


20:18-20:25
In elementary school, I could get away with staring out the window or getting distracted during tests because I was smart.


20:25-20:30
We stayed in one classroom all day and there were sticker charts and prizes to motivate me to do my work.


20:30-20:39
By middle school, when I was responsible for motivating myself to do my work, bringing the right books to the right class and managing assignments on my own, my academic life fell apart.


20:39-20:48
Around 12, I was struggling so much that my mom brought me to a doctor who diagnosed me with attention deficit disorder, ADD.


20:48-20:51
This is an abbreviated version of the story.


20:51-20:55
In reality, she took me to a doctor who said I couldn't have ADD.


20:55-20:56
I was too smart.


20:56-21:00
My mom thanked this doctor for his opinion and asked to see a specialist.


21:00-21:05
The specialist knew that not smart isn't on the list of symptoms and gave me a proper evaluation.


21:06-21:10
I was prescribed daily stimulant medication and it helped me focus.


21:10-21:15
My GPA went up a whole point without me doing anything else differently.


21:15-21:18
The effort I was already putting in suddenly worked.


21:18-21:25
As far as everyone I knew was concerned, my ADD had been successfully treated and that was supposed to be the end of that.


21:25-21:29
But medication added new expectations.


21:29-21:30
Now, stop messing around.


21:30-21:31
Are you ready for school yet?


21:31-21:34
Was followed by, did you take your meds?


21:34-21:39
Now, I figured there was no excuse for me failing to be what everyone wanted me to be.


21:39-21:43
The gifted student who received straight A's and was a delight to have in class.


21:43-21:52
On top of completing my schoolwork and finding time for extracurriculars, I also needed to remember to schedule my doctor's appointments every month.


21:52-21:59
Go to them, pick up my prescription, refill it within the two-day window and then take my meds at the right time.


21:59-22:04
After I woke up, but not too late if I wanted to go to sleep that night.


22:04-22:09
And when my meds wore off or I forgot to take them, I struggled even harder than I did before.


22:09-22:12
Get a college degree.


22:12-22:15
As a gifted student, I was supposed to graduate college.


22:15-22:22
Though I had completely missed every deadline to apply for universities, I did very well on my community college entrance exams.


22:22-22:24
My counselor told me she wasn't worried.


22:24-22:28
I'd be able to transfer to a four-year university, no problem.


22:28-22:33
Somehow, despite her confidence in me, I missed another expectation.


22:33-22:38
The part of college where you make a plan to complete your course requirements.


22:38-22:51
I wanted to be a journalism major, but instead of writing classes, I took fencing and those music business classes I mentioned and ballet and opera and Italian so I could understand what I was singing in opera.


22:51-22:56
One semester, I decided to take statistics, a class I actually needed to graduate.


22:56-23:01
I forgot to register for the course in time, but the professor told me to show up for class anyway.


23:01-23:07
Honestly, forgetting to register for classes in time was half the reason I ended up taking so many courses I didn't need.


23:07-23:13
They were the only classes left because the students who signed up early for courses took all the spots for the required ones.


23:13-23:20
Once I completed the course, he said, he would give me whatever grade I got when I officially registered for the class next semester.


23:20-23:23
Statistics is tough.


23:23-23:28
I went to every lecture, spent two hours a day doing homework and aced the class.


23:28-23:31
The next semester, however, I forgot to sign up.


23:31-23:37
Again, I spent the rest of the year looking for bushes to hide behind when I saw this professor on campus.


23:37-23:46
By the time I worked up the courage to admit my mistake a year later and ask if I could still register for his class and get that A, he told me it had been too long.


23:46-23:48
I'd need to take it again.


23:48-23:52
I was so discouraged, I dropped out shortly after.


23:52-23:56
Statistically speaking, I knew my odds of graduating anytime soon were not great.


23:56-23:59
Be successful.


23:59-24:03
I couldn't reach my potential as a student so I tried to reach it in my career.


24:03-24:07
After dropping out of college, I decided to give acting another shot.


24:07-24:11
My theater teacher had previously introduced me to his acting manager who also believed in me.


24:11-24:29
I pursued acting the same way I pursued everything, with total enthusiasm, unless I got distracted or had to do something boring, like memorize lines, listen while someone else spoke or hold relatively still, which turns out to be 90% of being a professional actor.


24:30-24:36
I had a handful of early successes, but as time went by, there were fewer and fewer opportunities.


24:36-24:39
Coming up, you'll hear a frank discussion of disordered eating.


24:39-24:43
My manager and agent had ideas to boost my career.


24:43-24:46
Lose 10 pounds and we can get you a pilot.


24:46-24:52
I'd try a new diet or a different workout, but I could never sustain my efforts long enough for them to matter.


24:52-25:02
I'd get discouraged and give up, or run out of money from paying for personal trainers I couldn't afford, or purchasing dehydrators and other equipment so I could follow the latest diet.


25:02-25:08
I'm not sure if I would have qualified for an eating disorder, but I definitely ended up with disordered eating.


25:08-25:11
I'll discuss this later and let's talk about eating issues.


25:11-25:15
I carried my shame and frustration with me into the audition room.


25:15-25:17
I hadn't lost 10 pounds.


25:17-25:25
I saw other girls walk in competing for the same role, who I thought were thinner or prettier, or who seemed more confident in their preparation.


25:25-25:28
I couldn't even remember my lines.


25:28-25:38
I'd work for days preparing for a role, only to give an audition that said to the casting director, "I'm sorry for wasting your time." It wasn't just acting I struggled with.


25:38-25:45
In the 10 years after I left college, I quit or was fired from 15 jobs and abandoned several careers.


25:45-25:49
My performance as an employee was wildly inconsistent.


25:49-25:51
Sometimes I was a superstar.


25:51-26:01
At my first job as a server at a retirement home for millionaires, I noticed that they didn't have an organized system for order taking, so I created one.


26:01-26:06
I got to work early, did all my side work before going home, and stayed as late as they needed me to.


26:06-26:11
At another job, I left at lunch to see my boyfriend and didn't return.


26:11-26:13
I assume they fired me?


26:13-26:14
I don't know for sure.


26:14-26:19
If I remember correctly, I didn't pick up the phone when they called and I never went to that McDonald's again.


26:19-26:23
Take care of your home and everyone in it.


26:23-26:32
As a child of the '90s, I watched my mom raise three kids, build a career, keep her house clean, manage the finances, and put dinner on the table every night.


26:32-26:35
At 30, I couldn't even keep my car clean.


26:35-26:38
My glove box was full of unpaid parking tickets.


26:38-26:43
My acting career had stalled, and I paid rent by working part-time jobs waiting tables.


26:43-26:46
I was ashamed of how messy my house was.


26:46-26:50
There were times I got so overwhelmed that my mom would come over and help me clean.


26:50-26:54
My mom had mobility problems and chronic pain.


26:54-27:01
She carried things to the rooms where they belonged and put them away, did my dishes, and cleaned out my car on crutches.


27:01-27:03
I was perfectly able-bodied.


27:03-27:05
Why couldn't I do this myself?


27:05-27:11
Desperate to prove my worth as an adult and a woman, I married my long-term boyfriend.


27:11-27:19
I planned a beautiful wedding, signed up for dance classes, found the perfect ball gown style wedding dress, and memorized a waltz.


27:19-27:21
The marriage lasted four months.


27:22-27:27
I saw women my age with dazzling careers, stable relationships, and perfectly manicured nails.


27:27-27:32
Meanwhile, I was broke, divorced, and working in restaurants, so my hands were a mess.


27:32-27:38
Cutting lemons during the dinner rush because you underestimated how many you'd need for that shift is hard, especially when you're clumsy.


27:38-27:40
But hey, that was my side work.


27:40-27:41
Gotta do it, Jess.


27:41-27:42
Don't be lazy.


27:42-27:44
What I learned.


27:44-27:48
I knew I wasn't the person I was supposed to be, at least not consistently.


27:48-27:53
And in lieu of other explanations, I accepted and internalized the ones given to me.


27:53-27:56
I'm irresponsible.


27:56-27:59
Responsible people follow through, I thought.


27:59-28:03
They don't bail, procrastinate, or avoid things they're supposed to do.


28:03-28:10
But me, I spent money I didn't have on things I didn't need or couldn't find, and then forgot to pay my bills.


28:10-28:14
I showed up late or unprepared to classes, meetings, and work.


28:14-28:18
I made promises to friends and broke them when I became distracted or overwhelmed.


28:19-28:25
I don't think anyone ever called me irresponsible to my face, but I heard what people said when others failed in similar ways.


28:25-28:27
It wasn't hard to put two and two together.


28:27-28:30
I'm messy.


28:30-28:35
Growing up, I had a nickname, Messy Jessie, and I earned it.


28:35-28:38
My room generally looked like something had exploded in it.


28:38-28:40
My backpack and desk were constantly disorganized.


28:40-28:42
I spilled things on myself regularly.


28:42-28:47
Even though I grew out of the nickname, I never grew out of the mess.


28:47-28:51
I dreaded having company over because I didn't want people to see the state of my apartment.


28:51-28:58
When friends asked if I could drive them home, my decision was never based on how far they needed to go or what else I had planned that day.


28:58-29:08
It was based on how much stuff I had in the front seat and whether I could shove it into the back seat quickly enough so I wouldn't leave them standing on the curb for five minutes with nothing to do but watch and judge.


29:08-29:12
My thoughts, my emotions, and my speech were just as messy.


29:12-29:17
I'd get confused about where I was supposed to meet someone and show up at the wrong time or place.


29:17-29:23
I skipped steps in relationships, like making sure you like the person before you start dating them.


29:23-29:29
I overshared with complete strangers and didn't think to mention important stuff to the people I cared about.


29:29-29:32
I wanted to be the cool, fun friend and girlfriend.


29:32-29:38
I wanted to be put together and witty and succinct, but I had big emotions and too many thoughts.


29:38-29:43
And when I tried to express them, the words would tumble out together, always ending in a slew of apologies.


29:43-29:45
I'm sorry I'm such a mess.


29:46-29:48
I'm careless.


29:48-29:51
My teachers were the first to point out how careless I was.


29:51-29:55
I'd neglect to turn in my homework, miss deadlines, and forget my lunch.


29:55-30:00
My tests came back marked up in red with careless mistakes scribbled in the margin.


30:00-30:07
I was the kid who always won second place in the spelling bee but could have done better if I'd actually studied.


30:07-30:10
I would have been able to answer the teacher's question if I'd bothered to pay attention.


30:10-30:16
As an adult, small oversights like this had more significant consequences.


30:16-30:19
I interviewed for a corporate job and it went well.


30:19-30:23
The company offered me a salary that was more than I'd ever made in my life.


30:23-30:27
I quickly accepted and they started clearing out a desk for me.


30:27-30:28
I was gonna have a company car.


30:28-30:30
I was gonna be a real adult.


30:30-30:33
I was so excited until I got the call.


30:33-30:38
The background check they'd run on me showed that I had a suspended license.


30:38-30:42
They couldn't hire me anymore because they couldn't let me drive a company car.


30:42-30:47
The suspended license was over a broken tail light from months earlier.


30:47-30:51
I'd fixed the tail light but I'd forgotten to follow up on the ticket.


30:51-30:53
Another careless mistake.


30:53-30:56
The echoes of these beliefs linger.


30:56-31:01
I still say these negative things to myself sometimes even though I know better now.


31:01-31:18
These judgments, even once I learned how inaccurate they are, even now that I understand the biology behind the invisible obstacles I kept tripping over and blaming myself for, are long solidified by decades of neural pathways wiring together and firing together.


31:18-31:26
The day I wrote this section, I was scheduled to moderate an online panel that had taken months of preparation.


31:26-31:31
I'd glanced at my calendar too fast and missed the fact that I was supposed to log on half an hour early.


31:31-31:41
As soon as I realized my mistake, I raced to my office, jumped on the video meeting 15 minutes late and breathed a sigh of relief that at least I was there before the event started.


31:41-31:50
Only to realize in slow horror that my laptop's battery was at 3% and I forgot my charger.


31:50-31:51
I knew how it would look.


31:51-31:53
I knew the assumptions people would make.


31:53-32:03
How irresponsible, I imagined them saying as I dug through my mess of a backpack, fighting back tears, hoping I was wrong, hoping my charger was here somewhere.


32:03-32:04
It was not.


32:04-32:06
She must not care.


32:06-32:11
But I do care and I desperately want to be the person I was supposed to be.


32:11-32:13
I've tried.


32:13-32:24
In fact, I have tried everything suggested to me by well-meaning teachers, doctors, friends, parents, professionals and strangers on the internet, as well as what I've learned on my own.


32:24-32:37
I've evolved past the largely maladaptive coping strategies I'm listing in this chapter, but I'm including them because this is how many of us go through life when we aren't getting support and don't have strategies that make sense for us.


32:37-32:40
They were my very first set of tools.


32:40-32:42
The toolbox.


32:42-32:48
When you boil it down, there were five strategies that I used to use, constantly.


32:48-32:50
I've compiled them here for your convenience.


32:50-32:54
One, deny.


32:54-33:00
Very few people ever knew how much I was struggling because I became a master of pretending everything was fine.


33:00-33:02
Anxiety helped mask my forgetfulness.


33:02-33:03
Did I do the thing?


33:03-33:05
Better check, check again.


33:05-33:08
I covered my anxiety with a mask of cheerfulness.


33:08-33:08
I'm fine.


33:08-33:09
This is fine.


33:10-33:14
I pretended I remembered people's names while racking my brain for clues.


33:14-33:18
I pretended I'd absolutely started on the project that was due next week.


33:18-33:23
I pretended I'd stuck to my budget and could totally afford to go out to dinner that night.


33:23-33:28
I pretended I didn't have needs, or at least that it was fine that they weren't getting met.


33:28-33:31
I pretended I hadn't forgotten my laptop for class.


33:31-33:33
I just preferred to take notes by hand.


33:33-33:35
I pretended I didn't need help.


33:35-33:38
Two, apologize.


33:38-33:43
If people had to know I couldn't meet basic expectations, at least I could feel properly bad about it.


33:43-33:52
It got to the point where I regularly apologize for things that weren't even remotely my fault because I assumed that they were, because they always were.


33:52-33:57
I apologized for whatever it was they were upset about, and I accepted the moral judgments that came with it.


33:57-34:03
After all, I wasn't supposed to be struggling to this extent, certainly not at the age I was at the time.


34:03-34:04
I knew better.


34:04-34:06
It must be my fault.


34:06-34:08
Three, beg.


34:08-34:26
I begged for forgiveness, for a loan, for another chance for an extension on a deadline, to be let out of a ticket, for my boss not to fire me, to not be judged for the state of my house or car, for help even once it was finally clear that I needed it, for a chance to make up for it, to do better next time.


34:26-34:29
Four, try to do better next time.


34:29-34:32
If I couldn't meet expectations, I could try to exceed them.


34:32-34:41
I could overgive, overwork, overplan, forgot to buy someone a $30 birthday gift, fix it with a $100 gift card, got to work 15 minutes late.


34:41-34:43
How about I stay two hours late?


34:43-34:48
I pulled all-nighters just so I didn't have to let someone down again.


34:48-34:51
I made lists and lists to help me keep track of those lists.


34:51-34:57
I started getting ready to go somewhere hours in advance just so I'd have some hope of arriving on time.


34:57-35:01
I thought through everything that could go wrong and tried to plan for all of those scenarios.


35:01-35:04
Inevitably, I still missed something.


35:05-35:07
Five, try harder.


35:07-35:12
I never forgot the line I saw on so many report cards right after so much potential.


35:12-35:14
Needs to try harder.


35:14-35:17
So when I wasn't reaching my potential, that's what I did.


35:17-35:19
I tried harder.


35:19-35:27
But as life got more complicated and there was more to do than just homework, the belief that I wasn't trying hard enough morphed into a more insidious belief.


35:27-35:31
I'm not doing enough.


35:31-35:34
I constantly felt like I was supposed to be doing more.


35:34-35:36
My meds allowed me to do that.


35:36-35:48
At 15, I was going to high school, taking courses on writing children's books, attending swim team practices, working at the local fast food joint and dating a string of boyfriends, all while trying to be a professional actress.


35:48-35:51
I pushed the limits of what my brain and body could handle, even with the meds.


35:51-35:54
As an adult, I kept the same pace.


35:54-36:04
There was always more I could be doing for my career, for my parents, for my partners, for my friends, for my financial health, for my physical appearance and for my future.


36:04-36:12
I pushed myself to get that second or third job, finish that extra work out, take that class and show up for whoever needed me.


36:12-36:18
I tried self-help books, seminars and every organizational strategy anyone ever suggested.


36:18-36:22
Whenever anyone needed something from me, my needs went out the window.


36:22-36:26
When I failed to do the things I set out to do, I chastised myself.


36:26-36:29
Stop being lazy, you're flaky, you give up too easily.


36:29-36:39
After watching me struggle ineffectively with some task, one of my friends asked me, "Have you ever asked yourself if there's an easier way to do this?" I looked at him.


36:39-36:42
No, I'm just used to things being hard.


36:42-36:47
The more I tried to meet everyone's expectations, the less I seemed to be able to.


36:47-36:51
I tried harder and moved faster until my efforts were frantic.


36:51-36:54
I would memorize lines while driving and doing my makeup.


36:54-36:56
I ate while I worked.


36:56-37:05
I sent my boyfriend a text after another fight while I was supposed to be hanging out with friends or I canceled on them at the last minute to pick up an extra shift at work.


37:05-37:11
The only time I ever stopped was when I physically couldn't keep going and passed out from exhaustion.


37:11-37:13
Eventually, I burned out.


37:13-37:16
The one thing I hadn't tried.


37:16-37:21
At 32 years old, I was broke, divorced and living with my mom.


37:21-37:24
My credit was terrible.


37:24-37:30
I couldn't remember the last time I'd actually spent time with my friends and I wasn't entirely sure they even liked me.


37:30-37:33
It was as if my brain had gone on strike.


37:33-37:35
I was exhausted.


37:35-37:36
I was disillusioned.


37:36-37:38
I didn't care anymore.


37:38-37:40
I didn't know how to keep going.


37:40-37:44
I didn't know how to do better, make everyone happy or lose those 10 pounds.


37:44-37:47
I just knew what I was doing wasn't working.


37:47-37:51
I was ready to accept that I couldn't be this person everyone wanted me to be.


37:51-37:54
I was no longer someone who had so much potential.


37:54-37:57
I was quickly becoming someone who hadn't reached it.


37:57-38:06
I was trying so hard to force myself to fit the stencil cutout of who others expected me to be that I never got to know the person I was.


38:06-38:10
I thought I was working to meet my potential and be my best self.


38:10-38:14
But what I was actually doing was trying my hardest to be someone I'm not.


38:14-38:22
The first person to challenge me on the belief that I had to change to be successful wasn't a therapist or a doctor or my mom.


38:22-38:25
It was Alison Robertson, a life coach for actors.


38:25-38:31
At this point, I had been struggling to get traction as an actress for over a decade.


38:31-38:37
I went to one of her acting workshops where she explained her role as a life coach and let us ask her questions.


38:37-38:38
I raised my hand.


38:38-38:40
How do I lose 10 pounds?


38:40-38:41
She smiled at me.


38:41-38:44
Why? You don't need to lose 10 pounds.


38:44-38:45
I shook my head.


38:45-38:46
You don't understand.


38:46-38:47
Yes, I do.


38:47-38:51
If I want to be a successful actress, I need to lose 10 pounds.


38:51-38:52
How do I do it?


38:52-38:59
What I was really asking in retrospect was how do I meet these expectations my agent and manager have of me?


38:59-39:02
How do I be the person everyone is telling me I'm supposed to be?


39:02-39:06
She responded, "You don't need to be smaller.


39:06-39:09
You need to be bigger." I was confused.


39:09-39:14
You think you need to be small, so you're making yourself small.


39:14-39:19
And maybe it made sense 10 years ago when you were trying to play a teenager, but you're a woman now.


39:19-39:21
You're allowed to take up space.


39:21-39:23
This answer annoyed me.


39:23-39:24
I was sure she was wrong.


39:24-39:35
But the more I thought about it, the more I realized that she was the first person to tell me I didn't have to keep struggling to accomplish something that I couldn't achieve despite my best efforts.


39:35-39:40
I decided I needed to hear more of what she had to say, even if I could only afford a couple of sessions.


39:40-39:48
I showed up to my first session with my grocery money in hand and asked, "So, what do I need to do?" "Nothing," she said.


39:48-40:09
"What do you mean nothing?" Alison told me to stop doing everything, stop taking acting classes, stop going to auditions, stop trying to lose weight, stop reading self-help books, stop responding to other people's demands and crises, stop trying to make up for lost time in my career, my friendships, my relationships.


40:09-40:12
We don't know where you need to put your effort yet.


40:12-40:18
After a lifetime of trying harder, it felt terrifying and wrong to just stop trying it all.


40:18-40:21
But I had tried everything else.


40:22-40:30
So, as behind on life as I believed I was, as much as it felt like I didn't have time to stop, I stopped.


40:30-40:37
No auditions, no calling my manager, no diets, no making up for my past failures with family and friends.


40:37-40:38
And it worked.


40:38-40:44
In the space of a month that felt like a lifetime, I realized where I needed to be putting in my effort.


40:44-40:49
It wasn't trying to do all the things that I was convinced I needed to do to be successful.


40:49-40:52
I finally saw that it wouldn't work.


40:52-40:54
If it could have, it would have.


40:54-40:59
I'd been doing it for so long and all I'd gotten out of it was levels of exhaustion.


40:59-41:07
Instead, I realized I needed to put my effort into figuring out why it wasn't working when everyone around me kept telling me that it should.


41:07-41:10
I went back to Allison with an idea.


41:10-41:15
I need to put my effort into figuring out what's getting in my way and what to do about it.


41:15-41:15
She agreed.


41:15-41:21
I was diagnosed with ADD as a kid, so maybe there's something to that.


41:21-41:29
Every once in a while, I've come across a strategy that helped for a bit until I stopped using it and then couldn't find it again when I wanted to.


41:29-41:36
Maybe I can figure out what I'm struggling with, find strategies that can help, and put them somewhere I can actually find them again.


41:36-41:38
Like a notebook?


41:38-41:39
I shook my head.


41:39-41:40
I lost notebooks.


41:40-41:42
I lost everything.


41:42-41:44
YouTube.


41:44-41:45
I knew I wouldn't lose YouTube.


41:46-41:48
(mouse clicking)


41:48-41:51
Chapter two.


41:51-41:52
How to ADD.


41:52-41:53
Strike that.


41:53-41:55
How to ADHD.


41:55-41:59
We know what we are, but know not what we may be.


41:59-42:02
Ophelia from Hamlet by William Shakespeare.


42:02-42:06
I don't have ADD.


42:06-42:14
The first thing that happened when I started my YouTube channel, which I was planning to name How to ADD, was learning I do not, in fact, have ADD.


42:14-42:15
What?


42:15-42:17
It's all called ADHD now?


42:17-42:27
I thought about the stereotype of someone with ADHD, a Bart Simpson type boy bouncing off the walls, and compared it to my staring out the window, daydreamy self.


42:27-42:32
Saying I had attention deficit hyperactivity disorder felt weird.


42:32-42:36
I soon learned that there was an inattentive presentation of ADHD.


42:36-42:41
I learned that hyperactivity can show up differently based on biological sex.


42:41-42:49
I learned it can look like tearing voraciously through a book series, or interrupting in class, or speaking really quickly with those spaces between your words.


42:49-42:55
I also discovered you could qualify for both the inattentive and hyperactive presentations of ADHD.


42:55-42:57
It was called combined type.


42:57-42:58
Huh.


42:58-43:02
At my next doctor's appointment, I asked which presentation I had.


43:02-43:08
He glanced at his copy of the DSM, the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders.


43:08-43:10
See how your leg is fidgeting?


43:10-43:12
Yeah, you have ADHD combined type.


43:12-43:15
He checked his watch and wrote a prescription.


43:15-43:16
See you next month.


43:16-43:18
Thanks doc.


43:18-43:20
I turned back to Google.


43:20-43:21
Executive function?


43:21-43:22
What's that?


43:22-43:23
[[working memory]]?


43:23-43:24
Define please.


43:24-43:26
[[motivation deficit]]?


43:26-43:27
Oh man.


43:27-43:34
Maybe everything I dealt with wasn't all in my head, or more accurately, I wasn't imagining it.


43:34-43:36
The struggle was real.


43:36-43:40
I mean, it technically was in my head because that's where your brain is.


43:40-43:50
I had no idea that all of the showing up late difficulty staying organized, irresponsible spending, and feeling like a hot mess were related to the condition I'd been diagnosed with 20 years ago.


43:50-43:55
I knew I struggled to focus, which is why I took meds.


43:55-44:12
But as I read about the executive function challenges, which I didn't know I had, associated with ADHD, which I also didn't know I had, I wondered how it was possible that I had been seeing a doctor every one to three months for two decades and none of them had told me any of this.


44:12-44:18
I felt relieved, angry, delighted, sad, vindicated.


44:18-44:22
There was more to having ADHD than I'd thought.


44:22-44:27
I found article after article sharing strategies and explaining why we needed them.


44:27-44:33
You might think learning about your brain's impairments would be depressing, but for me, this information gave me hope.


44:33-44:37
If these were real impairments, there must be real solutions too.


44:38-44:43
In my earliest videos, I explored one aspect of ADHD every week.


44:43-44:49
I researched and explained one ADHD related challenge and I presented one possible strategy that could help.


44:49-44:57
Gaining this knowledge about my brain from ADHD experts and researchers empowered me in a way I'd never experienced before.


44:57-45:08
I learned about the specific deficits involved, which meant I could now see the invisible obstacles I kept tripping over, label them, and understand how to navigate them.


45:08-45:14
It was like reading error codes on a printer and actually knowing what the error codes meant.


45:14-45:19
If I knew it was out of paper, I could refill the empty tray, which was way more useful than banging on the printer.


45:19-45:22
It definitely worked better than trying harder.


45:22-45:27
I found joy and validation in what I learned, but also grief.


45:27-45:31
How much suffering could I have avoided if I had known this information?


45:31-45:33
How many failed relationships?


45:33-45:35
Could I have finished college?


45:35-45:37
Could I have been a better daughter?


45:37-45:41
Could I have saved all the money I spent on purchasing self-help books that never seemed to help?


45:41-45:48
Yes, I realized the irony of writing one of these books myself, but this one takes into account my ADHD.


45:48-45:56
Sometimes I sat reading research papers with tears streaming down my cheeks, mourning my past self.


45:56-46:02
That little girl who thought she was doing everything wrong had no idea what obstacles she faced.


46:02-46:11
She spent her whole young life blaming herself for struggles that, according to researchers and ADHD experts, were totally normal for those with ADHD.


46:11-46:19
But my grief was overshadowed by my passion, my fierce determination to tell everyone what I had learned.


46:19-46:31
Needless suffering is my least favorite thing in the world, but I could live with my regrets if I could give meaning to my pain, if I could use my experience to help others not have to go through what I went through.


46:32-46:35
I talked about the channel to my tables at the restaurant where I worked.


46:35-46:38
I gave them fidgets and glitter bottles and asked them to subscribe.


46:38-46:55
I learned about website promotion and started hosting giveaways of sticky notes and fidget toys to encourage people to watch the videos to get more people the information they needed, the information I needed anyway, the information I hadn't even known I needed because I thought I understood my condition.


46:55-46:58
I wasn't just learning about ADHD.


46:58-47:05
I was also unlearning all the assumptions and misunderstandings that I had accumulated in the years since my diagnosis.


47:05-47:15
As excited and shocked and sometimes saddened as I was by what I discovered, the viewers in the comments were just as enthused and confused.


47:15-47:17
How come I never knew this?


47:17-47:20
Why did nobody tell me this about my ADHD?


47:20-47:23
Or even, wait, do I have ADHD?


47:23-47:28
How could a condition that is so well known be so poorly understood?


47:28-47:34
Why were so many of us still struggling so much even after years or decades of diagnosis and treatment?


47:34-47:40
And why were so many people learning about their medical condition for the first time from some college dropout on YouTube?


47:40-47:43
What I learned.


47:43-47:49
ADHD is incredibly misunderstood, both by those who have it and by many professionals who treat it.


47:49-47:50
Why?


47:50-47:57
Research and publishing peer-reviewed papers takes time and it takes even longer for new information to trickle down to those who need it.


47:57-48:03
This means a lot of what we think we know about ADHD and what our doctors know is already outdated.


48:03-48:09
Many people also think ADHD is less serious than other mental health conditions.


48:09-48:13
So much so that even health professionals frequently aren't taught much about it.


48:13-48:17
After all, its symptoms describe tendencies that everyone experiences sometimes.


48:17-48:29
Comedic media portrayals of ADHD, comments like, "Isn't everyone a little ADHD?" And dismissive friends and or relatives who may have undiagnosed ADHD themselves because it runs in families.


48:29-48:33
Reinforce the notion that ADHD is nothing more than a funny quirk.


48:33-48:34
Oh look, butterfly.


48:34-48:38
Plus, misinformation is everywhere.


48:38-48:45
Inaccurate information about ADHD spreads on social media and by word of mouth and often more quickly than accurate information.


48:45-48:51
People tend to gravitate towards simple answers and well, the truth is usually annoyingly complicated.


48:51-49:00
Even though the truth is often complicated, it's infinitely more helpful to have an accurate understanding of your own or a loved one's mental health condition.


49:00-49:06
Let's start our updated understanding by correcting some common misconceptions about ADHD.


49:06-49:10
ADHD doesn't involve an attention deficit.


49:10-49:16
ADHD is a terrible name for the condition because attention deficit implies a lack of attention.


49:16-49:26
However, our brains can focus quite well sometimes, particularly on things we find engaging such as our hobbies, crushes, video games and so on.


49:26-49:31
The trouble is we can't control the intensity of our focus or what we focus on.


49:31-49:33
We'll learn more about this later.


49:33-49:40
This big misconception makes people less likely to seek a diagnosis because they are able to focus sometimes.


49:40-49:45
You don't have to look hyperactive to have ADHD.


49:45-49:49
Many people believe if you don't look hyperactive, you must not have ADHD.


49:49-50:00
This leads to missed diagnoses and leaves those who were originally diagnosed with ADD, I, confused about how they could possibly have ADHD hyperactivity D.


50:00-50:02
There are three presentations of ADHD.


50:02-50:13
Primarily inattentive, ADHD-PI, primarily hyperactive-impulsive, ADHD-HI, and combined type, ADHD-C.


50:13-50:25
Most people with ADHD are combined type, which means they have enough symptoms of the inattentive presentation and the hyperactive-impulsive presentation that they qualify for both.


50:25-50:38
For the primarily inattentive presentation of ADHD, however, you don't need to have any symptoms of hyperactivity to qualify for the diagnosis because turns out same condition, different presentations.


50:38-50:45
Also, even those who do have symptoms related to hyperactivity and impulsivity aren't always physically hyperactive.


50:45-50:51
Verbal hyperactivity is a thing and it's one way hyperactivity in girls and women tends to show up.


50:51-50:54
I get a lot of comments about how quickly I speak.


50:54-51:00
Physical hyperactivity also often involves into mental restlessness, plus presentations can change over time.


51:00-51:05
In short, ADHD can look like a kid bouncing off the walls.


51:05-51:12
It can also look like a full grown adult unable to sleep because of racing thoughts or a child staring dreamily out a window.


51:12-51:17
ADHD is a neurological issue, not behavioral.


51:17-51:29
While ADHD used to be considered a behavioral condition and it can sure look that way, it's now understood that the behavioral issues related to ADHD exist for neurological reasons.


51:29-51:36
ADHD is a neurodevelopmental disorder, which means the nervous system, including the brain, develops and functions differently.


51:36-51:43
Fundamental differences in brain development and structure contribute to differences in behavior.


51:43-51:49
This is why behavior strategies that work for neurotypical people are frequently unsuccessful for ADHDers.


51:49-51:52
Our behaviors happen for different reasons.


51:52-52:05
In fact, research shows that compared to neurotypical children, punishment is less effective for ADHD kids while immediate positive feedback and salient rewards are more effective.


52:05-52:12
This is one of the reasons that can be so frustrating for parents of ADHD children to be judged by strangers when their kids misbehave.


52:12-52:14
It might look as though they need discipline.


52:14-52:16
What they actually need is support.


52:16-52:22
Unlike punishment, rewards can address [[motivation deficit]]s inherent to ADHD.


52:22-52:26
ADHD has a serious impact.


52:26-52:35
While individual ADHD symptoms might not seem like that big of a deal, the extent and consistency with which those with ADHD struggle are a big deal.


52:35-52:42
ADHD affects multiple aspects of our lives on a daily and even moment by moment basis.


52:42-52:51
While there's much I personally value about my ADHD brain, life outcomes on average are worse for those with ADHD, especially if it's untreated.


52:51-52:56
Those with ADHD are more likely to get divorced, be fired, and get into car accidents.


52:56-53:07
Research by psychologist Dr. Russell Barkley, a prominent ADHD researcher, demonstrates that ADHD has a significantly negative impact on life expectancy.


53:07-53:23
By 12.7 years on average, ADHD makes our lives harder and shorter, and when combined with the effects of gender, race, socioeconomic status, and coexisting mental health conditions, many of which are also more common with ADHD, the outcomes can be even more dire.


53:23-53:33
These factors and the interplay among them make ADHD a lot more serious than people think, and definitely more serious than just being a cute quirk.


53:33-53:36
There is no one solution.


53:36-53:40
Every time I went to my doctors, they asked if my stimulant medication was working for me.


53:40-53:44
It was, and if I was having any negative side effects, I was not.


53:44-53:47
They'd give me another prescription and send me on my way.


53:47-53:51
ADHD treated, except that it wasn't, not entirely.


53:51-54:00
In the words of one of our team members, also named Jessica, there's no magical rainbow unicorn tool that will solve all your ADHD woes.


54:00-54:07
According to research, the optimal treatment for ADHD is multimodal and involves more than one type of treatment option.


54:07-54:22
In fact, the National Institute of Mental Health conducted an enormous study about ADHD treatment and gave it an equally enormous name, the Multimodal Treatment of Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder Study, or MTA study for short.


54:22-54:32
The MTA study found that medication alone was not enough to effectively treat ADHD, and the most successful treatments combined therapy and medication.


54:32-54:39
While the MTA study only looked at two treatment options, there are many ways to effectively treat ADHD.


54:39-54:41
A multimodal approach can include some of the following.


54:43-54:43
Medication.


54:43-54:51
Stimulant medication is the most common treatment for ADHD, although there are non-stimulant options available as well.


54:51-54:56
Every brain is different, so it can take some trial and error to find the right medication and dose.


54:56-55:06
However, medication is typically highly effective for ADHD and offers the greatest immediate improvement in ADHD symptoms out of all the available treatment options.


55:06-55:15
The effects are temporary, wearing off when the meds do, but for many, the difference they make in our focus, productivity, and even [[emotional regulation|emotion regulation]] can be profound.


55:15-55:17
Psychoeducation.


55:17-55:25
Psychoeducation means providing information and education that helps someone understand and cope with their mental health condition.


55:25-55:37
While not specific to ADHD, research across multiple diagnoses shows there are significant benefits of psychoeducation, not just for individual client outcomes, but for family members as well.


55:37-55:41
It also improves self-awareness, which is helpful for those of us who struggle with that.


55:41-55:45
Turns out it helps to understand the brain you're working with.


55:45-55:46
Who knew?


55:46-55:48
Skills training.


55:48-55:51
Skills training can focus on many things.


55:51-56:00
For example, social skills, parenting skills, life skills, and organizational skills, and can benefit both the person with ADHD and their caregivers.


56:00-56:10
For parents of children with ADHD, parent management training, PMT, can help them learn how to parent in a way that accounts for and supports their child's ADHD challenges.


56:10-56:20
For adults, skills training can also focus on things like ADHD-informed training and career development, personal finance, communication, goal setting, and prioritization.


56:20-56:23
Therapy.


56:23-56:37
Several types of psychotherapy have been proven highly effective for ADHD, including cognitive behavioral therapy, CBT, dialectical behavioral therapy, DBT, and acceptance and commitment therapy, ACT.


56:37-56:38
ACT.


56:38-56:47
All of these can help us untangle our unhelpful thought patterns and adjust behaviors that worsen our functional impairments and make our emotions more difficult to manage.


56:47-56:55
Therapy is most successful when you develop a good therapeutic relationship with your therapist, meaning you trust them to help you toward your goals.


56:55-57:06
This is easier to do when our therapists have a solid understanding of ADHD and other important factors in our experience, because they're more likely to understand our struggles and offer guidance that works for us.


57:06-57:08
Coaching.


57:08-57:17
An ADHD coach is someone who offers accountability, guidance, insights, and practical strategies that help minimize the functional impairments related to ADHD.


57:17-57:22
They can be an incredibly helpful resource for setting and achieving goals.


57:22-57:32
Often, ADHD coaches have ADHD themselves and work exclusively with ADHD clients, which means they have a wealth of personal experience and knowledge specific to ADHD.


57:32-57:34
Support.


57:34-57:36
Having ADHD is hard.


57:36-57:44
One of the most important factors for successfully managing it is having support from family, friends, work or school, and our peers.


57:44-57:53
Connecting with other ADHDers can be particularly helpful because it can normalize the struggles of having a brain that works differently from the vast majority of the world.


57:53-57:56
I'll talk more about how to develop a support network and how to people.


57:56-58:02
How to heart covers how to offer support to someone you care about, even if that person is you.


58:02-58:07
Unfortunately, there are barriers that often make one or more of these options inaccessible.


58:07-58:16
Thankfully, many professionals offer free resources, some of which you can find on my website, howtoadhdbook.com, and I started to learn about my ADHD by digging through them.


58:16-58:20
A lot of people struggle with ADHD.


58:20-58:27
Because I didn't know anyone with ADHD outside my own family, I assumed everyone around me was neurotypical.


58:27-58:29
I have since learned not to assume.


58:29-58:32
The more I learned, the more I shared on my channel.


58:32-58:35
The more I shared on my channel, the more I realized I wasn't alone.


58:35-58:44
People from all over the world started commenting and openly sharing their ADHD struggles after hearing me talk openly about mine.


58:44-58:50
For the first time, I didn't need to explain why I had trouble doing something to someone who looked at me as if I were defective or weird.


58:50-59:01
There were so many others who were nodding along and saying, "Me too." Not only was ADHD a lot more common than I realized, the struggle was real and not just for me.


59:02-59:06
If this condition is this common, where had everybody been hiding?


59:06-59:11
How did the struggles of so many people go unnoticed and unspoken, and why?


59:11-59:16
Depending on the study, between 3% and 8% of the population has ADHD.


59:16-59:20
There's a ton of stigma around ADHD.


59:20-59:28
Many ADHDers don't feel comfortable disclosing their diagnosis because of the stigma, misunderstandings, and misconceptions around it.


59:28-59:36
While it is illegal, discrimination from employers, by professors, even medical professionals does happen.


59:36-59:39
Some people don't even realize they have ADHD.


59:39-59:46
Many parents either don't get their kids evaluated or don't tell their kids about their diagnosis because they are afraid of what will happen if they have a label.


59:46-59:56
There is also serious stigma regarding the medications used to treat ADHD, which leads to prejudice and discrimination against those who take them.


59:56-01:00:02
Those with ADHD sometimes feel the need to hide the fact that they take them, even from those closest to them.


01:00:02-01:00:10
Parents of children with ADHD are accused online, in person, and even by healthcare providers of drugging their children into compliance.


01:00:10-01:00:21
When I shared a heartfelt letter to my mom on an episode, thanking her for drugging me, it wasn't long before I saw a picture of myself on the internet with the word evil written across my forehead.


01:00:21-01:00:28
We're held to the same expectations as our neurotypical peers, and we're punished for not meeting them.


01:00:28-01:00:39
Regardless of our level of impairment and whether we're receiving treatment and our accommodations, ADHDers are often expected to meet the same standards as our neurotypical peers and punished when we don't.


01:00:39-01:00:44
So we learn to mask our ADHD behaviors and do what's expected of us.


01:00:44-01:00:48
Be quiet, sit still, pay attention when we're in public.


01:00:48-01:00:49
We pay the price later.


01:00:49-01:00:56
Melt down, feel exhausted, stare blankly at a wall, or scroll through social media for hours just trying to recharge.


01:00:56-01:01:03
Even when others are aware of our diagnosis, we're often taught how to hide our struggles rather than effectively cope with them.


01:01:03-01:01:07
We might suppress our too big emotions by overeating.


01:01:07-01:01:12
We may force ourselves to sit still even though the effort means we can't focus on the lesson.


01:01:12-01:01:20
We clean our house in a whirlwind of panic when someone is coming over, often at the expense of being able to find our stuff again when they leave.


01:01:20-01:01:22
Why did I put the good frying pan under the bed?


01:01:22-01:01:30
We pull all-nighters trying to get projects done on time then sleep through the class where we were supposed to turn them in.


01:01:30-01:01:33
We often develop anxiety, depression, or both.


01:01:33-01:01:40
Many of us self-medicate using alcohol or other drugs, and these conditions too can mask the ADHD.


01:01:40-01:01:47
No one, including us, realizes how much harder we're struggling.


01:01:47-01:01:53
A lot of our individual symptoms are something everyone struggles with sometimes, so we think that what we're struggling with is normal.


01:01:53-01:02:01
Likewise, others may not realize the extent to which we're struggling because the behaviors they can see are only the tip of the iceberg.


01:02:01-01:02:08
We're trying so hard to meet neurotypical expectations, it might seem we're doing okay, even when we're not.


01:02:08-01:02:13
The soul-crushing effort it took for us to meet those expectations isn't obvious.


01:02:13-01:02:21
When I was in college, what people saw was a self-sufficient student who made the dean's honor roll, worked on campus, and had a boyfriend.


01:02:21-01:02:33
What no one saw was that I was eating lemon cookies for lunch every day, signing up for classes that weren't gonna help me graduate, and choosing my boyfriends based on who could help me with my math homework.


01:02:33-01:02:37
I learned later that others in my life had similar tip of the iceberg experiences.


01:02:37-01:02:45
People who impressed me with their accomplishments, who seemed to have their lives together in ways I definitely didn't, revealed to me years later, "Hey, guess what?


01:02:45-01:02:49
I have ADHD," and poured out the litany of struggles beneath the surface.


01:02:49-01:02:53
Someone with ADHD can be highly successful.


01:02:53-01:03:04
I've spoken to ADHD entertainers, doctors, and CEOs, but it's critical to understand that success doesn't mean they don't have ADHD, or that it isn't impairing.


01:03:04-01:03:13
We can accomplish great things, but without appropriate support, it tends to come at great expense in other areas of our life, or at the expense of ourselves.


01:03:14-01:03:19
For those with access to all the supports they need, ADHD can feel like a superpower.


01:03:19-01:03:24
They can lean into their creativity and hyper-focus without worrying about drowning in the sea of life.


01:03:24-01:03:28
For those without enough supports, ADHD can be a nightmare.


01:03:28-01:03:33
They constantly need to defend themselves against criticism and shame from their families and friends.


01:03:33-01:03:37
They burn out at work and struggle through tasks that everyone tells them should be easy.


01:03:37-01:03:41
They take longer to succeed, and it comes at a much greater cost.


01:03:43-01:03:46
Sean T., 39, California.


01:03:46-01:03:54
I've assumed I had ADHD for the past 20 years, but didn't realize a diagnosis would make a difference until I discovered Jessica's channel and started learning.


01:03:54-01:03:57
I got diagnosed two months ago, and I'm now trying out medication.


01:03:57-01:04:04
This diagnosis has been very powerful for me already, because I now have such a wealth of knowledge about the nature of my struggles in life.


01:04:04-01:04:11
The solidarity of other ADHDers has probably been an even bigger help than meds, but don't take my meds, they help too.


01:04:12-01:04:15
Daniel C., 36, Kansas.


01:04:15-01:04:18
I was diagnosed with ADHD as an adult.


01:04:18-01:04:19
I am a college professor.


01:04:19-01:04:22
I had a month to prepare for a challenging class.


01:04:22-01:04:27
Instead, I binged an entire season of a Netflix show in a single day sitting at my desk.


01:04:27-01:04:31
I decided I was depressed and reached out to a therapist.


01:04:31-01:04:34
She almost immediately asked if I had ever wondered if I had ADHD.


01:04:34-01:04:41
I looked back on my life and started remembering things, like how I locked my keys in my car eight times the first year I had a car.


01:04:41-01:04:47
I remembered how the only time I ever got detention was when I forgot to get my straight A report card signed.


01:04:47-01:04:50
My therapist went through the diagnostic criteria with me.


01:04:50-01:04:54
I went to my primary care doctor, and he agreed with my therapist's assessment.


01:04:54-01:04:56
The diagnosis was such a relief.


01:04:56-01:05:00
Brody S., 26, Pennsylvania.


01:05:00-01:05:08
It's really hard to explain to others when I'm having an ADHD struggle, because I don't wanna sound like a broken record, where my whole personality starts to become, "Hi, I have ADHD.


01:05:08-01:05:12
"Did you know I have ADHD?" I also don't wanna sound like I'm making excuses.


01:05:12-01:05:17
It took me a year of knowing I have ADHD to actually figure out what that means.


01:05:17-01:05:24
Being fully open about it is difficult when I know others won't understand the experience of living with a brain that behaves the way mine does.


01:05:24-01:05:29
That said, I try to be open about it while taking responsibility for the impact it has on those around me.


01:05:29-01:05:30
It's still just difficult.


01:05:30-01:05:34
Jill C., 32, Virginia.


01:05:34-01:05:40
I was diagnosed with ADHD when I was seven, but didn't actually know that it was a medical disorder until I was 14.


01:05:40-01:05:43
I think I saw it as a kind of personality trait.


01:05:43-01:05:47
I thought that it was normal to spend hours and hours on homework every night.


01:05:47-01:05:56
I thought it was normal for everything to be hard, for your teachers to be constantly frustrated with you, and to feel like you were in the way of everyone else's progress, because you couldn't do anything right.


01:05:56-01:05:59
I'm not the stereotypical picture of ADHD.


01:05:59-01:06:03
I'm pretty neat and organized on the outside, but inside my head is a mess.


01:06:03-01:06:08
As an adult, sifting through the mental clutter to find what I need takes a lot of time and effort.


01:06:08-01:06:18
I realized recently that my whole life has been watching people get frustrated with me for not moving fast enough or not understanding something fast enough, and that is probably not going to change.


01:06:18-01:06:24
I've been able to make my life much more pleasant by expecting this reaction, not hating them for it, and not hating myself for it.


01:06:24-01:06:27
The Toolbox.


01:06:27-01:06:31
It now made sense why my old approach to ADHD didn't work terribly well for me.


01:06:31-01:06:35
Trying harder didn't account for how hard I was already trying.


01:06:35-01:06:43
It also didn't account for the fact that I have ADHD, which affects many aspects of my life and much more significantly than I'd realized.


01:06:43-01:06:49
Now that I understand this, I take a much different approach to my struggles, and it's what I recommend for anyone with ADHD.


01:06:49-01:06:53
One, take ADHD seriously.


01:06:53-01:06:59
It's easy to dismiss unseen disabilities, but ADHD can be incredibly disabling.


01:06:59-01:07:05
If it's not clear by now, our invisible obstacles can keep us from reaching our goals, even when we try our hardest.


01:07:05-01:07:09
Even mild ADHD significantly impacts multiple aspects of our lives.


01:07:09-01:07:12
If it didn't, it wouldn't qualify for a diagnosis.


01:07:12-01:07:19
Mild is an example of clinical language not meaning the same thing as everyday language, even when the same word is used.


01:07:19-01:07:24
Imagine a struggle scale from one to 10, and typical struggles are one to seven.


01:07:24-01:07:30
Diagnoses would be eight to 10, and a mild diagnosis would be an eight, which is already a big deal.


01:07:31-01:07:38
Reassurances of how great it is to have ADHD or pretending the struggles we face don't exist might feel good in the moment.


01:07:38-01:07:45
However, our impairments exist whether we acknowledge them or not, just like the assignments we're avoiding.


01:07:45-01:07:54
Our sense of self and sense of empowerment ends up being much stronger if we are able to accept and understand ourselves as we are, not as we feel we should be.


01:07:54-01:07:58
Some people think that acknowledging your impairments means placing limits on yourself.


01:07:58-01:08:01
I've found quite the opposite is true.


01:08:01-01:08:06
Learning to recognize them and figuring out how to navigate them has made me more functional, not less.


01:08:06-01:08:11
Taking ADHD seriously starts with recognition.


01:08:11-01:08:18
The only way we can recognize and work with our challenges is by being honest about them, whether or not we think they're a big enough deal.


01:08:18-01:08:28
None of the treatments, strategies, accommodations, or environmental changes I lay out in this audiobook will be as useful if we deny the extent of our challenges and don't think we really need help.


01:08:29-01:08:32
Two, connect with others.


01:08:32-01:08:39
As much as reading the research helped me understand my impairments and take them seriously, being around others with ADHD helped normalize them.


01:08:39-01:08:48
Something magical happened when we swapped stories about the struggles we felt ashamed of and the times we needed supports that others thought were childish.


01:08:48-01:08:50
Sticker charts work for adults too.


01:08:50-01:09:03
The stencil of who we were supposed to be fell away and what was left was who we were, curious, passionate, divergent thinkers, struggling to exist in a world not built for us.


01:09:03-01:09:07
In myself, I only saw deficits.


01:09:07-01:09:10
I didn't see the ways in which my ADHD traits could be valuable.


01:09:10-01:09:12
In others, I could.


01:09:12-01:09:22
They were funny, caring, fascinating, generous, creative, silly, passionate, ambitious, and they were like me.


01:09:22-01:09:26
Because I liked them so much, I finally started to like me.


01:09:26-01:09:29
Maybe I had value too, despite my struggles.


01:09:29-01:09:34
