Spending time with people whose brains work the way yours does is an incredible experience.


01:09:34-01:09:47
The shame begins to fall away and we begin to see ourselves through one another's eyes as the funny or talented or curious or ambitious humans we are, with struggles that are perfectly normal.


01:09:47-01:09:52
Because while they might not make sense to those who are neurotypical, they are normal when you have ADHD.


01:09:53-01:09:56
Three, work with your brain, not against it.


01:09:56-01:10:02
Whenever you experience that gear grinding feeling that tells you something isn't working, don't try harder, try different.


01:10:02-01:10:07
Trying different starts by learning how to work with your brain.


01:10:07-01:10:15
Those of us with ADHD often work far better and faster when we're not forcing ourselves to do things the right, read, neurotypical way.


01:10:15-01:10:19
When our brains are engaged, our performance can be exceptional.


01:10:19-01:10:26
It can be worthwhile to spend a little extra time figuring out how to do something in a way that works for your brain, even if it seems unconventional.


01:10:26-01:10:29
Here's what working with my brain looks like for me.


01:10:29-01:10:33
Focusing on what does work for me rather than what should.


01:10:33-01:10:38
Building a toolbox of strategies I can use for tasks with which I struggle.


01:10:38-01:10:42
Doing my most challenging work at times when my brain works best.


01:10:42-01:10:46
When possible, choosing ADHD-friendly products and services.


01:10:46-01:10:51
Asking for the tools and accommodations I need or providing them myself.


01:10:52-01:10:55
Approaching tasks as a negotiation.


01:10:55-01:10:57
What do I need to get the thing done?


01:10:57-01:10:59
What does my brain need for that to be able to happen?


01:10:59-01:11:02
Keep going.


01:11:02-01:11:10
I failed so often, had so much, and so consistently throughout my life that I was riddled with self-doubt while I was building my channel.


01:11:10-01:11:15
There was a near constant voice in the back of my head telling me, "You can't do this.


01:11:15-01:11:16
"You'll fail.


01:11:16-01:11:27
"You're just gonna give up someday, "just like you always do." When I was just starting out and still hustling as a server, it was really hard not to give up because I was running into obstacles constantly.


01:11:27-01:11:32
Some weeks I'd fall behind and need to skip shifts at work to catch up.


01:11:32-01:11:40
Sometimes I wouldn't notice the battery in my camera had died because I had to go to work, I couldn't reshoot the video until midnight, sometimes one or two a.m.


01:11:40-01:11:46
Once I couldn't get myself to write a script for weeks because the research on the topic was so painful.


01:11:46-01:11:56
Another time I was trying to steam my new backdrop from underneath and the lid from the steamer came off, pouring scalding hot water onto my face.


01:11:56-01:11:59
I ended up in the ER with face burns on the day I was supposed to shoot.


01:11:59-01:12:09
A litany of things went wrong, but I fought through all of it, partly because I cared so deeply about what I was doing and partly because I wanted to prove that voice wrong.


01:12:09-01:12:13
But one week, I couldn't argue with it anymore.


01:12:13-01:12:18
I hadn't written an episode by the time it was supposed to have been shot and edited.


01:12:18-01:12:21
I wasn't just failing, I had failed.


01:12:21-01:12:28
I posted a tweet formally communicating this failure, explaining to my community, maybe that voice was right, maybe I can't do this.


01:12:28-01:12:34
I waited for my community to agree with me and release me from the promise I'd made to help them.


01:12:34-01:12:38
I expected them to tell me it was okay to go do something else now.


01:12:38-01:12:41
Maybe they would berate me, which I figured I deserved.


01:12:41-01:12:44
Instead, I got encouragement.


01:12:44-01:12:47
What? No, you're doing great, keep going.


01:12:47-01:12:49
Keep going.


01:12:49-01:12:52
I didn't know that was an option.


01:12:52-01:13:16
It sounds ridiculous, but living with ADHD and grappling with its challenges left me believing that you could only fail so many times before it was game over, before I'd run out of time, out of understanding, before I'd get fired or someone would stop being my friend, before I'd hit my limit of disappointing people, before I'd hit my limit of disappointing myself, before I could give up.


01:13:16-01:13:20
Once you've failed failed, you had to move on and try something else.


01:13:20-01:13:21
That's how it went, right?


01:13:21-01:13:24
I had been given permission to stop.


01:13:24-01:13:27
Now I expected permission to quit.


01:13:27-01:13:30
What I got was permission to keep going.


01:13:30-01:13:39
My community's encouragement inspired me to make a video about how success isn't about avoiding failure, it's about continuing despite it.


01:13:39-01:13:43
Does that mean you should always keep trying to do something you keep failing at?


01:13:43-01:13:52
No, sometimes we fail because something isn't a great fit for us and continuing to bang your head against the wall won't get you anywhere or anything except a headache.


01:13:52-01:13:59
But if something is important to you, you can keep going, even if you fail.


01:13:59-01:14:02
Failing doesn't make you a failure.


01:14:02-01:14:05
It isn't the opposite of succeeding as I'd feared.


01:14:05-01:14:09
It's something that happens and will happen all along the way.


01:14:09-01:14:15
What allows us to keep going when the failures pile up is the encouragement of others.


01:14:15-01:14:20
It's like the quarters you use to keep playing an arcade game after you run out of lives.


01:14:20-01:14:29
Sometimes someone says something so perfect, so powerful that it's like a magical quarter, one you can use again and again.


01:14:29-01:14:35
I've failed hard, utterly and undeniably, a few times since that day.


01:14:35-01:14:43
Every time shame and despair almost derailed me, I used that magical quarter given to me by someone in my community telling me I was doing great.


01:14:43-01:14:44
Keep going.


01:14:44-01:14:57
If anyone thinks they're unimportant or that it doesn't make a difference to offer encouragement to someone they believe in, know that this audio book you are listening to wouldn't exist if it weren't for someone who did.


01:14:57-01:15:00
I hope this audio book leaves you with pockets full of quarters.


01:15:00-01:15:03
For now, I'll lend you mine.


01:15:03-01:15:04
You're doing great.


01:15:04-01:15:05
Keep going.


01:15:09-01:15:12
Chapter three, how to hyper focus.


01:15:12-01:15:16
I can't blame modern technology for my predilection for distraction.


01:15:16-01:15:20
Not after all the hours I've spent watching lost balloons disappear into the clouds.


01:15:20-01:15:22
Colson Whitehead.


01:15:22-01:15:26
Focus.


01:15:26-01:15:28
Jessica, focus.


01:15:28-01:15:35
People have told me to focus, to pay attention, as if it is a choice I can make or an action I can take, something I should be able to just do.


01:15:36-01:15:39
But focus for me wasn't a verb.


01:15:39-01:15:47
It was a noun, not something I could do so much as an elusive, unreliable creature I attempted to capture over and over again.


01:15:47-01:15:58
It felt as if I were on a relentless quest for some mythical animal, one that was magically bound to me, but also had a penchant for mischief and liked to play hide and go seek, the focus beast.


01:15:58-01:16:05
More often than not, I didn't have enough focus, noun, to be able to focus, verb.


01:16:05-01:16:09
At least not on whatever task I was trying to accomplish at the time.


01:16:09-01:16:13
When I managed to find it, I learned quickly to take advantage of it.


01:16:13-01:16:16
As a kid, I would try to pay attention to my teachers in class.


01:16:16-01:16:21
I loved to learn, but it was never long before the words and dates would blend together.


01:16:21-01:16:29
I can still recall the terror of being called on when I knew I wasn't focused, the teacher knew I wasn't focused, and the entire class was about to find out.


01:16:29-01:16:31
The shame that followed.


01:16:31-01:16:33
I'm sorry, I got distracted.


01:16:34-01:16:41
When I was told to focus, what I actually heard was that I should stop hunting for my focus and pretend I'd found it.


01:16:41-01:16:49
Like the writers in Monty Python and the Holy Grail, I would perform all the gestures one does when riding a focus beast without actually having one to ride.


01:16:49-01:16:55
It felt ridiculous, but at least pretending to focus was doable to a certain extent.


01:16:55-01:16:59
I could sit relatively still by digging my fingernails into my palm.


01:16:59-01:17:00
I could stare at my book.


01:17:00-01:17:06
In reality, I was about as focused as a potato, but hey, it kept me from getting called on in class.


01:17:06-01:17:07
It kept teachers happy.


01:17:07-01:17:09
It kept me from getting yelled at.


01:17:09-01:17:15
When I'm focused or starting to find my focus, I often don't look as if I'm doing it at all.


01:17:15-01:17:18
I'm probably eating something or playing with my hair.


01:17:18-01:17:23
I might be listening to music, swaying or rocking, or my eyes are closed.


01:17:23-01:17:27
Maybe I'm texting my friend because I can't wait to share what I just learned.


01:17:27-01:17:34
If I'm in class, I might be raising my hand every five seconds to ask a question or picking up my phone to Google what I just learned.


01:17:34-01:17:39
In stories, you can capture and tame a magical creature by casting a spell.


01:17:39-01:17:45
I tried to make my own magic, experimenting with different ingredients and incantations.


01:17:45-01:17:51
Occasionally, if I happened on the right combination and no one interrupts my spell casting, my focus appears.


01:17:51-01:18:02
I can tune out all the other things pulling on my attention, the clock ticking, the squeaky chair, the tags in my clothing, and ride my focus long enough to get where I need to go.


01:18:02-01:18:08
Unfortunately, casting the spell involves some weird ingredients and they seem to change every time.


01:18:08-01:18:15
One time I cast a focused spell by doing my work while eating animal crackers in a kigurumi on a swing set.


01:18:15-01:18:24
The next time though, the swing set hurt my hands, the kigurumi was too hot, and the animal crackers reminded me that I'd been wanting to go to the zoo and hey, where even is the nearest zoo?


01:18:24-01:18:25
I should look it up.


01:18:25-01:18:29
Like so many junior wizards, my spell is sometimes backfired on me.


01:18:29-01:18:34
Other times, I wasn't the one charming the beast, it was casting its spell on me.


01:18:34-01:18:43
I'd sit down only to find myself hours later, pretzeled up in my chair, having missed lunch, but having planned an entire wedding, despite the fact that I was not in fact engaged.


01:18:43-01:18:49
My laptop would be almost dead, sticky notes everywhere, I'd gotten 17 text messages and I hadn't heard any of them ding.


01:18:49-01:18:56
If I ran out of steam and came out of the state naturally, if I had the chance to ride hard and slow down, it was an incredible experience.


01:18:56-01:19:04
If on the other hand, I rode right past the time I was supposed to leave for work or meet the friend I was supposed to have lunch with, it would get me into trouble.


01:19:04-01:19:11
Ironically, not only did I have to learn to hunt down focus, I also had to learn to rein it in so I wouldn't crash into things.


01:19:11-01:19:21
After I was diagnosed with attention deficit disorder and given stimulant medication, it was like someone handed me a potion, makes focus appear, duration four to six hours.


01:19:21-01:19:26
Something that would give me focus, even on things that were boring, it was magical.


01:19:26-01:19:34
Finally, I wasn't burning all my energy hunting down focus or running out of spell slots or animal crackers trying to summon it.


01:19:34-01:19:37
I now had focus and I could spend my energy on using it.


01:19:37-01:19:41
Meds weren't a perfect solution.


01:19:41-01:19:45
I still had to be careful about what I was doing once my focus potion kicked in.


01:19:45-01:19:52
Once it kicked in while I was in the bathtub scrolling through social media and I didn't get out of that bathtub for four hours.


01:19:52-01:19:57
Sometimes I couldn't afford my meds because I didn't have insurance or my new insurance didn't cover them.


01:19:57-01:20:04
Occasionally the pharmacy didn't have them in stock or I get to the pharmacy but couldn't pick them up because they are a controlled substance and I'd forgotten my ID.


01:20:04-01:20:06
Have I mentioned I have ADHD?


01:20:06-01:20:09
I might forget to make an appointment to get a new prescription.


01:20:09-01:20:12
Again, controlled substance, you can't just refill it.


01:20:12-01:20:16
There were times I couldn't find a provider willing to prescribe ADHD meds at all.


01:20:16-01:20:21
When I didn't have my meds, everything seemed even foggier than before.


01:20:21-01:20:27
Having a focus potion didn't make me any better at focus casting and it made me fall out of practice.


01:20:27-01:20:34
I'd gotten so used to having focus when I needed it, it felt even more frustrating and discouraging to have to search for it again.


01:20:34-01:20:42
I was grateful to have found a potion that worked for me and one I had access to most of the time but I didn't like feeling so powerless without it.


01:20:42-01:20:47
Trouble with focus was an ongoing lifelong battle I was incredibly tired of fighting.


01:20:47-01:20:52
So when I started digging into ADHD research, my first focus was on focus.


01:20:52-01:20:55
What I learned.


01:20:55-01:20:59
For 20 years, I thought I had a deficit of attention.


01:20:59-01:21:03
I mean, it's in the name of my diagnosis, attention deficit disorder.


01:21:03-01:21:08
Brains need a full tank of attention to work properly so I must be a few quarts low, right?


01:21:08-01:21:09
Wrong.


01:21:09-01:21:12
The reality is we have plenty of attention.


01:21:12-01:21:15
What we lack is the ability to regulate our attention.


01:21:15-01:21:19
You know how lizards can't internally regulate their body temperature?


01:21:19-01:21:25
ADHD brains have difficulty regulating their focus and emotions and sleep and okay, yeah, we'll get to that later.


01:21:25-01:21:32
The ability to control our focus, also called top down attentional control, relies on the prefrontal cortex.


01:21:32-01:21:37
It is the last part of the brain to develop and it develops even more slowly in those with ADHD.


01:21:37-01:21:40
And even once it is fully developed, it's still impaired.


01:21:40-01:21:47
This is why we often have to go to great spell casty, potiony lengths to support our ability to focus.


01:21:47-01:21:56
It's easy to assume we're not focused because we're closing our eyes or doodling or shuffling things around on our desk, but we're often doing those things because we're trying to find focus.


01:21:56-01:22:02
Just as lizards externally regulate their temperature, we try to externally regulate our attention.


01:22:02-01:22:05
It's like somebody left the door open.


01:22:05-01:22:15
In the first video I ever watched about ADHD, a woman posting under the name Just Jen described her doctor's explanation of attention as a door that was always open.


01:22:15-01:22:20
Other people could close their door and focus, but in ADHD brains, the door lets everything in.


01:22:20-01:22:23
We can't ignore the things other people might be able to tune out.


01:22:23-01:22:42
Remember how often you heard a teacher say, "Keep your eyes on your own work." In laboratory studies, scientists used eye tracking to measure how often people with ADHD shifted their attention away from the task they were working on and, "Wow, we can't keep our eyes on our own work." At least not to the extent that our non-ADHP peers are able to.


01:22:42-01:22:46
It isn't just external distractions that are hard to tune out either.


01:22:46-01:22:53
Our brains also have a harder time shutting the door on our anxieties, negative thoughts, or brilliant new ideas.


01:22:53-01:22:55
We also have a harder time remembering things.


01:22:55-01:23:00
Sometimes what's distracting us is our effort to remember or communicate something we don't wanna forget.


01:23:00-01:23:03
Sometimes focus is more like a tunnel.


01:23:03-01:23:15
The flip side of the open door distractibility is hyper-focus, the experience of being so engaged we don't notice anything outside our hyper-focused tunnel and often can't pull our attention away.


01:23:15-01:23:19
A lot of missed diagnoses can be attributed to the phenomenon of hyper-focus.


01:23:19-01:23:24
People think someone can't have ADHD because they can focus so well when they want to.


01:23:24-01:23:31
But hyper-focus is a function of the wonky attention regulation typical of ADHD brains.


01:23:31-01:23:33
We don't choose to hyper-focus on something.


01:23:33-01:23:34
We get sucked in.


01:23:34-01:23:42
When that happens, we lose track of time and we don't come out of it until we're done, dead, or something or someone pulls us out.


01:23:42-01:23:46
Sometimes hyper-focus works out great for us and sometimes it doesn't.


01:23:46-01:23:53
Dr. Russell Barkley calls this perseveration, the inability to pull ourselves away from things we don't even want to be doing or doing anymore.


01:23:53-01:23:59
Hyper-focusing on that paper we need to get done when we've got nowhere else we need to be, fantastic.


01:23:59-01:24:05
Hyper-focusing on a book you're reading and missing the book club meeting you were reading at four, not so much.


01:24:05-01:24:09
True story, my aunt Susie, who also has ADHD, told me that this just happened to her.


01:24:09-01:24:13
I was really looking forward to talking about the book.


01:24:13-01:24:20
Because focusing is so hard for us, hyper-focus on something we're supposed to be working on can feel like a get out of struggling free card.


01:24:20-01:24:22
For once, we don't have to fight with our brains.


01:24:22-01:24:27
Still, it's important to remember that any focus comes at a cost.


01:24:27-01:24:35
The time may have flown by, but if our brains were working for 10 hours straight, we're gonna feel it the next day and probably have a harder time focusing.


01:24:35-01:24:39
Our ability to focus is interest-based.


01:24:39-01:24:45
ADHD brains are chronically under-stimulated, which is why treatment involves stimulant medication.


01:24:45-01:24:53
When something isn't interesting to us, it's harder for us to pay attention to it, even if we care about learning or doing the thing we're being asked to do.


01:24:53-01:24:58
There's a clip from "Parks and Recreation" that perfectly illustrates this phenomenon.


01:24:58-01:25:13
Lovable goofball Andy Dwyer interrupts his boss's instructions to enthusiastically exclaim, "I wasn't super paying attention "to what you just said we would be doing, "but I will give it 110% "as soon as you repeat yourself in a more interesting way." Andy doesn't mean to ignore his boss.


01:25:13-01:25:19
His brain was likely operating in what brain scientists call the default mode network, DMN.


01:25:19-01:25:26
It was on autopilot, listening without actually listening, while his attention wandered off in search of something more engaging.


01:25:26-01:25:29
His amusing outburst is honestly quite insightful.


01:25:29-01:25:34
He's trying to self-advocate for an accommodation that will allow him to focus on his boss's request.


01:25:34-01:25:40
The default mode network is more active in ADHD brains than neurotypical brains.


01:25:40-01:25:44
One of my favorite explanations of ADHD I've ever seen in the research is chronic mind wandering.


01:25:44-01:25:47
This tendency isn't a choice.


01:25:47-01:25:49
It's due to our brain structure.


01:25:49-01:25:55
There is more gray matter in the default mode network of ADHD brains than neurotypical brains.


01:25:55-01:26:03
Because of this, it's easier for this part of the brain to be activated and stay activated, leaving the door open for distractions and shower thoughts.


01:26:03-01:26:07
This is also why those with ADHD are better at [[divergent thinking]]
.


01:26:08-01:26:17
Divergent thinking, noun, a cognitive process that generates creative ideas by exploring many possible solutions or bouncing from one thought to the next.


01:26:17-01:26:24
Divergent thinking generally occurs spontaneously, is seldom linear, and tends to produce abundant and unique ideas.


01:26:24-01:26:28
We often aren't sure what to focus on.


01:26:28-01:26:34
In addition to having difficulty regulating our attention, we have a harder time prioritizing what to focus on.


01:26:34-01:26:40
The signal, the task we need to do, is harder for us to distinguish from the noise, which is everything else.


01:26:40-01:26:45
If something stands out as urgent, it's easier to see it as a priority.


01:26:45-01:26:50
We may end up focusing on that, often at the expense of things that are more important.


01:26:50-01:26:54
When it isn't clear what is urgent, the signals can all sound the same, and we often just get stuck.


01:26:54-01:26:58
Decision paralysis is a common experience for those of us with ADHD.


01:26:58-01:27:09
When we try to narrow down our choices, a process that relies on convergent thinking, our divergent thinking brains often keep handing us more options to choose from, and we can end up overwhelmed.


01:27:09-01:27:15
Anxiety about choosing the wrong thing can also play a role, as can our difficulties with how we perceive time.


01:27:15-01:27:22
On the flip side, if everything we need to do is urgent, we might try to get it all done at the same time, with mixed results.


01:27:22-01:27:25
A note on multitasking.


01:27:25-01:27:30
Those with ADHD are more likely than our neurotypical peers to multitask.


01:27:30-01:27:35
Unfortunately, research shows that multitasking is not the time saver we think it is.


01:27:35-01:27:42
As amazing as it feels to be doing multiple things at once, brains aren't actually capable of focusing on more than one thing at once.


01:27:42-01:27:52
What actually happens when we're multitasking is either that one of the tasks doesn't truly require our attention, or we're shifting our attention back and forth quickly between the different tasks.


01:27:52-01:28:02
Each shift of attention costs a tiny bit of time, and getting fully refocused when we switch back to a task we've switched away from can take up to 25 minutes.


01:28:03-01:28:10
Mistakes are also more common while multitasking, and for us, so is completely forgetting about one of the tasks entirely.


01:28:10-01:28:18
For tasks that don't require our full attention, like folding laundry for the thousandth time while watching reruns of a sitcom, multitasking works fine.


01:28:18-01:28:23
It can even be beneficial because it makes the task less boring, which means we're more likely to do it.


01:28:23-01:28:31
Tasks that do require attention, like cooking a meal you've never made before while helping your kid with their calculus homework, can end in comedic disaster.


01:28:32-01:28:34
There's a lot of noise.


01:28:34-01:28:42
We are living in a world where we carry shiny supercomputers in our pockets that can access every book ever written in seconds and also play games.


01:28:42-01:28:46
Our watches beep, and our phones bloop, and our web browsers show us pop-ups in pop-ups.


01:28:46-01:28:52
We all deal with a barrage of stimuli competing for our attention, whether or not we have ADHD.


01:28:52-01:29:02
This incredibly distracting environment is leading even those without ADHD to struggle with focus, what Dr. Ned Halliwell calls the attention deficit trait.


01:29:02-01:29:04
This is not the same thing as having ADHD.


01:29:04-01:29:14
According to Halliwell, if you, an attention-challenged person, abandon all the distractions of modernity, go live on a farm, and finally find peace, you don't have ADHD.


01:29:14-01:29:20
If instead you show up to the farm and get so bored you decide to build a carnival, congratulations, you are one of us.


01:29:20-01:29:24
Cone S, 33, Belgium.


01:29:24-01:29:29
My normal focus feels like squeezing the last bit of toothpaste from the tube.


01:29:29-01:29:33
If it's not really fun or challenging, it's really draining to have to focus.


01:29:33-01:29:37
Joshua S, 31, Germany.


01:29:37-01:29:41
Focus needs a lot of strategies or tools to work for an ADHD brain.


01:29:41-01:29:43
It's like carrying a bucket of water up a hill.


01:29:43-01:29:46
If you got the tools, the bucket, it can work, but it's exhausting.


01:29:46-01:29:49
It's difficult to get started and easy to stop.


01:29:49-01:29:53
Miriam R, 30, Canada.


01:29:53-01:29:57
Focus and hyper-focus is the difference between riding a bicycle and riding a bullet train.


01:29:57-01:30:02
Both will get me to my destination, but one requires exertion on my part and the other is effortless.


01:30:02-01:30:05
The Toolbox.


01:30:05-01:30:08
So, what's a brain to do?


01:30:08-01:30:17
ADHD meds, if they work for you, can work wonders and are a good place to start because they give our brains the stimulation they need and allow us to better regulate our focus.


01:30:17-01:30:24
But they don't work for everyone, typically wear off at the end or even middle of the day, and aren't always an option.


01:30:24-01:30:29
I have found developing other tools to support our attention to be critical.


01:30:29-01:30:35
That way, when you need to, or someone tells you to focus, you have options.


01:30:35-01:30:46
My brilliant friend and middle school teacher, Jo Moleck-Voigt, teaches her students to think of focus using this acronym, figure out the cause, and use a strategy.


01:30:46-01:30:47
Focus.


01:30:47-01:30:53
This changes focus from a noun, something we don't always have, to a verb, something we can actually do.


01:30:55-01:30:59
One, boost the signal and decrease the noise.


01:30:59-01:31:10
Because the signal of what we're supposed to be doing easily gets lost in the noise of everything else, what can help is both to strengthen that signal and decrease the noise around it.


01:31:10-01:31:16
That way, it's clearer what needs our focus and it's easier to find our way back to it when we inevitably wander away.


01:31:16-01:31:18
There are a few ways to do this.


01:31:18-01:31:21
Create clear cues.


01:31:21-01:31:25
ADHD brains are more sensitive to environmental cues than neurotypical brains.


01:31:25-01:31:34
We can take advantage of this fact by intentionally adding reminders of what we're supposed to be doing into our line of sight and moving reminders of things we don't wanna be doing out of it.


01:31:34-01:31:37
Lay down the tracks.


01:31:37-01:31:45
Thinking through the order of what you wanna accomplish before you start doing it can make it easier to stay on track when there are a million distractions being thrown your way.


01:31:45-01:31:49
Our operations director, J2, calls this setting up her dominoes.


01:31:49-01:31:51
There are lots of ways to do this.


01:31:51-01:31:56
Make a list, review your calendar, imagine how you'd like your day to go before getting out of bed.


01:31:56-01:32:00
Fight distraction with distraction.


01:32:00-01:32:12
One thoughtfully chosen distraction, such as music, a TV show, or using a fidget, can provide our brains with enough stimulation so that they don't have to go searching for it and get distracted by something more exciting.


01:32:12-01:32:16
David A. 47, Canada.


01:32:16-01:32:21
I walk the dog, meditate, and then journal, putting anxieties on paper so that I don't forget.


01:32:21-01:32:25
Drink coffee and make sure I have a workspace that is quiet and private.


01:32:25-01:32:28
I'm good for about four hours until lunchtime before I really drift.


01:32:28-01:32:32
Mike G. 33, Montana.


01:32:32-01:32:34
Hacking my focus is hard.


01:32:34-01:32:39
Meds help me overcome the obstacles to focus, which helps me slide into hyper-focus more easily.


01:32:39-01:32:47
Pomodoro timers, timers or apps that help break up tasks into doable portions, help overcome the switching/starting problems.


01:32:47-01:32:50
I can trick myself into doing something for 10 minutes.


01:32:50-01:32:53
Natasha L. 25, Florida.


01:32:53-01:32:55
Fill the stimulation meter.


01:32:55-01:33:00
Listen to a podcast while doing dishes or play music while cleaning to increase stimulation.


01:33:00-01:33:02
But find a balance.


01:33:02-01:33:09
You wanna fill the empty space by adding whatever stimulation is needed for the meter to hit 100%, which helps me focus.


01:33:09-01:33:14
If I am doing a high-focus task, I add low stimulation, like instrumental music, and vice versa.


01:33:14-01:33:16
That way, there's no room for my brain to stray.


01:33:18-01:33:21
Two, practice non-judgmental redirection.


01:33:21-01:33:23
Our brains are chaotic places.


01:33:23-01:33:25
Our thoughts are going to wander.


01:33:25-01:33:27
No one is able to control their thoughts entirely.


01:33:27-01:33:30
And if you have ADHD, it's even harder.


01:33:30-01:33:34
Thankfully, there are many strategies we can use to gently bring our attention back.


01:33:34-01:33:36
Practice mindfulness.


01:33:36-01:33:46
Mindfulness is the practice of intentionally bringing your attention back to whatever you're doing, thinking or feeling in the present moment in a non-judgmental, curious way.


01:33:46-01:33:49
If you are doing the dishes, you might notice how the water feels on your hands.


01:33:49-01:33:54
If you are avoiding doing the dishes, you might notice the feelings that come up for you.


01:33:54-01:33:57
Huh, interesting.


01:33:57-01:33:58
Moving on.


01:33:58-01:34:04
Practicing mindfulness improves self-awareness and strengthens our ability to redirect our attention when we need to.


01:34:04-01:34:06
Install some bumpers.


01:34:06-01:34:12
My favorite low-tech way to do this is to create a doing and not doing list on separate sticky notes.


01:34:12-01:34:16
Put both lists where you can see them while you work so you can notice if you've wandered off track.


01:34:16-01:34:19
You can also install physical barriers.


01:34:19-01:34:22
When I kept getting distracted while I was cooking, I put up a baby gate.


01:34:22-01:34:26
I knew I'd forget about the pot on the stove as soon as I wandered away from the kitchen.


01:34:26-01:34:32
Running into a physical barrier was an excellent reminder of what I was supposed to be doing and brought my attention back too.


01:34:32-01:34:35
If stuff pops up, put it down on paper.


01:34:35-01:34:41
If you suddenly think of something you need to do, write it down in a parking lot instead of acting on it right away.


01:34:41-01:34:47
You can take a look at what you've written when you're done working on the task at hand and see if it actually needs tending to right away.


01:34:47-01:34:52
Often, upon reviewing the things that came to mind, you might realize that it wasn't quite as important as you thought.


01:34:52-01:35:02
This strategy helps strengthen our distractibility delay, the ability to wait before responding to a distraction so you can stay on task, especially when it's one our brains want to avoid.


01:35:02-01:35:07
Madeleine R. 24, Canada.


01:35:07-01:35:11
I do five minutes of meditation and 10 minutes of easy yoga every single day.


01:35:11-01:35:12
It's a practice.


01:35:12-01:35:21
When I get distracted, I am now better able to simply be aware of what's happening, pay attention to my breath, and slowly veer myself back to the thing I want to be focusing on.


01:35:21-01:35:24
Nikki M. 35, USA.


01:35:24-01:35:27
I keep elastic coil bracelets around my house.


01:35:27-01:35:31
Whenever I do something that would be dangerous to forget about, like cooking, I put on a bracelet.


01:35:31-01:35:37
I will inevitably forget what I was doing and wander off, but will remember what I was doing when I start fidgeting with the bracelet.


01:35:37-01:35:40
Free P. 78, Georgia.


01:35:40-01:35:43
I grab a small legal pad and write down whatever is distracting me.


01:35:43-01:35:45
I have them in each room.


01:35:45-01:35:48
Then I take the note to myself and put it on my office desk.


01:35:48-01:35:54
When I find 10 minutes or so available, I go through my pile and ruthlessly discard most all of my notes.


01:35:54-01:36:00
Three, make space for hyperfocus and install guardrails.


01:36:00-01:36:03
Hyperfocus is a bit of a double-edged sword.


01:36:03-01:36:08
When we're in that kind of flow, we can get deeply absorbed in a task and forget about everything else.


01:36:08-01:36:14
But also, when we're in that kind of flow, we can get deeply absorbed in a task and forget about everything else.


01:36:14-01:36:24
Giving ourselves enough time to slip into hyperfocus and guardrails that help us come out of it when we need to move on, can allow us to take advantage of it without driving straight off a metaphorical cliff.


01:36:24-01:36:29
Create the right conditions for hyperfocus to happen.


01:36:29-01:36:38
Different brains achieve hyperfocus in different ways, but most of us require a certain amount of dedicated time plus an environment that is conducive to long periods of focused work.


01:36:38-01:36:46
Before work, I try to avoid doing things that will get my brain spinning on something else and intentionally get my brain spinning on the task I want it to do instead.


01:36:46-01:36:48
This is obviously not always possible.


01:36:48-01:36:52
I'm currently learning to build in pallet cleansers when I can't focus.


01:36:52-01:36:58
These pallet cleansers are activities I can do for a short period of time to unstick from one task so that I can move on to the next.


01:36:58-01:37:01
Establish a cutoff time.


01:37:01-01:37:07
Just because you might be able to hyperfocus for long periods doesn't mean it's helpful to keep working as long as you can.


01:37:07-01:37:18
Sometimes limiting your intense focus and having a cutoff so you can start winding down for bed, eat food, or do whatever else your body needs can help you to be productive the next day too.


01:37:18-01:37:22
This can lead to more good brain days and better productivity overall.


01:37:22-01:37:24
Just make sure to set an alarm.


01:37:24-01:37:27
Leave yourself breadcrumbs.


01:37:27-01:37:34
In some situations, we know we really need to stop, but we're afraid to stop because we're not sure if we can get started again.


01:37:34-01:37:40
When it's time for a hyperfocus spell to come to an end, use a bit of that remaining mental energy to write down these two things.


01:37:40-01:37:42
One, what did I just do?


01:37:42-01:37:46
And two, what would I have done if I could keep going?


01:37:46-01:37:55
These clues, like the breadcrumb path in the story of Hansel and Gretel, can make it easier to find your way back into the task the next day, unless they get eaten.


01:37:55-01:37:59
Pw32, Arizona.


01:37:59-01:38:01
I can easily get into hyperfocus with the right sort of music.


01:38:01-01:38:06
Upbeat but no outrageous tempo or lyrics, video game music works great.


01:38:06-01:38:11
An environment, low light, no distracting noises from other people, no notification noises.


01:38:11-01:38:15
Miriam R, 30, Canada.


01:38:15-01:38:20
When I enter hyperfocus, unless I've prepared an alarm in advance, I simply won't break out of it.


01:38:20-01:38:25
I'll keep reading or whatever until I reach the end or something really big jolts me out of it.


01:38:25-01:38:30
I can block out any audio except for something totally unexpected when I'm deep in thought.


01:38:30-01:38:33
Four, move your body.


01:38:33-01:38:40
While we're often encouraged to sit still to help us pay attention, it's typically easier to focus when we build movement into our routines.


01:38:40-01:38:42
Exercise.


01:38:42-01:38:50
Exercise produces dopamine and norepinephrine, the same chemicals that stimulant medication does to help with focus.


01:38:50-01:38:54
The focus boosting effects of exercise typically last for about an hour afterward.


01:38:54-01:38:57
So if you're trying to do something brain heavy, working out beforehand can help.


01:38:57-01:39:00
Explore alternative seating.


01:39:00-01:39:09
Walking while you work, by using a standing desk with an under the desk treadmill, for example, is a way to fidget while also getting some of the benefits of exercise.


01:39:09-01:39:17
Special seating, such as yoga balls, rocking or swivel chairs, or stationary bike chairs can provide additional stimulation to help focus your brain.


01:39:17-01:39:20
You can even use a regular chair and add bouncing bands.


01:39:20-01:39:24
It's often helpful to change it up based on our brain's needs that day.


01:39:24-01:39:31
My absolute favorite classroom ever was one that had desks and chairs close to the teacher and couches placed around the room.


01:39:31-01:39:38
I sat at the desks when I needed accountability to focus and I could relax on the couches during high anxiety days or when I was deeply engaged.


01:39:38-01:39:46
Being able to select seating based on my needs on that day was empowering and I learned so much more than I otherwise would have.


01:39:46-01:39:47
Go to another room.


01:39:47-01:39:56
When focus is hard, moving to a different room, building or even state, I wrote this chapter in a vacation rental in Arizona, can help you reset and refocus.


01:39:56-01:39:59
Sometimes finding focus is about the lack of distractions.


01:39:59-01:40:01
Sometimes it's about the novelty.


01:40:01-01:40:04
Ana Luisa, 26, Brazil.


01:40:04-01:40:06
I focus while moving.


01:40:06-01:40:08
It can be on a bus, walking or in a hammock.


01:40:08-01:40:10
If I'm moving, I'm engaged.


01:40:10-01:40:17
Usually when I have an impossible essay to get through, I record myself reading it at night and listen to it during my daily exercise.


01:40:17-01:40:25
I used to read on the go, but then I ran into a tree and well, let's just say one split eyebrow is enough to learn my lesson.


01:40:25-01:40:29
Alison B, 44, Virginia.


01:40:29-01:40:33
I chose my professional path, education, because it allows me to move around.


01:40:33-01:40:35
I am able to focus much better when I can move.


01:40:35-01:40:39
Teaching virtually was agonizing because I had to stay seated.


01:40:39-01:40:44
Thankfully, an under desk elliptical machine allowed me to get some activity while still doing my job.


01:40:44-01:40:49
Five, rest your brain.


01:40:49-01:40:53
Focus takes energy and any kind of focus comes at this cost.


01:40:53-01:40:56
Bad brain days are a thing with ADHD.


01:40:56-01:41:03
And while fighting through them is sometimes possible and necessary, it's important to consider whether the benefits outweigh the drawbacks.


01:41:03-01:41:09
If your brain needs a break, sometimes the best thing you can do is give it one so it has a better chance of focusing later.


01:41:09-01:41:12
Take brain breaks.


01:41:12-01:41:17
If you are losing mental energy but want to keep going, consider a time-limited brain break.


01:41:17-01:41:25
Taking a walk around the block, playing a relaxing game, or just staring out the window can be the difference between continued productivity and a pooped and cranky brain.


01:41:25-01:41:28
Plan time away from your task.


01:41:28-01:41:37
If you have a long, cognitively intense project, like studying for finals or writing a presentation, make sure you're scheduled to take time away from it if you can.


01:41:37-01:41:40
Visit the park on your lunch break or sign up for an exercise class.


01:41:40-01:41:42
Put the thing in a drawer for a week.


01:41:42-01:41:48
Not only will your brain get a chance to recharge, but time away from a stressor can also help prevent burnout.


01:41:48-01:41:51
Take a break from self-reg.


01:41:51-01:41:58
Many people with ADHD feel like they're bad at relaxing because while they're trying to relax, they end up doing something else.


01:41:58-01:42:03
What I have learned is that for us, taking a break from self-regulating is a break.


01:42:03-01:42:10
Sometimes letting your brain do what it wants, even if that's starting a new project, is more restful than asking it to focus on a relaxing activity.


01:42:10-01:42:14
Lucila S. 30, Mexico.


01:42:14-01:42:19
In college, the only way I could write my essays was by watching rom-coms.


01:42:19-01:42:22
I'd work for 20 minutes, then watch like 15 minutes, and then go back.


01:42:22-01:42:30
It was a bit like the Pomodoro method, a time management technique, but it had to be with a movie that had a really easy to track plot, and one I hadn't yet watched.


01:42:30-01:42:35
Emress H. 32, California.


01:42:35-01:42:37
I take brain breaks between lectures.


01:42:37-01:42:40
I need them, or I cannot cram more information into my head.


01:42:40-01:42:43
I watch a cat video or take a walk outside.


01:42:43-01:42:44
They work incredibly well.


01:42:44-01:42:49
There's a noticeable difference in how much I retain when I take brain breaks versus when I don't.


01:42:49-01:42:53
Jennifer S. 38, Michigan.


01:42:53-01:43:02
This might seem counterintuitive, but I rest my brain by doing something physically challenging and repetitive enough that my brain stops partying because my body is so tight up in the movement.


01:43:02-01:43:07
Rowing, and sometimes just a circuit or intervals of something, works.


01:43:07-01:43:10
Afterward, there's this tiny window of relative quiet.


01:43:10-01:43:13
In defense of the noise.


01:43:13-01:43:20
During an interview with journalist Arushi Anyi, I proudly explained how I boost the signal and decrease the noise.


01:43:20-01:43:33
I described all the ways in which I set up my environment to help me focus, using different computers for gaming and work, limiting my to-do list so I don't get overwhelmed and avoid it entirely, putting sticky notes with a doing list and a not doing list on my desk.


01:43:33-01:43:35
She nodded, thoughtful.


01:43:35-01:43:39
But sometimes the noise becomes the signal.


01:43:39-01:43:40
What do you mean?


01:43:40-01:43:41
I asked.


01:43:41-01:43:48
She explained to me that sometimes when working on a task, the thought that distracts her becomes the new project she wants to focus on in the future.


01:43:48-01:43:51
She grinned at the irony.


01:43:51-01:43:53
I nodded slowly, a realization dawning.


01:43:53-01:44:01
Once, instead of working, I was scrolling through Twitter and came across a thread asking people what their favorite musicals were.


01:44:01-01:44:03
"Into the Woods," someone said.


01:44:03-01:44:04
I'd never seen it.


01:44:04-01:44:07
I decided to watch it instead of whatever I was supposed to be doing that day.


01:44:07-01:44:09
It was powerful.


01:44:09-01:44:14
I ended up creating a video essay of sorts about it, and to date, it's one of my proudest accomplishments.


01:44:14-01:44:19
While it's great to focus, there's value in getting distracted.


01:44:19-01:44:22
That's where innovation lives, and I'd forgotten that.


01:44:22-01:44:28
Thinking back, I started seeing how my pursuit of focus made me considerably less creative.


01:44:28-01:44:32
When I started the channel, my brain was off leash most of the time.


01:44:32-01:44:34
It could sit in the sandbox and play.


01:44:34-01:44:37
I came up with a lot of metaphors back then.


01:44:37-01:44:41
The paper monster, my term for all the mail and paper clutter that piles up.


01:44:41-01:44:42
Magical quarters of encouragement.


01:44:42-01:44:44
[[Motivation Bridge]].


01:44:44-01:44:48
I dressed up like I was in the matrix to explain why we focus on things that don't matter.


01:44:48-01:44:52
I made finger puppets that kicked over dominoes to explain how routines work.


01:44:52-01:44:56
I danced around in a homework hat and ate giant pizzas on camera.


01:44:56-01:45:02
As I got better at controlling my focus, I got more productive, but I lost some of that creativity.


01:45:02-01:45:07
If your brain can't wander off, it can't come back with something unexpected.


01:45:07-01:45:11
If you're farming and planting corn, you're gonna get corn.


01:45:11-01:45:17
If you're off foraging, or even just wandering through the woods, there might be berries, or mushrooms, or fairies.


01:45:17-01:45:18
Why not?


01:45:18-01:45:27
Our brains are incredible at divergent thinking, coming up with lots of ideas, new ways to combine things, and innovative ways to solve problems.


01:45:27-01:45:33
This is why many of us are inventors, early tech adopters, industry leaders, and disruptors.


01:45:33-01:45:39
And in the relentless pursuit of improving our ability to focus, we might forget to allow space for that.


01:45:39-01:45:46
It's normal to take what we have for granted while searching for what we don't, but there is a trade-off.


01:45:46-01:45:51
You can't have metaphorical shower thoughts if you don't make time for metaphorical showers.


01:45:51-01:45:53
Now, I'm aiming for more of a balance.


01:45:53-01:45:58
I have tools to use when I need to focus, but I also give myself time to wander.


01:45:58-01:46:00
I've done a good job taming my focus beast.


01:46:00-01:46:01
I know how to direct it.


01:46:01-01:46:05
Sometimes, though, I let it take me where it wants to go.


01:46:05-01:46:09
I let it surprise me, and I'm excited by what we discover together.


01:46:09-01:46:15
Regardless of where we're at in our journey, our brains are going to roam, and maybe that's okay.


01:46:15-01:46:17
We can always eventually bring them back.

### Chapter 4: How to Executive Function [01:46:17]
01:46:17-01:46:24
Chapter four, how to [[Topics/Psychology/executive function/index|executive function]].


01:46:24-01:46:29
And now that you don't have to be perfect, you can be good.


01:46:29-01:46:30
[[John Steinbeck]], East of Eden.


01:46:30-01:46:34
ADHD New Year's.


01:46:34-01:46:47
I'm about to move into a new office space, and I find myself slipping into the same fantasy I had every time I started a new school year, the fantasy of finally being organized, of finally getting it right.


01:46:47-01:46:54
The fantasy that this time, I'll be the person I'm supposed to be, that my space will look like it's supposed to look.


01:46:54-01:46:56
This time, it'll be perfect.


01:46:56-01:46:57
Why not?


01:46:57-01:46:59
I'm starting from scratch.


01:46:59-01:47:00
Here's the thing.


01:47:00-01:47:01
I'm not really starting from scratch.


01:47:01-01:47:04
I'm bringing my habits, my routines, and my brain.


01:47:04-01:47:06
I'm bringing my chaos with me.


01:47:06-01:47:07
You just can't see it yet.


01:47:07-01:47:17
I'd spend a whole blissful, optimistic week setting up my backpack, new binder, tabs, planner, and all the folders I could ever need to get and stay organized.


01:47:17-01:47:21
My mom would show me how to set up my folders and explain how to maintain them.


01:47:21-01:47:24
Two weeks into the school year, none of this mattered.


01:47:24-01:47:25
I couldn't find anything.


01:47:25-01:47:30
There'd be cracker jacks stuck to forgotten homework crumpled at the bottom of my bag.


01:47:30-01:47:33
And my mom would regularly have to dump it out and help me triage.


01:47:33-01:47:35
What happened?


01:47:35-01:47:36
I couldn't explain it.


01:47:36-01:47:38
The systems just fell apart.


01:47:39-01:47:48
As an adult, I celebrated ADHD New Year's with new purses, apartments, and desks, with new hobbies, friendships, careers, and credit cards.


01:47:48-01:48:02
I'd move into a new place, and by organizing bins and cleaning supplies, putting everything where it belonged, and feeling so proud of myself, two weeks later, all the surfaces would be cluttered, the sink would be full, and I'd spend hours a day looking for things I swear I just saw.


01:48:02-01:48:07
Sometimes I'd come across a new system, and that too would offer a blank slate.


01:48:07-01:48:12
Okay, sure, my finances are a mess, and everything I've tried hasn't worked out, but I haven't tried this system yet.


01:48:12-01:48:14
Maybe this is the one.


01:48:14-01:48:23
Almost every system I set up to manage the stuff in my life broke down almost immediately, usually due to some combination of the following.


01:48:23-01:48:24
I forgot to use it.


01:48:24-01:48:25
I forgot how to use it.


01:48:25-01:48:26
I lost it.


01:48:26-01:48:27
I got bored of it.


01:48:27-01:48:28
I forgot it existed.


01:48:28-01:48:31
I put it in a very important place, and now I can't find it.


01:48:31-01:48:33
I promised myself I'd update it later.


01:48:33-01:48:34
I did not.


01:48:34-01:48:35
I got distracted.


01:48:35-01:48:37
I broke my phone and hadn't backed it up.


01:48:37-01:48:40
I accidentally deleted the app or couldn't remember my password.


01:48:40-01:48:42
I couldn't afford it anymore.


01:48:42-01:48:43
Life happened.


01:48:43-01:48:50
I got sick or went to a friend's house on a day I was supposed to clean/cook/organize a thing, and I never went back to it again.


01:48:50-01:48:58
And when a system fell apart, so did whatever it was supposed to help me manage, my stuff, my time, my relationships, my finances.


01:48:58-01:49:09
There were instances and areas in my life in which I'd be perfectly organized and could stay that way, but only because I protected my systems fiercely, obsessively.


01:49:09-01:49:11
I didn't care how tired I was.


01:49:11-01:49:16
I was folding my clothes Marie Kondo style, because the moment I didn't, I knew it would all collapse.


01:49:16-01:49:18
No, I couldn't go out to dinner with everyone.


01:49:18-01:49:21
It would throw off the meal plan I had made.


01:49:21-01:49:27
Sometimes having my colored pencils organized by color felt like the only thing holding back the chaos.


01:49:27-01:49:30
So no, sorry, you couldn't borrow one.


01:49:30-01:49:32
In fact, even I wouldn't use them.


01:49:33-01:49:38
Trying to stay on top of things when I did not know how to stay on top of things came with a trade-off.


01:49:38-01:49:43
I could do it as long as life didn't happen, so I didn't let life happen.


01:49:43-01:49:50
This, of course, defeats the entire purpose of being on top of things, to make it easier to live your life, but it was a trade-off I felt I had to make.


01:49:50-01:49:53
What I learned.


01:49:53-01:50:01
In retrospect, my need to be organized was a way to gain some semblance of control in a life that often felt far outside my control.


01:50:01-01:50:09
I was constantly trying to find systems and rules to contain the chaos, to help me be able to do what I needed to do when I needed to do it.


01:50:09-01:50:12
I felt like a hot mess, and what do you do with a mess?


01:50:12-01:50:13
You clean it up.


01:50:13-01:50:19
My usual level of disorganization had a snowball effect on my ability to function, too.


01:50:19-01:50:28
I can't find the thing, which makes me late, which makes me rush and put something where it doesn't belong, telling myself I'll reorganize it later, which I absolutely forget to do.


01:50:28-01:50:39
I had trouble functioning, and because I kept being told that being more organized would make me more functional, I put a ton of time, effort, money, and other resources into trying to be.


01:50:39-01:50:43
The truth is the opposite of what most of us were told.


01:50:43-01:50:50
The reason those of us with ADHD have trouble functioning isn't because we haven't found the right system yet and/or don't stick to it.


01:50:50-01:50:52
It's actually the other way around.


01:50:52-01:51:03
The reason we have trouble sticking to these systems, and why our stuff, time, actions, emotions, and words spill over onto those around us, is because we have trouble with executive function.


01:51:03-01:51:07
What the heck is executive function?


01:51:07-01:51:10
Executive function, EF, is like the CEO of the brain.


01:51:10-01:51:20
It's a set of top-down cognitive processes, executive functions, that help us self-regulate so we can effectively plan, prioritize, and sustain effort toward long-term goals.


01:51:20-01:51:33
[[Topics/Psychology/executive function/index|executive function]], noun, a set of top-down cognitive processes, executive functions, that help us self-regulate so we can effectively plan, prioritize, and sustain effort toward long-term goals.


01:51:33-01:51:40
These cognitive processes originate from the prefrontal cortex, which is the last part of the brain to develop.


01:51:40-01:51:45
In most people, the executive function system finishes developing by age 25.


01:51:45-01:51:57
This is why we associate a lot of skills reliant on executive function, such as being able to manage a career, make good decisions, pay our bills on time, and drive a car without crashing into things with being an adult.


01:51:58-01:52:08
While a lot of organizational, financial, and project management systems are designed to support executive function, they also rely on the executive function of the person using them.


01:52:08-01:52:10
You can probably see where I'm going with this.


01:52:10-01:52:15
[[Topics/Psychology/executive function/index|executive function]] is impaired in ADHD brains.


01:52:15-01:52:23
When I started the channel, I thought my inattention, which I now understand is difficulty regulating my attention, was the only impairment I had.


01:52:23-01:52:25
Turns out, that is not the case.


01:52:25-01:52:30
ADHD struggles are more broadly due to executive functioning difficulties.


01:52:30-01:52:37
Executive functions that tend to be impaired in ADHD brains include response inhibition.


01:52:37-01:52:46
Response inhibition refers to the suppression of actions that interfere with a goal, or are inappropriate, or no longer appropriate in a given context.


01:52:46-01:52:52
It creates mental and temporal space between a stimulus and an action.


01:52:52-01:53:07
Have you ever shouted out answers without raising your hand as soon as a teacher asked a question, left your seat just when the meeting is starting, blurted out thoughts that popped into your head without thinking, or continued to overshare or over-explain even when you knew you should stop?


01:53:07-01:53:13
These actions are all common and frustrating results of impaired response inhibition.


01:53:13-01:53:15
Stimuli happened, and we responded.


01:53:15-01:53:18
Working memory.


01:53:18-01:53:25
[[working memory]] refers to our ability to temporarily hold information in our mind, manipulate it, and produce a response or action.


01:53:25-01:53:32
For example, when you're cooking dinner, you may use your working memory to hold the ingredients you saw when scanning inside your fridge.


01:53:32-01:53:37
Think of how you can mix and match those ingredients, and then decide what you're going to make.


01:53:37-01:53:43
Or if you're like me, and this overloads your working memory, you may order food or eat garbanzo beans straight out of the can instead.


01:53:43-01:53:44
Set shifting.


01:53:44-01:53:55
[[task switching|set-shifting]] refers to our ability to switch between tasks that have different cognitive demands, like reading a recipe and cooking it, or switching from being a speaker to being a listener during a conversation.


01:53:55-01:54:01
When we multitask on tasks that require our attention, we're relying on set shifting.


01:54:01-01:54:04
It allows us to be flexible when demands change.


01:54:04-01:54:07
Set shifting is moderately impaired in ADHD.


01:54:07-01:54:12
In situations where we're expected to shift between tasks, we often operate slowly or make more mistakes.


01:54:12-01:54:17
These executive functions work together to help us, well, function.


01:54:17-01:54:25
Research has found that impairments in set shifting, for example, may be due to deficits in working memory and response inhibition.


01:54:25-01:54:39
When we can't remember the rules of the task we're switching back to, say, writing an email to our boss, or we're still stuck following the ones of the task we were just focused on, texting our friend, it's harder to set shift both quickly and accurately.


01:54:39-01:54:44
All of this explains some of my organizational difficulties as a student.


01:54:44-01:55:01
In the five to 10 minutes between classes, I had to stop taking notes, figure out what the homework assignment was, look for my planner to write that down, put my books away, go to my locker, remember what my combination was and what supplies I needed for the next class and transition to the next class.


01:55:01-01:55:06
Doing all of this and organizing my things required a level of executive function I hadn't yet developed.


01:55:06-01:55:11
Executive function develops more slowly in ADHD brains.


01:55:11-01:55:21
Most human built systems assume that the person using them has typical levels of executive function, whatever is considered normal for the target age group or audience.


01:55:21-01:55:27
If a system is made for kids, it's probably simple to navigate or designed to be used with the support of a parent or teacher.


01:55:27-01:55:32
If it's made for college students, it might assume its users have an almost developed executive function system.


01:55:32-01:55:39
People expect an 18 year old going off to college to have the executive function abilities of an 18 year old.


01:55:39-01:55:45
They wouldn't be expected to be great at, say, running a company yet, but they should be able to handle registering for classes.


01:55:45-01:55:48
For people with ADHD, that's often not true.


01:55:48-01:55:54
With ADHD, executive function is delayed up to 30%.


01:55:54-01:56:00
That means the 18 year old you're sending off to college might have the executive function of someone who is 12.


01:56:00-01:56:04
This is why those with ADHD can seem immature.


01:56:04-01:56:09
It's also why we can seem as if we're misbehaving intentionally when we're not.


01:56:09-01:56:19
When we're not following instructions or meeting expectations, it doesn't necessarily mean we don't understand what the expectations are or that we don't want to meet them.


01:56:19-01:56:25
Often, we don't have the executive function yet or right now to do those things effectively.


01:56:25-01:56:30
There are hot and cool [[Topics/Psychology/executive function/index|executive function]] systems.


01:56:30-01:56:37
What we do or say in the heat of the moment is often much different from what we plan to do or recommend to someone else.


01:56:37-01:56:45
That's because there are two narrow pathways underlying the executive function systems that help us make decisions and reach our goals.


01:56:45-01:56:50
The hot executive function system refers to affective and motivational processes.


01:56:50-01:56:53
It kicks in when emotions and stakes are high.


01:56:53-01:57:03
When we're not in the heat of the moment or the stakes are low, the cool executive function, EF, system kicks in and our decisions tend to be much more logical.


01:57:03-01:57:08
Cool EF refers to the cognitive processes such as response inhibition, working memory, and set shifting.


01:57:09-01:57:12
We can switch quickly between these two systems.


01:57:12-01:57:17
They can also both be active at the same time, although one typically overrides the other.


01:57:17-01:57:19
This is true for everyone.


01:57:19-01:57:24
But it's especially important to understand for those of us with ADHD.


01:57:24-01:57:29
While executive function impairments explain many of our struggles, they don't paint the entire picture.


01:57:29-01:57:33
Otherwise, ADHD would just be called executive function disorder.


01:57:33-01:57:36
Emotions run high in those with ADHD.


01:57:36-01:57:45
There are also differences in our [[motivation]]al processes that make us more likely to choose smaller rewards now over larger rewards later.


01:57:45-01:57:53
And successful problem solving, which uses cool EF, relies heavily on one's motivational and emotional influences, our hot EF.


01:57:53-01:58:02
This explains why when I ran into challenges setting up and initially maintaining my organizational systems, I was able to successfully problem solve.


01:58:02-01:58:09
I was excited to set the systems up and had the immediate reward of seeing everything look pretty and organized.


01:58:09-01:58:22
It also explains why when I was running from one class to the next, I made the objectively illogical choice to shove my papers wherever they fit in my backpack, telling myself contrary to all past experience, that I'd organize them later.


01:58:22-01:58:28
The emotions from the stress I felt about getting to the next class and the consequences of being late were more immediate.


01:58:28-01:58:35
Many people, regardless of whether they have ADHD, make choices that aren't exactly logical when their hot EF system is dominant.


01:58:35-01:58:49
But as research has demonstrated, tapping into our cool EF through reflection, contextualizing and analyzing abstractly can make it easier to make logical decisions, even when our emotions and motivation are pulling us toward impulsive ones.


01:58:49-01:58:51
And these are skills that can be learned.


01:58:51-01:58:55
Joshua S. 31, Germany.


01:58:55-01:58:58
With my ADHD, every day is different.


01:58:58-01:59:02
I have amazing days and terrible days, but rarely average days.


01:59:02-01:59:04
I have tons of ideas or a blank mind.


01:59:04-01:59:11
I am 100% unproductive for most of the time, but then manage to catch up with everything I should have done in an extremely short amount of time.


01:59:11-01:59:15
Madeleine R. 24, Canada.


01:59:15-01:59:17
I struggle with executive function every day.


01:59:17-01:59:20
There are so many small things to remember for every task.


01:59:20-01:59:33
Remember to take meds, turn the oven off after cooking, grab grocery bags before heading to the grocery store, save a cup of pasta water before straining, put the leftovers into the fridge after they cool down, go to this appointment, make plans to meet up with this friend, grab the clothes from the dryer.


01:59:33-01:59:35
So many things.


01:59:36-01:59:38
Merle D. 21, Netherlands.


01:59:38-01:59:43
When I'm struggling with tasks and whatnot, almost everyone in my life has told me to just start.


01:59:43-01:59:50
It hurts me every time because I feel like they don't take the struggles I have because of an actual brain disorder, seriously.


01:59:50-01:59:54
Maggie K. 40, Pennsylvania.


01:59:54-02:00:02
Becoming a mom has increased my executive function challenges because now it's not just my own life I have to manage, it's theirs too, their childhoods.


02:00:02-02:00:08
I make sure that I'm on top of the essentials, but it's nowhere near the picture-perfect childhoods they're comparing theirs to.


02:00:08-02:00:10
The worst are the whys.


02:00:10-02:00:13
Why didn't we bake cookies for the holidays last year?


02:00:13-02:00:15
Why did we get out late trick-or-treating?


02:00:15-02:00:17
Remember that one time you made a spooky treasure hunt?


02:00:17-02:00:19
Why haven't we done that again?


02:00:19-02:00:21
I answer them as best as I can.


02:00:21-02:00:24
It's not that I don't want to, I'm doing my best.


02:00:24-02:00:27
Deep down, I can't help but feel that I'm still failing them somehow.


02:00:27-02:00:30
The Toolbox.


02:00:30-02:00:37
Many mental health professionals focus on ADHD symptoms alone or on our relative strengths and weaknesses in EF.


02:00:37-02:00:42
But what's worth considering is how we function given our brain's differences.


02:00:42-02:00:48
While we can't change the fact that we have these differences in EF, the impairments we face as a result can be minimized.


02:00:48-02:00:51
There are many things we can do to help compensate for our EF differences.


02:00:51-02:00:55
One, have less stuff to manage.


02:00:55-02:01:01
There's a common saying that regularly makes the rounds in the ADHD community, if you want to do more, do less.


02:01:01-02:01:08
The more you're trying to do, the more you have to keep track of, and the harder it is for your executive function system to keep up.


02:01:08-02:01:12
That's true for everyone, but it's especially true for those of us with impaired executive function.


02:01:12-02:01:20
Because we often take on way the F more than we can handle, one of the most helpful ways for those with ADHD to better manage their stuff is simply having less of it.


02:01:20-02:01:24
Delegate Areas of Responsibility.


02:01:24-02:01:30
Delegating individual tasks often requires more cognitive resources than it saves for those of us with ADHD.


02:01:30-02:01:41
Delegating entire areas of responsibility, however, can be more executive function efficient, because it allows someone else to take over both the doing of the task and the management of it.


02:01:41-02:01:51
Handing your partner a grocery list will save you one trip to the store, but mutually deciding that your partner is the one in charge of making sure there is food in the house frees up a ton of brain bandwidth.


02:01:51-02:01:55
Keep Systems Simple.


02:01:55-02:02:06
While it might be fun to set up an elaborate organizational system when we're hyper-focusing on it, being able to maintain that system when we need to turn our attention to other things is another story.


02:02:06-02:02:13
Simplifying your systems so they're easier to maintain can make it more likely to be and stay functional in the long term.


02:02:13-02:02:20
An example of this is books go on the bookshelf, as opposed to books need to be put on the correct bookshelf sorted by color and size.


02:02:20-02:02:23
Practice Minimalism.


02:02:23-02:02:26
Minimalism essentially means owning less stuff.


02:02:26-02:02:31
It's a lot easier to manage clutter if you don't have a lot of stuff to create it.


02:02:31-02:02:39
Many ADHDers I've met swear by minimalism because they can function more effectively when they have less to manage, lose, organize, and/or clean.


02:02:39-02:02:43
Say no to at least some things.


02:02:43-02:02:49
Limiting the number of ongoing projects, especially long-term ones, takes pressure off our executive function.


02:02:49-02:02:52
The stuff we could do in Life Buffet is unlimited.


02:02:52-02:02:54
Our capacity is not.


02:02:54-02:02:57
If your plate is full, don't get another plate.


02:02:58-02:03:00
Do we have to do less forever?


02:03:00-02:03:07
Not necessarily, but because of our neurodevelopmental delay, adults with ADHD are often stretched to their EF limit.


02:03:07-02:03:16
If our coping skills don't increase faster than the demands do, keeping up gets harder and harder, and we sacrifice more and more of ourselves and our wellbeing.


02:03:16-02:03:20
The ADHD cycle of overwhelm.


02:03:20-02:03:23
Take on more than you can, fail.


02:03:23-02:03:27
Try to catch up on life by taking on even more, fail.


02:03:27-02:03:29
Efforts become frantic and panicked, fail.


02:03:29-02:03:32
Experience burnout, depression, and anxiety, fail.


02:03:32-02:03:41
The way to step out of this cycle is often by starting to do less, ideally an amount that you can currently handle, and before committing to more, level up your skills.


02:03:41-02:03:50
By the way, as someone pointed out when I shared this with our community, we can fall into this same cycle if we succeed because now we're given more responsibility and have more to manage.


02:03:50-02:03:54
Mark N. 66, USA.


02:03:54-02:03:59
As a certified financial planner, I only see clients and offer advice.


02:03:59-02:04:06
Someone else manages my calendar, records notes, builds client plans, manages revenue collection, and handles anything operational.


02:04:06-02:04:09
Jen M. 46, Colorado.


02:04:09-02:04:11
Lower the bar.


02:04:11-02:04:12
Let the floors be dirty.


02:04:12-02:04:14
Let the laundry sit in baskets for two weeks.


02:04:14-02:04:15
Who cares?


02:04:15-02:04:20
Make sure you and the kids/plants/pets are fed.


02:04:20-02:04:23
Make sure there's time for sleep, if possible, and fun.


02:04:23-02:04:24
Laundry will keep.


02:04:24-02:04:26
Life won't.


02:04:26-02:04:29
Two, accommodate yourself.


02:04:29-02:04:33
When we face struggles that others don't, we need tools that others don't.


02:04:33-02:04:35
For example, I am short.


02:04:35-02:04:40
I can reach the top shelf of my cabinet, but if I want to do that safely, I'll need a step stool.


02:04:40-02:04:47
In environments where stuff is on high shelves, it levels the playing field and helps me avoid struggling significantly more than someone who is taller.


02:04:47-02:04:50
The same idea applies to ADHD.


02:04:50-02:05:02
Accommodations are generally well worth the cost, and many are free, because they make tasks and environments more ADHD accessible, which can make a huge difference in our ability to perform at the level we are otherwise capable of.


02:05:02-02:05:08
It's ideal to put accommodations in place before we desperately need them, because finding the most effective ones can take time.


02:05:08-02:05:11
Add scaffolding.


02:05:11-02:05:18
Just as you use training wheels to learn how to ride a bike, you can put extra supports in place to help you learn a new system or take on new responsibilities.


02:05:18-02:05:23
For example, first, I have someone sit with me and do a thing.


02:05:23-02:05:25
Then I'll sometimes do it on my own.


02:05:25-02:05:28
Once I get the hang of it, I'll do it totally on my own.


02:05:28-02:05:36
Scaffolding benefits us because we get the support we need to learn a task, and as we become more confident with it, that support can be pulled away.


02:05:36-02:05:38
Self-advocate.


02:05:38-02:05:44
Speaking up when someone can make a task more doable for you lets others know how they can help you.


02:05:44-02:05:48
For example, you could ask if someone can share a document so you can read and follow along.


02:05:48-02:05:51
Ask for formal accommodations.


02:05:51-02:06:01
In the US, students and workers with disabilities, and again, ADHD can qualify as a disability, are legally entitled to reasonable accommodations.


02:06:01-02:06:12
Individualized education plans, IEPs, and 504 plans protect students, and the ADA, the Americans with Disabilities Act, protects those who are no longer in school.


02:06:12-02:06:22
The job accommodation network's website, askjan.org, lists accommodations by disability or impairment, and it even has a live chat option for those who need guidance.


02:06:22-02:06:27
Stephanie R., 33, North Carolina.


02:06:27-02:06:30
My sister and I both have ADHD and are roommates.


02:06:30-02:06:32
We body double so we can get things done.


02:06:32-02:06:37
If I'm washing dishes, she'll sit in the kitchen and grade papers, and we keep each other on task.


02:06:37-02:06:41
Jessie A., 42, Washington.


02:06:41-02:06:49
I live in fear of the surprise let's talk meeting with my boss where I find out I'm falling behind or have been doing something wrong for six plus months.


02:06:49-02:06:55
So we started weekly check-ins where he would give me a quick reassuring green light that everything was thumbs up from his perspective.


02:06:55-02:06:58
Reese, 40, Virginia.


02:06:58-02:07:03
When I first had kids at school, I was shocked by the amount of baking they expected parents to do for class parties.


02:07:03-02:07:09
I find baking simultaneously boring and stressful, which plays hell with my executive function.


02:07:09-02:07:17
So I modified the expectations by telling teachers, "I don't bake, but I would be happy "to bring themed plates and napkins for every party." Boom, accommodated.


02:07:18-02:07:21
Three, account for the ADHD tax.


02:07:21-02:07:32
The ADHD tax is a colloquial term used in the community to refer to the additional expenses we incur simply by having ADHD in a world that doesn't account for our challenges.


02:07:32-02:07:41
While the term typically refers to financial costs, the ADHD tax can include energy, time, and other resources, and it is expensive.


02:07:41-02:07:48
While we can't eliminate the ADHD tax entirely, accounting for it can help us avoid at least some of the crises we face.


02:07:48-02:07:59
And we can lessen it by, when we can, paying the ADHD tax upfront, investing our time, money, and energy in tools and systems that can reduce the cost in the long run.


02:07:59-02:08:06
As many in this community, and those who love and work with us have discovered, it is not only less stressful, it typically ends up being much cheaper.


02:08:06-02:08:10
Examples of the ADHD tax.


02:08:10-02:08:41
Late fees, rush shipping fees, fees for subscriptions and memberships we forgot to cancel, beverages that have gone flat or missing, replacements for things we lost or broke, last minute tickets for traveling or missed flights because you were late/forgot your ID/went to the wrong airport, leads we forgot or were too overwhelmed to follow up on, car accidents we got into because we were distracted by billboards, time spent looking for everything constantly, vegetables.


02:08:41-02:08:42
Yep, go check the drawer, I'll wait.


02:08:44-02:08:47
Use services for tasks that aren't error tolerant.


02:08:47-02:08:54
Many people with ADHD hire others to manage important tasks they may otherwise forget or mess up.


02:08:54-02:08:58
Accountants can help us pay taxes on time and catch deductions we might miss.


02:08:58-02:09:05
Lawyers can read important documents or, depending on their specialty, help you plan the future of your estate or handle your divorce.


02:09:05-02:09:12
Speaking of which, asking a lawyer to draft a prenup is an effective way of lowering the ADHD tax too.


02:09:12-02:09:25
The number of documents you have to find and the amount of stuff you have to remember during a divorce, one of the most emotionally devastating experiences of your entire life, and one that ADHDers are statistically more likely to experience, is ridiculous.


02:09:25-02:09:30
Many services that feel like a luxury are actually an accommodation for us.


02:09:30-02:09:33
Set up shortcuts.


02:09:33-02:09:38
For things you do often, learn or practice ways to cut down on the steps that they involve.


02:09:38-02:09:45
This reduces the demand on your working memory and makes it less likely you'll get distracted or lose something along the way.


02:09:45-02:09:48
Almost all programs have built-in shortcuts.


02:09:48-02:09:50
It can be worth taking the time to learn them.


02:09:50-02:09:52
We can create physical shortcuts too.


02:09:52-02:10:02
Coat racks condense the process of putting your coat where it belongs from four steps, open closet door, find empty hanger, hang coat, close door, to one step, hang coat on rack.


02:10:02-02:10:06
Invest in tools that can help.


02:10:06-02:10:11
If you know that you lose things, look into Bluetooth trackers like Tile or features like Find My iPhone.


02:10:11-02:10:15
If you forget to take your medication, consider investing in timer caps.


02:10:15-02:10:26
If you know you tend to abandon planners unless they're covered in shiny stickers or are colorful AF, you might as well buy the shiniest stickers and the most exciting colors you can find.


02:10:26-02:10:34
It will be surprisingly cheaper than going without them because you won't spend as much money and frustrated effort on planners that aren't accessible to you.


02:10:34-02:10:39
Trayona W, 47, Ohio.


02:10:39-02:10:41
My smartwatch reminds me of all the things.


02:10:41-02:10:47
My watch is always on vibrate, so I am tapped every time there's a reminder app notification or an alarm I set.


02:10:47-02:10:52
I have repeating alarms for waking up, leaving for work, three breaks at work, leaving work, et cetera.


02:10:52-02:10:57
Certain health apps remind me of things like when to fast, when to check blood sugar, et cetera.


02:10:57-02:11:01
If I need to get poked at for some reason, I set my watch to notify me somehow.


02:11:01-02:11:06
Having that physical feedback is essential because I miss a lot of notifications on my phone.


02:11:06-02:11:10
Rory W, 17, Michigan.


02:11:10-02:11:19
My family, six people, three with ADHD, uses smart speakers and smart home technology to automate shortcut routines that save time and brain space.


02:11:19-02:11:28
For example, running the good night routine turns off all the lights, starts the robot vacuum, and tells us if our dishwasher has been started and our devices are charging.


02:11:28-02:11:32
Jin A, 40, Maryland.


02:11:32-02:11:37
My psychiatrist told me if you find a workaround that solves the problem, it's just as good as not having the problem.


02:11:37-02:11:42
For example, I kept shoes in my car because I sometimes left for work still wearing my slippers.


02:11:42-02:11:47
So instead of being mad at myself for forgetting to put on shoes, I could let it go because it didn't matter.


02:11:47-02:11:49
When I got to the office, I still had what I needed.


02:11:49-02:11:52
Four, build what works for you.


02:11:52-02:11:56
We often build systems for the person we'd like to be rather than the person we are.


02:11:56-02:12:04
The person we are already has habits, preferences, aversions, strengths, and a history of what does and doesn't work for them.


02:12:04-02:12:11
If we build systems with them in mind, we'll often be more successful at creating a system that works than if we try to build one from the ground up.


02:12:11-02:12:14
Look at what's worked before.


02:12:14-02:12:18
We can often reuse and repurpose strategies that have helped us in the past.


02:12:18-02:12:22
Even if they're not a perfect fit, we can often tweak them to make them work better.


02:12:22-02:12:26
New systems might be more fun and exciting, but that novelty wears off.


02:12:26-02:12:33
The stuff that's lasted beyond the new and shiny phase is often worth adapting as needed and trying again because it's more likely to stick.


02:12:33-02:12:35
Look at what you currently do.


02:12:35-02:12:44
If you usually spend an extra half hour after an exciting meeting brainstorming and info dumping at the person in the next meeting, maybe let them know that's the plan and make sure they're cool with that.


02:12:44-02:12:51
If they're not, give yourself a buffer between meetings to let your super spinny brain wind down and transition to the next thing.


02:12:51-02:12:53
Consider your preferences.


02:12:53-02:12:56
[[motivation]] can be a huge challenge for those with ADHD.


02:12:56-02:13:02
If you love Doctor Who, getting a Doctor Who key holder might make it more likely you'll hang your keys there.


02:13:02-02:13:07
If you hate spending time in the garage, is that really a good place to put the elliptical, Jessica?


02:13:07-02:13:08
The elliptical is still in the garage.


02:13:08-02:13:10
I still do not use it.


02:13:10-02:13:13
