I'm thinking of putting a pretty rug down there though and making it feel less garagey.


02:13:13-02:13:15
I have not done this yet.


02:13:15-02:13:16
It's been a year.


02:13:16-02:13:19
Remember that you have ADHD.


02:13:19-02:13:22
Current us isn't neurotypical.


02:13:22-02:13:25
Future us isn't neurotypical either.


02:13:25-02:13:31
While we can improve our skills and mitigate some of our impairments, this takes time and progress isn't always linear.


02:13:31-02:13:35
Plus the deficits don't go away, even if some of the impairments do.


02:13:35-02:13:40
I'm still working with the same brain, even after some of my impairments have lessened through the use of strategies.


02:13:40-02:13:43
There are still places where my brain is gonna trip over itself.


02:13:43-02:13:47
Ron W. 49, Michigan.


02:13:47-02:13:49
I love using my riding lawnmower.


02:13:49-02:13:51
I hate raking leaves.


02:13:51-02:14:01
So after years of letting leaves sit on my lawn all winter, I figured out that by taking the bag off and running my mower over the thick fallen leaves, I can chop the leaves into mulch.


02:14:01-02:14:05
Then all I have to do is put the bag on and run the mower over the yard again.


02:14:05-02:14:06
Done.


02:14:06-02:14:13
The neighbors may think it's weird to mow leaves, but as far as I'm concerned, this all season pain in the butt now takes one afternoon and it's fun.


02:14:13-02:14:17
Ashley W. 26, Singapore.


02:14:17-02:14:21
I'm bad at remembering things, so I religiously take notes.


02:14:21-02:14:26
I've set up a custom style sheet on my word processors because I once hyper-focused on typography.


02:14:26-02:14:31
So now every time I look at my word processors, I get a dopamine rush just from how clean and good it looks.


02:14:33-02:14:34
Manual mode.


02:14:34-02:14:38
I was still waiting tables while working on the channel and learning about my brain.


02:14:38-02:14:44
At this point, I had worked at restaurants for so long and gotten so good at it that I barely had to think about what I was doing.


02:14:44-02:14:45
I was on autopilot.


02:14:45-02:14:49
I was one of the best servers at the restaurant.


02:14:49-02:14:53
I could step out back to check YouTube, reply to comments, and still keep my tables happy.


02:14:53-02:14:57
When I put in an order, my fingers flew over the computer screen.


02:14:57-02:14:59
The sense of urgency, the stimulation.


02:14:59-02:15:00
It was exhilarating.


02:15:00-02:15:03
I loved doing that work because I'd get into flow.


02:15:03-02:15:05
Sometimes I felt superhuman.


02:15:05-02:15:10
One day, the system was updated and the menus were rearranged.


02:15:10-02:15:16
They didn't change completely, but they were different enough to keep me from relying on muscle memory or mental autopilot.


02:15:16-02:15:19
I was now in what I call manual mode.


02:15:19-02:15:27
I had to think about each action, actively look for what I needed, figure out which category a menu item was in, and then scan the screen for what I was looking for.


02:15:27-02:15:31
In other words, I had to rely on my executive function.


02:15:31-02:15:33
These changes were disabling.


02:15:33-02:15:40
Sometimes I went through the same folder three times before I could find the menu item I was looking for.


02:15:40-02:15:45
The other servers became impatient and stood behind me, waiting for me to finish putting in orders.


02:15:45-02:15:50
The anxiety of knowing someone was watching me wrestle with the new system made it even harder to think.


02:15:50-02:15:54
It took me so long to put in a single order that I started to fall behind.


02:15:54-02:15:59
My tables became angry and needing to attend to them through everything else out of whack.


02:15:59-02:16:03
I couldn't keep up with the demands I'd so easily handled the day before.


02:16:03-02:16:08
I was scared my managers would notice and decide I couldn't handle more tables, which would affect my income.


02:16:08-02:16:17
I tried to make myself go faster, but it just made me more likely to make mistakes, which slowed me down further because now I needed to find a manager to correct them.


02:16:17-02:16:22
I started doing the rest of my job differently to try to keep up, which threw those parts into manual mode too.


02:16:22-02:16:25
Before I could get everything done, no problem.


02:16:25-02:16:30
Now I needed to prioritize which tables to keep happy and figure out which steps of service to skip.


02:16:30-02:16:38
I went from being the quickest server in the restaurant to the slowest because of the increased demand on my executive function.


02:16:38-02:16:47
Going from automatic mode to manual mode in just one aspect of my job was enough to make me struggle to even do my job.


02:16:47-02:16:51
If the location of the menu items had changed every day, I would have gotten fired.


02:16:51-02:16:59
Thankfully, my managers were understanding and they didn't also change where the lemons were or what the side work was.


02:16:59-02:17:04
They expected that we'd have trouble with the changes, though I definitely had more trouble than most.


02:17:04-02:17:10
They understood we need to get to the point where that system became automatic again before they changed anything else.


02:17:10-02:17:18
Now, when I'm struggling to executive function, I check if any of the systems I rely on have been thrown into manual mode.


02:17:18-02:17:24
And to remind myself that when a lot of my life is on manual mode, it's not a good time to add more to my plate.


02:17:24-02:17:33
When you're getting adjusted to something new, be aware of how many new systems are changing and try to keep as much of your life on automatic as possible.


02:17:33-02:17:40
In the effort to improve our lives, it can be easy to forget that some parts are already working for us.


02:17:40-02:17:49
And keeping these parts the same, even if they're in areas we'd eventually like to improve, can provide a more stable foundation for the changes we're trying to make.


02:17:49-02:17:52
And if you are struggling with a new system, give it time.


02:17:52-02:17:57
Even a system that will eventually work beautifully is often going to be a struggle at first.

### Chapter 5: How to Sleep [02:18:00]
02:17:57-02:18:04
Chapter five, how to sleep.


02:18:04-02:18:09
I want to sleep, but my brain won't stop talking to itself.


02:18:09-02:18:10
ADHD brains everywhere.


02:18:10-02:18:14
Chaos theory.


02:18:14-02:18:18
If sleep were a Dungeons and Dragons character, its alignment would be chaotic neutral.


02:18:18-02:18:28
Sleep doesn't care what you have going on the next day, what time you were supposed to wake up, or the fact that in certain situations, sleeping is rude or even illegal.


02:18:28-02:18:33
For many of us, sleep does what it wants, when it wants, and doesn't care whose day it ruins.


02:18:33-02:18:36
And for me, it's been doing it since day one.


02:18:36-02:18:44
According to family lore, my mom kicked me out of her room on the first night I came home from the hospital because I was so dang noisy, even when I wasn't crying.


02:18:44-02:18:47
And I wouldn't settle down no matter what she tried.


02:18:47-02:18:55
She deposited me in my crib across the hall, which she hadn't intended to use for six months, and shut the door with a sardonic whispered prayer.


02:18:55-02:18:57
Please God, don't let her die.


02:18:57-02:19:00
Then she dove into her own bed to rest for the next day's battle.


02:19:00-02:19:07
I often slept in weird places, only in my bouncy chair, or while being driven around, or on my dad's chest.


02:19:07-02:19:10
I can't get up, the baby is sleeping.


02:19:10-02:19:13
This annoyed my mom to no end, but I am grateful for it.


02:19:13-02:19:18
To this day, laying my head or face on someone I love is the most comforting thing in the world for me.


02:19:18-02:19:23
It makes me feel safe, and is often part of how I wind down for bed.


02:19:23-02:19:31
Some nights as a kid, I'd lie awake for hours, staring at the popcorn ceiling in my bedroom, or cracking the door open to peek down the hall at the TV.


02:19:31-02:19:38
Other nights, I would wake up a few short hours after bedtime, my legs hurting so badly I needed ibuprofen and a hot bath to get back to sleep.


02:19:38-02:19:42
Growing pains, the doctor said, not uncommon.


02:19:42-02:19:45
In the morning though, I slept like the dead.


02:19:45-02:19:50
I'd miss everything from my alarm to breakfast to an entire class.


02:19:50-02:19:57
Frequently, even when I did wake up on time, I was exhausted, my jaw sore, as if I'd been having fist fights in my nightmares.


02:19:57-02:20:00
She grinds her teeth, the dentist said, it's not uncommon.


02:20:00-02:20:07
My doctor and dentist weren't wrong, my symptoms weren't uncommon among children and adolescents.


02:20:07-02:20:12
What deserved more attention though, was how significantly I had struggled, and continue to struggle with sleep.


02:20:12-02:20:16
Some kids grow out of their sleep difficulties, mine persisted.


02:20:17-02:20:21
As a teenager, I would fall asleep before I made it to bed, or even into the house.


02:20:21-02:20:26
One night, I woke up in my parents' minivan, still in my marching band uniform, hat and all.


02:20:26-02:20:30
My boyfriend might suffer the most from my difficulties with sleep.


02:20:30-02:20:35
Sleep problems can be the hardest aspects of ADHD to mask, especially from the people you live with.


02:20:35-02:20:41
While watching TV before bed in the evening, I constantly shift positions or pace around the room.


02:20:41-02:20:47
If I do manage to settle down, I might kick him as I doze off or wake him up with bed yoga at 3 a.m.


02:20:47-02:20:53
I do actual yoga positions in bed, not because it's a great time to get in a workout, but because my brain and body are too restless.


02:20:53-02:20:59
Sometimes I find the right position to calm my nervous system, or get enough energy out to finally fall asleep.


02:20:59-02:21:07
Last night, I scared him because just as he was falling asleep, my leg shot straight up into the air and stayed there.


02:21:07-02:21:08
I don't remember this.


02:21:08-02:21:16
My sleeping and waking behaviors have always felt oddly out of my control, and I was pretty sure many of them were weird.


02:21:16-02:21:18
I was incredibly frustrated by them.


02:21:18-02:21:24
I also had no idea what was causing them, as I described it in the episode on sleep that I created for my channel.


02:21:24-02:21:29
I didn't know my sleep problems were related to my ADHD.


02:21:29-02:21:43
I just thought I was a borderline narcoleptic night owl with restless leg syndrome who'd grind her teeth at night, fall asleep every time I sat down to relax, not go to bed so much as pass out from exhaustion, and sleep through every alarm short of the ice bucket challenge.


02:21:43-02:21:46
What I learned.


02:21:46-02:21:49
Sleep disorders are common with ADHD.


02:21:49-02:21:50
Really common.


02:21:50-02:21:53
Like if you have ADHD, you probably have one common.


02:21:53-02:21:58
My doctors never looked more deeply at my sleep habits, but they should have.


02:21:58-02:22:04
Sleep problems are so prevalent with ADHD, they used to be part of the diagnostic criteria.


02:22:04-02:22:20
According to the first edition of "Sleep and ADHD, an Evidence-Based Guide to Assessment and Treatment" published in 2019, 73% of children and adolescents and 80% of adults with ADHD experience sleep disorders.


02:22:20-02:22:23
These conditions commonly coexist with ADHD.


02:22:23-02:22:26
Obstructive sleep apnea.


02:22:26-02:22:36
This sleep disorder, characterized by snoring and pauses in breathing during sleep, is more common among adults, but it does occur in children, especially children with ADHD.


02:22:36-02:22:39
Restless leg syndrome.


02:22:39-02:22:47
This condition causes an intense, often irresistible urge to move your legs, particularly when sitting or lying down.


02:22:47-02:22:53
Unlike ADHD-related hyperactivity, it happens mostly at night and often gets worse with age.


02:22:53-02:22:56
Periodic limb movement syndrome.


02:22:56-02:23:00
You know how your leg kicks or your arm flops all of a sudden when you're falling asleep?


02:23:00-02:23:01
It has a name.


02:23:01-02:23:07
At least it does when it keeps happening every 20 to 40 seconds and long enough to interfere with sleep.


02:23:07-02:23:12
This is different from the hypnic jerk, the sudden movement of your entire body that happens as you're falling asleep.


02:23:12-02:23:15
Those don't usually interfere with sleep.


02:23:15-02:23:17
Sleepwalking and night terrors.


02:23:17-02:23:22
These sleep disorders occur when the lines between awake and asleep are blurred.


02:23:22-02:23:24
They're often first observed in childhood by parents.


02:23:24-02:23:27
Insomnia.


02:23:27-02:23:28
You've probably heard of this one.


02:23:28-02:23:36
Insomnia occurs whenever you want to sleep, but can't sleep due to difficulties falling asleep or staying asleep.


02:23:36-02:23:39
And it is also one of the criteria for delayed sleep phase syndrome.


02:23:39-02:23:42
Delayed sleep phase syndrome.


02:23:42-02:23:49
This syndrome occurs when your body's internal clock or its circadian rhythm is delayed by two or more hours.


02:23:49-02:23:53
For example, you might naturally want to sleep from 3 a.m. to noon.


02:23:53-02:23:56
Excessive daytime sleepiness.


02:23:56-02:23:58
This condition is exactly what it sounds like.


02:23:58-02:24:06
If you're falling asleep in the middle of a movie at your friend's house or missing a shift because you can't stay awake, it doesn't mean you're a bad friend or a lazy employee.


02:24:06-02:24:08
It could be a sign that something is wrong.


02:24:08-02:24:18
Current research suggests that more severe ADHD symptoms are associated with more severe sleep disorder symptoms and vice versa.


02:24:18-02:24:23
Sleep disorders are also more prevalent with certain ADHD presentations.


02:24:23-02:24:35
For instance, excessive daytime sleepiness is more strongly associated with the primarily inattentive presentation, while restless legs syndrome is more strongly associated with the primarily hyperactive impulsive presentation.


02:24:35-02:24:40
Those of us with combined presentation are at higher risk for both conditions.


02:24:40-02:24:41
Woo!


02:24:41-02:24:44
We're more likely to miss sleep.


02:24:44-02:24:51
There are all kinds of reasons people sacrifice sleep, grad school, caring for a baby, or meeting book deadlines.


02:24:51-02:24:56
ADHDers, however, are more likely to regularly experience the following reasons for staying awake.


02:24:56-02:24:59
We're not tired yet.


02:24:59-02:25:06
Those with ADHD tend to have a later chronotype, that is the time we're naturally inclined to sleep is later than usual.


02:25:06-02:25:13
For those with these chronotypes, melatonin, the sleep hormone, is released later, delaying our bedtime.


02:25:13-02:25:25
Dr. Steven Becker, an expert in both ADHD and sleep, who shared his insights with me, says this is especially problematic in adolescents when chronotypes are naturally later and more sleep is required.


02:25:25-02:25:36
Chronotype, noun, your body's natural disposition to be awake/alert or sleepy/asleep at certain times of the day, based on your circadian rhythm.


02:25:36-02:25:39
We're not done yet.


02:25:39-02:25:45
Because we have difficulty focusing, homework, chores, and bedtime routines can take longer for us to finish.


02:25:45-02:25:53
We might find ourselves staying up late to take care of things we forgot, cramming for tests at the last minute, or finishing projects the night before their due.


02:25:53-02:26:01
[[Time Management]], [[motivation]], and [[focus]] also pose significant challenges for those with ADHD, and our struggles in these areas often cut into our sleep.


02:26:01-02:26:06
We're too stimulated, or not stimulated enough.


02:26:06-02:26:13
Stimulant medications and caffeine are commonly used to treat ADHD, or self-medicate in the case of caffeine.


02:26:13-02:26:15
They can also keep us awake.


02:26:15-02:26:19
However, for some with ADHD, stimulants have the opposite effect.


02:26:19-02:26:27
Many doctors report some patients have an easier time making the transition to bed when they're medicated and find it more difficult when their meds have worn off.


02:26:27-02:26:34
Under-stimulated brains might race with a million thoughts, pick a fight with their bedmate, or bounce out of bed for one more bedtime snack.


02:26:34-02:26:38
We experience revenge bedtime procrastination.


02:26:38-02:26:48
[[revenge bedtime procrastination]] is a phrase that describes the experience of staying awake to play video games, message friends, let our brains dive down Wikipedia rabbit holes.


02:26:48-02:26:51
You know, the things we didn't get to do while we were awake.


02:26:51-02:26:54
Bedtime? What's a bedtime?


02:26:54-02:27:02
Research has found that inconsistent sleep schedules can make it harder to get enough sleep, and a lack of routine is associated with inconsistent sleep schedules.


02:27:02-02:27:10
Since ADHD brains tend to have trouble sticking with routines, it isn't surprising that we have trouble sleeping at the same time every night.


02:27:10-02:27:14
But sometimes our lack of routine might be due to our difficulty sleeping.


02:27:14-02:27:19
After all, what's the point of going to bed at a certain time when you know you're just gonna stare at the ceiling for three hours?


02:27:19-02:27:23
Going to bed is boring.


02:27:23-02:27:31
Boredom is painful for ADHD brains, and sleep can be boring, especially if we need to transition from doing something fun to a tedious bedtime routine.


02:27:31-02:27:38
When we finally make it to bed, we often deal with the distress that boredom causes by distracting ourselves with activities that don't support a good night's sleep.


02:27:38-02:27:39
What's up, blue light?


02:27:39-02:27:43
We need sleep.


02:27:43-02:27:48
Skipping sleep can, in some circles, become a point of pride and even earn you bragging rights.


02:27:48-02:27:52
Certain phrases from college and from living with other neurodivergent brains come to mind.


02:27:52-02:27:53
I'll sleep when I'm dead.


02:27:53-02:27:55
Sleep is for quitters.


02:27:55-02:27:56
I don't need sleep.


02:27:56-02:27:58
Sleep is for the weak.


02:27:58-02:28:03
When sleep often isn't an option, not sleeping can become an identity, a lifestyle.


02:28:03-02:28:05
You lean into it.


02:28:05-02:28:09
Unfortunately, all this bravado doesn't change the fact that we need sleep.


02:28:09-02:28:18
Out of all the self-care practices that support brain function, such as eating well and exercising, getting enough sleep is by far the most crucial.


02:28:18-02:28:25
When we forego sleep, it's at the expense of our energy levels, alertness, cognitive capabilities, and [[emotional regulation|emotion regulation]].


02:28:25-02:28:29
Missing out on sleep makes our ADHD symptoms worse.


02:28:29-02:28:38
Research on children with ADHD has found that as little as a 30-minute reduction from normal sleep duration is enough to impact their daytime functioning and behavior.


02:28:38-02:28:42
Inadequate sleep impacts many of the same executive functions affected by ADHD.


02:28:42-02:28:52
Attention regulation, recall and working memory, processing speed, response inhibition.


02:28:52-02:28:57
While it's tempting to tell ourselves we can make up for missed sleep on the weekend, it doesn't work that way.


02:28:57-02:29:00
Sleep is not a side quest.


02:29:00-02:29:05
Human beings need to sleep consistently, and those of us with ADHD aren't exceptions.


02:29:05-02:29:11
It might be harder for us to sleep well, but the consequences are greater for us when we don't.


02:29:11-02:29:15
Shelly S. 49, Canada.


02:29:15-02:29:18
I am an active sleeper, both physically and mentally.


02:29:18-02:29:20
When I sleep, I rub my feet together.


02:29:20-02:29:22
We call it cricket feet.


02:29:22-02:29:27
I jump when I flip over, I roll up in the blanket, and I flip my pillow about four times a night.


02:29:27-02:29:30
Mentally, I am very alert and wake up easily to noises.


02:29:30-02:29:35
Sometimes I wake up with my heart pounding if I hear people outside being too loud.


02:29:35-02:29:40
I also do a ton of problem solving through dreams and come up with ideas for art projects I am working on.


02:29:40-02:29:44
Kristen H. 33, USA.


02:29:44-02:29:50
Procrastination, sleep, and I are in a toxic mid-2000s love triangle.


02:29:50-02:29:55
Logically, I know an adequate amount of sleep will help me think more clearly and lead me to make better decisions.


02:29:55-02:30:02
It's the safe, reasonable choice, but bedtime procrastination lures me back in with promises of excitement and adventure.


02:30:02-02:30:06
Luca H. 26, Australia.


02:30:06-02:30:11
For me, getting to bed is a constant struggle against the dopamine slot machine that is my phone.


02:30:12-02:30:15
Adrian G. 20, Norway.


02:30:15-02:30:17
I've had trouble sleeping for as long as I can remember.


02:30:17-02:30:25
My overactive brain also used to give me a lot of nightmares as a kid, to the point of me waking up screaming every night for a long period of time.


02:30:25-02:30:27
My biggest problem now is falling asleep.


02:30:27-02:30:32
Weighted blanket, medication, and trying every single advice available.


02:30:32-02:30:33
Nothing works.


02:30:33-02:30:36
Simply put, sleeping is stressful.


02:30:36-02:30:40
The Toolbox.


02:30:40-02:30:49
While there are effective treatments available for every single sleep disorder associated with ADHD, there are many practices that you can do for yourself or loved ones right now.


02:30:49-02:30:53
One, prioritize the right amount of sleep.


02:30:53-02:31:01
While there are times when we have trouble sleeping, there are other times when we skip sleep to have extra hours in our day to get stuff done.


02:31:01-02:31:08
Prioritizing sleep means no longer seeing sleep as optional or less important than whatever else needs to happen in our day.


02:31:08-02:31:13
None of the other strategies will matter much if we throw sleep out the window the second something comes up.


02:31:13-02:31:20
You might be wondering, but isn't that 6 a.m. yoga class/gym session worth waking up early for?


02:31:20-02:31:24
If you can do that and still get enough sleep, sure.


02:31:24-02:31:30
But according to Dr. Patrick LeCount, if you need to pick between exercise and getting enough sleep, sleep.


02:31:30-02:31:35
This is coming from someone who researches the positive impacts of exercise on ADHD.


02:31:35-02:31:42
However, prioritizing sleep doesn't mean getting as much sleep as possible or even getting eight hours.


02:31:42-02:31:48
The amount of sleep we need varies from person to person and changes over our lifetime.


02:31:48-02:31:54
According to experts, you should aim to sleep long enough to fulfill your intrinsic sleep needs.


02:31:54-02:32:00
In other words, we should be aiming for enough sleep so that we feel refreshed and alert, not dull and sleepy.


02:32:00-02:32:05
Daniel C., 36, Kansas.


02:32:05-02:32:07
I absolutely love the quiet nights.


02:32:07-02:32:14
I love that I get to forget about deadlines and requirements as the day is usually over and just about the only time I don't feel like there's a demand on me.


02:32:14-02:32:18
I also enjoy those nights because I'm alone and don't have to mask in any way.


02:32:18-02:32:21
But losing sleep makes me more impulsive.


02:32:21-02:32:29
When I stay up as an impulsive decision and then continue to make impulsive decisions in the following days, I regret it and try to adjust my habits.


02:32:29-02:32:33
Shizu T., 23, Oregon.


02:32:33-02:32:38
I've trained my body to go to sleep early because I need 11 hours of sleep a night to be fully rested.


02:32:38-02:32:42
I can fall asleep around nine, but I'll wake up a couple of times between then and one.


02:32:42-02:32:46
After midnight, I'm pretty much guaranteed to go to sleep and stay asleep.


02:32:46-02:32:50
Two, practice good sleep hygiene?


02:32:50-02:32:51
Question mark.


02:32:51-02:33:01
Practicing good sleep hygiene means following the personal practices and before bed rituals that help us sleep better and avoiding the ones that make us sleep worse.


02:33:01-02:33:03
But what is that stuff?


02:33:03-02:33:05
That's tricky.


02:33:05-02:33:12
While there's enough research to inform general guidelines for good sleep hygiene, what works for each person varies.


02:33:12-02:33:23
Plus, because much of the research on sleep hygiene has been done on the general population, there are still a lot of gaps in that knowledge when it comes to the population with ADHD.


02:33:23-02:33:30
Even when we know what good sleep hygiene is, we may struggle to follow it and follow it consistently.


02:33:30-02:33:36
Having ADHD means laughing heartily at common sleep hygiene tips because they are so unrealistic for us.


02:33:36-02:33:39
Avoid electronics for two hours before bed?


02:33:39-02:33:40
Are they kidding?


02:33:40-02:33:46
Still, there are a few research-backed sleep hygiene strategies worth trying for most of us.


02:33:46-02:33:50
Time your stimulants.


02:33:50-02:33:55
Many of us use stimulant medication, caffeine, and even nicotine to manage our ADHD.


02:33:55-02:34:00
Some stimulants naturally last longer than others, and some last longer for certain individuals.


02:34:00-02:34:04
For some, caffeine at bedtime can help them focus on getting to sleep.


02:34:04-02:34:08
For others, any stimulants after noon can make it harder.


02:34:08-02:34:12
Try tracking what time you take any stimulants and how you sleep that night.


02:34:12-02:34:15
Use this information to figure out the best time for you to take them.


02:34:15-02:34:18
Avoid conflict right before bed.


02:34:18-02:34:27
Since sleep happens more easily when you feel safe, give yourself and your loved ones enough time to return to your emotional baselines before going to bed.


02:34:27-02:34:35
If there's a non-urgent issue, address it well before bedtime, or leave it for another day so you have a better chance of being able to sleep.


02:34:35-02:34:44
Not only will everyone be less cognitively drained while you're trying to discuss the issue, it also avoids a common ADHD bedtime trap of I'm bored and fighting is stimulating.


02:34:44-02:34:49
Keep bedtime and wake times as consistent as possible.


02:34:49-02:34:51
Sleep depends on two factors.


02:34:51-02:35:01
One, your circadian rhythm, and two, your homeostatic regulation, the process that regulates your biological systems, including your need for sleep.


02:35:01-02:35:03
It works like hunger and thirst.


02:35:03-02:35:06
The longer we go without eating, the hungrier we get.


02:35:06-02:35:09
The longer we go without sleeping, the more tired we get.


02:35:09-02:35:15
Sleep is most effective when your circadian rhythm and homeostatic regulation are working together.


02:35:15-02:35:21
This is why your best bet for good sleep is to keep your bedtimes and wake times as consistent as you can.


02:35:21-02:35:27
This way, your body has a chance to build up the need for sleep as your body clock starts nudging you toward bed.


02:35:27-02:35:35
If you do need to adjust your bedtime, Dr. Becker recommends doing it incrementally, moving it no more than 15 minutes at a time.


02:35:35-02:35:38
Associate your bed with sleep.


02:35:38-02:35:46
The more time we spend in bed staring at the ceiling or completing a task such as answering emails, the more we associate our bed with wakefulness.


02:35:46-02:35:51
This can weaken the brain's cue to sleep when you settle into bed.


02:35:51-02:35:59
Experts suggest that if you have been lying awake for 20 minutes, get out of bed, do something boring, and come back when you're ready to sleep.


02:35:59-02:36:07
If you do need to use your bed for other tasks, create a cue that only happens at bedtime to help you differentiate between workbed and sleepbed.


02:36:07-02:36:10
For example, you might keep your pillows off the bed until it's time to sleep.


02:36:10-02:36:14
A note on electronics.


02:36:14-02:36:17
There's conflicting research about electronics at bedtime.


02:36:17-02:36:21
The standard advice says to limit screen time and keep devices out of the bedroom.


02:36:21-02:36:27
However, there isn't strong evidence that avoiding electronics in the hours before bedtime is necessary.


02:36:27-02:36:28
Thank the screen time gods.


02:36:28-02:36:36
If watching reality TV or wiki walking on your tablet before bed makes you sleepy, by all means, go for it.


02:36:36-02:36:47
No matter what you choose to do, keep in mind that there is strong support in the scientific literature for leaving electronics out of the bedroom as a way to promote healthy sleep and healthy relationships.


02:36:47-02:36:47
Wink.


02:36:47-02:36:51
Sean P. 46, Michigan.


02:36:51-02:36:55
For the bulk of my life, I've slept poorly, regardless of the time.


02:36:55-02:36:59
Last year, my doctor asked if caffeine helped me sleep.


02:36:59-02:37:01
Trying such a thing never occurred to me.


02:37:01-02:37:07
Now, a cup of caffeinated tea right before bed helps me fall asleep, and I sleep better now than ever in my life.


02:37:07-02:37:11
Romana N. 34, North Macedonia.


02:37:11-02:37:15
I sleep the best when I do everything opposite of what is recommended.


02:37:15-02:37:20
I sleep from 2 a.m. to 10 a.m., drink caffeine before bed, and fall asleep with the TV droning on.


02:37:20-02:37:24
Raven M. 27, Tennessee.


02:37:24-02:37:33
One of the side effects of getting medicated for my anxiety and CPTSD, complex post-traumatic stress disorder, has been the ability to regulate my sleep schedule.


02:37:33-02:37:37
But I still follow a nightly routine to the best of my ability.


02:37:37-02:37:44
It includes relaxing with hobbies, aromatherapy, a really good eye mask, and listening to the Great British Baking Show with a sleep timer on the TV.


02:37:44-02:37:48
Three, motivate yourself to sleep.


02:37:48-02:37:55
Knowing that we should sleep and that it's important often isn't enough to convince an ADHD brain to shut down for the night.


02:37:55-02:37:58
We are attracted to things that are exciting, urgent, and new.


02:37:58-02:38:01
Sleep is pretty much the opposite of all that.


02:38:01-02:38:06
The societal and social pressures to skip it don't help either.


02:38:06-02:38:09
Here are some strategies you might consider to make bedtime a better time.


02:38:09-02:38:13
Get your needs met during the day.


02:38:13-02:38:20
We might assume prioritizing the full amount of sleep our brains need means giving up hobby time, alone time, or social time.


02:38:20-02:38:23
But those activities are important too.


02:38:23-02:38:31
If we give ourselves guilt-free time for them during the day, we'll be less likely to do them while we're supposed to be sleeping and more motivated to actually sleep.


02:38:31-02:38:36
And getting sleep can make us faster at getting our other stuff done, so we're more likely to have time for both.


02:38:36-02:38:40
Create wind down rituals you enjoy.


02:38:40-02:38:43
Our brains and bodies need time to wind down before bed.


02:38:43-02:38:52
The key for those with ADHD is to choose wind down rituals that are interesting enough for you to want to do them, but not so engaging that you can't stop doing them.


02:38:52-02:38:57
Think putting together puzzles, reading, trading massages, or watching reruns of old TV shows.


02:38:57-02:39:00
Consider your sensory needs.


02:39:00-02:39:08
A lot of ADHDers are sensitive to rough blankets, weird-tasting toothpaste, the fit of their clothes, or tags on their PJs.


02:39:08-02:39:12
On the flip side, we can be drawn to sensory good experiences.


02:39:12-02:39:26
To make bedtime more appealing, it can be helpful to seek out soft, cozy blankets, silk pillowcases, weighted comforters, essential oil diffusers, different flavors of toothpaste, or various states of undress.


02:39:26-02:39:30
If your body feels good, it will be easier to relax, mentally and physically.


02:39:30-02:39:34
Marie S. 32, New Jersey.


02:39:34-02:39:37
The more I can do to wind down before I sleep, the better.


02:39:37-02:39:45
Dimming the lights in the bathroom, using lavender shower gel, changing into clean PJs, I do all that at least 45 minutes before bed.


02:39:46-02:39:49
Joe 42, Ohio.


02:39:49-02:39:53
I have listened to the same Lord of the Rings audiobook recordings for over a decade.


02:39:53-02:39:55
Now I associate it with sleep.


02:39:55-02:39:57
It's Pavlovian when I hear it.


02:39:57-02:40:01
Anne Bettina P. 44, Denmark.


02:40:01-02:40:05
About six months ago, doing Sudoku on my phone helped me sleep.


02:40:05-02:40:08
Then it was crossword puzzles, and now it's some TV series.


02:40:08-02:40:10
The main thing is to distract my brain.


02:40:10-02:40:15
Andrew F. 37, Washington.


02:40:15-02:40:19
Since elementary school, I've always drifted off to sleep listening to someone talking.


02:40:19-02:40:23
It was talk radio, a baseball game, or old time radio replays when I was younger.


02:40:23-02:40:26
And since college, it's been podcasts.


02:40:26-02:40:34
For the last 15 years or so, I also have a ritual around Sunday nights where I always listen to, "Wait, wait, don't tell me," as I fall asleep.


02:40:34-02:40:36
That's how I mentally prepare for Mondays.


02:40:36-02:40:40
Four, work with your chronotype.


02:40:40-02:40:47
Those with ADHD tend to have night owl chronotypes, which can make it hard to get enough sleep in a world that expects us to wake up early.


02:40:47-02:40:56
Still, even though late to bed may be the most common chronotype for ADHD brains, it's not the only scenario, especially when chronotypes can shift over time.


02:40:56-02:41:01
Fun fact, chronotypes shift earlier and fairly dramatically with pregnancy.


02:41:01-02:41:02
I am exhausted.


02:41:02-02:41:03
It's 4 p.m.


02:41:03-02:41:11
Understanding and working with our current chronotype can help us plan our day around the times when we'll be most alert and when we'll get our best sleep.


02:41:13-02:41:14
Figure out your chronotype.


02:41:14-02:41:22
For a couple weeks, maybe over the summer or on vacation, try going to bed when you feel sleepy and waking up without an alarm clock.


02:41:22-02:41:26
This can give you a clue as to what your current chronotype might be.


02:41:26-02:41:29
Plan to tackle focus-heavy work when you're most alert.


02:41:29-02:41:34
Try signing up for later classes if you're night owl or requesting earlier work shifts if you're a morning lark.


02:41:34-02:41:41
Even a traditional nine-to-five job might offer a more flexible schedule as an accommodation for your ADHD.


02:41:41-02:41:47
Even if you can't shift your work hours, it's often possible to choose when you complete tasks that require more focus.


02:41:47-02:41:52
Your chronotype doesn't just affect when you get sleepy, but also when you're most alert.


02:41:52-02:41:55
Planning work around this can boost your overall productivity.


02:41:55-02:41:58
Prep for mornings.


02:41:58-02:42:03
If you need to wake up earlier than your natural chronotype would prefer, prepare what you can the night before.


02:42:03-02:42:04
Lay out your clothes.


02:42:04-02:42:07
Put the things you need for work on a launch pad by the door.


02:42:07-02:42:09
Throw together some overnight oats.


02:42:09-02:42:10
You get the idea.


02:42:10-02:42:18
This prep work lets you spend as much time as possible in your chronotypical sleep zone and makes good use of the time when you're still wide awake.


02:42:18-02:42:20
Create light cues.


02:42:20-02:42:24
Circadian rhythms are sensitive to environmental cues, specifically light.


02:42:24-02:42:28
Set smart light bulbs to gradually dim at night when it's time to go to bed.


02:42:28-02:42:33
This will cue your brain to produce melatonin naturally, readying you for sleep.


02:42:33-02:42:36
You can also use smart lights to simulate sunrise.


02:42:36-02:42:44
And just going outside first thing in the morning and absorbing the sun like a heckin' houseplant can help calibrate your body's circadian rhythm.


02:42:44-02:42:49
The sunlight communicates to your brain and body that it's daytime and that you should be awake.


02:42:49-02:42:53
Olivia L., 34, Texas.


02:42:53-02:42:57
I normally don't get ready for bed on weekdays until after 11 p.m.


02:42:57-02:42:59
because I'm more productive in the evenings.


02:42:59-02:43:03
My brain does not function at its finest until at least 1 p.m.


02:43:03-02:43:06
My mom has always said that I'm like a bat in the morning.


02:43:06-02:43:09
Moving slowly, don't like lights, would rather not talk.


02:43:09-02:43:11
I wish I could be a morning person.


02:43:11-02:43:12
I really have tried.


02:43:12-02:43:16
Going to bed early and getting up early does not work for me, sadly.


02:43:16-02:43:20
Shania, 49, Michigan.


02:43:20-02:43:23
I have to start my evening unwinding with a hot bath.


02:43:23-02:43:26
I have a physically demanding job and I have to relax my muscles.


02:43:26-02:43:30
The challenge is I'm a night owl, so my cutoff is 11 p.m.


02:43:30-02:43:34
I make a point as an early riser Monday through Friday to take vitamin supplements every morning.


02:43:34-02:43:39
I allow myself to sleep in on weekends because of the demands of my job during the week.


02:43:39-02:43:42
A note on melatonin.


02:43:42-02:43:48
If you have a later chronotype and you don't produce melatonin when it's supposed to be sleepy time, store-bought is fine.


02:43:48-02:43:52
In fact, many ADHDers fall asleep with the help of melatonin supplements.


02:43:52-02:44:01
Melatonin is the most commonly used treatment for disrupted sleep caused by ADHD, and it can significantly improve our ability to fall asleep earlier.


02:44:01-02:44:13
That said, over-the-counter melatonin is not regulated, and there is controversy over who should be using it, at what dosage, and for how long, as well as how it may impact our other sleep challenges, such as restless leg syndrome.


02:44:13-02:44:16
Before trying it, talk to your doctor.


02:44:16-02:44:17
I am not a doctor.


02:44:17-02:44:20
Five, have a backup plan.


02:44:20-02:44:23
Sometimes you do everything right and still have trouble sleeping.


02:44:23-02:44:28
Anxiety about getting to sleep can make it harder to fall asleep, so have a backup plan.


02:44:28-02:44:37
Not only will this help you survive the aftermath of a restless night and get through the next day, it might also help you sleep better because you know you've got options in case you can't.


02:44:37-02:44:42
That said, if you find yourself relying on backup plans more than usual, check in with yourself.


02:44:42-02:44:43
Are you okay?


02:44:43-02:44:51
More sleep disturbances than usual for you can be a sign of other issues with your mental or physical health, relationships, or work life.


02:44:51-02:44:53
When in doubt, ask a professional.


02:44:53-02:44:56
Sleep somewhere else.


02:44:56-02:45:03
Sometimes switching locations can help you fall asleep because you don't associate a different spot with the frustration of trying to doze off.


02:45:03-02:45:08
At the very least, you can relax better knowing you won't be disturbing anyone else if you don't.


02:45:08-02:45:10
You can also switch sleeping positions.


02:45:10-02:45:14
I often flip around so my feet are where my pillow should be.


02:45:14-02:45:15
Rest.


02:45:15-02:45:19
If you really can't sleep, you can get some of its benefits by simply resting.


02:45:19-02:45:26
Getting rest can include meditating, practicing restorative yoga, or even just lying in bed with your eyes closed.


02:45:26-02:45:28
Make a wake-up plan.


02:45:28-02:45:33
While those with ADHD have trouble falling asleep, by morning, the struggle is typically waking up.


02:45:33-02:45:39
Set backup alarms, have someone call or check on you to make sure you're awake, or have whatever helps you wake up at the ready.


02:45:39-02:45:40
Cats are helpful.


02:45:40-02:45:44
Flexible start times at work can help here too, as can coffee.


02:45:44-02:45:48
Jessica, 36, Los Angeles.


02:45:48-02:45:57
When I was taking meditation classes, the teacher explained how meditation can be deeply restful and restore energy in a way that's similar to sleep, just not at first.


02:45:57-02:46:02
First, it kicks out all the stress props that have been holding us up, so we may actually be more tired.


02:46:02-02:46:04
I found he was right.


02:46:04-02:46:09
Now when I can't sleep, I spend more time meditating to try and recharge my body and brain batteries.


02:46:09-02:46:15
As a bonus, it helps with my anxiety, so I have a better chance of being able to sleep the next night.


02:46:15-02:46:18
Raphael B, 41, Seattle.


02:46:18-02:46:26
When I can't fall asleep, I leave the bedroom and read something that's just interesting enough to keep my focus, but not interesting enough to stimulate me.


02:46:26-02:46:28
Often, that's factoids on Wikipedia.


02:46:28-02:46:33
Teresa W, 43, Michigan.


02:46:33-02:46:37
Some nights, my odd-EHD teenager can only sleep on hard mode.


02:46:37-02:46:43
Instead of resting in their too quiet bedroom, I'll find them on the couch, fully clothed, occasionally with the lights on.


02:46:43-02:46:46
Owls and larks.


02:46:46-02:46:52
For most of my life, I considered going to bed on time to be early enough to wake up at five or six a.m.


02:46:52-02:46:55
so I can do my sun salutations at sunrise.


02:46:55-02:46:58
Anything past that time was clearly late.


02:46:58-02:47:00
Needless to say, I went to bed late a lot.


02:47:00-02:47:12
When I did manage to go to bed on time, I often felt proud of myself as if doing so somehow transformed me into a morally superior human being, even just for that day.


02:47:12-02:47:15
There are a lot of sayings that reinforce this message.


02:47:15-02:47:19
Early to bed and early to rise makes a man healthy, wealthy, and wise.


02:47:19-02:47:22
The early bird gets the worm.


02:47:22-02:47:25
Success comes to those who have the willpower to win over their snooze buttons.


02:47:25-02:47:33
When I was researching this chapter, I stumbled across an article in The New Yorker with a title that immediately caught my eye.


02:47:33-02:47:36
No, mornings don't make you moral.


02:47:36-02:47:49
Journalist Maria Konikova explains there is some research to suggest that people behave more ethically during the day than they do at night, what the researchers call the morning morality effect, but there's a caveat.


02:47:49-02:47:53
This effect is only observed if it aligns with your chronotype.


02:47:53-02:47:59
Some people did cheat less in the morning, Konikova found, but only if they were early birds to begin with.


02:47:59-02:48:02
The opposite was also true.


02:48:02-02:48:05
Night owls cheated less in the evening.


02:48:05-02:48:14
Time of day had less effect on honesty, the group concluded, than did the synchronicity between person and environment, emphasis mine.


02:48:14-02:48:21
Even if being morally superior isn't a priority, I still feel the pressure to wake up early because it helps me be productive.


02:48:21-02:48:26
If I wake up early, I can get more done during the day because that's when businesses are open.


02:48:26-02:48:32
So can we change our chronotype and become the kind of people who do yoga every day at 6 a.m.?


02:48:32-02:48:35
"Good question," said Dr. Becker when I asked him.


02:48:35-02:48:43
He explained that while chronotypes do shift naturally over the course of our lifetime, intentionally shifting them is another story.


02:48:43-02:48:46
Other researchers agree.


02:48:46-02:49:00
For her article, Konikova spoke with behavioral scientist Sunita Sa, who found that it's incredibly difficult, if not impossible, to overcome your predisposition and train yourself to function better at times that don't match up with your inner clock.


02:49:00-02:49:08
This made me wonder if I'd be a better boss, partner, or pal if I accepted the bedtime appropriate for me based on my chronotype, not someone else's.


02:49:08-02:49:14
As the morningness, eveningness questionnaire, and plenty of past experience suggests, mine is 12.30 a.m.


02:49:14-02:49:21
What would happen if and when I made this bedtime work and got enough sleep and felt proud of myself for doing so?


02:49:21-02:49:26
The article also got me thinking about how we judge people based on their sleep patterns.


02:49:26-02:49:35
It's common to assume someone is lazy because they sleep longer than other people, more dedicated if they're awake by 6 a.m., or no fun because they skipped the nightclub.


02:49:35-02:49:46
Now that we understand that people need different amounts of sleep and have significant natural variations in when they need to sleep, maybe we can start to let go of these moral judgments.


02:49:46-02:49:52
The truth is, there are incredibly productive people who sleep until noon and social butterflies who get tired by 8 p.m.


02:49:52-02:49:57
We can also try building a world that accounts for the diversity in chronotypes.


02:49:57-02:50:01
Technology has made flexible hours and work-from-home options more common.


02:50:01-02:50:05
The internet has allowed us to connect with others beyond our own time zone.


02:50:05-02:50:14
Online options for everyday tasks, such as corresponding with loved ones and depositing checks, makes post office and bank hours practically obsolete.


02:50:14-02:50:18
You can even find an online notary who's available 24/7.


02:50:18-02:50:21
Maybe late night bookstores and dance party brunches will be next.


02:50:21-02:50:29
And hey, if you happen to hear of and/or start any businesses that operate outside of standard business hours, let me know.


02:50:29-02:50:33
I know some night owls and morning larks who would love to drop by.


02:50:33-02:50:41
Chapter six, how to see time.


02:50:41-02:50:53
People assume that time is a strict progression from cause to effect, but actually from a non-linear, non-subjective viewpoint, it's more like a big ball of wibbly wobbly, timey wimey stuff.


02:50:53-02:50:55
Doctor Who, The Tenth Doctor.


02:50:55-02:50:58
The machinations of time.


02:50:58-02:51:03
Time folds and unfolds itself all the time in my world.


02:51:03-02:51:11
Sometimes it feels as if it's folding in on me, crushing me with the weight of all the things that need to be done now, tomorrow, last year, and five minutes ago.


02:51:11-02:51:20
Inevitably, whatever amount of time I give myself to do a task, travel to a place, or get ready to meet a friend is not the right amount of time.


02:51:20-02:51:29
The task takes longer than predicted or I forget to account for traffic, or I hop out of my five minute shower to find that a half hour has passed.


02:51:29-02:51:29
How?


02:51:29-02:51:41
Whereas focus feels like something solid, something that is either there or isn't, and it is very clear when it isn't, time is not a shape shifter exactly, but a state shifter.


02:51:41-02:51:48
Right before a deadline, or when I'm booked back to back, I can feel the cold, crushing press of unyielding time.


02:51:48-02:51:54
When I'm immersed in a hobby or daydreaming, it passes by in an unnoticed cloud.


02:51:54-02:51:59
When I'm bored or anxiously waiting for something, or someone, it's a tedious, maddening drip.


02:51:59-02:52:07
As a kid, homework assignments that were supposed to take 15 minutes often took an hour, which often felt like five.


02:52:08-02:52:12
Middle school me, this is so long and forever taking.


02:52:12-02:52:16
The clock, tick, infinite silence, talk.


02:52:16-02:52:20
Hyperfocus, on the other hand, was like a time slip and slide.


02:52:20-02:52:25
I'd set out to spend half an hour on a school project only to look up and see it was already dark outside.


02:52:25-02:52:35
Middle school me, absorbed, let's see, if I glue the popsicle sticks like this, I bet they could support the structure here.


02:52:35-02:52:41
The clock, hands whirling cartoonishly behind me, tick, tock, tick, tock, tick, tock, tick, tock, tick.


02:52:41-02:52:46
By the time I was a teenager, it was clear that I was terrible at managing my time.


02:52:46-02:52:50
This did not stop people from expecting me to do so.


02:52:50-02:52:51
Don't forget to do this next week.


02:52:51-02:52:54
Remember, this is due in a month, plan accordingly.


02:52:54-02:52:57
Can you start dinner in 15 minutes?


02:52:57-02:52:59
I'd nod and pretend I could.


02:52:59-02:53:09
As I got older and became more responsible for managing my time, minutes late turned into weeks, months, or even years late, and the effects on my life were profound.


02:53:09-02:53:15
I missed deadlines for college applications, parking tickets, credit card bills, and taxes.


02:53:15-02:53:18
I was fired for being perpetually late.


02:53:18-02:53:19
I was constantly playing catch-up.


02:53:19-02:53:21
I was always running behind.


02:53:21-02:53:27
I tried to plan my time better, but for most of my life, my relationship with schedules has looked like this.


02:53:27-02:53:30
One, set up a schedule.


02:53:30-02:53:34
Two, fail to follow the schedule.


02:53:34-02:53:36
Three, repeat steps one and two.


02:53:36-02:53:45
For years, I was able to neatly sidestep my time management issues by having a job that didn't require it, waiting tables.


02:53:45-02:53:49
Yes, time was critical, but everything I needed to do had to happen now.


02:53:49-02:53:53
Besides getting to work, I didn't need to be anywhere at a specific time.


02:53:53-02:53:57
I didn't have to plan how long anything was going to take.


02:53:57-02:54:04
I just had to complete my tasks as quickly as possible and in the right order, offer drinks, take the order, put it in the computer.


02:54:04-02:54:16
For anything that needed to be done outside of work, I did it when I remembered to, or when I felt like it, or when I was close enough to a deadline that my brain kicked into gear.


02:54:16-02:54:21
Planning and replanning took so much time, it felt as if I were wasting it.


02:54:21-02:54:27
Forget about getting things done at a certain time, I'd decide I just need to get stuff done.


02:54:27-02:54:37
So when I had a lot to do, I would do everything I remembered to do as I remembered to do it, which sometimes meant all at once, until I lost consciousness.


02:54:37-02:54:38
Then I would wake up and do it again.


02:54:38-02:54:45
This was my life in college, and it became my life again when I was working on the channel and waiting tables.


02:54:45-02:54:52
As the channel grew, it presented a whole new smorgasbord of responsibilities, deadlines, and expectations.


02:54:52-02:55:02
I was already researching, writing, shooting, editing, and responding to comments seven days a week on top of my actual job, because again, what is time?


02:55:02-02:55:05
And now there were exponentially more comments.


02:55:05-02:55:14
I was reading dense research papers on PubMed, and I had perks to deliver on Patreon, podcast interviews to prep for, and conferences to attend.


02:55:14-02:55:17
Before, I'd been doing more and more to make up for past failures.


02:55:17-02:55:21
Now, I was doing more and more because of my success.


02:55:21-02:55:23
It was becoming harder and harder to keep up.


02:55:23-02:55:25
It was exhausting.


02:55:25-02:55:34
There were times it was clear, often when I was shooting an episode at 2 a.m., that this schedule, or lack thereof, wasn't working for me.


02:55:34-02:55:36
I really didn't know what else to do, though.


02:55:36-02:55:38
I felt ashamed.


02:55:38-02:55:45
Time management was such a basic requirement for every pursuit in life, and everyone else seemed to have it on lock.


02:55:45-02:55:49
I felt ridiculous for being a grown-ass adult who still hadn't figured it out.


02:55:49-02:55:52
What I learned.


02:55:52-02:56:00
I thought how I experienced time was how everyone experienced time, only other people were better at wrestling it into submission.


02:56:00-02:56:02
Nope.


02:56:02-02:56:12
Turns out, those with ADHD are at a serious disadvantage when it comes to time management, for reasons ranging from how we experience it to the fact that we forget things so often.


02:56:12-02:56:22
According to Dr. Barkley, 8% of the general population has difficulties with time management, and those with ADHD, 98%.


02:56:22-02:56:25
I'm gonna say that again so you know it wasn't a mistake.


02:56:25-02:56:32
98% of people with ADHD self-reported time management difficulties, and their partners agreed.


02:56:32-02:56:38
98% of people don't self-report liking ice cream.


02:56:38-02:56:43
According to new data from YouGov Omnibus, 96% of Americans eat ice cream.


02:56:43-02:56:52
The most popular flavor is chocolate, 14%, followed by vanilla, 13%, and butter pecan, 11%.


02:56:52-02:57:00
Just know, if you related to what I've shared here, you can stop kicking yourself now, although you should probably check what time it is in case you're running late.


02:57:00-02:57:03
We experience time differently.


02:57:03-02:57:14
Dr. Barkley explains in lectures, books, and research papers that those with ADHD are actually time-blind, or more accurately, time-nearsighted.


02:57:14-02:57:18
A significant body of research and lived experience from the ADHD community backs this up.


02:57:18-02:57:30
Time-blindness/nearsightedness, noun, inability, or exceptional difficulty, recognizing how much time has passed, and/or estimating how long something will take.


02:57:30-02:57:34
We have differences in temporal processing.


02:57:34-02:57:38
Some people have a sharp sense of time.


02:57:38-02:57:43
They know exactly how long they've been chatting with a friend, or when it's time to turn off the bath.


02:57:43-02:57:47
They can sense how much time has passed relatively accurately.


02:57:47-02:57:55
This may be due to their circadian rhythms, or internal clocks, combined with environmental cues such as light, sound, and temperature changes.


02:57:55-02:57:59
I didn't know not everyone lacks this sense because my family doesn't have it.


02:57:59-02:58:05
I would be at my aunt's house editing this right now, but her house isn't guest-friendly at the moment.


02:58:05-02:58:08
She flooded her main bathroom, and bedroom.


02:58:08-02:58:12
Those with ADHD tend to have a soft sense of time.


02:58:12-02:58:20
Unless we're intentionally tracking it, we can't tell how much time has passed, or sense time passing the way others can.


02:58:20-02:58:28
Even without slipping into hyperfocus, we may not realize it's time to move on to something else without the help of alarms and reminders.


02:58:28-02:58:31
And in hyperfocus, we may completely miss them.


02:58:31-02:58:36
My fellow YouTuber and friend, Jesse J. Anderson, describes it this way.


02:58:36-02:58:39
It's like a sense I don't have.


02:58:39-02:58:44
If I couldn't feel pain, I would use clues to guess how much pain I should be in.


02:58:44-02:58:46
My skin is red from impact.


02:58:46-02:58:47
The pot is boiling.


02:58:47-02:58:49
Oh, I'm bleeding.


02:58:49-02:58:51
But I'm still just guessing.


02:58:51-02:58:53
It's the same with time.


02:58:53-02:58:56
I can use clues, but never sense it in myself.


02:58:56-02:58:59
We have shorter time horizons.


02:58:59-02:59:07
Time horizons, the point at which events are close enough to begin to feel real, tend to be much shorter for those with ADHD.


02:59:07-02:59:16
For many of us, projects, tasks, and events exist either now or not now, and anything not now can feel as if it doesn't exist at all.


02:59:16-02:59:34
We often live in the moment, responding only to what's in front of our face and needs to be done now, which in the words of Eddie, one of our producers, is based on whichever client is screaming the loudest and have trouble planning for events later than the next day, unless they're particularly exciting.


02:59:34-02:59:37
This makes it difficult for us to learn from our mistakes.


02:59:37-02:59:40
We've already forgotten them, or plan for the future.


02:59:40-02:59:42
It doesn't feel real.


02:59:42-02:59:47
Our shorter time horizons are one reason why we might start studying for a test the night before.


02:59:47-02:59:53
The point at which things land on our mental plate is typically much closer to the deadline for us.


02:59:53-03:00:07
We might know intellectually that we have something coming up, but unlike our neurotypical peers, we haven't had it land on our I should get started on this radar yet, and the switch won't be flipped from not now to now until it does.


03:00:07-03:00:11
Time flies, or drags, more for us.


03:00:11-03:00:14
Time flies when you're having fun.


03:00:14-03:00:21
While this is true for everyone to some extent, those with ADHD experience a more extreme version of this.


03:00:21-03:00:29
Boring tasks can seem to stretch on forever, while activities that are engaging to our brains can easily make us lose track of hours or even days.


03:00:29-03:00:39
When estimating how long things take, we often underestimate the time needed for tasks we're looking forward to, and overestimate the time needed for tasks we're dreading.


03:00:39-03:00:45
I still forget sometimes that just because something sounds fun to me doesn't mean it won't take a significant amount of time to do it.


03:00:45-03:00:48
We forget to account for more things.


03:00:48-03:00:55
An ADHD coach once pointed out that play has three parts, setup, the play itself, and cleanup.


03:00:55-03:01:01
The same is true for pretty much everything we do, from making dinner to attending a meeting, right?


03:01:01-03:01:13
I didn't know that either, but yeah, we have to get there, get our materials or notes ready, attend the meeting, then capture the follow-up tasks, update people on information that we learned, and process what we learned.


03:01:13-03:01:21
But those of us with ADHD often forget about the setup and cleanup part and only account for the time we think it'll take us to do the thing.


03:01:21-03:01:29
When it comes to driving somewhere, for example, we might leave ourselves 15 minutes because my map app says it'll take 15 minutes to get there.


03:01:29-03:01:34
And don't account for the time it takes to get to the car, get gas if necessary, and find parking.


03:01:34-03:01:42
Because we tend to forget that we need to plan, prep, put things back, and follow up, we're often already running late before we even get started.


03:01:43-03:01:45
Other factors we often forget to account for?


03:01:45-03:01:48
Things going wrong.


03:01:48-03:01:52
We often plan for the version of events where everything goes perfectly.


03:01:52-03:01:54
Unfortunately, things don't always go perfectly.


03:01:54-03:01:57
Biological needs.


03:01:57-03:02:03
As humans, we need to eat, drink water, move, use the restroom, and sleep.


03:02:03-03:02:08
When predicting how long something will take us, we often don't build in time for bathroom breaks or rest.


03:02:08-03:02:10
Transition time.


03:02:10-03:02:12
If one activity ends at 1 p.m.


03:02:12-03:02:18
and the next starts at the same time, you're probably gonna be late, even if it's just a video meeting.


03:02:18-03:02:23
Transitions take time, and our brains need time to switch between two things, too.


03:02:23-03:02:24
Contingencies.


03:02:24-03:02:28
Sometimes we can't do something before we've done something else first.


03:02:28-03:02:34
For instance, it's hard to wrap presents if they haven't arrived yet, or if we don't have wrapping paper.


03:02:34-03:02:36
Our energy levels.


03:02:36-03:02:40
A lot of time management is actually energy management.


03:02:40-03:02:44
We might technically have the time for something, but not the energy to actually do it.


03:02:44-03:02:46
Ideation time.


03:02:46-03:02:49
If the work we're doing is creative, we can't just create.


03:02:49-03:02:56
We also need time to consume content that can spark new ideas, brainstorm, and let ideas percolate.


03:02:56-03:02:58
Mistakes.


03:02:58-03:03:01
Mistakes are doubly problematic to forget to account for.


03:03:01-03:03:04
We're not just more likely to make them and possibly need to fix them.


03:03:04-03:03:08
We're also more likely to need extra time to double or triple check our work.


03:03:09-03:03:13
We plan for a version of ourselves that doesn't have ADHD.


03:03:13-03:03:22
While time nearsightedness is independent of executive function challenges, these EF challenges can play an enormous role in our difficulties with time management.


03:03:22-03:03:30
Working memory impairments make it more likely we'll forget what we're doing or have to run back for something we left behind.


03:03:30-03:03:38
Our need to self-accommodate these impairments makes certain tasks, especially admin ones, take longer than they otherwise would.


03:03:38-03:03:48
Organizational challenges mean we're more likely to misplace what we need, have trouble finding it, or struggle to put it back where it needs to be, if we even know where that is.


03:03:48-03:03:51
We often have trouble organizing our thoughts and our speech, too.


03:03:51-03:04:03
As divergent thinkers, we might be wonderfully imaginative writers, but narrowing down what we want to say, which relies on convergent thinking, and wrestling the brain chaos into some sort of structure, is hard.


03:04:03-03:04:10
I've personally rewritten entire keynote speeches because it was faster than looking for and adapting the one I'd already written.


03:04:10-03:04:16
Difficulty regulating our attention means there are vast differences in our productivity levels.


03:04:16-03:04:20
Some days, we might end up in hyper-focus and get a lot done.


03:04:20-03:04:24
Other days, we'll be distracted by everything and we won't accomplish much at all.


03:04:24-03:04:33
Deficits in response inhibition make it more likely we'll impulsively start or have trouble stopping a task we didn't even intend to do.


03:04:33-03:04:35
Those with ADHD can get a lot done.


03:04:35-03:04:38
It's just often not what we set out to do.


03:04:38-03:04:47
Unfortunately, we often plan our time, or are expected to plan our time, as if these differences don't exist.


03:04:47-03:04:57
We expect ourselves to be able to get things done in the same timeframe and with the same consistency as someone without ADHD, despite all evidence to the contrary.


03:04:57-03:05:10
We expect ourselves to do things when we planned to do them, so rather than giving ourselves the flexibility of hopping around, we drag our feet starting on or full on avoid the task we were supposed to do.


03:05:10-03:05:15
The result, work often spills over into nights and weekends, or we fall behind.


03:05:15-03:05:22
We cancel plans with friends, eat into sleep, and don't have time to move, relax, or have fun.


03:05:22-03:05:31
We spend excessive amounts of time on things that others ask of us at the expense of things we need to feel fulfilled, or even to be okay.


03:05:31-03:05:35
We burn out, or we give up on trying to manage our time at all.


03:05:35-03:05:39
Nathan F. 44, Australia.


03:05:39-03:05:42
I am both 15 and 44.


03:05:42-03:05:45
Everything in the last 30 years happened in the last month.


03:05:45-03:05:50
The future is all happening both tomorrow and at some point I don't need to think about yet.


03:05:50-03:05:52
I have no constructive way of coping with this.


03:05:52-03:05:56
Dan M. 40, Ireland.


03:05:56-03:05:59
For me, there are only three different time experiences.


03:05:59-03:06:01
One, now versus not now.


03:06:01-03:06:04
Two, why is everything taking so long?


03:06:04-03:06:07
Three, wait, how is it X time already?


03:06:07-03:06:11
Justin D. 29, Kentucky.


03:06:11-03:06:13
Time stretches and compresses.


03:06:13-03:06:16
It changes day to day and has since I was a kid.


03:06:16-03:06:21
I would always check the time a lot and had multiple clocks around my room and a watch.


03:06:21-03:06:24
Regularly checking the time was just how I dealt with it.


03:06:24-03:06:31
Knowing the time was such a habit that the first thing that I asked after waking up from my wisdom teeth removal was the current time.


03:06:31-03:06:37
I couldn't open my eyes or speak, so I had to gesture that I wanted something to write with and wrote the question down.


03:06:37-03:06:41
Angie T. 42, Missouri.


03:06:41-03:06:47
My unique ADHD experience is being time nearsighted while also having precise chronoception, sense of time.


03:06:47-03:06:55
For example, I'm really good at knowing how much time has passed, but my sense of estimating the reasonable time to complete a task is flawed.


03:06:55-03:06:58
So I'm still late basically all the time.


03:06:58-03:07:03
To correct it, I have to grossly overestimate and run the risk of being bored when I'm early.


03:07:03-03:07:05
The toolbox.


03:07:05-03:07:13
A lot of generic time management strategies do not work for those with ADHD because they don't account for our specific challenges.


03:07:13-03:07:16
Here, I've compiled targeted strategies that do.


03:07:16-03:07:19
Does that mean they'll make us good at time management?


03:07:19-03:07:22
Not necessarily, and definitely not at first.


03:07:22-03:07:28
The time-related challenges those with ADHD face aren't magically fixable by using a calendar.


03:07:28-03:07:38
Trying to stick to a schedule we made with the same brain that has no concept of how long things take is an exercise in immediate failure, as many of us have long ago discovered.


03:07:38-03:07:45
Interestingly, we're usually better at making a schedule for someone else, maybe because it taps into cool executive function.


03:07:45-03:07:51
We're not being influenced by how fun or horrible the task sounds because we aren't the ones who have to do it.


03:07:51-03:08:03
But over time, we can begin to gain more control over our days, weeks, months, and years, so we can choose how we want to spend our time rather than time choosing how it spends us.


03:08:03-03:08:06
One, build up your time wisdom.


03:08:06-03:08:10
Time wisdom is understanding how long things take.


03:08:10-03:08:17
Read how long they take you under specific circumstances and knowing what to do with that information.


03:08:17-03:08:22
Building your time wisdom can help compensate for a softer, innate sense of how much has passed.


03:08:22-03:08:25
When you plan, work backward.


03:08:25-03:08:30
Start with what you want to accomplish and by when, and go backward from there.


03:08:30-03:08:35
What step needs to happen right before you do the thing and how long do you think it'll take?


03:08:35-03:08:37
What's the step before that?


03:08:37-03:08:43
This one worked fairly well for me, even before I learned how to manage my time effectively as someone with ADHD.


03:08:43-03:08:55
If I knew I needed to post a video for my channel on Tuesday, this meant that I needed to edit the video on Monday, which meant I needed to shoot it on Sunday, which meant I needed to have a script written by then.


03:08:55-03:09:03
When deadlines are so far in the future that they don't feel real, this strategy can help us see how our efforts now affect an outcome later.


03:09:03-03:09:11
It also makes it easier to see all the steps involved in getting something done, which also gives us a better gauge of how long everything will take.


03:09:11-03:09:12
Track your time.


03:09:12-03:09:17
Guess how long a task will take and then track how long you actually spend doing it.


03:09:17-03:09:26
Those of us with ADHD tend to be time optimists and we often discover that certain tasks tend to take twice or even three times longer than expected.


03:09:26-03:09:35
Knowing how long you typically need for those activities will help you schedule the right amount of time for them in the future, making it much less frustrating to try to stick to a schedule.


03:09:35-03:09:38
Notice where you're stealing time.


03:09:38-03:09:41
Making up for lost time is not a thing.


03:09:41-03:09:43
It's gone forever.


03:09:43-03:09:49
The next time you're trying to play catch up, notice if you're stealing time from another area of your life to do it.


03:09:49-03:09:55
Then consider whether it's worth taking time away from that area or whether you wanna make a new plan.


03:09:55-03:10:00
This way you don't accidentally neglect something else that matters to you and end up having to play catch up there too.


03:10:00-03:10:04
Taryn G, 29, Illinois.


03:10:04-03:10:08
I use my smartwatch to remind me to stand every hour.


03:10:08-03:10:11
This helps me realize when I've done something or nothing for an hour.


03:10:11-03:10:15
Laura P, 41, New York.


03:10:15-03:10:20
One of my more expensive solutions for sensing time is my under the desk treadmill.


03:10:20-03:10:25
It's easier for me to feel time passing when I combine my office work with physical movement.


03:10:25-03:10:29
Two, make time real.


03:10:29-03:10:35
If you don't have a sharp sense of time, it's important to put systems in place that help you understand when it's passing.


03:10:35-03:10:40
This can make time feel less fuzzy and abstract and more visible and concrete.


03:10:40-03:10:46
Clocks are an obvious and often recommended example of this, but you can also try the following.


03:10:46-03:10:49
Get specific.


03:10:49-03:10:54
If you decide to do something later, decide when later is and add it to your calendar.


03:10:54-03:11:00
You can schedule later to happen at a specific time or tie it to another activity you typically perform.


03:11:00-03:11:03
For example, Saturday at 10 a.m.


03:11:03-03:11:06
or tomorrow night after dinner before you sit down to watch TV.


03:11:06-03:11:09
The point is to make later exist.


03:11:09-03:11:12
Use time pillars to prop up your day.


03:11:12-03:11:18
Time can quickly become meaningless if you don't have anything to do or anywhere to be at a certain time.


03:11:18-03:11:26
Time pillars are regular time-based events and rituals that add structure to your day and make it easier to use your time effectively.


03:11:26-03:11:30
A lot of people think they'll be way more productive when they quit their day jobs.


03:11:30-03:11:32
The opposite is often true.


03:11:32-03:11:38
Without regular time-based events to plan around, there's less pressure to get anything done at a certain time.


03:11:38-03:11:48
Even if you don't like planning out your day, a few recurring activities like lunchtime, winding down for bed, or even just a few alarms can give your day structure.


03:11:48-03:11:55
At the vacation rental where I was writing this book, there was a "treat yo self" sign that lit up automatically at 5 p.m.


03:11:55-03:11:59
We decided this would be the pillar that told us when to stop working and start winding down.


03:11:59-03:12:01
Create some time buckets.


03:12:01-03:12:11
Time buckets are areas of your day, week, or month dedicated to certain types of activities such as hobbies, admin stuff, or deep work.


03:12:11-03:12:19
Time buckets can help us reserve space and time for the things we care about while giving us the flexibility within that time to do what we feel like doing.


03:12:19-03:12:25
If hobby night happens on Tuesdays, you don't need to know which hobby you'll feel like doing beforehand.


03:12:25-03:12:31
But because you've set aside this time, it will feel real and make it less likely for you to plan to do something else.


03:12:31-03:12:35
Ryan S. 47, USA.


03:12:35-03:12:38
I have to use an analog watch with a face.


03:12:38-03:12:42
12 o'clock to 12.15 on a digital clock means nothing to me.


03:12:42-03:12:47
But the minute hand moving one quarter of the face of the watch, I understand that.


03:12:47-03:12:49
Matt G. 45, Ohio.


03:12:49-03:12:57
My calendar has blocks of time scheduled to work on specific tasks as well as scheduled time to take breaks, to eat lunch, and to take a walk.


03:12:57-03:13:04
I find a lot of comfort in structure, so I create simple structures in my life that I can manage to maintain in order to reduce any anxiety.


03:13:04-03:13:07
Three, communicate about time.


03:13:07-03:13:16
While it's not great to be late, what often upsets people most about tardiness is that their time has been wasted and you don't care.


03:13:16-03:13:26
As terrifying as it can be to admit you're running behind or having trouble managing your time, communicating your difficulties with others can help them understand that it's really not about them.


03:13:26-03:13:34
It might even give the people around you opportunities to offer support, making it less likely that anyone's time will be wasted, yours or theirs.


03:13:34-03:13:40
A natural place to start practicing this type of communicating is to let people know when you're running late.


03:13:40-03:13:43
But there are a few other scenarios where this can be very helpful.


03:13:43-03:13:46
Ask for help prioritizing.


03:13:46-03:13:53
If you realize there isn't time to do all of the things, you don't have to figure out what's important and what's not on your own.


03:13:53-03:13:58
Getting input from someone else is a win-win, especially at work or with your relationships.


03:13:58-03:14:05
You don't have to stress yourself out by trying to get it all done, and they can ask you to prioritize the tasks most important to them.


03:14:05-03:14:09
Share your plan with others and get a reality check.


03:14:09-03:14:17
If you share your plan with someone who doesn't have challenges with time management, they might be able to recognize immediately, yeah, that's not how time works.


03:14:17-03:14:24
Even sharing it with someone with similar challenges can help because they can offer a perspective that is more objective and logical.


03:14:24-03:14:28
Before committing to something, ask what it entails.


03:14:28-03:14:33
This can help you understand all the steps involved in a task and what exactly is expected of you.


03:14:33-03:14:39
You might realize that a quick favor will actually take you 14 to 38 hours.


03:14:40-03:14:43
Ask for time-related accommodations if needed.


03:14:43-03:14:55
A lot of ADHD accommodations, such as more frequent check-ins, a later or more flexible start time, or extra time or time away on a test or project, are meant to accommodate in part our time nearsightedness.


03:14:55-03:14:55
Use them.


03:14:55-03:15:00
Henrika H. 42, Finland.


03:15:00-03:15:14
When working on my thesis, I asked my supervisor to give me hard deadlines, so instead of just meeting him regularly to show what I had, he gave me specific goals, like you need to show me a full outline by this date, have this chapter finished, et cetera.


03:15:14-03:15:20
I didn't even have my diagnosis then, just knew by then very well that deadlines set by someone else were a must.


03:15:20-03:15:24
Lisa G. 48, Australia.


03:15:24-03:15:34
My boss knows about my issues with hyperfocus and my lack of time perception, and I've asked for and received permission to use timers and alarms in a small office setting.


03:15:34-03:15:42
I use alarms to remind me to take lunch break, medication, to remember when to ring home to remind my kids of things they have to do and when it's time to pack up to go home.


03:15:42-03:15:48
Visual timers that count down with a disappearing wedge of color are more useful for shorter deadlines.


03:15:48-03:15:51
Tony S. 53, Australia.


03:15:51-03:15:54
My wife knows to put everything in the shared calendar.


03:15:54-03:15:58
If it's not there, I don't know about it, no matter how often she mentions it.


03:15:58-03:16:01
A note on extra time.


03:16:01-03:16:11
When planning workflows, ADHDers are often advised to estimate how long we think a task will take and then double or even triple it to make sure we give ourselves enough time to do it.


03:16:11-03:16:14
This can backfire sometimes.


03:16:14-03:16:20
Having more time to do something might mean that we make less progress than we otherwise would have, because now we have time.


03:16:20-03:16:31
Having extra time doesn't make us any better at managing it, and oftentimes it can take away the sense of urgency we need to get started and stay focused.


03:16:31-03:16:37
To find the right balance, it helps to play around with the amount of time that you dedicate to a task.


03:16:37-03:16:41
You can also try these alternatives to simply giving yourself extra time.


03:16:41-03:16:43
Create buffer time.


03:16:43-03:16:50
Only give yourself the amount of time you think you need while making sure the next activity is something you can cut into if needed.


03:16:50-03:16:54
Bonus points if it's something you don't want to infringe on, like video game time.


03:16:54-03:16:56
Schedule it twice.


03:16:56-03:17:03
Only give yourself the amount of time you think you need, but schedule a safety block for the same amount of time on another day in case you run into issues.


03:17:04-03:17:06
Take time away.


03:17:06-03:17:10
Only give yourself the amount of time you think you need to work, but set a timer.


03:17:10-03:17:19
Allow yourself to hit pause on the timer to that time, and step away to stretch, do some pushups, or think, and resume the timer again when you're ready to come back to the task.


03:17:19-03:17:33
This gives you the urgency of having a limited amount of time while also offering a chance to reset your focus, unstick from a problem, or get a quick dopamine boost to help push through without eating into the time you've allotted.


03:17:33-03:17:45
Dr. Barkley argues that taking time away from a task for those with ADHD is a better way of accommodating time management issues on assignments because extra time alone is meaningless for those with no sense of time.


03:17:45-03:17:49
Four, have times when time doesn't matter.


03:17:49-03:17:57
Worrying about time and doing things on a schedule is unnatural and stressful for a lot of ADHD brains.


03:17:57-03:18:04
While time management can be necessary, especially when you are, you know, living in a society, it's not always essential.


03:18:04-03:18:10
Having time to wander can be wonderful and highly restorative for our brains.


03:18:10-03:18:14
It gives us a chance to focus on what we're doing, not on the clock.


03:18:14-03:18:17
This can help us work or play at a deeper level.


03:18:17-03:18:31
In fact, losing track of time helps us enter flow states, the experience of being so immersed and absorbed by an engaging activity that we can effortlessly focus on it, also known as being in the zone.


03:18:31-03:18:34
If you know there's somewhere you need to be, you can't let yourself go too deep.


03:18:34-03:18:40
For those who can't control how deep they go, that often means not doing anything at all.


03:18:40-03:18:46
