I hear lots of stories from brains who are afraid to start any task because they have to go to the dentist in five hours.


03:18:46-03:18:51
There are a few strategies you can use to let your brain off leash without running into traffic.


03:18:51-03:18:55
Sequence instead of schedule.


03:18:55-03:19:00
Doing things in a particular order is far easier on our brains than doing them at a particular time.


03:19:00-03:19:04
This is why checklists are popular in our community.


03:19:04-03:19:09
We can get from A to Z in a way that's a lot less stressful than doing C, E, and F at a certain time.


03:19:09-03:19:12
Keep some days meeting free.


03:19:12-03:19:22
A lot of ADHD brains benefit from keeping at least one work day free of scheduled meetings and appointments so that they can find their flow without interruptions.


03:19:22-03:19:25
At the very least, set aside some longer time blocks for deep work.


03:19:25-03:19:28
Set aside flexible days.


03:19:28-03:19:33
If you can, decide on a couple of days when it doesn't matter if you start working or come home late.


03:19:33-03:19:41
This gives you built-in buffer time to catch up on stuff you missed, which can make it easier to stick to your schedule the rest of the week.


03:19:41-03:19:43
Take a time vacation.


03:19:43-03:19:50
Sometimes it's good to commit to a day where nothing needs to get done at any particular time or even in any particular order.


03:19:50-03:19:51
Brains need rest.


03:19:51-03:19:55
Lindell C, 30, Canada.


03:19:55-03:20:01
I learned that ancient Greek has two different words for time, kairos and kronos.


03:20:01-03:20:09
Kronos is where we get the word chronological, and it refers to specific amounts of time, like hours, days, months.


03:20:09-03:20:14
Kairos is about an opportune time, like seasons, timing, and moments.


03:20:14-03:20:20
An example of kronos time is, I go grocery shopping on Monday afternoon.


03:20:20-03:20:24
An example of kairos time is, I go grocery shopping when I run out of bananas.


03:20:24-03:20:32
Both happen regularly, but one is about an arbitrary date and time, and the other is about the conditions being right.


03:20:32-03:20:35
As ADHDers, we struggle with kronos time.


03:20:35-03:20:43
However, not all cultures actually use or care about precise kronos time, and many operate more off kairos-style time.


03:20:43-03:20:48
Knowing this really helped me lessen the guilt and anxiety around time management.


03:20:48-03:20:50
If I'm on my own, I don't worry about kronos time.


03:20:50-03:20:53
I live my life in kairos time mostly.


03:20:53-03:20:58
That gives me more energy for time management when I need to be in line with the broader North American culture of kronos time.


03:20:58-03:21:02
Kirsten C., 49, Michigan.


03:21:02-03:21:07
As a teacher, I reserved one day a week where I told my family not to expect me home.


03:21:07-03:21:12
Instead, I stayed at school until all the planning, paperwork, and other nagging tasks were done.


03:21:12-03:21:18
Whether I was done at five or worked until security kicked me out, I always knew I had that time available.


03:21:18-03:21:21
Kurt W., 47, Canada.


03:21:21-03:21:25
For the past three years, I've actually been leaning into the fuzziness of time.


03:21:25-03:21:29
Things are easier if I don't know how long it's been or I don't feel the passage of time.


03:21:29-03:21:38
Schedule Jenga, a year or so into my channel, my wake to crash schedule had me a little wound up.


03:21:38-03:21:43
I felt like a feral cat, wild-eyed and clinging onto the ceiling by my claws.


03:21:43-03:21:52
What was meant to be an interview with ADHD coach Eric Tivers became more of an intervention with him coaxing me down with tasty nuggets of wisdom and reassuring pets.


03:21:52-03:21:57
"What comes to mind when you think of a schedule?" he asked.


03:21:57-03:21:59
I don't know, a prison?


03:21:59-03:22:05
The only times I'd ever been able to stick to a schedule were when I was rigid about following it.


03:22:05-03:22:09
No matter what else was happening in my life, I would grit my teeth and obey the schedule.


03:22:09-03:22:12
Life, there is no life, only schedule.


03:22:12-03:22:18
It didn't make me super fun to be around because I was constantly anxious about making it to my next appointment.


03:22:18-03:22:20
It wasn't super fun for me either.


03:22:21-03:22:25
He explained that schedules weren't supposed to be rigid.


03:22:25-03:22:26
A schedule should work for you.


03:22:26-03:22:29
You shouldn't be working for the schedule.


03:22:29-03:22:36
As for my failures, turns out it's really hard to stick to a schedule when it's based on completely unrealistic time estimates.


03:22:36-03:22:38
No wonder I was anxious.


03:22:38-03:22:43
Because of that conversation, I braved Google Calendar again.


03:22:43-03:22:49
I learned to time block, put blocks in my schedule for my tasks and projects and not worry about sticking to them perfectly.


03:22:49-03:22:51
Time blocks weren't marching orders.


03:22:51-03:22:54
They only existed to reserve time for that activity.


03:22:54-03:22:56
They could be moved.


03:22:56-03:23:04
I timed my regular tasks and I was shocked to discover how long things took me had not much to do with how long it seemed like they should.


03:23:04-03:23:11
The strategies I learned from him, from others, and on my own, all of which I just shared in the toolbox, worked.


03:23:11-03:23:18
I was no longer shooting episodes at 2 a.m. most of the time and I was able to take Sundays off and still get everything done.


03:23:18-03:23:23
I optimized my time to the point where my entire week was scheduled down to the hour.


03:23:23-03:23:30
Knowing that work-life balance is important, I ended my work days at 6 p.m. so I could walk my dog and spend time with my boyfriend.


03:23:30-03:23:32
But there was always more to do.


03:23:32-03:23:35
If an extra task popped up, it spilled over into the weekend.


03:23:35-03:23:37
Where else would I put it?


03:23:37-03:23:47
Because extra tasks popped up all the time, I delegated anything that took time but wasn't productive, like engaging with my community, brainstorming new ideas, and chatting with my team.


03:23:48-03:23:54
I kept optimizing and soon my calendar turned into the rigid prison I had been so afraid it would be.


03:23:54-03:23:58
I didn't feel there was space for me in all these blocks and buckets.


03:23:58-03:24:07
If I missed one time block, it would mess up my entire schedule for the next three months, which meant I had to block some time to redo the schedule, which would bump more blocks out of place.


03:24:07-03:24:10
I started rebelling.


03:24:10-03:24:13
I worked on project C when I was supposed to be working on task A.


03:24:13-03:24:18
Then I would wake up at 4 a.m. to work on thing Q and fully sleep through Endeavor X.


03:24:18-03:24:22
I ignored some blocks, shifted others, yanked a few.


03:24:22-03:24:27
I was playing schedule Jenga and the tower was getting really wobbly.


03:24:27-03:24:32
Chaos crept back in, followed by a familiar friend, anxiety-fueled depression.


03:24:32-03:24:35
Who cared if I missed a day now, it would all fall apart anyway.


03:24:35-03:24:37
I started hiding from my calendar.


03:24:37-03:24:44
In that fog, I realized time management isn't some magical solution that lets us do all of the things.


03:24:44-03:24:57
In fact, overscheduling myself had cost me things I cared most about, like visiting the dog park, allowing my brain and body to wander, losing myself in video games, or going down random Wikipedia rabbit holes.


03:24:57-03:25:03
There's a limit to how much we can optimize every minute, every hour, every day, and still be okay.


03:25:03-03:25:05
We are humans, not computers.


03:25:05-03:25:07
We aren't supposed to live that way.


03:25:07-03:25:14
Since then, I've recognized that time management, like many things in life, is a balancing act.


03:25:14-03:25:19
You wanna manage your time well enough to be productive without feeling that your time is about producing rather than living.


03:25:19-03:25:25
One off day shouldn't ruin your week, let alone throw off the next three months.


03:25:25-03:25:30
Time management shouldn't take the spontaneity out of your life or the joy out of your work.


03:25:30-03:25:35
The truth is, if you have to optimize to that extent, you have too much on your plate.


03:25:35-03:25:39
You're going to burn out, or, like me, rebel.


03:25:39-03:25:48
Even neurotypical people leave space in their day and give themselves buffer time to breathe or pee or to work on tasks that take longer than expected.


03:25:48-03:25:51
And because I have ADHD, I need a little more of that space.


03:25:51-03:25:56
I need time to wander, time when time doesn't matter.


03:25:56-03:26:00
If I don't give that to myself, I have learned, my brain will take it anyway.

### Chapter 7: How to Motivate Your Brain [03:30:00]
03:26:00-03:26:08
Chapter seven, how to [[motivation|motivate]] your brain.


03:26:08-03:26:13
If we have our own why of life, we shall get along with almost any how.


03:26:13-03:26:14
Friedrich Nietzsche.


03:26:14-03:26:17
I do what I feel like.


03:26:17-03:26:18
Bart Simpson.


03:26:18-03:26:21
Me versus brain.


03:26:21-03:26:29
Most of my life has been a battle between me, who knows this thing is important, and my brain, which doesn't want to do it.


03:26:29-03:26:33
Me, I'm going to do the thing.


03:26:33-03:26:35
My brain, no.


03:26:35-03:26:39
Me, but I really need to get it done to stay on track with this important life goal.


03:26:39-03:26:41
My brain, what life goals?


03:26:41-03:26:45
Wipes life goals from hard drive, overwrites with cat videos.


03:26:45-03:26:51
On the flip side, I've often found myself randomly motivated to do things I don't even care about.


03:26:51-03:26:56
Me, watches a video showing you how to make a cake out of melted ice cream.


03:26:56-03:26:59
My brain, yes, that's amazing, let's do it right now.


03:26:59-03:27:04
Me, right, so I guess we aren't answering emails today.


03:27:04-03:27:08
Grabs pint of Cherry Garcia, leaves laptop in freezer.


03:27:09-03:27:19
I usually understand why I should do or avoid doing a thing, but that understanding in and of itself doesn't translate into usable motivational fuel.


03:27:19-03:27:26
It doesn't matter if the task is critical to something I care deeply about or entirely irrelevant and a giant waste of my time.


03:27:26-03:27:29
What matters is whether or not my brain feels like doing it.


03:27:29-03:27:34
Sometimes I really can't do something unless my brain cooperates.


03:27:34-03:27:36
It feels like a hostage negotiation.


03:27:36-03:27:42
Sometimes I can push through a task with my brain kicking and screaming, I don't wanna, and actually get the thing done.


03:27:42-03:27:44
But there's a trade-off.


03:27:44-03:27:51
It takes three times as long, I'm less effective, and there's a good chance it'll be harder to complete the task the next time around.


03:27:51-03:27:59
Sometimes I can excite and engage my brain only for it to completely lose interest, forget what I was doing, or get stuck at the first confusing obstacle.


03:27:59-03:28:03
I have a lot of half-knitted blankets and half-finished degrees.


03:28:03-03:28:19
No matter how much I cared about getting a role, graduating college, having a clean home, or landing a real job, I'd find myself avoiding or procrastinating the tasks needed to stick with and accomplish these goals.


03:28:19-03:28:29
Fun fact, while reviewing the copy edits for this chapter, I went down a rabbit hole about why my copy editor changed procrastinating on to simply procrastinating.


03:28:29-03:28:31
And wow, he was right.


03:28:31-03:28:36
It's correct to use procrastinate intransitively when we're speaking about acting on a task.


03:28:36-03:28:38
And yeah, this is how this happens.


03:28:38-03:28:43
Not only are we more motivated by the new shiny things, we're more distracted by them too.


03:28:43-03:28:52
I often spent so much energy trying to convince myself to do the thing or figuring out how to do the thing that I didn't have enough left to actually complete it.


03:28:52-03:28:56
If something became urgent enough, I'd kick into gear.


03:28:56-03:29:07
Everything else fell away while I finished a paper or scrambled to get to class on time or apologetically rolled into Jiffy Lube's six months overdue for an oil change because I was about to go on a road trip.


03:29:07-03:29:17
The things that were important to me but never became urgent, like finishing my novel, learning to meal prep or hosting a murder mystery dinner party, I just never got to them.


03:29:17-03:29:21
It made no sense to me or the people around me.


03:29:21-03:29:23
There were explanations, of course.


03:29:23-03:29:25
I must be lazy.


03:29:25-03:29:26
I had no willpower.


03:29:26-03:29:29
Maybe I didn't actually care about this.


03:29:29-03:29:33
Occasionally, I was convinced I was self-sabotaging.


03:29:33-03:29:39
Why else would I spend an entire summer getting an A in statistics but not bother to register for the class?


03:29:39-03:29:41
On some level, I must have wanted to fail.


03:29:41-03:29:45
It was frustrating and exhausting.


03:29:45-03:29:50
Most things in life require you not only to do things but also do them consistently.


03:29:50-03:29:53
And motivation, for me, was anything but consistent.


03:29:53-03:29:56
So my efforts rarely paid off.


03:29:56-03:29:57
I wanted to know why.


03:29:57-03:29:59
What I learned.


03:30:00-03:30:03
ADHD brains aren't motivated by what's important.


03:30:03-03:30:13
In fact, many of our most important tasks are ones that ADHD brains find viscerally painful to do, the ones that are lengthy, repetitive or boring.


03:30:13-03:30:19
Even if we have the motivation to accomplish a goal, we might still have trouble working toward it.


03:30:19-03:30:29
Goals frequently come with multiple tasks and many of these tasks involve these characteristics, which our brains can't sustainably tolerate.


03:30:29-03:30:36
In fact, there's even a term for our tendency to escape and avoid the distress that a delay causes us, delay aversion.


03:30:36-03:30:49
ADHD brains often turn to the more immediate reward of doing something fun now or escaping the distress of facing something tedious, even when we deeply care about the goal.


03:30:49-03:30:52
So what does motivate an ADHD brain?


03:30:52-03:30:59
Things that are urgent, new or novel, the right level of challenging and of personal interest.


03:30:59-03:31:02
In other words, stimulating.


03:31:02-03:31:07
This is due to fundamental differences in the reward system of ADHD brains.


03:31:07-03:31:10
We're dopedifferent.


03:31:10-03:31:14
Dopamine is a neurotransmitter that motivates and reinforces behavior.


03:31:14-03:31:23
When something is pleasurable, dopamine, along with other feel-good neurotransmitters, is released and makes its way to its respective receptors in the brain.


03:31:23-03:31:27
This signals our brain to remember what led to feeling good.


03:31:27-03:31:28
That felt good.


03:31:28-03:31:29
Do it again.


03:31:29-03:31:39
The brain might even start to release anticipatory dopamine to reinforce behaviors that will eventually lead to something pleasurable, such as filling out tax forms that will result in a refund.


03:31:39-03:31:46
Evidence suggests that ADHD brains don't release anticipatory dopamine the same way neurotypical brains do.


03:31:46-03:31:54
Reuptake, the reabsorption of the dopamine, may also happen before it makes it to a receptor.


03:31:54-03:32:00
When this happens, our brains don't learn that a behavior like filling out tax forms will lead to anything good.


03:32:00-03:32:04
And if nothing good comes from a behavior, what's the point of doing it?


03:32:04-03:32:10
This is why it feels as though activities that are more immediately rewarding hijack our brains.


03:32:10-03:32:16
When we abandon tax forms and start playing video games, we feel good and dopamine gets released.


03:32:16-03:32:28
If there's enough dopamine to hit at least some receptors, our brain learns to prioritize video games, making it more likely that we will end up firing up the game console the next time we need to do something similarly tedious.


03:32:28-03:32:37
Dopamine levels also shape our perception of life, our emotions, and how capable we perceive ourselves to be.


03:32:37-03:32:45
When dopamine levels are low, we feel unmotivated, derive less pleasure from everyday tasks, and feel physically tired.


03:32:45-03:32:48
Our brains are understimulated.


03:32:48-03:32:52
This is why we wait until right before the deadline to get started on a project.


03:32:52-03:32:56
It's why we might make simple tasks overly complicated.


03:32:56-03:33:01
It's why we switch up our writing style or the color pens we're using or doodle while taking notes.


03:33:01-03:33:04
It's why we do serious things in silly ways.


03:33:04-03:33:12
We're instinctively stimulating and motivating our brains by adding a sense of urgency, challenge, novelty, and interest.


03:33:12-03:33:16
Rewards are often too far away.


03:33:16-03:33:21
There are two types of [[motivation]], intrinsic and extrinsic.


03:33:22-03:33:28
Intrinsic motivation happens when you do something because it is enjoyable and satisfying in and of itself.


03:33:28-03:33:34
Intrinsically rewarding activities don't require any external incentives for you to want to do them.


03:33:34-03:33:38
For example, eating cookies is intrinsically motivating.


03:33:38-03:33:40
No one needs to pay you to do it.


03:33:40-03:33:42
They just taste good.


03:33:42-03:33:45
You want to eat cookies because you enjoy eating cookies.


03:33:45-03:33:48
And the reward of doing so is immediate.


03:33:48-03:34:01
For me, tasks that tap into intrinsic motivation include learning something new, spending time with animals, setting up new organizational systems, and cuddling up with a blanket and my Nintendo Switch.


03:34:01-03:34:06
These are all activities I enjoy doing for the sake of doing them.


03:34:06-03:34:13
Extrinsic motivation happens when you do something because of the external consequences of doing or not doing it.


03:34:13-03:34:19
For example, you might work hard on your talk because someone or a lot of people will know if you didn't.


03:34:19-03:34:24
You might deal with the icky stickiness of making bread because you'll have fresh bread once you're done.


03:34:24-03:34:33
Or file your taxes, an enjoyable experience for absolutely no one, I know, because you'll face financial penalties if you don't.


03:34:33-03:34:41
Many important life goals, such as getting good grades or getting a raise, are motivating because of the extrinsic reward involved.


03:34:41-03:34:44
Of course we'd like to get into a good school and graduate.


03:34:44-03:34:46
Of course we'd like to make more money.


03:34:46-03:34:49
And we're willing to do what it takes to make that happen.


03:34:49-03:34:50
In theory.


03:34:50-03:35:04
In practice, those extrinsic consequences, both positive and negative ones, often feel too far away to be motivating, especially when they're competing with the more immediate rewards of playing video games or partying now.


03:35:04-03:35:14
This is due to temporal discounting, where we perceive a desired result in the future as less valuable than one we could have now.


03:35:14-03:35:22
Temporal discounting happens for everyone, which is why you might often hear suggestions to reward yourself for a successful week rather than a successful semester.


03:35:22-03:35:28
But along with temporal discounting, those with ADHD also have shorter time horizons.


03:35:28-03:35:36
A reward that will come next week isn't gonna be motivating in the same way because to us, next week doesn't even exist.


03:35:36-03:35:47
ADHD brains are highly sensitive to rewards, but if the rewards aren't particularly salient, read alluring, they need to happen more immediately to have any relevance in our decision-making.


03:35:48-03:35:56
If a task is intrinsically rewarding, enjoyable enough that we don't need extrinsic rewards to do it, temporal discounting has less impact.


03:35:56-03:36:00
It doesn't matter as much that a reward is far off in the distance.


03:36:00-03:36:05
In fact, there's arguably a benefit to not relying on extrinsic motivation.


03:36:05-03:36:10
Research has shown that adding extrinsic rewards can actually decrease intrinsic motivation.


03:36:10-03:36:23
For situations where we're not intrinsically motivated enough to act, we rely on a sense of urgency that kicks in once an extrinsic consequence does appear on the horizon, which is later for us than for others.


03:36:23-03:36:32
As a result, we end up in crisis after crisis trying to get things done at the last minute, and this behavior is reinforced.


03:36:32-03:36:41
When we pull off getting something done at the last minute, our brains release a ton of dopamine, which tells our brains that felt good, do it again.


03:36:41-03:36:53
To break out of this cycle, it's crucial to add extrinsic rewards that are immediate or exciting enough that they're actually, you know, motivating, like eating cookies while you work.


03:36:53-03:37:05
What we often forget to consider when talking about motivation is that there is always motivation not to do the thing too, and that this motivation can be stronger than the motivation encouraging us to do it.


03:37:05-03:37:14
Filling out a decisional balance worksheet, which you can find at howtoadhdbook.com, can help you understand what is motivating you to head in one direction or the other.


03:37:15-03:37:18
The emotions we associate with a task matter.


03:37:18-03:37:24
When we do a simple task, say making a phone call, we're not just dealing with that task.


03:37:24-03:37:30
We're also dealing with an emotional barrier that has been built from past failures with the task.


03:37:30-03:37:37
Brendan Mahan, ADHD coach and founder of ADHD Essentials, calls this barrier a wall of awful.


03:37:37-03:37:44
The more we've struggled with a task in the past, the more failure, disappointment, rejection, and worry we've experienced as a result.


03:37:44-03:37:48
The more of these negative experiences we've endured, the higher that wall.


03:37:48-03:37:53
We don't just need enough motivation to complete the task.


03:37:53-03:37:58
We also need enough motivation, and often time, to climb the emotional wall in front of it.


03:37:58-03:38:08
Everyone has walls of awful around tasks they've failed at, but according to Brendan, those with ADHD have a lot of them, and they tend to be higher than most.


03:38:08-03:38:14
We experience far more failure, criticism, and rejection than our neurotypical peers.


03:38:14-03:38:22
Some of the most common emotions that come up for those with ADHD include overwhelm, discouragement, hopelessness, fear, and confusion.


03:38:22-03:38:29
To make matters even more complicated, ADHDers typically have relatively weak working memory.


03:38:29-03:38:36
This means we might not have enough mental bandwidth to remember why we're doing something, or how it might feel to finish doing it.


03:38:36-03:38:45
Instead, all our working memory slots may be taken up by trying to figure out or remember what we need to do and how we feel about it right now.


03:38:45-03:38:48
As a result, all we see is that wall of awful.


03:38:48-03:38:50
We can't peer over it.


03:38:50-03:38:53
We don't get any windows to show us what might be on the other side.


03:38:53-03:38:58
According to Brendan, there are different ways we deal with this wall.


03:38:58-03:39:02
We might get angry enough that we can Hulk smash through it to get a task done.


03:39:02-03:39:10
Unfortunately, that often hurts our relationships, because we're not always nice about what we say to ourselves or to others when we bust through the wall.


03:39:10-03:39:13
Sometimes we can put a door in the wall.


03:39:13-03:39:21
We might change our mood with music, turn on a favorite TV show in the background, or work somewhere new to distract ourselves long enough to get the task done.


03:39:21-03:39:25
But often, we need to climb the wall.


03:39:25-03:39:38
Before we can face the task and effectively engage with it, both now and in the future, we need to do the emotional work of gearing up for it, facing our anxiety and preparing ourselves to do the thing.


03:39:38-03:39:49
My friend and colleague, Danny Donovan, creator of "The Anti-Planner," a workbook that includes activities, games, and strategies she designed specifically to address the feelings that get in the way of our task, puts it well.


03:39:49-03:39:58
Learning how to identify what's causing your mental resistance makes it easier to find or create a tailored solution that addresses those specific feelings.


03:39:58-03:40:02
Behaviors precede motivation.


03:40:02-03:40:08
When I was an actor, I used to wait until I had enough motivation to spend two hours memorizing my lines.


03:40:08-03:40:14
This almost never happened, so I inevitably found myself trying to cram the day of an audition.


03:40:14-03:40:21
What I didn't know then but understand now is that we don't need motivation to take action.


03:40:21-03:40:24
In fact, it often happens in reverse.


03:40:24-03:40:27
Action can generate motivation.


03:40:27-03:40:28
Here are some examples.


03:40:28-03:40:35
Picking up our phone often motivates us to check our email, text messages, or social media.


03:40:35-03:40:38
Sitting on the couch motivates us to pick up the remote.


03:40:38-03:40:43
Planning a road trip motivates us to make music playlists.


03:40:43-03:40:48
This is the idea behind what psychologists call behavioral activation.


03:40:48-03:40:53
You don't have to feel like going for a bike or motor scooter ride to go for a ride.


03:40:53-03:40:54
You just put on your gear.


03:40:54-03:40:56
You check the air in the tires.


03:40:56-03:40:59
You get on the dang thing and then see how you feel.


03:40:59-03:41:04
This example is a nod to the family bike rides my family and I went on when I was a kid.


03:41:04-03:41:10
My mom, who couldn't ride a bike due to her physical disability, rode a scooter along with us.


03:41:10-03:41:14
These rides are some of my favorite memories and I think I just convinced myself to go ride my bike.


03:41:14-03:41:16
Yerby, you can keep listening.


03:41:16-03:41:20
Is it possible you still don't want to go at this point?


03:41:20-03:41:21
Sure.


03:41:21-03:41:25
You're also more likely to be motivated to do it anyway once you're already ready to go.


03:41:25-03:41:33
As a bonus, this activation can also interrupt negative thought spirals, shifting your thinking from it's too much of a hassle to go for a ride.


03:41:33-03:41:34
I don't even know if there's air in the tires.


03:41:34-03:41:39
I never have time anymore to go riding to hang on, I'm literally riding.


03:41:39-03:41:49
Whatever it is we want to be motivated enough to do, it helps to know our behavior, the actions we take, can increase or decrease that motivation.


03:41:49-03:41:52
Behavior precedes motivation.


03:41:52-03:41:55
It's not entirely about motivation.


03:41:55-03:42:04
According to psychologist Dr. Ari Tuchman, motivation is often the first lever we pull when we want to get ourselves or someone we love to do a thing.


03:42:04-03:42:07
But it's not always the right lever to pull.


03:42:07-03:42:14
Motivation is just one part of a bigger get stuff done system, and it might not be where the problem is.


03:42:14-03:42:16
Consider these possibilities.


03:42:16-03:42:18
A skill gap.


03:42:18-03:42:23
You don't entirely know or remember how to do the thing or the steps involved.


03:42:23-03:42:25
A lack of resources.


03:42:25-03:42:28
You don't have what you need to get the thing done.


03:42:28-03:42:31
For example, you might not have enough time, supplies or energy.


03:42:31-03:42:34
Perfectionism.


03:42:34-03:42:40
Perfectionism and the anxiety that goes hand in hand with it can keep you from getting started or keep you stuck in mental loops.


03:42:40-03:42:43
Overly optimistic thinking.


03:42:43-03:42:48
You might assume that you can get to something tomorrow without checking to see if you're giving yourself enough time to do it.


03:42:48-03:42:50
This is known as positive illusory bias.


03:42:50-03:42:53
Forgetfulness.


03:42:53-03:42:59
Thanks to ADHD related memory challenges, more about these later, you might not remember what your goals even are.


03:42:59-03:43:02
Or an unrealistic goal.


03:43:02-03:43:05
The goal you chose isn't attainable or sustainable.


03:43:05-03:43:09
Melissa H. 38, USA.


03:43:09-03:43:15
I find that if I do something faster than my brain can realize I'm doing it, then it's like a cartoon.


03:43:15-03:43:21
I might be missing motivation slats in my bridge, but I'm just walking on air and not looking down.


03:43:21-03:43:22
Hashtag cartoon gravity.


03:43:22-03:43:26
Kyle T. 27, Malta.


03:43:26-03:43:35
The main way I get things done is by having structure forced on me from an outside source, like a job for instance, where the potential shame of failure is compelling enough to pull me through the wall.


03:43:35-03:43:40
With anything else though, I just don't do it until I literally have no choice.


03:43:40-03:43:43
All of my dishes are piled up in the sink so I can't eat.


03:43:43-03:43:50
Or the deadline is so close that I can ride the panic induced adrenaline rush of, for example, having to write an entire essay in one night.


03:43:50-03:43:54
Daniel C. 36, Kansas.


03:43:54-03:43:58
My biggest struggle is starting big and boring projects.


03:43:58-03:44:01
As a professor, this means grading.


03:44:01-03:44:05
I have no particularly useful strategy for accomplishing this task.


03:44:05-03:44:11
It's not regular enough for a timer or other scheduled alert, and it's not something that is part of a daily routine.


03:44:11-03:44:13
So my typical systems all fail.


03:44:13-03:44:15
The toolbox.


03:44:15-03:44:20
ADHDers instinctively do many things to motivate their brains.


03:44:20-03:44:22
Some are healthier for us than others.


03:44:22-03:44:35
It is a very unfortunate truth that many of us internalize the motivational techniques others have tried on us due to a misunderstanding of how our brains work, including berating and punishing ourselves.


03:44:35-03:44:37
This can get us through our wall of awful.


03:44:37-03:44:40
It also makes it taller.


03:44:40-03:44:47
While it can take time to undo the damage and learn to motivate ourselves more effectively, I promise there are better ways.


03:44:47-03:44:50
One, fill in the motivational planks.


03:44:50-03:44:57
Think of motivation as a bridge that helps us go from wanting to do a thing to actually getting it done.


03:44:57-03:45:01
Those with ADHD often don't have as many motivational planks to get across.


03:45:01-03:45:08
You can hop over little gaps with willpower, but if half the bridge is missing, you'll need to add some planks.


03:45:08-03:45:09
Medication can help.


03:45:09-03:45:11
There are also other strategies you can turn to.


03:45:11-03:45:14
Add urgency.


03:45:14-03:45:17
Invite a guest over if you wanna get yourself to clean.


03:45:17-03:45:21
Sign up for a class with a friend if you want to get started exercising.


03:45:21-03:45:24
Set up a get stuff done meeting with a coworker.


03:45:24-03:45:29
Turn a marathon project into a series of mini sprints so you can get started before it's almost due.


03:45:29-03:45:35
Adding a sense of urgency to tasks now can keep them from becoming a crisis later.


03:45:35-03:45:38
Find the right level of challenging.


03:45:38-03:45:41
If something is too easy, it might be too boring.


03:45:41-03:45:45
If it's too difficult, it can be too frustrating and discouraging to keep going.


03:45:45-03:45:50
If it's something you've already mastered and you're having trouble doing it, add some challenging elements to it.


03:45:50-03:45:53
If it's daunting, take it down a notch.


03:45:53-03:45:56
And if you're having a bad brain day, take it down a couple more notches.


03:45:56-03:46:00
Lowering the bar can help with perfectionism too.


03:46:00-03:46:07
My favorite example of this type of goal and one I used while I worked on this book comes from "Bird by Bird" by Anne Lamott.


03:46:07-03:46:09
Write shitty first drafts.


03:46:09-03:46:12
Tie a task to a personal interest.


03:46:12-03:46:19
When you're doing something you don't wanna do but need to do, ask yourself this, is there anything interesting about this?


03:46:19-03:46:23
You can also incorporate your personal interests into a task.


03:46:23-03:46:32
You might have to make the final draft of your paper sound professional, but who says you can't exclusively use Dungeons and Dragons metaphors to get the first draft done?


03:46:32-03:46:39
If your interest is in turtles, maybe you check off items on your to-do list with turtle stickers instead of check marks.


03:46:39-03:46:43
You can't always choose what you need to do, but you have some control over how you do it.


03:46:43-03:46:45
Add novelty.


03:46:45-03:46:53
Doing boring or repetitive tasks in a new location with different people or with a new tool can make them interesting enough for us to complete them.


03:46:53-03:46:56
This works for systems we've gotten bored of too.


03:46:56-03:47:01
There's a term coined by Barbara Luther that ADHD coaches use a lot.


03:47:01-03:47:03
Resparcalize.


03:47:03-03:47:12
When the system that's been working great for three weeks suddenly stops working for no apparent reason and it's something you'd like to continue, resparcalize it.


03:47:12-03:47:13
Find a way to make it shiny.


03:47:13-03:47:15
Read interesting again.


03:47:15-03:47:17
Natasha L.


03:47:17-03:47:19
25, Florida.


03:47:19-03:47:21
You always gotta start small.


03:47:21-03:47:23
Don't hang up the whole clothing pile.


03:47:23-03:47:24
Fold one shirt.


03:47:25-03:47:28
Caitlin D. 37, Ohio.


03:47:28-03:47:30
I have soundtracks for each type of task.


03:47:30-03:47:35
I have a getting dressed song, so I mentally get myself to listen to the song and get dressed.


03:47:35-03:47:40
I have a doing dishes song to remind myself that the task doesn't take as long as I think it does.


03:47:40-03:47:46
I always imagined I would have a soundtrack to my life, but I never thought I would actually use it as an adaptive self-support strategy.


03:47:46-03:47:50
Sarah G. 39, South Carolina.


03:47:50-03:47:53
It's helpful to find something I like about what I have to do.


03:47:53-03:47:59
When I meal prep, I focus on making pretty bento style lunches or using new ingredients.


03:47:59-03:48:01
I put on a show I like to get on the treadmill.


03:48:01-03:48:03
I use a new cleaning spray.


03:48:03-03:48:05
No, really, sometimes the newness helps.


03:48:05-03:48:09
Two, reduce friction and grease the wheels.


03:48:09-03:48:16
The more barriers between you and a task you can remove, the less motivation you'll need to get started and stay started.


03:48:16-03:48:21
Eliminating obstacles reduces the friction and makes a task more accessible.


03:48:21-03:48:27
Greasing the wheels means doing anything that makes it more likely we'll want to do it, including adding rewards.


03:48:27-03:48:28
More on this later.


03:48:28-03:48:31
Do what you can ahead of time.


03:48:31-03:48:32
Sleep in your gym clothes.


03:48:32-03:48:36
Book classes in advance or make yourself a checklist.


03:48:36-03:48:41
Doing what you can ahead of time means there is less to do or get stuck on when you need to get going.


03:48:41-03:48:44
Remove physical obstacles and other barriers.


03:48:44-03:48:49
If something is in the way of the piano, it's going to make it harder for you to play the piano.


03:48:49-03:48:56
If a task is overwhelming for sensory reasons, for example, there's something about it that is too bright, too noisy or too icky.


03:48:56-03:49:00
Find a tool that helps you avoid the unpleasant experience.


03:49:00-03:49:07
Use gloves while washing dishes, earplugs for noisy trips or sunglasses for running errands outside.


03:49:07-03:49:09
We don't always notice the barriers if we aren't looking for them.


03:49:09-03:49:16
Keeping a barrier log for tasks you're routinely having trouble with can help you figure out where exactly you're getting stuck.


03:49:16-03:49:19
So you can remove the removable barriers for future YouTube.


03:49:19-03:49:22
This is one of the most powerful things I have ever done.


03:49:22-03:49:26
And it is why I'm able to, generally speaking, get videos out on time.


03:49:26-03:49:29
I was able to recognize patterns and the barriers I was facing.


03:49:29-03:49:32
Invest in tools you enjoy using.


03:49:32-03:49:38
Many of us have negative associations with tasks partly due to the tools we associate with the task.


03:49:38-03:49:43
Choosing ones that feel good and look nice is a great way to grease the wheels toward doing the thing.


03:49:43-03:49:46
Plus it can make or save us money in the long run.


03:49:46-03:49:54
For example, how much have we spent on takeout because the nice pans or tools that might make cooking easier are too expensive?


03:49:54-03:49:56
Tap into your why.


03:49:56-03:49:59
There's a reason you're doing the thing.


03:49:59-03:50:00
What's the reason?


03:50:00-03:50:03
It might not be about the task itself.


03:50:03-03:50:22
There's a sweet example of this in an episode of "The Simpsons." When Homer went back to work after Maggie was born, he papered pictures of her over a foreboding sign from his boss that read, "Don't forget, you're here forever." The baby pictures covered enough letters so the sign now read, "Do it for her." Ride the wave.


03:50:22-03:50:28
If you feel like tackling an important task that you usually don't like doing, take care of it now.


03:50:28-03:50:32
Strike while the iron is hot, or more accurately, while the friction is low.


03:50:32-03:50:35
Eat the ice cream first.


03:50:35-03:50:43
This one came from Jesse J. Anderson, a friend and author of "Extra Focus." Rather than trying to eat the frog first, it can help to eat the ice cream first.


03:50:43-03:50:49
Doing something we enjoy rather than trying to tackle the hardest thing on our list is much more likely to keep us in a productive mode.


03:50:49-03:50:53
Jolie J., 32, Florida.


03:50:53-03:50:57
I set up a system to make a task easier and more fun.


03:50:57-03:50:59
I customize everything.


03:50:59-03:51:02
I use stickers and paint to make things more attractive to me.


03:51:02-03:51:06
As soon as something is pretty, fun, and super easy for me to access, I can do it.


03:51:06-03:51:08
Remove all obstacles.


03:51:08-03:51:12
Sky N., 22, Singapore.


03:51:12-03:51:15
I've been trying to change my thinking about tasks.


03:51:15-03:51:18
Rather than forcing myself to study, I invite myself to learn.


03:51:18-03:51:22
The positive connotations make it easier to overcome the wall of awful.


03:51:22-03:51:25
Spider, 39, Florida.


03:51:25-03:51:31
After I take my Adderall in the morning, I reserve about an hour to deliberately rev it up by sitting idle.


03:51:31-03:51:39
I play very chill video games, watch walk-around videos of gardens or parks until my meds have long kicked in and I'm literally seething at myself to get to work.


03:51:39-03:51:43
Three, add or increase accountability.


03:51:43-03:51:51
Accountability helps shorten the distance between the action we need to take and the consequences of taking or not taking it.


03:51:51-03:51:56
Accountability alone isn't enough because again, motivation isn't always the issue.


03:51:56-03:52:00
In these cases, adding accountability can make things worse.


03:52:00-03:52:04
Read increased anxiety, panic attacks, dissociation.


03:52:04-03:52:13
But if we do have the resources and skills to do what we need to do, added accountability can spur us to action because if we don't do the thing, people will know.


03:52:13-03:52:16
And if we do do the thing, people will know.


03:52:16-03:52:20
Decide on what you're doing and not doing.


03:52:20-03:52:25
Sometimes all we need in terms of accountability is to get clear on what we're doing and what can wait.


03:52:25-03:52:35
If you need support with figuring this out, coaches, therapists, and even a friend or coworker can help you make and adjust a plan to ensure progress on things that are important to you.


03:52:35-03:52:37
Find productivity partners.


03:52:37-03:52:42
There are a lot of groups designed to help people connect and support each other towards specific goals.


03:52:42-03:52:48
You can also ask someone to body double with you, sit quietly in a room with you while you work.


03:52:48-03:52:55
This can include going to public places where others are working or studying because there's low key social pressure for you to do the same.


03:52:55-03:52:58
Shorten the feedback loop.


03:52:58-03:53:02
Sometimes we have trouble finishing a project because we get stuck on one part of it.


03:53:02-03:53:07
Sometimes it's because the deadline is too far away to kick our brains into gear.


03:53:07-03:53:12
Either way, asking for shorter deadlines and or more frequent check-ins can help.


03:53:13-03:53:16
Those check-ins can be with your boss, colleagues, or random friends.


03:53:16-03:53:20
Hey, this Friday, can I show you what I've got on my project so far?


03:53:20-03:53:22
Make it a competition.


03:53:22-03:53:28
There are apps, programs, and competitions that gamify productivity, but you can also keep it simple.


03:53:28-03:53:33
Challenge a friend to work with you on building a habit, like making your bed in the morning.


03:53:33-03:53:38
The first person to text the other a picture of their beautifully made bed wins.


03:53:38-03:53:42
Solstice H, 33, USA.


03:53:42-03:53:47
I have a really good online support group that has normalized executive function trades.


03:53:47-03:53:58
One person goes, "Ugh, I need to do laundry." And another responds with something like, "If you put in a load of laundry, "I'll go microwave something for dinner." Amy H, 49, South Carolina.


03:53:58-03:54:05
I have pretended that I'm one of those YouTube channels that are just a person's life, and I narrate what I'm doing for the pretend camera.


03:54:05-03:54:06
Sometimes it works.


03:54:06-03:54:08
I still want to please the pretend audience.


03:54:09-03:54:12
Catherine E, 30, Virginia.


03:54:12-03:54:16
The best motivation for me is to attach a tangible handoff point for the task.


03:54:16-03:54:20
At work, who needs the baton from my work to do their own work?


03:54:20-03:54:23
Does my partner need clean dishes to make dinner tonight?


03:54:23-03:54:26
A note on procrastination.


03:54:26-03:54:32
[[procrastination|procrastinating]] is common for those with ADHD, and for good reason.


03:54:32-03:54:36
The sense of [[urgency]] we feel as a deadline approaches kicks our brain into gear.


03:54:36-03:54:44
Starting closer to a deadline instead of well ahead of time, as many of us have learned, can save us a lot of time and mental energy.


03:54:44-03:54:47
But how you procrastinate matters.


03:54:47-03:55:05
Research suggests that those who engage in what is called [[procrastination#active procrastination|active procrastination]], the wait until your brain kicks into gear, or put off homework until the night before it's due form of procrastination, experience similar performance and outcomes as non-procrastinators.


03:55:05-03:55:20
On the other hand, those who engage in passive procrastination, the head in the sand form, where you avoid even thinking about the thing, are more likely to experience negative outcomes such as poor grades, missed opportunities, and I don't remember what else.


03:55:20-03:55:23
Ironically, I keep procrastinating on revisiting the research.


03:55:23-03:55:27
Even so, active procrastination has its costs.


03:55:27-03:55:32
You might still have to blow off something important as you try to get the more urgent task done.


03:55:32-03:55:36
Your efforts might affect how well your brain will function the next day.


03:55:36-03:55:38
Sometimes these costs are worth it.


03:55:38-03:55:40
Sometimes they're not.


03:55:40-03:55:48
The takeaway, it's unrealistic to expect anyone, especially someone with ADHD, not to procrastinate at all.


03:55:48-03:55:51
But if you're going to procrastinate, procrastinate mindfully.


03:55:51-03:55:55
Four, make rewards more salient.


03:55:55-03:56:01
Make sure rewards are motivating to the person you're trying to motivate, who is you.


03:56:01-03:56:12
For tasks that aren't intrinsically motivating, adding extrinsic rewards that are can be a great way to generate immediate interest, especially when the goal and prize you're working toward feels so far away.


03:56:12-03:56:16
Pick a reward that is meaningful to you.


03:56:16-03:56:19
Different people respond differently to different rewards.


03:56:19-03:56:26
Maybe the best reward for you is something you don't often let yourself have or something that's related to the steps you're taking to reach a goal.


03:56:26-03:56:32
I like to buy myself fitness gear as I keep working out and soft cozy sweaters to write in as I make progress.


03:56:32-03:56:37
If a reward is personally meaningful, it's more likely to motivate you.


03:56:37-03:56:40
Scale a reward strategically.


03:56:40-03:56:41
Think economics.


03:56:41-03:56:47
If a reward is too big, it floods the reward market and inflation happens.


03:56:47-03:56:54
If you reward yourself with sushi for emptying the dishwasher, that is now what emptying the dishwasher costs.


03:56:54-03:56:58
Good luck trying to convince your brain to do the same task tomorrow for less.


03:56:58-03:57:03
At the same time, if it's not big enough, it won't be motivating enough.


03:57:03-03:57:06
Okay, I am not emptying the dishwasher for five minutes of video game time.


03:57:06-03:57:09
Emptying the dishwasher takes me longer than that.


03:57:09-03:57:12
Make the reward more immediate.


03:57:12-03:57:15
A reward that happens right away is one that is more salient.


03:57:15-03:57:18
Immediate positive feedback is a great example.


03:57:18-03:57:23
You can also try rewarding yourself as you go or pairing something rewarding with the activity itself.


03:57:23-03:57:32
If a reward is stuck in the future, like an upcoming trip you have planned, make your progress more tangible by posting a picture of your destination and marking off days on your calendar.


03:57:32-03:57:34
Tap into your excitement.


03:57:34-03:57:38
Give yourself the reward for doing the thing.


03:57:38-03:57:46
Rewards don't work as motivational fuel if you give them to yourself regardless of your efforts or don't give them to yourself at all.


03:57:46-03:57:54
Make sure you can enjoy your rewards soon after you've finished or made progress on a task, but put barriers in place until you reach a certain point.


03:57:54-03:57:58
A fellow writer friend gave me a care package when I was editing this book.


03:57:58-03:58:02
It had several mini gifts and cards, along with instructions.


03:58:02-03:58:04
I could open one gift immediately.


03:58:04-03:58:10
I could open another when I was a quarter of the way done, half done, three quarters done, and finished.


03:58:10-03:58:13
This was super cute and super motivating.


03:58:13-03:58:17
DJD 25, Ohio.


03:58:17-03:58:20
When I need to get work done, I start with the hardest thing.


03:58:20-03:58:22
Then I plan a fun thing to do when it's all done.


03:58:22-03:58:29
Making a list and checking off boxes gives my brain a dopamine rush because it feels like I've accomplished something as I move from one thing to a next.


03:58:29-03:58:33
Cricket W, 16, Michigan.


03:58:33-03:58:36
My mom used to try to get me to do my math homework using M&Ms.


03:58:36-03:58:39
At first, she would offer me a small bag when I finished my worksheet.


03:58:39-03:58:41
That didn't work.


03:58:41-03:58:46
Then one night, she opened the bag and put a single M&M next to each problem.


03:58:46-03:58:48
That worked.


03:58:48-03:58:50
Instant reward and an incentive to keep going.


03:58:50-03:58:54
Lucila S, 30, Mexico.


03:58:54-03:59:00
To motivate myself to work out, I bought myself the most bougie, delicious body foam for my showers after swimming.


03:59:00-03:59:04
I can only use it in that context, and it makes me feel like I'm in a spa.


03:59:04-03:59:08
Five, don't expect yourself to stick with things.


03:59:08-03:59:11
Hang with me here.


03:59:11-03:59:14
A lot of the time, we start a new venture without an end date.


03:59:14-03:59:20
We might assume this new habit, job, hobby is the one, and we're going to do it forever.


03:59:20-03:59:24
We understandably get frustrated and disappointed when it doesn't turn out that way.


03:59:24-03:59:36
Instead of expecting we'll magically be able to stick to things despite all past evidence to the contrary, it's often more helpful to plan for the variability in interest and motivation inherent to ADHD.


03:59:36-03:59:40
Cycle through hobbies, jobs, and interests.


03:59:40-03:59:44
When the novelty of one thing wears off, it's fine to switch to something else.


03:59:44-03:59:48
Stepping away from something gives it the opportunity to feel new again.


03:59:48-03:59:50
Be a bumblebee.


03:59:50-03:59:54
Carry what you learned from one experience and use it to pollinate your next venture.


03:59:54-03:59:58
Those with ADHD are often referred to as a jack of all trades, master of none.


03:59:58-04:00:01
But have you heard the expanded version of that quote?


04:00:01-04:00:07
It says a jack of all trades is a master of none, but oftentimes better than master of one.


04:00:07-04:00:10
There's value in building a diverse skillset.


04:00:10-04:00:12
Set a date to reevaluate.


04:00:12-04:00:18
Agree to stick to something for a set amount of time, whatever feels reasonable for you to commit to.


04:00:18-04:00:22
When you reach your end date, see if it's worth continuing with what you're doing.


04:00:22-04:00:26
That way, we don't keep putting time and energy into something that isn't working for us.


04:00:26-04:00:33
This strategy also helps with the ADHD tendency to give up on a project, system, or task at the first sign of failure.


04:00:33-04:00:37
Cashel R, 45, Louisiana.


04:00:37-04:00:41
Well, I can say that in a theater, having ADHD is a major boost.


04:00:41-04:00:46
I've got short-lived projects that vary enough from show to show to keep my interest peaked.


04:00:46-04:00:49
It's also let me slide into whatever position needs filling for that show.


04:00:49-04:00:50

04:00:50-04:00:52
I've got you covered.


04:00:52-04:00:57
Scene designer, set builder, set dresser, director, prop mistress, costumer, I'm your gal.


04:00:57-04:01:00
I've achieved just enough mastery of each to do the job well.


04:01:00-04:01:04
Then it's on to something new, keeping my ever-racing mind engaged.


04:01:04-04:01:08
Jeffrey C, 47, Alaska.


04:01:08-04:01:11

04:01:11-04:01:14

04:01:14-04:01:17
Sometimes we can exist and enjoy the world without getting stuff done.


04:01:18-04:01:20
Coach A versus Coach B.


04:01:20-04:01:30
While I was writing this book, Dr. Patrick LaCount, a psychologist and How to ADHD's research consultant, told me a story that he shares with his clients.


04:01:30-04:01:34
Imagine an eight-year-old kid who is the goalie of her soccer team.


04:01:34-04:01:40
It's almost the end of the game, and a player from the other team is about to take a shot at the goal.


04:01:40-04:01:46
Our goalie guesses which way the ball will go, dives in that direction, and misses it completely.


04:01:46-04:01:49
The ball flies in the other direction and into the goal.


04:01:49-04:01:52
The game is over, and her team loses.


04:01:52-04:01:55
Coach A calls her over.


04:01:55-04:01:56
He starts yelling, "What's wrong with you?


04:01:56-04:01:57
You cost us the game.


04:01:57-04:01:59
We went over this in practice.


04:01:59-04:02:05
You knew what to do." The kid feels terrible and goes home defeated.


04:02:05-04:02:11
The next time there's practice or even a game, she says she has a stomach ache and stays home.


04:02:11-04:02:16
Now imagine that same scenario, only this time Coach B calls her over after the team loses the game.


04:02:16-04:02:19
"Hey, come here.


04:02:19-04:02:24
Remember, when you're trying to figure out which way they're gonna kick it, look at where their eyes are looking and at the position of their foot.


04:02:24-04:02:25
You got it?


04:02:25-04:02:33
Yeah, you got it." The kid still feels awful that she cost her team the game, but now she has something to try differently.


04:02:33-04:02:36
And the next time she needs to play, she shows up.


04:02:36-04:02:47
After Dr. LeCount tells this story to his clients, he asks them, "Which coach would you want your child to have if you want her to have fun?" Everyone chooses Coach B.


04:02:47-04:02:52
But who do you choose if you want her to go pro?


04:02:52-04:02:56
Still, everyone chooses Coach B.


04:02:56-04:03:02
When it comes to motivating ourselves though, we often speak to ourselves as Coach A.


04:03:02-04:03:03
"What's wrong with you?


04:03:03-04:03:05
You knew this was important.


04:03:05-04:03:08
You should have started sooner." Or, "Why did you do it that way?


04:03:08-04:03:13
You know better." Or even, "God, you're such a..." Fill in your go-to, probably ableist term.


04:03:13-04:03:19
How could you forget, miss, mess up something so important, so obvious?


04:03:19-04:03:21
You're a grown adult, act like one.


04:03:21-04:03:27
For many of us with ADHD, we grew up hearing messages like these and internalized them.


04:03:27-04:03:28
They became our default.


04:03:28-04:03:36
Many people have asked how I, someone with ADHD, was able to write a book when we often have trouble even reading one.


04:03:36-04:03:38
To be honest, it's been hard.


04:03:38-04:03:42
This chapter was incredibly challenging.


04:03:42-04:03:44
I wrestled with having too much to say.


04:03:44-04:03:46
I didn't know where to start.


04:03:46-04:03:55
I sat in my car in the parking lot outside my office for an hour and 17 minutes, freezing my butt off because I didn't have the energy to go inside and finish writing the chapter.


04:03:55-04:03:58
But I also didn't wanna give up and go home.


04:03:58-04:04:04
I have had to use every single strategy in this chapter's toolbox and a lot from the other chapters to get it done.


04:04:04-04:04:08
And even still, I missed deadlines.


04:04:08-04:04:10
I read what I wrote and hated it.


04:04:10-04:04:16
I was embarrassed to show my editor the mess that I'd written and the Coach A voice crept in.


04:04:16-04:04:17
What's wrong with you?


04:04:17-04:04:18
You should have started sooner.


04:04:18-04:04:19
This is terrible.


04:04:19-04:04:21
Why did anyone let you write a book?


04:04:21-04:04:26
And when that happens, I use the trick Dr. LeCount teaches all his clients.


04:04:26-04:04:31
Notice when you're berating yourself and then ask yourself, what would Coach B say?


04:04:31-04:04:36
My Coach B would remind me I've never written a book before.


04:04:36-04:04:38
I'm figuring this out as I go.


04:04:38-04:04:56
Instead of telling me what I should have done, Coach B would suggest I adjust my deadlines based on the pace at which I was writing, ask my editor for guidance, swallow my pride and hire a writing buddy to help me with parts I struggle with, text a friend in that freezing parking lot and ask if she wanted to body double.


04:04:56-04:04:59
I also got advice from friends.


04:04:59-04:05:06
Danny, who was actually a goalie as a kid, told me, "It can feel like the entire game comes down to you and missing the ball.


04:05:06-04:05:09
So it's easy to take all the blame on yourself.


04:05:09-04:05:13
But there are a lot of other people on that team who are involved in whether you win or lose.


04:05:13-04:05:18
Sometimes the system that you're operating in isn't exactly setting you up for success.


04:05:18-04:05:24
Even if it is, it is so unrealistic to expect yourself to save every ball.


04:05:24-04:05:33
Come to terms with the fact that you're going to miss some and that's gotta be okay." When something I tried didn't work out, I learned from the experience.


04:05:33-04:05:37
I reminded myself what I could try differently next time.


04:05:37-04:05:40
And the next time I needed to write, I showed up.

### Chapter 8: How to Remember Stuff. [04:05:40]
04:05:40-04:05:47
Chapter eight, how to remember stuff.


04:05:47-04:05:52
The only thing faster than the speed of thought is the speed of forgetfulness.


04:05:52-04:05:55
Good thing we have other people to help us remember.


04:05:55-04:05:56
Vera Nazarian.


04:05:56-04:06:00
I forgot.


04:06:00-04:06:09
A year into working on this book, I connected with Pina Varnell, creator of the web comic, "ADHD Alien," about the challenges we faced writing our respective brain manuals.


04:06:09-04:06:12
As we talked, the topic of forgetting stuff came up.


04:06:12-04:06:15
She asked how I was covering it in my book.


04:06:15-04:06:16
I stared at her.


04:06:16-04:06:24
I had spent a year writing a book about ADHD and completely forgotten to include a chapter about forgetting stuff.


04:06:24-04:06:28
I figured that alone was proof I needed to include it.


04:06:28-04:06:29
Was it too late?


04:06:29-04:06:36
The manuscript was due in less than a month and I was not close to finishing it, even without this new edition.


04:06:36-04:06:41
People deeply concerned for my wellbeing asked me if there was another way to do it.


04:06:41-04:06:47
Could I combine it with another chapter, cover it quickly in a funny anecdote at the end, leave it for another book?


04:06:47-04:06:48
I shook my head.


04:06:48-04:06:50
I had to make it work.


04:06:50-04:06:54
90% of the ADHD experience is saying two things on loop.


04:06:54-04:06:57
I'm sorry I got distracted and I'm sorry I forgot.


04:06:57-04:07:00
I knew there was a lot to say on this topic.


04:07:00-04:07:05
I reached out to my research consultant, Dr. Patrick Waccount, who also has ADHD.


04:07:05-04:07:12
Me, hey Patrick, are you able to get research for how to remember stuff to me by Saturday morning?


04:07:12-04:07:14
I'm gonna start on that one next week.


04:07:14-04:07:15
Patrick, absolutely.


04:07:15-04:07:18
I followed up Monday morning.


04:07:18-04:07:22
Me, hey Patrick, did you send it?


04:07:22-04:07:24
If not, would you have time to touch base on it tomorrow?


04:07:24-04:07:28
I'm starting to write how to remember stuff on Thursday.


04:07:28-04:07:31
Patrick, I knew I was forgetting something.


04:07:31-04:07:35
I can do a write-up tomorrow morning and then I have an opening from three to 4 p.m. mountain time.


04:07:35-04:07:41
Then I almost missed the meeting because I forgot he said mountain time.


04:07:41-04:07:44
It's not that I don't remember anything.


04:07:44-04:07:47
I can recall the color of the curtains in my childhood home.


04:07:47-04:08:01
I'll never forget the first time I danced with a boy or more accurately stood facing a boy at a school dance and how long I stayed hiding in the bathroom in shame after I realized I didn't know what to do once the song started playing.


04:08:01-04:08:09
I can tell you how many crumpled dollar bills a group of six demanding adults left as a tip when I was working the graveyard shift at Denny's in my 20s.


04:08:09-04:08:10
It was six.


04:08:10-04:08:14
But remembering what I'm doing while I'm trying to do it?


04:08:14-04:08:15
Nope.


04:08:15-04:08:18
Remembering the lines I spent three hours memorizing?


04:08:18-04:08:19
Probably not.


04:08:19-04:08:22
Remembering the name of the person standing in front of me?


04:08:22-04:08:23
Maybe.


04:08:24-04:08:27
On the surface, it doesn't always seem like that big a deal.


04:08:27-04:08:28
I can laugh about it.


04:08:28-04:08:30
It's part of what makes me me.


04:08:30-04:08:34
As my mom often pointed out, I'd forget my head if it wasn't attached.


04:08:34-04:08:39
Under the surface though, it's not so cute or quirky.


04:08:39-04:08:45
ADHDers forget things all the time and we work ourselves into the ground compensating for the things that slip our minds.


04:08:45-04:08:48
It's cost me self-esteem.


04:08:48-04:08:55
When I'd realized I'd forgotten obvious or important stuff, the negative thoughts were immediate and automatic.


04:08:55-04:09:02
Judgments starting with I'm so, and I'm such a, played in my head and sometimes spilled out of my mouth.


04:09:02-04:09:04
It's cost me financial security.


04:09:04-04:09:08
I've lost jobs due to forgotten paperwork and follow through.


04:09:08-04:09:15
I've built up, then completely wrecked my credit twice because of missed payments on bills I had the money to pay.


04:09:15-04:09:20
It's cost me housing security when my credit wasn't good enough to rent an apartment.


04:09:20-04:09:22
It's cost me relationships.


04:09:22-04:09:30
Not only have I had friends give up on me for being unreliable, I've also had relationships go south because I can't keep track of how I'm being treated.


04:09:30-04:09:34
Is this friend saying something unkind for the first time or the 50th?


04:09:34-04:09:37
What was that last fight about anyway?


04:09:37-04:09:42
Forgetful people, particularly those of us with low self-esteem can be easy to manipulate.


04:09:42-04:09:45
I knew I was capable of remembering stuff.


04:09:45-04:09:49
I also knew I couldn't trust my memory to work how and when I needed it to.


04:09:50-04:09:53
A very incomplete list of things I've forgotten.


04:09:53-04:10:02
What you just said, what I just said, what I was doing, where I put my keys/phone/the thing I was just holding.


04:10:02-04:10:04
What happened yesterday?


04:10:04-04:10:06
Why I was mad 10 minutes ago?


04:10:06-04:10:08
Whether I've taken my meds?


04:10:08-04:10:11
The life-changing epiphany I had yesterday.


04:10:11-04:10:15
What I agreed to do tomorrow/next week/next month.


04:10:15-04:10:18
The supplies I needed for whatever it is I'm trying to do.


04:10:18-04:10:19
The boundaries you set.


04:10:19-04:10:21
The boundaries I set.


04:10:21-04:10:23
Everything I just read.


04:10:23-04:10:25
What I'm supposed to do differently now.


04:10:25-04:10:29
The name of the app I'm trying to open and have used a thousand times.


04:10:29-04:10:31
How to work the remote.


04:10:31-04:10:32
My jacket.


04:10:32-04:10:33
My socks.


04:10:33-04:10:37
The pants I fully intended to put on before I answered the door.


04:10:37-04:10:40
What I learned.


04:10:40-04:10:46
Before I started researching for the channel, I was aware of two types of memory, short-term and long-term.


04:10:46-04:10:50
Well, there are a bunch of other types that brain scientists are still debating.


04:10:50-04:10:56
Sensory memory, implicit memory, explicit memory, haptic memory, procedural memory, and more.


04:10:56-04:10:59
There are too many to remember, for me anyway.


04:10:59-04:11:06
The point is, memory is complicated, but here is what's most important for those of us with ADHD to understand.


04:11:06-04:11:10
Our long-term memory is fine, question mark?


04:11:10-04:11:22
In several studies on long-term memory, participants with ADHD performed just as well as, if not better than, neurotypical participants on tests for certain types of memory.


04:11:22-04:11:35
Case in point, episodic memory, a type of long-term memory that allows us to recall the details about a specific experience, what happened, what was said, how we felt, and so on.


04:11:35-04:11:45
A 2012 study found that when discussing a special event in their life, children with ADHD provided lengthier and more descriptive narratives than those without ADHD.


04:11:45-04:11:47
This is true for me.


04:11:47-04:11:54
Even as an adult, I can describe entire scenes from my childhood with as much visual and emotional detail as a movie.


04:11:54-04:12:01
My writing buddy, who doesn't have ADHD, can't, though she does remember what it is we're supposed to be working on today.


04:12:01-04:12:03
It's chapter eight, how to remember stuff, babe.


04:12:03-04:12:09
On the other hand, some studies on ADHDers have found deficits in long-term memory.


04:12:09-04:12:10
Why?


04:12:10-04:12:18
The ability to remember depends on three processes, encoding information, storing it, and finally, retrieving it.


04:12:18-04:12:30
A meta-analysis of long-term memory in adults with ADHD suggests that our difficulty remembering things long-term is actually a learning deficit that happens at the stage of encoding.


04:12:30-04:12:34
In other words, we can't remember what we never actually learned.


04:12:34-04:12:42
Findings like these are really interesting and also useful because there's a lot we can do to support the encoding process.


04:12:42-04:12:49
Being able to provide this support starts with understanding working memory and how it works, hee-hee, for ADHD brains.


04:12:49-04:12:53
Working memory is often impaired in ADHD.


04:12:53-04:13:02
Working memory, noun, a type of memory that gives us the ability to hold new information temporarily in our head while we work with it.


04:13:02-04:13:17
We use working memory for many everyday tasks, remembering a time and date while we look for a pen to write them down, knowing what we came into a room to do, remembering somebody's name and telling them hours while trying to gauge a new social situation.


04:13:17-04:13:20
Everyone's working memory has a limited number of slots.


04:13:20-04:13:24
When there is information we need to attend to, we hold onto it in these slots.


04:13:24-04:13:29
It then disappears quickly, constantly shuffling to make room for new information.


04:13:29-04:13:37
For the information that we need later, we can save it on our brain's hard drive through techniques that encode it, like taking notes and studying.


04:13:37-04:13:40
Working memory is often impaired in ADHD brains.


04:13:40-04:13:45
We essentially get fewer slots, especially when it comes to verbal and auditory working memory.


04:13:45-04:13:49
This makes it hard to remember something we just read or heard someone say.


04:13:49-04:14:00
Let's say a neurotypical student has a working memory capacity that allows her to temporarily hold onto five pieces of information, and another student with ADHD can only hold onto three.


04:14:00-04:14:06
The teacher asks the students a question followed by three possible answers.


04:14:06-04:14:13
The neurotypical student might be able to answer the question no problem while also thinking about the cute boy in the second row.


04:14:13-04:14:22
But by the time the teacher shares the third possible answer, the ADHD student has likely forgotten the question even if they're completely focused.


04:14:22-04:14:25
They don't have enough slots to store all the information.


04:14:25-04:14:31
Our ADHD student also needs to use more effort to direct their attention and actions.


04:14:31-04:14:38
Their difficulty with attention regulation means the information in those slots can get kicked out a lot more easily.


04:14:38-04:14:48
We also take up valuable working memory slots to remember that we need to sit still, be quiet, and all the other things we are told to do and that don't come naturally to our brains.


04:14:48-04:14:52
We also have trouble recalling information.


04:14:52-04:15:03
We can't remember what we weren't paying attention to in the first place, and as I explained in "How to Hyperfocus," those of us with ADHD have a hard time regulating our attention.


04:15:03-04:15:11
Still, that's not the only factor contributing to our difficulties with remembering things long-term, and by long-term I mean things that happened like yesterday.


04:15:11-04:15:14
We can't remember what we don't understand.


04:15:14-04:15:27
The process of encoding information into long-term storage requires our brains to compare new information with what we already know and then figure out where to file it.


04:15:27-04:15:33
For this to happen, we need to rely on our working memory to hold all of this information long enough for it to happen.


04:15:33-04:15:42
If the new information is easy for us to understand and related to a familiar subject, the encoding process can be quick and easy.


04:15:42-04:15:55
If, on the other hand, we have no clue what someone is talking about, possibly because we missed or didn't encode the foundational knowledge well enough, we may not have enough time for this process to take place.


04:15:55-04:16:01
Before our brains are able to encode it, new information comes in and bumps it out of our working memory.


04:16:01-04:16:07
I learned this the hard way when I was invited to sit in on a college professor's algebra class and offer feedback.


04:16:07-04:16:09
He handed out a quiz halfway through the class.


04:16:09-04:16:18
Despite knowing at this point how to support my working memory and attention, I couldn't answer any of the questions on the quiz about the day's lesson, none.


04:16:18-04:16:35
To the professor's credit, he had paused occasionally during his lecture to ask any questions, but because my working memory couldn't hold on to the information coming in long enough to make sense out of it, I didn't understand enough to even know what questions to ask.


04:16:35-04:16:43
I felt like a bad student, as I had so many times in my actual classes, then remembered, I'm not.


04:16:43-04:16:50
I just didn't have enough foundational knowledge about algebra to encode what the professor was saying before it slipped from my working memory.


04:16:50-04:16:54
The professor was teaching the same material to the next class.


04:16:54-04:17:01
During the 10 minute break between classes, I gave myself a quick foundation in algebra by doing what I do best, Googling it.


04:17:01-04:17:08
I scanned through articles about the basics of algebra and learned what some of the terms meant and the five steps to solving an equation.


04:17:08-04:17:10
There were steps?


04:17:10-04:17:12
Well, this explained a lot.


04:17:12-04:17:16
During the next class, I understood more of what the professor was saying.


04:17:16-04:17:22
And when he handed out the quiz, I remembered some of what he'd taught and answered several of the questions right.


04:17:22-04:17:33
Because I had a hook to hang the lesson on, which in this case was the information I Googled, I could chunk multiple pieces of data into one working memory slot.


04:17:33-04:17:42
And that in turn meant my working memory could hold on to more information, enough that I was able to understand and encode at least some of what the professor was teaching us.


04:17:42-04:17:46
We can't remember what we don't remember to remember.


04:17:46-04:17:55
In general, the long-term memory issues those with ADHD face are about difficulties within coding rather than storage or retrieval.


04:17:55-04:17:57
There is one notable exception.


04:17:57-04:18:08
While cued recall and serial recall aren't impaired in ADHD brains, studies done on ADHD children and teens suggest that free recall is.


04:18:08-04:18:16
Free recall, also referred to as un-cued recall, is the ability to spontaneously remember something without a cue to prompt us.


04:18:16-04:18:26
For example, we know we brought a jacket to school and maybe even remember where we put it, but we'll probably forget to grab it on our way out the door unless something or someone reminds us.


04:18:26-04:18:35
This tendency for things and people to be out of sight, out of mind is sometimes mistaken for an issue with object permanence.


04:18:35-04:18:42
Object permanence is an important developmental milestone achieved in early childhood, often through games like peekaboo.


04:18:42-04:18:47
It's the understanding that when we can no longer see an object or person, they continue to exist.


04:18:47-04:19:04
Understanding that our friend continues to exist when they leave the room is not the issue with ADHD, but remembering that our friend exists when they leave the room and that we should text them later and make sure they got home okay, that we do have trouble with because it depends on free recall.


04:19:04-04:19:06
I'm sorry, I forgot.


04:19:06-04:19:20
Our difficulties with free recall also helps to explain why perspective memory, the ability to remember to do something in the future, is impaired in those with ADHD, at least time-based perspective memory.


04:19:20-04:19:28
Time-based perspective memory is what allows us to remember to do an action at a specific time or after a specific time period has elapsed.


04:19:29-04:19:36
Let's say our friend wanted us to check in with them in a few hours, or perhaps we told them we would call at 4 p.m.


04:19:36-04:19:47
We may have made plans to do this, understood those plans, and stored that information somewhere in our brains, and we know the plans are in our brains because they often pop back into our consciousness at random times.


04:19:47-04:19:52
Still, when the time comes, we absolutely forget about those plans.


04:19:52-04:19:55
Half the time, we don't even realize what time it is.


04:19:55-04:20:03
If we forget to call our friend back at 4 p.m., then how come we remember to ask them about that book they said we could borrow the next time we see them in person?


04:20:03-04:20:13
That's because our intention to borrow that book when we see them again is stored in our event-based perspective memory, which doesn't seem to be impaired in ADHD brains.


04:20:13-04:20:19
Event-based perspective memory allows us to remember an action in response to an external cue.


04:20:19-04:20:35
When there's an external cue involved, our ability to remember to do something isn't impacted by our wonky sense of time or our difficulties with free recall, though it is entirely possible that we forget the exact wording of what we wanted to say, we will at least remember that we wanted to say it.


04:20:35-04:20:41
But again, just because someone asked us to do something after dinner doesn't mean we actually heard or encoded it.


04:20:41-04:20:44
Yes, even if we totally agreed to do it.


04:20:44-04:20:47
Again, our brains wander a lot.


04:20:47-04:20:51
Anonymous, 25, USA.


04:20:51-04:20:55
Remembering appointments, birthdays, and even holidays is generally a disaster.


04:20:55-04:20:59
I find myself constantly having to write reminders down.


04:20:59-04:21:02
Cone S, 33, Belgium.


04:21:02-04:21:12
I can tell myself not to forget things, but even if it's the most important thing in the world, I'll get distracted and the existence of the earth-shattering realization will just evaporate from my mind.


04:21:12-04:21:14
I won't even remember I had such an idea.


04:21:14-04:21:16
It's just gone.


04:21:16-04:21:19
Des C, 47, Washington.


04:21:19-04:21:23
I feel like things either fall out of my head or I never forget them.


04:21:23-04:21:24
There is no middle ground.


04:21:25-04:21:28
Suzanne S, 37, Alaska.


04:21:28-04:21:32
The biggest lie I tell myself is, I don't need to write that down.


04:21:32-04:21:33
I will remember.


04:21:33-04:21:36
99% of the time that I say that, I forget.


04:21:36-04:21:39
The toolbox.


04:21:39-04:21:43
So do we just like give up on remembering stuff?


04:21:43-04:21:45
Nah, well, sort of.


04:21:45-04:21:57
It's important to accept that we will forget things and understanding why we'll have a harder time remembering things makes it easier to figure out which strategies can help when we remember to use them, of course.


04:21:57-04:22:03
At this point, I have so many ways of supporting my memory challenges, it almost doesn't bother me anymore.


04:22:03-04:22:05
Almost.


04:22:05-04:22:06
Here are my favorites.


04:22:06-04:22:07
I use these all the time.


04:22:07-04:22:10
One, use an assistant.


04:22:10-04:22:16
Executive function is like the CEO of the brain and what CEO doesn't need an assistant?


04:22:16-04:22:24
Calendars, checklists, apps, and planners function as either virtual, digital, or in-person, analog, assistants.


04:22:24-04:22:32
Though it isn't always easy for us to use these tools, they help us reserve our working memory slots for actually doing a task at hand.


04:22:32-04:22:34
Don't worry, you're still the boss.


04:22:34-04:22:40
Your assistant is just there to keep your brain free for more important stuff, like deciding whether or not you actually want to do the thing.


04:22:40-04:22:42
Journal.


04:22:42-04:22:50
A journal gives you a place to record your thoughts, feelings, and dreams, somewhere you can look back at interactions and track how things are going.


04:22:50-04:22:55
If you'd like it to function as a planner as well, the bullet journal and the hero's journal are both great for this.


04:22:55-04:22:57
To-do list.


04:22:57-04:23:02
Sometimes our overwhelm comes from trying to hold too many to-dos in our head.


04:23:02-04:23:18
It can be helpful to dump out everything we want to do and then narrow that down to what we're actually able to do, which is often less than we think because we already have tasks related to our normal living life routines, and we also have brains that like to spontaneously do stuff we didn't have planned.


04:23:18-04:23:22
Dr. LeCount recommends choosing no more than three to five to-dos per day.


04:23:22-04:23:30
Because prioritizing can be a challenge for us, he suggests choosing ones that, if you do them, will make you feel like today was a success.


04:23:30-04:23:34
Remember, just because your to-do list is short doesn't mean you can't do more.


04:23:34-04:23:39
Many people in our community also use to-did lists to track all they accomplished.


04:23:39-04:23:41
Project management software.


04:23:41-04:23:45
For example, Asana, Monday, Trello, and Notion.


04:23:45-04:23:49
These programs can track to-do lists and entire projects.


04:23:49-04:23:50
Be careful, though.


04:23:50-04:23:51
Their capacity is unlimited.


04:23:51-04:23:53
Ours is not.


04:23:53-04:23:57
AnamarinV31, Belgium.


04:23:57-04:24:02
We have a big whiteboard that has our schedule and important notices for the current week in our kitchen.


04:24:02-04:24:04
It also has our menu.


04:24:04-04:24:07
By writing it down at the start of the week, I don't have to remember it during.


04:24:07-04:24:11
PhoenixR39, California.


04:24:11-04:24:15
I like to use my hyperfixations as tools to remember things.


04:24:15-04:24:21
Right now, it is on stationary items, so writing down my events in different colors helps me focus on which category that event is for.


04:24:21-04:24:23
For example, purple for family.


04:24:23-04:24:26
Each family member getting their own color.


04:24:26-04:24:28
