A separate color for school, et cetera.


04:24:28-04:24:32
GenM40, North Carolina.


04:24:32-04:24:35
Whenever I make a commitment, I immediately add it to my calendar.


04:24:35-04:24:36
No, I'll get to it later.


04:24:36-04:24:38
I do it on the spot.


04:24:38-04:24:42
I also have a bullet journal app on my phone that keeps my ongoing to-do list.


04:24:42-04:24:47
It has been helpful using an app because I'd misplace a paper list and totally forget about it.


04:24:47-04:24:49
A note on notes.


04:24:49-04:24:53
Sticky notes aren't great assistants.


04:24:53-04:24:57
Sticky notes do, however, make fantastic assistants to your assistant.


04:24:57-04:24:59
Think of sticky notes like short-term memory.


04:24:59-04:25:03
They're great for holding on to important information for a little bit of time.


04:25:03-04:25:09
But if you want that information to stick around, it helps to encode the information into long-term storage.


04:25:09-04:25:11
For example, your calendar.


04:25:12-04:25:16
Two, lower the demand on your working memory.


04:25:16-04:25:20
If we have fewer working memory slots, we need to use them more effectively.


04:25:20-04:25:26
This is especially important for new tasks because they place greater demands on working memory.


04:25:26-04:25:31
We haven't chunked any of that data yet, so we can't fit as much into one slot.


04:25:31-04:25:33
It is also important for stressful tasks.


04:25:33-04:25:39
When emotion kicks in, so does our hot executive function system, which shuts down cognition.


04:25:39-04:25:44
This is one reason tasks that are just as simple aren't necessarily just as doable.


04:25:44-04:25:48
Empty your working memory slots.


04:25:48-04:25:49
We've all been there.


04:25:49-04:25:56
During important conversations or brain-heavy tasks, a thought that is begging for your attention pops into your head.


04:25:56-04:25:59
The next time this happens, take a sec to write it down.


04:25:59-04:26:06
Offloading it allows you to use your entire working memory to hear and process what your conversation partner is saying or to work on the task at hand.


04:26:06-04:26:13
Not just the part that isn't trying to remember what you wanted to say or what you suddenly remembered you need to do next week.


04:26:13-04:26:19
Because this can look like I'm being distracted, I try to let people know, hey, this deserves my full brain.


04:26:19-04:26:22
Let me jot this thought down so I can give that to you.


04:26:22-04:26:25
Use a visual or auditory reference.


04:26:25-04:26:32
Checklists are one type of visual reference that many of us with ADHD use, but that's not the only way to support our working memory.


04:26:32-04:26:37
Dual monitors can keep more information in front of your eyes so you don't have to hold it in your brain.


04:26:37-04:26:43
Likewise, a notepad or whiteboard surface gives you a place to write on and serves as an at-a-glance reference.


04:26:43-04:26:48
You can even pull a tab out from your browser so you can reference the webpage in a separate window.


04:26:48-04:26:50
Just grab the tab and move it.


04:26:50-04:27:00
If what you're doing requires moving around like cooking or cleaning, or you have a visual impairment, a podcast, video or self-recorded tutorial can help.


04:27:00-04:27:03
Use an active body double.


04:27:03-04:27:15
While a body double can be someone who simply sits with you while you work, an active body double, like a study partner who can quiz you or a coworker who can read information aloud while you enter it, offers additional assistance.


04:27:15-04:27:23
Working with an active body double splits up the mental load of a task, which makes doing it easier and less frustrating for both people.


04:27:23-04:27:31
It also frees your working memory slots for more intense cognitive tasks like encoding, analysis or checking your work.


04:27:31-04:27:35
Work on one task at a time, aka monotasking.


04:27:35-04:27:43
While multitasking can help with motivation and weirdly focus, we can dedicate more working memory slots to a task when we're monotasking.


04:27:43-04:27:46
This is important if one of those tasks needs our full brain.


04:27:46-04:27:49
Take one step at a time.


04:27:49-04:27:54
Breaking the task down into individual steps and completing them one at a time can also help.


04:27:54-04:28:02
For example, decide what we want to say before trying to say it, or gather all the ingredients for a recipe before we start cooking it.


04:28:03-04:28:06
Andrea M, 34, Sweden.


04:28:06-04:28:13
I use dual monitors at work, one for showing the information I need and the other for writing or doing calculations.


04:28:13-04:28:19
Not having to alt tab means reducing distractions and removing the possibility of opening something I shouldn't.


04:28:19-04:28:22
Liscari V, 30, California.


04:28:22-04:28:23
I gather what I need beforehand.


04:28:23-04:28:30
For baking cookies, it means I take out all of the ingredients that I need and line them up in order based on the recipe.


04:28:30-04:28:34
Then I make sure all my measuring cups, mixing bowls, and other tools are within easy reach.


04:28:34-04:28:42
The less I have to think about trying to grab or find something in the moment, the less stress on my working memory and the more likely I wind up with delicious cookies.


04:28:42-04:28:45
Three, improve your encoding.


04:28:45-04:28:52
Since our long-term memory issues happen at the encoding stage, it makes sense to focus on supporting the encoding process.


04:28:52-04:29:00
Encoding requires multiple steps, paying attention, making sense of the new information, and giving our brains time to process.


04:29:00-04:29:07
The internet is full of suggestions to help with encoding, AKA learning and studying, but here are a few that are brain-friendly.


04:29:07-04:29:11
Give yourself a hook to hang new info on.


04:29:11-04:29:16
Those with great memory are those who can chunk data together more effectively.


04:29:16-04:29:19
Giving yourself a hook to hang it on can help.


04:29:19-04:29:22
Ask for questions or topics in advance.


04:29:22-04:29:24
Get an overview of what a meeting will cover.


04:29:24-04:29:28
Ask what a story is about so you understand how the details relate.


04:29:28-04:29:32
Or quickly review new material before trying to intentionally learn it.


04:29:32-04:29:35
Use active studying strategies.


04:29:35-04:29:39
Our brains are more likely to wander when we use passive study techniques, such as reading.


04:29:39-04:29:51
Doing something with the material engages the part of our brain that makes up the task-positive network, which shuts the door on the default mode network, which is responsible for our chronic mind wandering.


04:29:51-04:29:55
Write down the information, make and use flashcards, take notes from a textbook.


04:29:55-04:29:57
Make it sticky.


04:29:57-04:30:02
We remember things best when they're meaningful to us or when they stand out as odd.


04:30:02-04:30:06
Put what you learn into your own form of expression.


04:30:06-04:30:11
Turn something you want to remember into a story, acronym, joke, or drawing.


04:30:11-04:30:12
Act it out.


04:30:12-04:30:13
Use silly voices.


04:30:13-04:30:16
Teach it to someone else.


04:30:16-04:30:22
When we explain what we've learned well enough for someone to understand it, we reinforce our own understanding of the material.


04:30:22-04:30:26
You can also teach it to yourself or pretend to teach it to a five-year-old.


04:30:26-04:30:30
If you can explain something in a way that makes sense to a five-year-old, you've got it.


04:30:30-04:30:32
Give it time.


04:30:32-04:30:39
Space out learning sessions for new material and let your brain rest, meditate, nap, or review what you've already learned.


04:30:39-04:30:49
ADHDers often cram the night before a test, but giving our brain time to rest helps us encode information more effectively and strengthens our ability to remember it.


04:30:49-04:30:51
Sleep.


04:30:51-04:30:57
Getting enough sleep makes it easier to stay focused, and as a bonus, it helps us process and strengthen what we learned that day.


04:30:57-04:31:01
Think of sleep as a study session you don't have to be there for.


04:31:01-04:31:05
Anonymous, 20, Florida.


04:31:05-04:31:11
My dad helped me with my math in elementary school by making flashcards studying into a game, complete with funny voices.


04:31:11-04:31:15
He noticed that my brain didn't absorb any information if I wasn't interested.


04:31:15-04:31:20
This was way before I got diagnosed, so I'm still impressed he figured out how to help me study.


04:31:20-04:31:24
Laura W., 29, Australia.


04:31:24-04:31:30
I'm a PA, personal assistant, and I always make sure I have my laptop on me so I can write down any instruction I get.


04:31:30-04:31:35
Whenever I'm given a new job, I write down every step explicitly.


04:31:35-04:31:40
I create a lot of process documents for exactly this reason, which thankfully are useful for everyone else.


04:31:40-04:31:47
If I don't have something to write on, I chant the thing in my head until I get to somewhere I can write it or I can do it.


04:31:47-04:31:50
If I'm asked to do two things at once, I'm kind of doomed.


04:31:51-04:31:54
Four, use cues, but with caution.


04:31:54-04:32:02
Suggesting the use of cues to someone with ADHD is like handing a blowtorch to someone who really likes fire.


04:32:02-04:32:04
You can end up with a creme brulee.


04:32:04-04:32:06
You can also end up burning the house down.


04:32:06-04:32:11
Cues are a powerful and important tool for brains that struggle with free recall.


04:32:11-04:32:17
There are also a lot of skills required to use them artfully, and you'll need to learn what to do if something ends up on fire.


04:32:17-04:32:20
Put things where you can see them.


04:32:20-04:32:24
Make sure anything you want to interact with is easily visible.


04:32:24-04:32:26
Use labels and clear containers.


04:32:26-04:32:30
Create a launch pad by the door so you can grab anything you need before you head out.


04:32:30-04:32:34
Store vegetables on refrigerator shelves, not in drawers.


04:32:34-04:32:39
On the flip side, there is a point where it becomes hard to see what we need because there are so many things visible.


04:32:39-04:32:42
Use this moment as a cue to put some stuff away.


04:32:42-04:32:44
Or get creative with it.


04:32:44-04:32:46
My aunt puts her cell phone in her shoe.


04:32:46-04:32:51
When she goes on a walk, she might forget her phone, but she won't forget her shoes.


04:32:51-04:32:55
Keep to-do cues to times and places where you can follow through on them.


04:32:55-04:33:08
If the reminder to use a language learning app goes off while you're driving to the store, you'll either train yourself to ignore that reminder, or you'll respond to the reminder and risk l'incidente d'auto.


04:33:08-04:33:11
If you already tend to ignore reminders, make 'em weird.


04:33:11-04:33:14
A sock on the doorknob to remind you to change the laundry.


04:33:14-04:33:18
A metronome in your snack cupboard to remind you to practice.


04:33:18-04:33:24
Unusual cues jolt us out of default mode and increase the likelihood we'll notice them.


04:33:24-04:33:27
If you ignore a to-do cue, do it mindfully.


04:33:27-04:33:32
To-do cues always prompt action, even if that action is to ignore the cue.


04:33:32-04:33:34
To safeguard your cues, be mindful.


04:33:34-04:33:37
Try to pause before choosing a response.


04:33:37-04:33:41
If you choose to ignore or snooze the cue, notice why you are making that choice.


04:33:41-04:33:48
You can use this valuable information to refine the cue in the future, or to recognize barriers to action you can now remove.


04:33:48-04:33:52
Use cues to remind you of your intentions.


04:33:52-04:33:59
While we want to be careful with to-do cues and where we introduce them, we can plaster intention-related cues all over the place.


04:33:59-04:34:09
ADHD coach Caroline McGuire suggests we craft cues that remind us of the person we want to be and choose locations where we will find them throughout our day or week.


04:34:09-04:34:16
These cues will help us connect our daily tasks to our greater goals and values, helping us remember why we wanted to do them in the first place.


04:34:16-04:34:20
Examples of intention cues.


04:34:20-04:34:25
Intention cues can take many forms, including a single evocative word.


04:34:25-04:34:28
This can be great when you want to keep your intentions private.


04:34:28-04:34:31
Silly or inspirational posters.


04:34:31-04:34:34
We remember things better when they stand out or spark emotion.


04:34:34-04:34:38
Vision boards, analog or virtual.


04:34:38-04:34:45
These can help strengthen our vision of the person we want to become or the eventual outcome we're hoping to achieve.


04:34:45-04:34:47
Questions that hack our brains.


04:34:47-04:34:52
When we ask ourselves a question such as, why am I so interested in practicing the piano?


04:34:52-04:34:55
Or, why am I so good at saving money?


04:34:55-04:34:58
Our brains are inclined to look for answers.


04:34:58-04:35:00
Oh, because blank.


04:35:00-04:35:02
This can increase intrinsic motivation.


04:35:02-04:35:06
Holly K. 33, Oregon.


04:35:06-04:35:10
I try not to use cues for anything that will take more than two minutes to complete.


04:35:10-04:35:15
The longer the task, the more likely I am to tell myself, oh, I'll remember to come back to this.


04:35:15-04:35:20
When decades of experience has irrefutably proven that to be a dirty lie.


04:35:20-04:35:24
Chatay A. 26, Turkey.


04:35:24-04:35:26
I try to put cues where they will annoy me.


04:35:26-04:35:31
I work with a computer most of the time, so that means sometimes I post a note in the middle of the screen.


04:35:31-04:35:34
If I put it on my desk or something, I'll just get used to it.


04:35:34-04:35:40
But if it being there annoys me, I'm actually motivated to do what I'm supposed to and get rid of the post-it.


04:35:40-04:35:43
Scott H. 39, Japan.


04:35:43-04:35:49
I leave objects out if I need to do something with them, which is a huge part of why I come across as a messy person.


04:35:49-04:35:51
I leave things in places where I can't ignore them.


04:35:51-04:35:56
If I put bills away in a file or something, for example, I would 100% forget that they exist.


04:35:56-04:36:01
I leave the garbage bag out in the middle of the hallway, so I won't forget to take it out for trash day.


04:36:01-04:36:08
I leave unfinished tasks out and open, so that when my brain starts looking for something new, I might land on an existing project and work on that some more.


04:36:10-04:36:11
The joy of forgetting.


04:36:11-04:36:16
My working memory is the most impaired aspect of my ADHD brain.


04:36:16-04:36:21
I suspected this was the case from the moment I learned what working memory was.


04:36:21-04:36:24
It was confirmed later when I had it tested.


04:36:24-04:36:29
As the report delightfully understates, my working memory is a relative area of weakness.


04:36:29-04:36:34
The good news is, not everything is something I want to remember.


04:36:34-04:36:47
Sometimes when a friend asks how I'm doing because of an earlier conversation about a difficult situation I was navigating, I like being able to honestly ask, "What difficult situation?" I'd long since forgotten about it, which means I hadn't been reliving it.


04:36:47-04:36:49
I'd already moved on.


04:36:49-04:36:54
Forgetting what my limits are lets me jump into a new project with enthusiasm and ambition.


04:36:54-04:36:57
It also gives me the opportunity to test what those limits even are.


04:36:57-04:36:58
Sometimes they've changed.


04:36:58-04:37:05
Forgetfulness has given me experiences and an entire career that I wouldn't otherwise have.


04:37:05-04:37:10
The differences in my memory have helped me learn useful compensatory skills.


04:37:10-04:37:18
Because I have trouble remembering where I put things, I'm now a pro at using search functions on web pages and computer programs.


04:37:18-04:37:23
Because I don't assume anyone remembers anything, I've become a good science communicator.


04:37:23-04:37:27
I'm motivated to collect and share what I've learned because I know that I will forget if I don't.


04:37:27-04:37:31
I love the joy of rediscovery.


04:37:31-04:37:36
I used to leave $20 bills in coat pockets so I could dig through them when I inevitably ran out of cash.


04:37:36-04:37:38
Thanks, past me.


04:37:38-04:37:41
This week, it's, oh right, we bought a new car.


04:37:41-04:37:47
I keep forgetting about this development because I've been so immersed in trying to finish this book that I haven't seen the car in days.


04:37:47-04:37:49
Out of sight, out of mind.


04:37:49-04:37:56
I get to walk through much of the world with childlike wonder, experiencing moments as if for the first time.


04:37:56-04:38:03
I lose things, yes, but this gives me the joy of finding them again and appreciating what I thought I'd lost.


04:38:03-04:38:08
After all, we often don't realize how much something means to us until we can't find it.


04:38:08-04:38:18
My most prized possession at the moment is a button I got from a wonderful woman who works in the Disability Services Office at the University of Washington, Eau Claire.


04:38:18-04:38:31
It reads simply, "We all belong here." While I can't recall the name of the woman who gave it to me, I can vividly conjure the way she smiled at my enthusiasm as I dug through the basket of buttons as if I'd found buried treasure.


04:38:31-04:38:41
I remember the courage the button gave me when I was on stage, giving my first in-person keynote in years when I was scared that I would mess up because of my memory difficulties.


04:38:41-04:38:45
My button assured me I'd still belong if I did.


04:38:45-04:38:46
We all belong here.


04:38:46-04:38:58
I can't remember for the life of me where I put that button after returning home, but when I find it again, I plan to frame and hang it in a shadow box, if by then I remember I had wanted to.


04:38:58-04:39:02
This is how I move through life, and I kind of love it.


04:39:02-04:39:07
The way I kind of love how when I take my contacts out at night, all the lights look like snowflakes.


04:39:07-04:39:18
Especially now that learning these tools gave me more choice, I can move through life with a greater ability to choose what to remember and what to let myself forget.


04:39:18-04:39:32
My memory challenges have strengthened my faith, even in things I can't see or feel or touch, because so much in my life is a dream or a memory, fuzzy at the seams, brought into focus only by my imagination.


04:39:32-04:39:39
The picture that comes to mind of the earrings I lost as a kid is no clearer to me than the picture of the future I hope for.


04:39:39-04:39:43
Neither are here, but I believe in them both.


04:39:43-04:39:44
I trust in their existence.


04:39:44-04:39:53
One of my favorite things about my forgetfulness is that when I get excited about a new project, I forget how hard the last one was.


04:39:53-04:40:00
I forget about the downsides and potential negative consequences, the long hours and the sitting in my car crying.


04:40:00-04:40:05
There's a cost to this forgetfulness, as I learned and will share at the end of this book.


04:40:05-04:40:16
But the reason I dream so big and do big things is because I forget about the constraints of reality for a bit and I go after what I want without considering what might get in the way.


04:40:16-04:40:18
Many of us do the same.


04:40:18-04:40:19
It's one of our strengths.


04:40:19-04:40:25
Chapter nine, how to feel.


04:40:26-04:40:29
Your emotions make you human.


04:40:29-04:40:31
Even the unpleasant ones have a purpose.


04:40:31-04:40:34
Sabaa Tahir, a torch against the night.


04:40:34-04:40:40
Heads up, this chapter discusses drowning, panic attacks, and suicide.


04:40:40-04:40:43
Flooded.


04:40:43-04:40:49
When I was five, my dad took me out to sea on a boogie board that I'd insisted I was big enough to learn how to ride.


04:40:49-04:40:56
I remember the excitement of it, my dad tugging me along on that board, holding it in place as a wave began to swell behind us.


04:40:56-04:40:59
Then just as it crested, he let go.


04:40:59-04:41:02
I did not know this was part of the ride.


04:41:02-04:41:10
The wave pulled me under, the strap connecting me to my board came loose from my wrist and I plunged, tumbling, disoriented.


04:41:10-04:41:13
I knew I should swim, but not which way to go.


04:41:13-04:41:16
I knew it wasn't safe to breathe, but I needed to.


04:41:16-04:41:23
I inhaled, panicked and overwhelmed, drowning, until the wave had run its course and I landed coughing back on the shore.


04:41:23-04:41:28
My dad was unaware of the experience I'd had underwater.


04:41:28-04:41:30
He didn't know how terrified I'd been, how desperate.


04:41:30-04:41:31
Fun, huh?


04:41:31-04:41:32
Let's do it again.


04:41:32-04:41:36
This is how emotions often feel to me.


04:41:36-04:41:40
There's a wave behind me building and just as it crests, I am let go.


04:41:40-04:41:46
I lose my tether and I'm pulled under with an intensity that doesn't make sense to those around me.


04:41:46-04:41:54
Overcome, I frequently do and say things that make the situation worse, the equivalent of swallowing water because I'm desperate for air.


04:41:54-04:41:59
Often, I'm unaware of the rising tide of my emotions.


04:41:59-04:42:02
When I am, I'm often talked out of my concerns.


04:42:02-04:42:05
To everyone else, it's just a day at the beach.


04:42:05-04:42:09
I remember so many moments when I tried to tell them about the warning signs.


04:42:09-04:42:12
I am eight years old and I am upset about my socks.


04:42:12-04:42:17
The seam is in the wrong place over my toes and I can feel it with every step I take.


04:42:17-04:42:19
I ask if we can stop so I can fix them.


04:42:19-04:42:21
No, you're fine, let's go.


04:42:21-04:42:25
At school, someone teases me and I tell them my feelings are hurt.


04:42:25-04:42:27
They tell me I'm too sensitive.


04:42:27-04:42:32
When a teacher sees me cry, they tell me to go wash my face, so I do.


04:42:32-04:42:36
I spend the day trying to do what I am expected to do and feel what I'm expected to feel.


04:42:36-04:42:39
I fall apart when I get home.


04:42:39-04:42:41
I drop my toy and it breaks.


04:42:41-04:42:42
Stop crying.


04:42:42-04:42:44
I can't, I'm underwater.


04:42:44-04:42:45
Dad is angry.


04:42:45-04:42:49
Stop crying or I'll give you something to cry about.


04:42:49-04:42:50
More waves.


04:42:50-04:42:51
I can't breathe.


04:42:51-04:42:55
I run to my room and hide under the blankets, my face in a pillow.


04:42:55-04:42:57
This makes me feel better.


04:42:57-04:42:58
The wave sweeps me back to shore.


04:42:58-04:43:02
Later at dinner, my sister makes me laugh.


04:43:02-04:43:03
I make her laugh.


04:43:03-04:43:07
We both laugh uncontrollably until we are ordered to stop.


04:43:07-04:43:08
I can't stop.


04:43:08-04:43:10
I'm too swept up.


04:43:10-04:43:11
I am sent away from the table.


04:43:11-04:43:17
Throughout the day, I hear that I am wrong about what I'm feeling or that I shouldn't be feeling it.


04:43:17-04:43:18
It's nothing.


04:43:18-04:43:19
Don't be scared.


04:43:19-04:43:20
Calm down.


04:43:20-04:43:22
Don't cry.


04:43:22-04:43:24
At 10, I still cry in class.


04:43:24-04:43:26
The messages shift.


04:43:26-04:43:27
You're too old for this.


04:43:27-04:43:29
Stop being so dramatic.


04:43:29-04:43:30
It's not that big a deal.


04:43:30-04:43:36
At 12, after my mom is in a car accident, I have to go to a new school.


04:43:36-04:43:39
I feel guilt and fear and anger.


04:43:39-04:43:43
Change after change becomes too much and one day I yell.


04:43:43-04:43:47
My aunt scolds me, "You're a bad daughter." I shouldn't be angry with my mom.


04:43:47-04:43:49
It's not her fault.


04:43:49-04:43:50
I stop speaking up.


04:43:50-04:43:53
My new school sells cinnamon rolls at break.


04:43:53-04:43:56
I eat them every day.


04:43:56-04:43:58
The sweetness is like a life preserver.


04:43:58-04:44:01
I'm trying to keep my head above water.


04:44:01-04:44:03
At assembly, I reach my hand for the boy next to me.


04:44:03-04:44:07
I'm not allowed to be sad, so I seek out what makes me happy.


04:44:07-04:44:09
Not too happy though.


04:44:09-04:44:12
Happy enough so that I'm not a problem.


04:44:12-04:44:14
Not so happy that I become one.


04:44:14-04:44:19
I'm an adult now, but the messages I grew up with ring through my head.


04:44:19-04:44:20
Don't be scared.


04:44:20-04:44:21
Calm down.


04:44:21-04:44:22
Don't cry.


04:44:22-04:44:23
It's not that big a deal.


04:44:23-04:44:27
If I feel hurt, I explain to myself why I shouldn't.


04:44:27-04:44:31
If I feel lonely, I explain to myself why that's wrong.


04:44:31-04:44:34
I talk myself out of my feelings instinctively the moment they come up.


04:44:34-04:44:36
I play whack-a-mole.


04:44:36-04:44:37
That one's inconvenient.


04:44:37-04:44:38
Whack.


04:44:38-04:44:39
That one's wrong.


04:44:39-04:44:40
Whack.


04:44:40-04:44:40
Push past it.


04:44:40-04:44:41
Work through it.


04:44:41-04:44:46
When someone gives me permission to feel something specific, aren't you excited?


04:44:46-04:44:48
You must be so sad.


04:44:48-04:44:49
I learned to agree.


04:44:49-04:44:53
I had my first panic attack at age 32.


04:44:53-04:44:59
And it feels as if I'm unraveling, as though someone found a loose end in the sweater that is my knitted life and pulled.


04:44:59-04:45:03
I knit myself back together and keep on.


04:45:03-04:45:11
Things will be easier when I get through this day, this week, this month, this project, this miscarriage, this divorce, this pandemic, this.


04:45:11-04:45:15
After my mom dies, the waves come too quickly for me to recover.


04:45:15-04:45:20
They create a riptide that pulls me far from shore, farther than I have ever been.


04:45:20-04:45:24
No one wants to get too close because in my flailing, I might pull them under.


04:45:24-04:45:31
People don't think straight when they're drowning, and they don't think straight when they're flooded with emotions either.


04:45:31-04:45:35
I drown for almost a year, clutching at life preservers, never finding shore.


04:45:37-04:45:40
One day, I stop believing I ever will.


04:45:40-04:45:48
I've promised myself things will get easier when, and it hasn't gotten easier when so many times that I've stopped believing it ever can.


04:45:48-04:45:52
If there is no shore, it's easier to just swim down.


04:45:52-04:45:54
I can't do this forever.


04:45:54-04:45:55
I'm so tired.


04:45:55-04:46:04
I realize I can stop, that my fight to find shore can be over, not eventually or maybe or someday but now.


04:46:05-04:46:13
It is shocking how quickly it happens, how practical and even easy the solution suddenly seems compared to continuing on.


04:46:13-04:46:21
In the space of a few minutes, I go from a terrifying plunge into despair to a plan, and with it, relief.


04:46:21-04:46:27
The mental health expert part of me recognizes the danger and jumps in like an unwelcome lifeguard.


04:46:27-04:46:34
This is the part of me that has training and has helped others through similar situations before.


04:46:34-04:46:39
It knows what to do for the part of me that has stopped struggling and is now dangerously peaceful and calm.


04:46:39-04:46:41
Create time and distance.


04:46:41-04:46:51
I get myself away from anything I see as a great solution to my problems and call people who can help me write out this unexpected tidal wave.


04:46:51-04:46:57
Suicide hotlines, a friend a few hours away, and an ex who lives close enough to intervene if needed.


04:46:57-04:47:03
He has been trying to distance himself from me emotionally, but that night, he comes over and hugs me hard.


04:47:03-04:47:03
Don't go.


04:47:03-04:47:07
By then, it feels bizarre that he is saying these words.


04:47:07-04:47:08
No, of course I won't.


04:47:08-04:47:11
I feel guilty for worrying him.


04:47:11-04:47:13
There's no danger anymore.


04:47:13-04:47:15
I have no desire to hurt myself.


04:47:15-04:47:18
But it matters that he says it.


04:47:18-04:47:20
It tells me he cares.


04:47:20-04:47:24
And so I admit to my struggle and ask for help.


04:47:24-04:47:27
I cry and explain how exhausted I am.


04:47:27-04:47:29
I explain the endless ocean of pain.


04:47:30-04:47:33
I asked a therapist once how to get to land.


04:47:33-04:47:35
She told me I was good at the ocean.


04:47:35-04:47:38
My next therapist tells me something different.


04:47:38-04:47:49
The better I can get at my feelings, feeling the swell, riding the wave, even letting myself be pulled under sometimes, the more easily I will be able to feel the land beneath my feet.


04:47:49-04:47:54
My then ex that night tells me something different still.


04:47:54-04:47:59
When I tell him I've stopped believing this ocean of pain will ever end, he says simply, it doesn't.


04:47:59-04:48:00
There will always be pain.


04:48:00-04:48:05
All of them are right, and none of them entirely.


04:48:05-04:48:07
To understand why, let's start with the science.


04:48:07-04:48:14
What I learned, ADHD brains don't regulate emotions very well.


04:48:14-04:48:21
Not only do they hit us harder and last longer, we also tend to be more reactive to them than someone who is neurotypical.


04:48:21-04:48:27
This intensity and reactivity have an enormous impact on how we interact with the world, as well as how the world reacts to us.


04:48:29-04:48:40
[[emotional regulation#Emotional Dysregulation|Emotion dysregulation]], noun, an impaired ability to control your emotional response, which can lead to extreme and/or disproportionate reactions that are not necessarily appropriate to the situation.


04:48:40-04:48:46
Emotion dysregulation and emotional dysregulation are often used interchangeably, but refer to the same thing.


04:48:46-04:49:00
Unfortunately, like most people with ADHD and many doctors who treat ADHD, ==I didn't know that emotion dysregulation was part of the deal because the DSM doesn't list it as part of the diagnostic criteria for ADHD.==


04:49:00-04:49:13
While the emotional component of ADHD has been observed as long as ADHD has, the simple truth is that emotions are harder to measure in a lab than in attention, impulsivity, and hyperactivity.


04:49:13-04:49:18
So when the diagnostic criteria were formed in the DSM-2, they were based on that research.


04:49:18-04:49:20
Emotions were left out.


04:49:20-04:49:29
This is the reason people with ADHD are often misdiagnosed with mood disorders, and why we don't get the support we need even once we're diagnosed correctly.


04:49:29-04:49:33
We don't know the extent to which our struggle with emotions isn't normal.


04:49:33-04:49:40
Emotions hit us harder and faster and take us under in ways most medical providers, teachers, and loved ones don't understand.


04:49:40-04:49:44
Why emotion regulation is hard for ADHD brains.


04:49:44-04:49:54
Emotion regulation, the ability to exert control over one's emotional state, is what allows us to calm down and make good choices when something gets us worked up.


04:49:54-04:50:05
This sounds simple, but emotion regulation relies on skills the ADHD brain tends to have trouble with, such as inhibition, as in not impulsively reacting to an emotion.


04:50:05-04:50:15
According to Dr. Russell Barkley, the more generally impulsive we are, and impulsivity is in the DSM criteria for ADHD, the more emotionally impulsive we are too.


04:50:15-04:50:21
Self-soothing, which is the ability to calm and comfort ourselves after experiencing an emotion.


04:50:21-04:50:28
Most of us do have ways to self-soothe, but they're not always healthy, and our healthy ones aren't always socially acceptable.


04:50:28-04:50:35
Refocusing our attention, which is the exact problem for which our disorder is named, fantastic, moving on.


04:50:35-04:50:40
Responding to our emotions in a way that aligns with our goals for the situation.


04:50:40-04:50:44
This assumes, of course, that we know what our goals even are.


04:50:44-04:50:53
To make matters even more complicated, emotion regulation is a cool executive function, and emotions activate our hot executive function system.


04:50:53-04:50:59
Once we're emotionally in the red, we can no longer executive function our way out.


04:50:59-04:51:05
If we don't have automatic go-tos to de-escalate a situation, there is a tipping point at which we are not going to be able to.


04:51:05-04:51:07
This is true for everyone.


04:51:07-04:51:15
Cognitive ability declines as emotions rise, and that includes the cognitive ability required to regulate our emotions.


04:51:15-04:51:23
But for those of us with brains that impulsively react to an emotion, there are times we don't have any window to recognize our rising emotions and even try to regulate them.


04:51:23-04:51:26
We skip past yellow and go straight from green to red.


04:51:26-04:51:31
Emotion dysregulation gets us in trouble.


04:51:31-04:51:38
As kids, [[emotional regulation#Emotional Dysregulation|emotion dysregulation]] can get us labels like overly sensitive, immature, or sadly, a brat.


04:51:38-04:51:47
Our emotional meltdowns are sometimes misinterpreted as tantrums, and we're often shamed and even punished for having emotions our brains just don't know how to manage.


04:51:47-04:52:00
Dr. Barclay contends that out of all the challenges adults with ADHD have in the workplace, arriving late, being disorganized, struggling with focus, emotion dysregulation is the one that gets us fired.


04:52:00-04:52:05
We're quick to get emotionally flooded and to express raw emotions, even when it's not appropriate for the situation.


04:52:05-04:52:16
To make matters worse, when cycling from one mistake, obstacle, deadline, or oversight to the next, we frequently don't have the time we need to return to our emotional baseline.


04:52:16-04:52:22
And the words that come out of our mouths when we're frustrated or furious are tougher to forgive than a late clock in or a messy desk.


04:52:22-04:52:27
But ADHDers experience emotion dysregulation with other emotions too.


04:52:27-04:52:33
Sadness, fear, desire, or rejection, even perceived rejection can be unbearably intense.


04:52:33-04:52:36
More on rejection sensitivity in how to people.


04:52:37-04:52:43
Positive emotions such as excitement, joy, humor, and even love can spiral out of control too.


04:52:43-04:52:48
As anyone who's watched The Notebook can attest, too much of a good thing can lead to some questionable decisions.


04:52:48-04:52:57
Because our difficulties with emotion dysregulation create very real problems in our lives, we might come to believe that our emotions themselves are the problem.


04:52:57-04:53:00
Therefore, we do what we can to avoid them.


04:53:00-04:53:04
We try not to feel.


04:53:04-04:53:13
After years of struggling with the fallout from our too big, too loud, too unregulated feelings, it's common for us to go to great lengths to avoid and or suppress our emotions.


04:53:13-04:53:18
We avoid situations that might bring up difficult emotions.


04:53:18-04:53:20
We distract ourselves from them.


04:53:20-04:53:23
We reframe or intellectualize our emotions.


04:53:23-04:53:29
We try to make the situation more tolerable, often with food or substances such as alcohol or drugs.


04:53:29-04:53:31
We mask our feelings and pretend we're fine.


04:53:33-04:53:43
To be clear, there's nothing wrong with occasionally distracting ourselves from uncomfortable, inconvenient, or outsized emotions, or choosing situations that are less likely to bring them up.


04:53:43-04:53:47
Using these strategies mindfully can be helpful for managing our emotions on a day to day basis.


04:53:47-04:53:51
How we regulate our emotions.


04:53:51-04:53:58
According to Dr. James Gross's process model for [[emotional regulation|emotion regulation]], there are five strategies for managing our emotions.


04:53:58-04:54:01
One, situation selection.


04:54:01-04:54:06
The ability to choose situations that are less likely to lead to emotion regulation challenges.


04:54:06-04:54:12
I could have my birthday party at the club, but I think I'd feel less overwhelmed if we go to a restaurant instead.


04:54:12-04:54:15
Two, situation modification.


04:54:15-04:54:20
The ability to change or modify a situation known to trigger emotion dysregulation.


04:54:20-04:54:25
I'll change seats so I don't sit next to my friend during bio lecture.


04:54:25-04:54:26
I always end up giggling when I do.


04:54:26-04:54:31
Three, cognitive change or reappraisal.


04:54:31-04:54:36
The ability to change the way we view a situation or our emotional reaction to it.


04:54:36-04:54:41
We can also change how we think about how we manage our emotions in our particular situation.


04:54:41-04:54:41
You know what?


04:54:41-04:54:43
It's actually better that I got fired from that job.


04:54:43-04:54:45
I didn't like it anyway.


04:54:45-04:54:45
This is fine.


04:54:45-04:54:49
Four, attention deployment.


04:54:49-04:54:53
The ability to turn our attention away from the source of hard to regulate emotions.


04:54:53-04:54:55
Ugh, my house is such a mess.


04:54:55-04:54:57
Crap, feelings.


04:54:57-04:54:57
Where's the remote?


04:54:59-04:55:04
Five, response modulation, the attempt to alter our emotional response.


04:55:04-04:55:05
Performance anxiety, deep breaths.


04:55:05-04:55:07
Wait, how does breathing work?


04:55:07-04:55:08
All right, in for four.


04:55:08-04:55:14
Unfortunately, the way and the extent to which we use these tools isn't always healthy for us.


04:55:14-04:55:29
Even adaptive coping strategies can become maladaptive, as is the case when an occasional I'm just gonna escape into this book for a bit turns into I have spent my entire year in a book, or more accurately, 600 books.


04:55:29-04:55:43
Research finds that cognitive avoidance, a set of coping mechanisms in which a person uses cognitive techniques like avoidance, suppression, or rumination to escape mental and emotional distress, is particularly common among those with ADHD.


04:55:43-04:55:50
When mindful coping crosses the line into unhealthy avoidance, we can end up avoiding or repressing emotions we need to face.


04:55:50-04:55:55
Chronically avoiding emotions is problematic.


04:55:56-04:56:02
Emotions can't be avoided or suppressed forever, and ignoring them comes with many negative consequences.


04:56:02-04:56:08
If our fear of confrontation prevents us from asking for a promotion, we may miss a chance to advance in our career.


04:56:08-04:56:13
If we are afraid to be lonely, we might stay too long in an unhealthy relationship.


04:56:13-04:56:19
Chronically avoiding our emotions leaves us ill-equipped to handle them when we finally can't run from them anymore.


04:56:19-04:56:23
Suppressed feelings, don't stay suppressed.


04:56:23-04:56:30
While some emotions can naturally come and go, the ones we push down on a regular basis don't phase out of existence.


04:56:30-04:56:35
Often they intensify, especially if the situation that initially caused them isn't resolved.


04:56:35-04:56:45
Most parents of ADHD children have observed the phenomenon called after school restraint collapse, where their child comes home from school and melts down.


04:56:45-04:56:50
After a long day of trying to hide their symptoms and stifle their emotions, they all erupt to the surface.


04:56:51-04:56:57
In adults, psychological symptoms like irritability, mood swings, or even panic attacks can occur.


04:56:57-04:57:06
Physical symptoms like sleeplessness, chronic pain, gastrointestinal problems, and even sexual dysfunction can result when you persistently suppress intense emotions.


04:57:06-04:57:17
Not only can avoiding and suppressing our emotions cause damage to our lives, health, and mental wellness, it causes us to miss out on what our feelings are trying to tell us.


04:57:17-04:57:19
Feelings give us information we need.


04:57:19-04:57:25
Our emotions are always communicating with us, sending us signals about what works for us and what doesn't.


04:57:25-04:57:28
Think of your emotions like a smoke detector.


04:57:28-04:57:31
Sure, it might start blaring when there is not in fact a fire.


04:57:31-04:57:34
This alarm system can sometimes be hypersensitive.


04:57:34-04:57:40
But if there isn't a fire, it is at least sensing some smoke, and that's often worth investigating.


04:57:40-04:57:55
Unfortunately, many people with ADHD experience psychological symptoms that create distance between them and their feelings, including dissociation, the feeling of being disconnected or detached from your surroundings, or even from yourself.


04:57:55-04:58:01
And hedonia, the impaired ability to experience pleasure, even when doing something you usually enjoy.


04:58:01-04:58:08
Alexithymia, the inability or impaired ability to recognize and describe your feelings.


04:58:08-04:58:10
We might know something's wrong, but not what.


04:58:10-04:58:16
Avoiding our emotions doesn't necessarily cause these symptoms, but it can.


04:58:16-04:58:19
No matter the cause, the treatment is the same.


04:58:19-04:58:22
Learn to feel your feels.


04:58:22-04:58:29
Not only does feeling our emotions help us process them, but being able to accurately identify our emotions is critical to being able to manage them effectively.


04:58:29-04:58:34
As psychiatrist Dr. Dan Siegel puts it, you've got to name it to tame it.


04:58:34-04:58:44
Labeling the emotion can diminish the response of the amygdala and limbic system, decreasing our emotional reactivity to negative emotional experiences.


04:58:44-04:58:49
And understanding what we're feeling can help us identify which of our needs may not be getting met.


04:58:49-04:58:52
We have them, yes, all humans do.


04:58:52-04:59:02
Many of us weren't taught this stuff, so it can be worth taking some time to learn about emotions, what they are, what they're called, what purpose each one serves.


04:59:02-04:59:13
At howtoadhdbook.com, I include a link to an emotion tracker worksheet, as well as a great resource that has reference cards for common human needs and the feelings we experience when they aren't met.


04:59:14-04:59:17
I also include a resource to help with finding a therapist.


04:59:17-04:59:26
Therapists can help us explore and learn about our emotions, as well as establish healthier ways for us to cope with big feelings, making it safer for us to acknowledge them.


04:59:26-04:59:30
This is especially important for those who have a history of abuse or trauma.


04:59:30-04:59:38
Exploring our emotions opens feelings doors that were closed for a reason, and can bring up feelings we don't yet have the ability to cope with in a healthy way.


04:59:38-04:59:43
Jerrica T, 31, Virginia.


04:59:43-04:59:49
It's been difficult figuring out how to deal with my emotions in a healthy way, especially when everyone around me puts me down for having them.


04:59:49-04:59:53
Heinrich M, 28, Germany.


04:59:53-04:59:56
I have a hard time even realizing what I'm feeling.


04:59:56-05:00:01
During the analysis, I fall into a pit where executive function crawls to a halt.


05:00:01-05:00:07
This conditioned me to try to fence my emotions off, but when they inevitably break through, it's even harder to control.


05:00:07-05:00:11
Ellie M, 25, Colorado.


05:00:11-05:00:15
When experiencing intense emotions, I might appear catatonic.


05:00:15-05:00:20
Something major could be happening, and I'm either super calm or don't have any reaction at all.


05:00:20-05:00:23
Sometimes it makes me look like I don't care, but it's the opposite.


05:00:23-05:00:28
I care so much that I can only process the major emotion one small piece at a time.


05:00:28-05:00:32
JR, 38, Canada.


05:00:32-05:00:39
I've learned recently that I haven't been dealing with my emotions, just hiding them, bottling them up, boys don't cry and all that.


05:00:39-05:00:41
That isn't dealing with it.


05:00:41-05:00:45
Then I found out last year I have ADHD, and now I have answers.


05:00:45-05:00:49
I'm trying to allow my feelings to come out, because having big feelings is just how I am.


05:00:49-05:00:52
The toolbox.


05:00:52-05:00:56
When you have big emotions, it's especially important to learn how to cope with them.


05:00:56-05:00:59
Thankfully, there are many ways to do that.


05:00:59-05:01:01
I've collected some of my favorites here.


05:01:01-05:01:03
If these tools seem a bit daunting, don't worry.


05:01:03-05:01:09
According to research, even the act of noticing our emotions without judgment can make them easier to handle.


05:01:09-05:01:13
One, label your emotions.


05:01:13-05:01:17
Identifying your emotions is key to being able to manage them effectively.


05:01:17-05:01:22
This can be easier said than done for those of us who can't always tell what we're feeling.


05:01:22-05:01:26
Many of us have a hard time even telling the difference between our feelings and our thoughts.


05:01:26-05:01:29
Feelings are physical or emotional experiences.


05:01:29-05:01:33
Thoughts are mental cognitions, our ideas, opinions, and beliefs.


05:01:33-05:01:36
One word journaling can help you distinguish one from the other.


05:01:36-05:01:39
Thoughts take many words to describe.


05:01:39-05:01:41
I feel like everyone hates me.


05:01:41-05:01:44
Feelings, on the other hand, only require one, sad.


05:01:44-05:01:47
Here are some ADHD friendly ways to make it easier.


05:01:47-05:01:51
Label the intensity.


05:01:51-05:01:55
We can often tell how intense an emotion is before we can tell what the emotion is.


05:01:55-05:01:56
There are different ways to do this.


05:01:56-05:02:00
You can assign a color, such as green, yellow, or red.


05:02:00-05:02:05
Or use a one to ten rating scale, where one is the least intense and ten is the most.


05:02:05-05:02:10
Even if you can't tell what you're feeling yet, identifying its intensity can let you know if it's worth addressing.


05:02:10-05:02:12
If it's a ten, probably.


05:02:12-05:02:14
And if it's a good idea to do that right now.


05:02:14-05:02:16
If it's still a ten, probably not.


05:02:16-05:02:19
Use external prompts.


05:02:19-05:02:27
Feelings wheels, circular diagrams that help you identify your feelings, can help you put words to your emotional experiences.


05:02:27-05:02:29
So can paying attention to what your body is doing.


05:02:29-05:02:37
Daniel Jones of the ASPI World YouTube channel, who also has ADHD, points out that emotions are energy in motion.


05:02:37-05:02:39
What does the energy in your body make you want to do?


05:02:39-05:02:40
Laugh?


05:02:40-05:02:41
Cry?


05:02:41-05:02:42
Rock?


05:02:42-05:02:43
Throw rocks?


05:02:43-05:02:46
Create your own labeling system.


05:02:46-05:02:51
For some, describing their emotions isn't as easy as, say, pointing at a color.


05:02:51-05:02:55
I have a friend who communicates her feelings in terms of what kind of potato she feels like that day.


05:02:55-05:02:59
Look for the emotion behind the emotion.


05:02:59-05:03:05
Emotions that were less comfortable with having and/or expressing often quickly get masked by other emotions.


05:03:05-05:03:12
If you notice you constantly feel angry, there might be a different emotion behind it, such as hurt or even fear.


05:03:12-05:03:16
It's important to look for the first emotion that you experienced in a situation.


05:03:16-05:03:21
If you respond angrily when you actually felt scared, you probably won't get the results and safety you need.


05:03:21-05:03:24
We can do this with our thoughts too.


05:03:24-05:03:34
If a harsh thought comes up, especially one disguised as a feeling, such as, I feel like I'm going to fail and everyone is going to laugh at me, we can look for the emotion behind it.


05:03:34-05:03:38
In this case, it might be fear, specifically fear of humiliation.


05:03:38-05:03:42
Gen D, 29, Connecticut.


05:03:42-05:03:46
Understanding and properly labeling what I'm feeling is hugely important.


05:03:46-05:03:51
Often my body signal feels like the equivalent of a check engine light with no detail.


05:03:51-05:03:54
It's tough to process emotions if you don't know what they are in the first place.


05:03:54-05:03:58
Anonymous, 20s, USA.


05:03:58-05:04:04
I try to visualize big emotions, usually as a big rush of air or energy or a big wave.


05:04:04-05:04:13
I'm either something sturdy, like a cliff or a tree, or something very agile, like a bird, and I just wait it out for a moment or two.


05:04:13-05:04:15
Or I'm a lightning rod, just guiding it all into the ground.


05:04:15-05:04:19
Emily S, 24, Sweden.


05:04:19-05:04:25
If I get severe anxiety at home, I say out loud, my name is Name.


05:04:25-05:04:27
I'm at home in City.


05:04:27-05:04:30
I'm anxious about thing that set it off.


05:04:30-05:04:33
And it's okay to feel bad, this won't last forever.


05:04:33-05:04:36
It keeps me from spiraling or suppressing the thoughts.


05:04:36-05:04:38
Then they continue all day anyways.


05:04:38-05:04:43
Two, make space for your emotions.


05:04:43-05:04:49
We may have been told for years, even decades, that our emotions are wrong or that we're not supposed to feel a certain way.


05:04:49-05:04:56
Making space for our emotions can combat these beliefs, help us process our emotions, and validate how we are feeling.


05:04:57-05:05:03
It also communicates to yourself and others that it's okay to have emotions, so we can acknowledge them more easily when they come up.


05:05:03-05:05:06
Wait before taking action.


05:05:06-05:05:15
Immediately fixing or even reframing the situation that made you feel an emotion can reinforce the idea that our emotions aren't allowed to exist.


05:05:15-05:05:21
It also increases the likelihood that we'll take a less helpful action because we haven't gotten time to process and understand anything yet.


05:05:21-05:05:27
While putting on the brakes when emotions are running high is incredibly difficult, we can learn skills that can help.


05:05:27-05:05:32
Some resources can be found on my website, howtoadhdbook.com.


05:05:32-05:05:39
We can also ask others to give us time to think or take away our means of communicating, for example, our phones when we're running hot.


05:05:39-05:05:42
Sit with your feelings.


05:05:42-05:05:46
As my therapist taught me, even our biggest feelings can't last forever.


05:05:46-05:05:51
In most cases, the body can only sustain an intense emotion for about 20 minutes.


05:05:51-05:05:58
It can often be less emotionally distressing to sit with your feelings and watch them fade than to dodge them and have them hang around waiting for you to notice them.


05:05:58-05:06:01
Take time to explore your emotions.


05:06:01-05:06:11
This can mean drawing, painting, or talking them out with a journal or a third party who may be able to help us process and figure out what, if anything, we wanna do next.


05:06:11-05:06:14
Feelings are in their rawest form when they first come up.


05:06:14-05:06:20
While this is not the best time to communicate them, it can be a great time to write them down.


05:06:20-05:06:26
Exploring your feelings on your own while you're feeling them can make it easier to communicate them when you're in a better place to effectively do so.


05:06:26-05:06:30
Rowan N, 31, Colorado.


05:06:30-05:06:36
I take time alone and write out my feelings, sometimes in a doc on the computer, sometimes by hand.


05:06:36-05:06:41
It helps me sort things into a semblance of order and figure out not only what I'm feeling, but why.


05:06:41-05:06:47
I have CPTSD, complex post-traumatic stress disorder, so the why isn't always obvious.


05:06:47-05:06:51
Sharon G, 34, Massachusetts.


05:06:51-05:06:57
I do my damnedest to avoid making any irrevocable decisions while in the grip of a torrent of emotions.


05:06:57-05:06:59
I do anything I can to give myself time.


05:06:59-05:07:02
Take a nap, listen to an audiobook, hide in the bathroom.


05:07:02-05:07:06
Juliana N, 24, Pennsylvania.


05:07:06-05:07:09
I let myself feel the emotions.


05:07:09-05:07:13
I held back for so many years, then would have breakdowns every few months.


05:07:13-05:07:16
So now I let myself feel whatever it is.


05:07:16-05:07:22
After about 10 to 15 minutes, I write them down or talk it out with someone, asking questions like, did I overreact?


05:07:22-05:07:25
If I did, what did I overreact to?


05:07:25-05:07:28
If I didn't, is it something I just couldn't control?


05:07:28-05:07:34
Analyzing my emotions and realizing that they're often reactions to something I can't control helps me gain more sympathy for myself.


05:07:34-05:07:37
Three, use your emotions.


05:07:37-05:07:43
In our attempt to suppress or avoid our emotions, it's easy to forget how useful they are.


05:07:43-05:07:45
Feelings exist for a reason.


05:07:45-05:07:51
They're indicators that we might need more of something, less of something, to keep going with something or to do something different.


05:07:51-05:07:55
Use them as motivational fuel.


05:07:55-05:08:02
ADHDers often push past challenges and take on projects that no one else would because of the passion driving us.


05:08:02-05:08:06
We can also use our strong emotions and passion to motivate others, which can help us be effective leaders.


05:08:06-05:08:11
Successful problem solving relies on emotion and motivation.


05:08:11-05:08:19
When extrinsic motivation is lacking, for example, we're not getting the results we want quickly enough, we can tap into our emotions to get the engine revved back up.


05:08:19-05:08:22
Use them as a compass.


05:08:22-05:08:26
Sometimes our gut knows something is off before our brain does.


05:08:26-05:08:31
If something comes up on your emotional radar is danger, it might be worth checking out.


05:08:31-05:08:36
You might not be in actual danger, but your brain might be picking up on something that reminds it of something unsafe.


05:08:36-05:08:43
Our emotions can also let us know if our actions are or aren't in line with our values.


05:08:43-05:08:47
They can hint at whether something is being received well by another person.


05:08:47-05:08:49
They can indicate whether our needs are being met.


05:08:49-05:08:52
They point to where our yes boundaries and no boundaries are.


05:08:52-05:08:59
Paying attention to your emotions can help you navigate a situation more effectively and figure out if you're on the right track.


05:08:59-05:09:01
Enjoy them.


05:09:01-05:09:05
Some people, maybe you, enjoy experiencing emotions.


05:09:05-05:09:11
There's a reason we go to the movies, ride roller coasters, listen to sad songs, or fall in love.


05:09:11-05:09:13
We want to feel.


05:09:13-05:09:16
Feeling deeply reminds us we're alive.


05:09:16-05:09:22
Research has found that mindfulness, being fully present makes us happier, even when what we're experiencing is negative.


05:09:22-05:09:26
Foster connection.


05:09:26-05:09:31
Expressing what you think can be divisive, but expressing what you feel is typically connecting.


05:09:31-05:09:33
Pixar had it right.


05:09:33-05:09:36
Expressing sadness brings people together.


05:09:36-05:09:41
Communicating how you feel can help you bond with the people you love, mend rifts, and find common ground.


05:09:41-05:09:45
Feelings can also help us connect with the world.


05:09:45-05:09:52
Communication, whether it's personal, artistic, or even scientific, moves people more when there's genuine emotion behind it.


05:09:52-05:09:58
People connect better with what you say if you make them laugh or if you make them feel.


05:09:58-05:10:03
I tried to communicate my experience by intellectualizing it, and it didn't work as well.


05:10:03-05:10:06
I had to go back and rewrite to let myself feel enough to connect with you.


05:10:06-05:10:07
Hi.


05:10:07-05:10:11
Tanya Kay, 55, Washington.


05:10:11-05:10:14
I do use my emotions to connect with some people.


05:10:14-05:10:21
Excitement, exuberance, delight are of course really good ways of connecting with people, although I've met some who are turned off by these.


05:10:21-05:10:27
But sometimes frustration and anger can connect us with people who are also experiencing these things.


05:10:27-05:10:33
When something in the news upsets me, hearing someone else express frustration helps me feel less alone and hopeless.


05:10:33-05:10:37
Sam G, 28, France.


05:10:37-05:10:39
Love is a great motivational fuel.


05:10:39-05:10:45
If I don't wanna do something but it'll make life better for someone I love, I'm more likely to do it.


05:10:45-05:10:48
This works well to motivate myself to do chores or cooking.


05:10:48-05:10:52
I do them better if it's for my cat or for my partner than if I do them for myself.


05:10:52-05:10:56
Emily Z, 32, New Jersey.


05:10:56-05:11:08
I used to ignore my gut feelings, but now I'll take a moment after someone asks me to do something to see if there's any physical resistance, like upset stomach or tight chest when I think about doing the thing.


05:11:08-05:11:16
Ideally, I do this check before I agree to do something, but if the sensation is strong enough after I've said yes, I'll go back to the person afterward and tell them on second thought.


05:11:16-05:11:20
Four, find your emotional balance.


05:11:20-05:11:30
While there's nothing wrong with having emotions, being emotionally flooded can be deeply uncomfortable and diminish our choice in how to respond, often resulting in actions we regret.


05:11:30-05:11:37
Emotions might feel as if they have an on/off switch, especially if we've made it a habit to ignore or suppress them until they're screaming at us.


05:11:37-05:11:41
In actuality, emotions are more like a mixing board.


05:11:41-05:11:45
While we can't directly control our emotions, there are many things we can do to impact them.


05:11:45-05:11:49
Our thoughts, behavior, and environment all play a role.


05:11:49-05:11:52
We can adjust the emotional volume by being proactive about these factors.


05:11:52-05:11:56
Practice meditation.


05:11:56-05:11:59
Each stressor we experience increases our adrenaline level.


05:11:59-05:12:12
If our adrenaline continues to spike and doesn't get the chance to come back down, which is common with our hectic ADHD lives, at some point its level is high enough that even a small stressor can push us over our metaphorical edge.


05:12:12-05:12:20
Practicing meditation is a way to take regular breaks from the constant barrage of input and settle our emotional glitter, aka return to our emotional baseline.


05:12:20-05:12:24
So we're less likely to be pushed over the edge when stressors pop up.


05:12:24-05:12:28
The busier and more stressed we are, the more important this can become.


05:12:28-05:12:34
Seek support before and take breaks from emotionally difficult situations.


05:12:34-05:12:41
When possible, go into difficult situations with a plan, especially when you've worked through with a trusted friend or mental health professional.


05:12:41-05:12:43
And if you need to, step away.


05:12:43-05:12:48
While you don't want to avoid emotions chronically, that doesn't mean you have to constantly force yourself to feel them either.


05:12:48-05:12:52
Put your effort into things you can control.


05:12:52-05:13:01
One of my favorite ways to be okay when things are not okay for an extended period of time is to redirect my efforts to stuff I can directly control.


05:13:01-05:13:06
Putting emotional energy into stuff you cannot control is like putting your heart on a kite.


05:13:06-05:13:10
It might be fine on a sunny day, but if it's starting to look like tornado season, rain it in.


05:13:10-05:13:14
Nelly Yu, 36, United Kingdom.


05:13:14-05:13:20
When it feels like I'm falling in a deep black hole, then it's super important for me to feel present.


05:13:20-05:13:22
I make tea.


05:13:22-05:13:24
I can feel the warm mug in my hands.


05:13:24-05:13:25
I put cream on my face.


05:13:25-05:13:28
It smells nice and feels soft.


05:13:28-05:13:31
I walk my dog and I try to look at the pretty trees and buildings.


05:13:31-05:13:36
I might still feel awful, but it's kind of like grabbing onto the boat instead of falling in the ocean and drowning.


05:13:36-05:13:40
Nikki P, 22, Texas.


05:13:40-05:13:47
I take a deep breath in, hold my hands out, and as I exhale through my mouth, I lower my hands at the same time.


05:13:47-05:13:51
For some reason, having a physical and visual cue really chills me out.


05:13:51-05:13:55
Scott D, 35, Ohio.


05:13:55-05:13:58
I head to the gym or work out if I can.


05:13:58-05:14:00
I describe it as my reset button.


05:14:00-05:14:06
The combination of listening to music, focusing on the exercise, and the resulting endorphins settle me when the emotions surge or get overwhelming.


05:14:06-05:14:11
Samantha B, 37, Alabama.


05:14:11-05:14:13
I was prescribed a mood stabilizer to help.


05:14:13-05:14:16
I don't blow up as much as I used to, but it does still happen.


05:14:16-05:14:20
Megan C, 41, Vermont.


05:14:20-05:14:24
When I am overwhelmed, I don't do anything, I undo things.


05:14:24-05:14:27
I undo the expectation that the dishes will get done.


05:14:27-05:14:29
I cancel meetings and plans.


05:14:29-05:14:32
I identify what can come off the to-do list.


05:14:32-05:14:37
I decide what is too much for right now me, and I give it over to future me.


05:14:37-05:14:40
I have trust that future me will be able to put things into perspective.


05:14:40-05:14:42
So far, she has never let me down.


05:14:42-05:14:46
But not that feeling, right?


05:14:46-05:14:53
As I began to explore my emotions, I quickly realized my feelings about my feelings ranged widely.


05:14:53-05:14:58
Sadness, for example, was an emotion I felt I was allowed to have.


05:14:58-05:15:05
I was discouraged from experiencing it as a child, but as an actor, I was commended for expressing it.


05:15:05-05:15:09
Feeling vulnerable enough to cry on camera was a skill.


05:15:09-05:15:20
And the times I'd allowed myself to cry about my own feelings on camera when I was making videos for the channel, the community had been supportive and even grateful for making it seem okay for them to feel their feels too.


05:15:20-05:15:30
By the time I went to a therapist after my mom died, sadness was an emotion that, while not fun, felt at least socially acceptable.


05:15:30-05:15:34
Especially in this situation, people are supposed to be sad if someone close to them dies.


05:15:34-05:15:41
When my therapist asked me to imagine my grief in my body, I immediately could.


05:15:41-05:15:47
I imagined colored glass tubes that ran throughout my body and wrapped around my heart.


05:15:47-05:15:56
I explained to her that sometimes they lay dormant and benign, but then a reminder of my mom or someone's offhand comment lit up the whole system.


05:15:56-05:16:00
These grief and trauma tubes squeezed my heart and it hurt.


05:16:00-05:16:07
Later, when something triggered my grief, I imagined picking one of these tubes up and holding it above me.


05:16:07-05:16:10
I followed my therapist's advice about sitting with my feelings.


05:16:10-05:16:19
I held it and looked at it with curiosity until the intense sadness started to fade and the colorful glass tube burst into glass glitter.


05:16:19-05:16:23
It showered around me, disappearing like snowflakes when it hit the ground.


05:16:23-05:16:28
I finally understood how to sit with my sadness in a way that could make it fade.


05:16:28-05:16:37
When my therapist asked me to draw a picture of my anger, I drew it in my mind too, a harsh children's scrawl of red and black crayon.


05:16:37-05:16:41
She suggested I hang it on the wall and I imagined it on the wall.


05:16:41-05:16:45
Maybe you see how it feels inside you, she suggested.


05:16:45-05:16:46
I revolted.


05:16:46-05:16:48
Why would I want that?


05:16:48-05:16:50
Anger isn't okay, I told her.


05:16:50-05:16:52
Anger is bad.


05:16:52-05:16:54
Why would I let it in?


05:16:54-05:16:55
It's not good to feel anger.


05:16:55-05:17:00
When she asked why not, I patiently explained.


05:17:00-05:17:03
Anger makes people do bad things.


05:17:03-05:17:05
Anger is abusive.


05:17:05-05:17:11
My introduction to anger had come from my dad flying into a rage and spanking my siblings and me to make us behave.


05:17:11-05:17:15
She asked me what level of anger I was comfortable with.


05:17:15-05:17:16
I blinked.


05:17:17-05:17:24
She explained, if on a scale of 1 to 10, abusive anger is a 10, what level are you allowed to have?


05:17:24-05:17:32
Like if frustrated is a 4 and irritated is a frustration, I said I was allowed to be frustrated.


05:17:32-05:17:36
Later I recognized that was a bit of a cop out because of course I was allowed to be frustrated.


05:17:36-05:17:38
I was typically frustrated at myself.


05:17:38-05:17:45
So I chose a form of anger that I was allowed to feel about other people's actions, irritated.


05:17:45-05:17:48
I decided I was allowed to feel irritated, but even this was scary.


05:17:48-05:17:56
A few months later, as those of us with ADHD often do, I doubted whether I'd made any real strides in therapy.


05:17:56-05:18:01
My therapist told me, you've spent the last few sessions talking about how angry you are.


05:18:01-05:18:05
When you first came to me, you never let yourself express anger at all.


05:18:05-05:18:06
You're making progress.


05:18:06-05:18:13
It's progress to be able to feel and express your emotions.


05:18:13-05:18:15
Even the ones we've come to believe are bad.


05:18:15-05:18:22
Because if we can't do that, if we can't manage our emotions effectively, our emotions manage us.


05:18:22-05:18:26
If we don't know how to respond to them, we can only react to them.


05:18:26-05:18:34
Now when I'm angry, I'm able to acknowledge my anger and this allows me to communicate it and set boundaries based on it.


05:18:34-05:18:40
It doesn't build up and then explode out of me the way that it used to, confirming my fear that anger is bad.


05:18:40-05:18:41
It makes people hurt people.


05:18:42-05:18:46
I'm able to tame it, sit with it, and express it in healthier ways.


05:18:46-05:18:53
There might be less helpful ways to respond to feelings, but there aren't any bad feelings.


05:18:53-05:18:54
Feelings are signals.


05:18:54-05:19:00
It's important that we allow those signals to come through and know how to interpret what those signals mean.


05:19:00-05:19:08
We can maybe exclude a behavior like yelling from a repertoire, but we're more likely to be able to do so if we have a handle on the emotions that lead to it.


05:19:09-05:19:13
I still have ADHD and all the big feelings that come with it.


05:19:13-05:19:29
It was after learning this stuff that I went from intense distress to passive suicidal ideation to active suicidal ideation to a self-interrupted suicide attempt in the span of a few minutes, the speed of which was likely due to my issues with response inhibition and emotion dysregulation.


05:19:29-05:19:33
I think that's important to know.


05:19:33-05:19:41
Sometimes our emotions will swallow us regardless of how many coping skills we have because life is hard and ADHD makes it harder.


05:19:41-05:19:44
Grief happens, so does trauma.


05:19:44-05:19:47
And opening up these doors to big feelings can let in an absolute flood.


05:19:47-05:19:51
I'm grateful I opened them though.


05:19:51-05:20:01
Understanding the sheer vastness of the pain and that there is no magical point at which it goes away, made me decide not to add to the ocean I and others drown in.


05:20:01-05:20:07
It helped me realize I needed a boat, someplace safe and warm I could mentally escape to when I needed a break.


05:20:07-05:20:12
When the water levels are more manageable, my therapist was right.


05:20:12-05:20:18
Being better at recognizing and experiencing my emotions helped me feel the land beneath my feet.


05:20:18-05:20:23
Sometimes when I feel like I'm drowning, I'm really face down in a puddle and just need to stand up.


05:20:23-05:20:33
Learning to manage not outrun my emotions has helped me be still enough to be rescued too, not just by others, but by myself.


05:20:33-05:20:37
I'm a better life card for myself because I'm an easier person to rescue.


05:20:37-05:20:38
I'm easier to pull from the water.


05:20:38-05:20:50
Understanding my own emotions and having the ability to be present with them has made me better at sitting with others' emotions too, as well as stepping into support when their water levels rise.


05:20:50-05:20:56
Chapter 10, how to people.


05:20:56-05:21:01
The only way to have a friend is to be one.


05:21:01-05:21:02
Ralph Waldo Emerson.


05:21:02-05:21:05
I hate that quote.


05:21:05-05:21:09
I mean, Ralph Waldo Emerson isn't wrong.


05:21:09-05:21:12
Reciprocity is key to friendship.


05:21:12-05:21:14
Give and take, take and give.


05:21:14-05:21:17
Friend texts me, I text friend back.


05:21:17-05:21:19
Emerson makes it sound easy.


05:21:19-05:21:22
It's not, it's not, not for me.


05:21:22-05:21:29
As with many areas in my life, I tend to underdo or overdo it when it comes to social stuff.


05:21:29-05:21:33
And difficulty regulating my social efforts is often more of a problem.


05:21:33-05:21:38
Projects don't care if you ignore them for a month and then tackle them all in one day.


05:21:38-05:21:39
People do.


05:21:39-05:21:42
I've felt socially awkward as long as I can remember.


05:21:42-05:21:48
Eighth grader, hi, my name is Amanda, me.


05:21:48-05:21:49
Hi.


05:21:49-05:21:54
Long pause as 12 year old me at a new school waits for this yay new friend to continue.


05:21:54-05:21:58
Amanda, what's your name?


05:21:58-05:22:02
Me, right, Jessica, dies in shame.


05:22:02-05:22:09
Once I was medicated, I was more confident and outgoing, but just as bad at socially self-regulating.


05:22:09-05:22:13
Fellow sophomore, hi, I'm me.


05:22:13-05:22:15
Hi, here's my entire life story.


05:22:15-05:22:21
Like many people with ADHD, I didn't have many close friends growing up.


05:22:21-05:22:25
I generally understood the social rules, but I had trouble applying them.


05:22:26-05:22:34
I could pretend to belong for a little while, but soon I'd be too loud, too weird, too me, and the others would edge away.


05:22:34-05:22:39
Eventually, I learned to edge away, to escape to a place where I did belong.


05:22:39-05:22:44
I spent a lot of time in the water, in the pool in my grandparents backyard, then on the swim team.


05:22:44-05:22:48
At five years old, I decided I wanted to be a fish when I grew up.


05:22:48-05:22:52
Tellingly, I never imagined myself in a school of fish.


05:22:52-05:22:56
In these fantasies, it was just fishy Jesse swimming around, happy and free.


05:22:56-05:23:00
On land, I brought a book with me everywhere.


05:23:00-05:23:03
Books were a portal into another world.


05:23:03-05:23:05
I understood how to interact with books.


05:23:05-05:23:10
I knew what the characters were thinking, what they wanted, because it was all laid out on the page.


05:23:10-05:23:16
The first time I found a sense of belonging with my peers happened when I got a boyfriend.


05:23:16-05:23:19
When he broke up with me, my world caved in.


05:23:19-05:23:25
It hurt so badly to be expelled from the one place I had felt valued, accepted, and seen by someone my age.


05:23:25-05:23:28
I decided to never let that happen again.


05:23:28-05:23:34
From then on, I became an expert in the care and keeping of romantic relationships.


05:23:34-05:23:39
I only dated guys who I knew for sure were more interested in me than I was in them.


05:23:39-05:23:42
I learned what they liked and how to make them happy.


05:23:42-05:23:45
I became a people pleaser, well, a person pleaser.


05:23:45-05:23:48
I did not have the executive function required to please a crowd.


05:23:50-05:23:58
When I started How To ADHD, though, I began to develop deep, meaningful connections with people, peers, from all over the world.


05:23:58-05:24:03
I'd never thought to seek community online before, but here I was building one.


05:24:03-05:24:04
I found my people.


05:24:04-05:24:06
I could have friends.


05:24:06-05:24:07
They just happened to be online.


05:24:07-05:24:19
Finding these friendships started to undo some of my long held beliefs that I'd never fit in, that there was no place for me outside of whatever job or romantic relationship I had.


05:24:19-05:24:22
They gave me purpose, hope, and connection.


05:24:22-05:24:28
After my mom died in August 2020, though, I found the limits of online friendships.


05:24:28-05:24:31
You can't hug someone through Discord.


05:24:31-05:24:35
My long distance friends couldn't sit with me through the grief.


05:24:35-05:24:43
Leaning on a romantic relationship to meet my in person needs had been my go to before, but now my needs were too great for one person to handle.


05:24:43-05:24:51
I had moved to a new city to be with a new partner, but between my grief and pandemic isolation, that relationship fell apart.


05:24:51-05:24:56
Single again, I used my inheritance to put a down payment on my first home.


05:24:56-05:25:01
The day I got the keys, I wound up sitting on the floor of my empty house, crying.


05:25:01-05:25:06
This was a huge moment for me, but I had absolutely no one to share it with.


05:25:06-05:25:12
I had friends all over the world, but there was no one to sit with me, eat pizza, and talk about where the furniture should go.


05:25:13-05:25:21
After that experience, I realized no one relationship, no online community, no long distance friendship could truly meet all my social needs.


05:25:21-05:25:27
Humans are social creatures, even those of us who have trouble socializing.


05:25:27-05:25:34
We need people to share things with, to hug or sit with us when we're sad, to give us somewhere we can go and feel like we belong.


05:25:34-05:25:41
I couldn't get out of the in person part of friendships without missing out on an important part of life.


05:25:42-05:25:49
Which brings me to where I am now, a busy professional adult trying to make friends in a city I'm just beginning to explore.


05:25:49-05:25:56
By the time this book comes out, I will hopefully be farther along on this journey, especially with the insights and tools I learned while writing this chapter.


05:25:56-05:25:58
What I learned.


05:25:58-05:26:06
While some ADHDers may have no trouble making and keeping friends, that isn't the typical ADHD experience.


05:26:06-05:26:11
Those of us who struggle socially are not alone in being alone.


05:26:11-05:26:11
The irony.


05:26:12-05:26:18
When I learned this for the first time, I read the research papers on it with tears streaming down my cheeks.


05:26:18-05:26:20
This explains so much.


05:26:20-05:26:23
We struggle to develop our friend circles.


05:26:23-05:26:33
Disability advocate Judith Snow is one of my new heroes thanks to her work on how those with disabilities can develop strong social support networks.


05:26:33-05:26:36
She created a concept known as Circle of Support.


05:26:36-05:26:41
And according to this framework, the people in our lives exist in one of four circles.


05:26:41-05:26:45
The circle of intimacy, circle one.


05:26:45-05:26:56
This includes the people closest to us, who know us on a deep level and who we can't imagine our lives without, such as immediate family members, our partner, our best friend.


05:26:56-05:26:59
The circle of friendship, circle two.


05:26:59-05:27:08
This includes our good friends and allies, the people we call when we have good news, vent to when we fight with a family member, laugh with, and invite to our birthday parties.


05:27:09-05:27:15
If you're thinking, what, but that's what my best friend and/or partner are for, you probably see where I'm going with this.


05:27:15-05:27:20
Apparently, humans need other people for these moments in life, too, if we want to be fulfilled.


05:27:20-05:27:23
The circle of participation, circle three.


05:27:23-05:27:31
This includes the people we participate in shared interests with, the people with whom we interact in our community, work, classes, and clubs.


05:27:31-05:27:35
And the circle of exchange, circle four.


05:27:36-05:27:43
This includes people we pay or who pay us, including doctors, ride share drivers, therapists, housekeepers, hairstylists, and bosses.


05:27:43-05:27:57
According to Judith Snow, those with disabilities tend to have about the same number of people as non-disabled people do in their inner circle, but far fewer in circles two and three, and a much higher number in circle four.


05:27:57-05:28:09
This means all of our needs tend to fall disproportionately on our innermost circle four on paid transactions, aka people who go home at the end of the day and aren't going to answer a phone call at 2 AM.


05:28:09-05:28:14
This happens because, simply put, it's more difficult for us to make and keep friends.


05:28:14-05:28:22
While we have a lot to offer, our noticeable ADHD symptoms can make it harder for us to socialize in a world that stigmatizes them.


05:28:22-05:28:23
We're fidgety and impulsive.


05:28:23-05:28:28
We have trouble waiting our turn and staying focused on activities that don't interest us.


05:28:28-05:28:30
We keep forgetting everybody's name.


05:28:30-05:28:37
And because ADHD is a neurodevelopmental disorder, most of us grew up out of step with our peers.


05:28:37-05:28:49
We might have gotten along okay with kids two to three years younger than us or those older than us, but we seemed immature and lacked the self-regulation skills to stick to the social rules our peers were already able to follow.


05:28:49-05:28:52
As a result, we were left out.


05:28:52-05:28:56
We didn't have as many opportunities to practice socializing compared to our peers.


05:28:57-05:29:01
As adults, our social skills are often understandably underdeveloped.


05:29:01-05:29:07
And because we have busy, often chaotic lives, we also have trouble executive functioning well enough to keep up with friends.


05:29:07-05:29:10
We miss a lot of stuff.


05:29:10-05:29:14
We miss people's birthdays.


05:29:14-05:29:17
We miss the fact that we did not, in fact, hit send.


05:29:17-05:29:18
We miss social cues.


05:29:18-05:29:22
We miss the impact our behaviors are having on those around us.


05:29:22-05:29:29
According to the research, children with ADHD have trouble monitoring their social behavior and adjusting it when needed.


05:29:29-05:29:40
