They also have a harder time integrating and organizing social cues coherently, and are more likely to interpret social situations based on the most recently supplied social information.


05:29:40-05:29:46
As an adult with ADHD, I can tell you that this is true from experience.


05:29:46-05:29:52
There's a reason we return home from a social event and mentally review everything we could have possibly done wrong.


05:29:52-05:29:58
From past experience, we know that there are things that we may have done wrong socially that we missed.


05:29:58-05:30:04
And because we also base our interpretations on the most recently supplied social information.


05:30:04-05:30:13
If the current message we're getting is negative, like they haven't responded to our text messages saying we had a good time, we assume the status of the relationship is negative.


05:30:13-05:30:16
Empathy can get in the way.


05:30:16-05:30:26
The idea that empathy, or the lack thereof, contributes to our social struggles seriously surprised me, because we feel the feels hard.


05:30:26-05:30:29
However, there's a difference between sensitivity and empathy.


05:30:29-05:30:42
While those with ADHD tend to be sensitive and may even pick up on others' emotions, thanks to our higher likelihood to be hypervigilant, we might misunderstand other people's perspectives and emotions.


05:30:42-05:30:52
These misunderstandings can happen either because we miss crucial details about a situation, or because we're so caught up in our own stories and emotions that we can't see past them.


05:30:53-05:31:05
There are two types of empathy, cognitive empathy, which relies on the ability to take someone else's perspective, and affective empathy, which relies on the ability to understand and identify emotions.


05:31:05-05:31:08
It's possible to be great at one type of empathy while struggling with the other.


05:31:08-05:31:15
Even those who are highly empathic can have a difficult time applying their empathy effectively.


05:31:15-05:31:22
Feeling overly sad or distressed when someone else is suffering can make it hard to give them effective support.


05:31:22-05:31:28
We might have a hard time regulating our empathy, just like we have a hard time regulating our emotions and our attention.


05:31:28-05:31:33
A note on small talk.


05:31:33-05:31:41
Those with ADHD tend to loathe small talk because it feels fake and can be boring, unless the weather is of particular interest to us.


05:31:41-05:31:42
Who cares?


05:31:42-05:31:43
Why are we talking about this?


05:31:43-05:31:49
We prefer to get right into big talk, deep, meaningful conversation.


05:31:49-05:31:53
We're interest-based creatures and have difficulty focusing on things that feel meaningless.


05:31:53-05:32:00
However, there is a reason small talk exists and why it involves talking about inconsequential things.


05:32:00-05:32:01
In fact, there are many of them.


05:32:01-05:32:10
Some people use small talk to see if they have chemistry with another person, which helps us decide if a deeper relationship is possible.


05:32:10-05:32:14
Small talk is a less risky way to connect.


05:32:14-05:32:18
Sharing details about ourselves can put us at emotional and physical risk.


05:32:18-05:32:22
It's safer to stick to small talk until we get a sense of who someone is.


05:32:22-05:32:28
Small talk allows you to see which conversational topics might be welcome.


05:32:28-05:32:34
If someone's small talk is all about adorable kittens, they might not be in the mood for a more intense conversation.


05:32:34-05:32:39
Small talk is expected in many situations.


05:32:39-05:32:44
When we start with big talk, others might feel it's jarring or coming out of left field.


05:32:44-05:32:46
Think of small talk like a social lubricant.


05:32:48-05:32:54
While small talk may be challenging for our ADHD brains, the benefits are at least sometimes worth the effort.


05:32:54-05:32:57
Mindset is a part of the puzzle.


05:32:57-05:33:07
Much of the research that exists on ADHD and social difficulties focuses on our behaviors and our trouble noticing the impact of those behaviors.


05:33:07-05:33:14
However, being awkward in a social situation isn't typically what damages our relationships as adults.


05:33:14-05:33:17
After all, everyone behaves awkwardly sometimes.


05:33:18-05:33:29
According to Caroline McGuire, author of Why Will No One Play With Me, and an ADHD coach who is pioneering this field of understanding, how those who are neurodivergent make friends.


05:33:29-05:33:34
It's not the ADHD behaviors themselves that flag us as needy, too much, or weird.


05:33:34-05:33:42
It's the 15 anxious text messages we send when we get home apologizing, explaining, and seeking reassurance.


05:33:42-05:33:49
And what drives us to send those texts is the mindset with which we view and approach social situations.


05:33:49-05:33:59
Our mindsets are part of what leads to the anxious overcorrection, rumination, and thought spirals that many in our community experience when we navigate social settings.


05:33:59-05:34:06
We get stuck trying to understand what went wrong, or why something happened, or how we can fix it.


05:34:06-05:34:08
We slip into emotional reasoning.


05:34:08-05:34:11
I feel this way, so therefore it must be true.


05:34:11-05:34:18
We might even expect a relationship to fail, and this can become a self-fulfilling prophecy.


05:34:18-05:34:22
There are factors affecting our mindset that we may not even be aware of.


05:34:22-05:34:26
Having unrealistic expectations of others.


05:34:26-05:34:34
We sometimes expect someone to be there every time we need them, and that they'll have unlimited capacity to do what we ask them to.


05:34:34-05:34:39
We think they can do something just because we need them to, or because they've done it before.


05:34:39-05:34:46
The social expectations we place on others require a lot of hypervigilance, and that's not something they can always maintain.


05:34:46-05:34:50
We can forget that they have their own goals, needs, emotions, and lives to tend to.


05:34:50-05:34:55
Having unrealistic expectations of ourselves.


05:34:55-05:35:02
We sometimes expect ourselves to be there every time someone needs us and to have unlimited capacity to do what others ask or need.


05:35:02-05:35:07
We think we can do something just because someone needs us to, or because we sometimes can.


05:35:07-05:35:14
The social expectations we place on ourselves require a lot of hypervigilance, and that's not something we can always maintain.


05:35:14-05:35:19
We forget that we have our own goals, needs, emotions, and lives to tend to.


05:35:19-05:35:23
Operating with a scarcity mindset.


05:35:23-05:35:28
We often have this I'll take anyone or I'll do anything approach to relationships.


05:35:28-05:35:33
We underestimate our worth to the point that we feel we're lucky to have anyone in our lives.


05:35:33-05:35:41
As a result, we spend a lot of time and energy trying to maintain relationships with people who are a bad fit, thinking we're unlovable.


05:35:41-05:35:46
Because we have fewer people to lean on, we can seem high maintenance.


05:35:46-05:35:55
Those closest to us sometimes get frustrated or need a break from our need for support, which can trigger our rejection sensitivity, I'll get to that in a second.


05:35:55-05:35:59
And reinforce the idea that we're too much, not worth it, or even unlovable.


05:35:59-05:36:04
And thinking we need to fit in to belong.


05:36:04-05:36:11
Fitting in has its place, like when you're moving through airport security, but it does not lead to belonging.


05:36:11-05:36:21
As Brene Brown puts it, when we sacrifice who we are, we not only feel separate from others, but we even feel disconnected from ourselves.


05:36:21-05:36:24
Rejection sensitivity and mindset.


05:36:24-05:36:33
Rejection sensitivity is the tendency to find rejection or even perceived rejection profoundly painful.


05:36:33-05:36:44
Rejection sensitivity isn't unique to ADHD, but it is a very common experience for those who have it, thanks to difficulties with emotion regulation combined with a lifetime of experiences of actual rejection.


05:36:44-05:36:57
Learning how to cope with emotion dysregulation can help us manage rejection sensitivity, but I'm including a sidebar about it here because our mindsets and behaviors affect how we experience rejection sensitivity and vice versa.


05:36:57-05:37:04
While we can't control our emotions, we can influence them through our thoughts and behaviors.


05:37:04-05:37:09
When it comes to rejection sensitivity, we quite naturally adjust our behavior to avoid it.


05:37:09-05:37:10
We people please.


05:37:10-05:37:15
We suppress our ADHD and cut off pieces of ourselves trying to fit in.


05:37:15-05:37:18
We avoid risky social situations entirely.


05:37:18-05:37:25
We've been experiencing rejection since before we had metacognition, the ability to think about our thoughts.


05:37:25-05:37:29
By the time we're adults, we've learned that we're often rejected for our behavior.


05:37:29-05:37:35
So it makes sense that our go-to strategy for avoiding the pain of rejection is adjusting our behavior.


05:37:35-05:37:43
But as I mentioned in the motivation chapter, the lever we instinctively pull is not always the right lever to pull.


05:37:43-05:37:53
Those with ADHD often have significantly skewed mindsets when it comes to social situations, including the mindset that we need to be someone else entirely to belong.


05:37:53-05:37:57
Or it's other extreme, this is who I am and people just need to deal.


05:37:57-05:38:04
And adjusting those mindsets, not our individual behaviors, which often stem from them, is often what's needed most.


05:38:04-05:38:09
For me, the most important mindset that I needed to shift was this.


05:38:09-05:38:21
Making friends is a one, nope, multi-step process that relies on other people liking me, nope, connection, today, uh-uh, over time.


05:38:21-05:38:27
Making friends is a multi-step process that relies on connection over time.


05:38:28-05:38:35
Making friends is a process, and like any process that occurs between two or more people, it carries with it an element of uncertainty.


05:38:35-05:38:43
Many with ADHD, including me until ChexWatch last month, try to skip past this uncertainty.


05:38:43-05:38:52
When we like someone and are excited about the possibility of a new friendship, that feeling, like all our feelings, can be very intense.


05:38:52-05:38:58
Sometimes we respond to that feeling by acting as if a secure connection already exists.


05:38:58-05:39:10
We share intimate details about our lives, make elaborate future plans, hyper-focus on them to the detriment of other relationships and obligations, or blow up their phone or social media to a cringe-inducing extent.


05:39:10-05:39:14
Sometimes this behavior scares people off.


05:39:14-05:39:19
Other times, the person responds well, but is disappointed later when we can't keep up this level of intensity.


05:39:19-05:39:27
In the worst cases, we find ourselves deeply attached to people who take advantage of us or treat us poorly.


05:39:27-05:39:35
In the best cases, these relationships settle into a more balanced rhythm, ultimately becoming the kind of friendship we so badly wanted to already have.


05:39:35-05:39:43
When these friendships do work out, it's not because of how intensely they started, but because of how they continued.


05:39:43-05:39:47
Friendship generally isn't made or lost in a moment.


05:39:47-05:39:52
It requires a level of intimacy and mutual trust that is earned with time and experience.


05:39:52-05:39:58
The more moments of connection you have with someone, the harder it is to break your bond with them.


05:39:58-05:40:09
If you think back to the circles of support I talked about earlier, the way you reach the circle of friendship is by going through the circle of participation and working your way in over time.


05:40:09-05:40:16
You can't get to real friendship unless you spend time in the participation circle over and over again.


05:40:16-05:40:23
Trying to have close friendship levels of intimacy with someone before doing this is kind of like writing a bunch of checks we can't always cash.


05:40:24-05:40:27
Mike, who manages our Discord community, points out one reason why.


05:40:27-05:40:35
Most folks will assume they're one of the few people who know the big details of your life and thus expect to be kept up to date.


05:40:35-05:40:40
When you suddenly drop someone from this intimate role, it can damage the relationship.


05:40:40-05:40:48
Either they find out they're not unique or special because you talk intimately with everyone, or they feel rejected because you've suddenly cut them off.


05:40:48-05:40:52
Friendship takes time to build and maintain.


05:40:52-05:40:59
The quality of the time you spend together is important, but so is the amount of time you put into it over time.


05:40:59-05:41:07
Friendships are held together with many individual threads of connection, and there is no magic formula for how long it takes to create them.


05:41:07-05:41:11
Some people become friends because you spend a lot of time with them.


05:41:11-05:41:16
Other people become friends because the time you spend together is always amazing.


05:41:16-05:41:21
What matters isn't strictly how much time you spend with them or whether every minute you spend is incredible.


05:41:21-05:41:25
But rather, is the time you spend together enough to stitch you together.


05:41:25-05:41:34
Disappointingly, particularly for those of us who are averse to uncertainty, that's not always something we can control, rush, or even predict.


05:41:34-05:41:43
Lots of hurdles can get in the way of a budding friendship, time constraints, stress from life events, scheduling logistics.


05:41:43-05:41:45
Life is complicated.


05:41:45-05:41:53
All we can really control is whether we engage in that circle of participation often enough to give a potential friend a chance to become an actual friend.


05:41:53-05:41:58
If even this sounds daunting, you're not alone.


05:41:58-05:42:08
For many of us who have a limited number of activities we can participate in or have difficulties inviting people into our circle of participation, we may need support to build connections.


05:42:08-05:42:15
In From Behind the Piano, you can learn about how Judith Snow, who was physically disabled, built her unique circle of friends.


05:42:16-05:42:20
But all this takes too long and I just want people to do stuff with.


05:42:20-05:42:21
Good news, buddy.


05:42:21-05:42:25
You don't need to make friends in order to have people to hang out with.


05:42:25-05:42:29
That was how I thought it worked too, but no, I had it backward.


05:42:29-05:42:35
It's not friend then participation, it's participation then maybe friend.


05:42:35-05:42:37
You can't pick your friends.


05:42:37-05:42:42
You can only pick who to do stuff with that you find meaningful and then see what kind of relationship evolves over time.


05:42:44-05:42:47
Nary D, 42, California.


05:42:47-05:42:52
I'm friendly with many people, but I have few friends and I dream of someday having a best friend.


05:42:52-05:42:57
I can't manage a good balance between being too aloof and too over involved, and I think that may turn others off.


05:42:57-05:43:01
Chantal, 20, Egypt.


05:43:01-05:43:05
I realized I've always loved people so much, so much.


05:43:05-05:43:08
But I always let people down one way or another.


05:43:08-05:43:10
I wanted to call, but I forgot.


05:43:10-05:43:13
I wanted to answer your texts, but I'm kind of dreading the task.


05:43:13-05:43:17
I wanted to wish you a happy birthday, but I got caught up in a million things.


05:43:17-05:43:21
And it looks to them as if I don't care about them, which is completely false.


05:43:21-05:43:23
I just was never good at showing the love I have.


05:43:23-05:43:27
Chris P, 39, Wyoming.


05:43:27-05:43:29
I didn't necessarily make friends.


05:43:29-05:43:32
I have a few people that just picked me out of a group and said, I like you.


05:43:32-05:43:34
I don't know why they picked me, but I'm forever grateful.


05:43:34-05:43:39
Lena C, 53, Scotland.


05:43:39-05:43:41
I don't think I ever really believe that people like me.


05:43:41-05:43:48
It's like I constantly have to prove why they should be friends with me, and as if I owe them something for being kind enough to be my friend.


05:43:48-05:43:51
The toolbox.


05:43:51-05:43:54
At this point, I hope it's clear that friends are not something we find.


05:43:54-05:43:56
This is good news, really.


05:43:56-05:43:58
It takes a lot of pressure off ourselves.


05:43:58-05:44:04
If we can't just make friends, we can start to focus on something more approachable and low stakes.


05:44:04-05:44:08
Doing activities we find meaningful with people who agree.


05:44:08-05:44:12
I have collected some tools to help me on my friendship journey that I hope will be useful on yours.


05:44:12-05:44:16
One, figure out what you have to offer.


05:44:16-05:44:21
We tend to attract people who treat us in a way that aligns with our view of ourselves.


05:44:21-05:44:28
It's helpful to spend time considering what's great about you and what you have and want to offer.


05:44:28-05:44:36
This also helps us show up as ourselves, not as a version of ourselves we think others want us to be, which increases our sense of belonging in our relationships with others.


05:44:38-05:44:42
Notice the qualities you appreciate in others that you yourself have.


05:44:42-05:44:46
It's often easier to appreciate characteristics that others have than to value them in yourself.


05:44:46-05:44:51
This is one of the reasons I love hanging out with others with ADHD.


05:44:51-05:44:58
Spending time with people who have interesting things to say and start sharing enthusiastically when they arrive has made me realize others may feel the same about me.


05:44:58-05:45:02
Listen to what others say they appreciate about you.


05:45:02-05:45:07
Notice when you're about to auto reject a compliment and consider writing it down instead.


05:45:07-05:45:09
Say thank you, take it in.


05:45:09-05:45:12
You know they might be right.


05:45:12-05:45:15
If you're confused about the compliment, ask for specifics.


05:45:15-05:45:19
While general compliments can be hard to accept, what do you mean I did a good job?


05:45:19-05:45:24
I was late and this went wrong and specifics make the compliment harder to argue against.


05:45:24-05:45:32
Because it is a fact that the person did enjoy this one specific aspect of what we contributed, it can be easier to accept it.


05:45:32-05:45:34
Figure out what you're looking for.


05:45:34-05:45:39
If I know I'm looking for people I can go hiking with, that means being a hiking buddy is something I have to offer too.


05:45:39-05:45:42
Reassess.


05:45:42-05:45:47
What we have to offer and want to, can and will change over time.


05:45:47-05:45:51
Maybe we have more, fewer, or different things to offer now.


05:45:51-05:45:53
Maybe we're a pretty cool YouTuber now with a book out, I don't know.


05:45:53-05:45:57
Anonymous, 23, Oregon.


05:45:57-05:46:00
Make a list of your positive qualities.


05:46:00-05:46:10
I'm funny, I'm creative, I'm empathetic, I'm a good listener, I'm insightful, I'm kind, I try hard, I'm good at giving compliments, and on and on.


05:46:10-05:46:14
Fake your belief you're a friend worth having until you realize that you are.


05:46:14-05:46:18
Not everyone will want to be friends with you and that's okay.


05:46:18-05:46:21
You won't be everybody's cup of tea just like some other people aren't yours.


05:46:21-05:46:22
Your people are out there.


05:46:22-05:46:27
Yesenia R, 27, Texas.


05:46:27-05:46:31
I had such bad social anxiety due to my ADHD.


05:46:31-05:46:38
Going through CBT helped a lot, especially learning to point out logical fallacies I'd use to convince myself that an interaction went badly.


05:46:38-05:46:42
People will naturally gravitate toward you when you are learning to accept yourself.


05:46:42-05:46:47
Michael K, 43, Ontario, Canada.


05:46:47-05:46:51
There will always be someone who does not see the unique gifts of my mind.


05:46:51-05:46:53
That person will not be me.


05:46:53-05:46:57
Two, meet new people.


05:46:57-05:47:03
We're not going to click with everyone, but meeting new people is the best way to find the ones with whom we will.


05:47:03-05:47:09
This can keep us from getting stuck trying to be friends with people who aren't a great fit or who treat us badly.


05:47:09-05:47:15
It can also help us avoid over relying on any one person for support, making our relationships more sustainable.


05:47:15-05:47:18
Go where people meet people.


05:47:18-05:47:26
There are online dating apps that have a friend mode and websites for people with common interests to meet like BumbleBFF or Meetup.org.


05:47:27-05:47:31
There's an immediate advantage to meeting people through channels like these.


05:47:31-05:47:34
They're probably there for the same reason, to find potential friends.


05:47:34-05:47:38
Go where your strengths will shine.


05:47:38-05:47:43
If your conversation is better than your dance moves, hang out where people can hear you talk.


05:47:43-05:47:47
If your dance moves are killer but you get awkward when talking, hang out where you can dance.


05:47:47-05:47:49
There are many ways to connect socially.


05:47:49-05:47:52
When you can, choose ones that are in line with your strengths.


05:47:52-05:47:56
Prep some conversation topics in advance.


05:47:56-05:47:58
Remember cool executive function?


05:47:58-05:48:03
Planning what to talk about is much easier before you're on the spot or when your foot is already firmly in your mouth.


05:48:03-05:48:08
Caroline suggests having three topics ready to go.


05:48:08-05:48:12
The topics are ideally non-controversial and ones that people actually talk about.


05:48:12-05:48:14
Come up with some get to know you questions too.


05:48:14-05:48:16
People love talking about themselves.


05:48:16-05:48:19
Look for shared values.


05:48:19-05:48:25
While shared interests are great for short term connection, shared values help relationships stand the test of time.


05:48:25-05:48:29
Examples of shared values include kindness, honesty, humor, and playfulness.


05:48:29-05:48:32
Siona L, 32, Nevada.


05:48:32-05:48:35
How to make friends.


05:48:35-05:48:38
Step one, practice D&D with your family for years.


05:48:38-05:48:42
Step two, find nerdy, queer, neurodiverse people online.


05:48:42-05:48:45
Step three, beg them to play D&D with you.


05:48:45-05:48:47
Step four, stay friends for years.


05:48:47-05:48:50
Max V, 32, Oregon.


05:48:52-05:48:58
To make friends, I started by just finding a place I felt I belonged that also required other people to be there regularly.


05:48:58-05:49:00
For me, that place was theater.


05:49:00-05:49:04
When I got there, I found a lot of people who were like me, which was ideal.


05:49:04-05:49:07
Lindsay J, 37, Maryland.


05:49:07-05:49:16
I find people who have similar weird niche interests as me then basically say, hi, I have no idea how to make friends as an adult.


05:49:16-05:49:18
Want to grab coffee?


05:49:18-05:49:19
Sometimes it actually works.


05:49:20-05:49:22
Three, level up your social skills.


05:49:22-05:49:34
When I was learning about social skills for the channel, I realized the reason I thought I was bad at making friends was because in video game terms, I was playing levels I wasn't ready for.


05:49:34-05:49:41
Thanks to my neurodevelopmental delay, I've spent much of my life playing on levels that required skills I hadn't yet developed.


05:49:41-05:49:47
This left me frustrated and not wanting to play, and not playing left me further behind.


05:49:47-05:49:50
The truth is, no one is born with social skills.


05:49:50-05:49:53
We learn and acquire them, even as adults.


05:49:53-05:49:56
Observe others.


05:49:56-05:49:58
Caroline calls this being a social spy.


05:49:58-05:50:06
Watching what others typically do and don't do in social situations can help us calibrate our understanding and expectations.


05:50:06-05:50:09
You can also study how those with your brain type interact with each other.


05:50:09-05:50:15
Social norms are often different in neurodivergent communities, and it's good to be fluent in your own language too.


05:50:15-05:50:18
Otherwise, how will you connect with others who speak it?


05:50:19-05:50:21
Ask for stronger social cues.


05:50:21-05:50:25
Social feedback loops help people get good at socializing.


05:50:25-05:50:31
Unfortunately for us, these feedback loops typically depend on subtle social cues.


05:50:31-05:50:37
If you know you tend to miss or misread those cues, it's totally fine to ask for them to be more overt.


05:50:37-05:50:44
When my friend Alex realized I wasn't getting a chance to jump into the conversation, she said, hey, I know I can talk over people when I get excited.


05:50:44-05:50:47
If I do that, say pongo, and I'll know to let you talk.


05:50:48-05:50:50
Try a tutorial.


05:50:50-05:50:55
If you're anxious about not knowing how to navigate a social situation, it can help to know what to expect.


05:50:55-05:50:58
Ask someone you trust if there are any pitfalls to look out for.


05:50:58-05:51:01
Call the place you're going and ask what the vibe is like.


05:51:01-05:51:06
You can also look up tutorials about how to navigate specific social situations online.


05:51:06-05:51:08
WikiHow is amazing.


05:51:08-05:51:11
Play on levels you enjoy.


05:51:11-05:51:17
If you get frustrated or incredibly anxious while socializing, it might mean that you're playing a level you're not ready for.


05:51:17-05:51:24
If level three, going to yoga together, is enough of a challenge, it may not make sense to struggle through level 10, going on a yoga trip together.


05:51:24-05:51:30
Spend most of your time on levels you're comfortable with, and you'll probably have a lot more fun.


05:51:30-05:51:32
You'll also play more, which helps with leveling up.


05:51:32-05:51:36
But also, expose yourself to new levels once in a while.


05:51:36-05:51:37
It's good to limit test.


05:51:37-05:51:42
We might not enjoy something the first time because of the stress from not knowing what to expect.


05:51:42-05:51:45
We might have more fun the next time, now that we do.


05:51:46-05:51:49
A note on masking.


05:51:49-05:51:54
Leveling up your social skills does not mean learning how to mask your neurodivergence 24/7.


05:51:54-05:52:05
Masking for long periods of time is damaging to our emotional and mental well-being because it requires suppressing behaviors that help us cope with a world not built with our needs in mind.


05:52:05-05:52:12
While masking can help us fit in, it does not lend itself to deep meaningful relationships long term.


05:52:12-05:52:18
Masking our neurodivergence so much that we can't find each other leaves us lonely and disconnected.


05:52:18-05:52:21
What we want to aim for here is interfacing effectively with others.


05:52:21-05:52:27
Or as Brene Brown puts it, learn how to be present with people without sacrificing who we are.


05:52:27-05:52:31
Sebastian L, 29, Guatemala.


05:52:31-05:52:35
Bring your friends into whatever really interests you in the moment.


05:52:35-05:52:36
Really into baking right now?


05:52:36-05:52:38
Bring cupcakes to the next party.


05:52:38-05:52:40
Really into why the sky is so dark?


05:52:40-05:52:42
Find some articles so you can talk about it.


05:52:42-05:52:45
Or invite someone to a museum with a space exhibit.


05:52:45-05:52:47
You'll be surprised how interesting you become to others.


05:52:47-05:52:50
Sarah N, 52, USA.


05:52:50-05:52:55
I like gravitating toward folks who like to low key collect and connect people.


05:52:55-05:52:57
You probably know the sort.


05:52:57-05:53:02
They're not the obvious social superstars, but they make friends easily and delight in mixing them gently.


05:53:02-05:53:05
Their need to be social helps with ADHD related inertia.


05:53:05-05:53:10
Rachel, 28, Washington DC.


05:53:10-05:53:14
Being a social spy is a skill that makes such a difference in social and professional settings alike.


05:53:14-05:53:21
Taking the time to be a social spy and relate to people on their level is vital to being successful in any relationship.


05:53:21-05:53:25
Four, make socializing routine.


05:53:25-05:53:30
We aren't great at free recall or remembering to do things spontaneously.


05:53:30-05:53:36
When you build socialization into your routine, this means you don't have to rely on free recall to remember to do it.


05:53:36-05:53:44
It also makes connection a regular part of your life, which according to a ton of studies is critical to our happiness and well being.


05:53:44-05:53:47
Like sleep, friends are not a side quest.


05:53:47-05:53:50
They're part of the main storyline.


05:53:50-05:53:50
Weave them in.


05:53:50-05:53:55
Set aside a regular day or time to check in with people.


05:53:55-05:54:03
This can take the form of a weekly class, club meeting, game, or short blocks of time in your schedule set aside for socializing.


05:54:03-05:54:07
If you're like me and get overwhelmed, it can help to prioritize by circles.


05:54:07-05:54:11
For example, respond to circle one peeps on breaks or after work.


05:54:11-05:54:13
Reach out to circle two on set days.


05:54:13-05:54:16
Get in touch with circle three people only on weekends.


05:54:16-05:54:19
When I need to cocoon, I take it down a circle.


05:54:19-05:54:25
When I need to cocoon, my first instinct is to stop responding to everybody.


05:54:25-05:54:31
But then, as soon as I come out, I'm overwhelmed because now I have to respond to everybody.


05:54:31-05:54:39
I'm learning to recognize when I'm getting socially overwhelmed and take it down a circle, so I still get more alone time without neglecting all of my relationships at once.


05:54:39-05:54:43
Have go to people for certain activities.


05:54:43-05:54:46
When you want to go bowling, maybe you text a certain friend.


05:54:46-05:54:53
Errand buddies are a great way to build friendship into everyday life and make tedious tasks less mundane.


05:54:53-05:54:57
Going to a friend's house to wrap Christmas presents together is way more fun than doing it alone.


05:54:57-05:55:00
Reach out when you remember to.


05:55:00-05:55:05
I used to be afraid to start a conversation when I thought of someone because I didn't want to get distracted.


05:55:05-05:55:11
Now I know that it's fine to send a message that says, hey, still working, but saw this and thought of you, catch up later, and leave it at that.


05:55:11-05:55:15
Tanya S, 31, Canada.


05:55:15-05:55:21
I have daily calls with my besties where we play a video game or do a video chat during our lunch hours.


05:55:21-05:55:24
The regular schedule means we hop in and out as needed.


05:55:24-05:55:28
Philip K, 35, Germany.


05:55:28-05:55:29
Be proactive.


05:55:29-05:55:33
Be the one who organizes meetings and don't only wait for invitations.


05:55:33-05:55:34
Today is the best day to plan.


05:55:34-05:55:38
Gwyneth T, 30, Michigan.


05:55:38-05:55:43
I have weird little things I do to make sure I'm keeping in touch with my friends.


05:55:43-05:55:47
I use the Sweepie app for housework, but I've added a social section.


05:55:47-05:55:52
It helps me see how long it's been since I've texted someone and makes it a part of my to-do list.


05:55:52-05:56:00
It helps me remember that the social part of my life is just as vital to my life as cleaning a toilet, and it gives me a little boost from checking something important off my list.


05:56:01-05:56:04
Five, communicate your boundaries.


05:56:04-05:56:06
People can't read one another's minds.


05:56:06-05:56:14
It isn't obvious to others why we're interrupting, if we're overwhelmed or overstimulated, or if we've hit our limit on sitting still for the evening.


05:56:14-05:56:19
It also isn't obvious to them that we're really hoping they'll invite us to go to the event they just told us about.


05:56:19-05:56:22
This means we might have to like, tell them.


05:56:22-05:56:27
Not only does communicating this stuff make it more likely that we'll get our needs met, it makes it safer for them to do the same.


05:56:29-05:56:32
Differentiate between want to and willing to.


05:56:32-05:56:40
We often say, I'd love to, when what we really mean is, I'm willing to do this thing you clearly want because I value your friendship.


05:56:40-05:56:41
Both are good.


05:56:41-05:56:43
Both are important.


05:56:43-05:56:48
Doing some want tos and some willing tos means the friendship is reciprocal.


05:56:48-05:56:51
Talk about expectations.


05:56:51-05:56:53
This could be an elaborate and emotional conversation.


05:56:53-05:56:58
It can also be as simple as, hey dude, gym tonight?


05:56:58-05:56:59
You do you.


05:56:59-05:57:04
Ask for what you want, but respect their limits.


05:57:04-05:57:14
As Betty Martin and Robin Dalsen share in the art of giving and receiving, when receiving, it's your job to ask for what you want, communicate expectations, and respect the limits of the giver.


05:57:14-05:57:18
When you're the one giving, respect your limits.


05:57:18-05:57:25
In the excitement of, I have something to offer that somebody wants, we can forget to ask ourselves if this is okay for us.


05:57:25-05:57:28
Set your own policies.


05:57:28-05:57:35
Creating policies that aren't about a specific person or situation makes it easier for you to remember them and others to hear them.


05:57:35-05:57:38
Here are some examples of my own.


05:57:38-05:57:40
I don't loan money to friends.


05:57:40-05:57:42
I'm currently looking for in-person friendships.


05:57:42-05:57:45
And I'm going to take a lunch break every day from 1 to 2 PM.


05:57:45-05:57:50
Claudia S, 32, Mexico.


05:57:50-05:57:54
I used to be shy and self-conscious about my character flaws when meeting new people.


05:57:54-05:58:00
Now I always try to be my loud, chaotic, silly self when I meet someone new because I want them to know who I am from the beginning.


05:58:00-05:58:05
Merlin S, 32, Maryland.


05:58:05-05:58:08
It helps to have some plain discussions about expectations at the start.


05:58:08-05:58:12
Knowing if someone sucks at texting or prefers phone calls helps me to adjust accordingly.


05:58:12-05:58:17
Sebastian L, 29, Guatemala.


05:58:17-05:58:22
Be honest about your limits and challenges in a way that is respectful to yourself.


05:58:22-05:58:25
Hey Becky, could you text me later this week to remind me about our hangout?


05:58:25-05:58:26
Could we share a ride?


05:58:26-05:58:27
I wanna make sure I get there on time.


05:58:27-05:58:30
Could we stay one night instead of two?


05:58:30-05:58:32
Come with me to find X's birthday gift or I might forget to get them one.


05:58:32-05:58:35
What's the point?


05:58:35-05:58:41
One day in frustration, I asked my therapist, what's the point of friends?


05:58:41-05:58:45
Friends felt like something I was supposed to have just because I was supposed to have them.


05:58:45-05:58:49
Like I have to stop what I'm doing to message somebody back or go hang out with them.


05:58:49-05:58:52
And usually it's not as fun or productive as what I could have been doing instead.


05:58:52-05:58:55
So what's the point of having them?


05:58:55-05:58:57
My therapist's response?


05:58:57-05:58:58
Good question.


05:58:58-05:59:00
What is the point of friends?


05:59:00-05:59:01
Get curious about that.


05:59:01-05:59:04
The next time you spend time with someone, ask yourself, what are you getting out of it?


05:59:04-05:59:09
I had never in my life asked myself this question.


05:59:09-05:59:14
I mean, wasn't the point of friendship to have friends so I wasn't alone?


05:59:14-05:59:18
So I could check off some arbitrary box that meant I was doing life right?


05:59:18-05:59:32
Until this point, I'd learned to find all of the benefits that friends could provide through other avenues, entertainment, video games, connection or attention, my partner, connection or attention when my partner isn't around.


05:59:32-05:59:34
I had a dog.


05:59:34-05:59:37
Going without friends is easier now than ever.


05:59:37-05:59:43
As Caroline pointed out to me, apps now provide a lot of everyday support that we used to turn to other people for.


05:59:43-05:59:45
Need a ride to the airport?


05:59:45-05:59:47
Feel sick and need soup?


05:59:47-05:59:49
Need someone to pick up groceries for you?


05:59:49-05:59:52
There are apps, in some cases many apps, for all this.


05:59:53-05:59:58
Apps improve accessibility, which is important, especially for those of us with disabilities.


05:59:58-06:00:01
But when they completely replace people in your life, it can get lonely.


06:00:01-06:00:05
They say friends help you build your life.


06:00:05-06:00:08
Since I didn't have many of them, I'd learned to build it myself.


06:00:08-06:00:12
In fact, the life I'd built didn't even have room for them.


06:00:12-06:00:20
If I was going to weave friendship into my main storyline, I needed to know why it was worth the effort, the potential rejection and the uncertainty.


06:00:20-06:00:23
And it couldn't just be so you won't be lonely.


06:00:23-06:00:29
Because honestly, my attempts to make friends often made me feel far lonelier than I would if I'd stayed home alone.


06:00:29-06:00:33
So I started getting curious, what was the point of friends?


06:00:33-06:00:39
Maybe I didn't turn to friends to meet specific needs, but I could pay attention to what I was getting out of it when I did spend time with them.


06:00:39-06:00:43
At first, the answer was disappointing, not a whole lot.


06:00:43-06:00:48
I realized how often I went along with activities that weren't really for me.


06:00:48-06:00:52
I wasn't getting much out of a hangout, especially compared to how much it was costing me.


06:00:52-06:00:59
Because the friendship was about making them happy and serving their needs, it wasn't making me happy or serving any of mine.


06:00:59-06:01:03
My original goal was just to have friends, so I tried to keep them happy.


06:01:03-06:01:07
But now I had a new goal, to have friendships that benefited me too.


06:01:07-06:01:09
This changed the game.


06:01:09-06:01:15
I realized I could invite people to do activities I wanted to do.


06:01:15-06:01:25
I changed my BFF profile on Bumble from one that said pick me, look at what I have to offer, to one that said, here's what I'm looking for, and here's what I need in a friend.


06:01:25-06:01:30
And I found people who fit with me, who I could be myself around.


06:01:30-06:01:32
Several had ADHD.


06:01:32-06:01:41
When I came home in tears one night because I thought I'd gotten too excited about the activity we were doing, and that's why they looked annoyed at the end of the night, this time I was wrong.


06:01:41-06:01:44
They were just tired, things were fine.


06:01:44-06:01:47
I wasn't too much, not for them.


06:01:47-06:01:55
What I wanted to get out of in-person friends, I realized, was the same thing I had found in my online community, a sense of belonging.


06:01:55-06:01:58
And I'm slowly finding that.


06:01:58-06:02:04
When I wrote this chapter, I was thinking about what I wanted to do with the D&D game I was going to that night.


06:02:04-06:02:10
Even though I was behind schedule on finishing the edits for this book, I took a day off to explore my city with a long distance friend who was in town.


06:02:10-06:02:18
I know now that I can't completely avoid the pain or the work inherent to the process of making friends.


06:02:18-06:02:21
But I can pursue friendships that are worth it to me.


06:02:21-06:02:24
Instead of trying to fit, I can select for fit.


06:02:24-06:02:27
I can look for places I might belong.


06:02:27-06:02:33
I can look for potential friends who have qualities I value, who value what I want to give.


06:02:33-06:02:39
Who value me not for the persona I effortfully present to others, but for the person I actually am.


06:02:43-06:02:47
Chapter 11, how to make ADHD harder.


06:02:47-06:02:55
There's no limit to how complicated things can get on account of one thing always leading to another, E.B. White.


06:02:55-06:02:59
ADHD and question mark.


06:02:59-06:03:06
For most of this audiobook, I've talked about the experiences those of us with ADHD tend to have in common.


06:03:06-06:03:11
Difficulties with attention, executive function, time management, sleep, and more.


06:03:13-06:03:25
I can't tell you how many times I've posted about my specific ADHD struggles, only to be quote tweeted a dozen times by brains across the globe who are all saying, this, this right here, this explains my whole personality.


06:03:25-06:03:29
Apparently a lot of us have at least five different drinks on our desks at any given time, who knew?


06:03:29-06:03:35
In this chapter though, I wanna talk about the ways our experiences differ.


06:03:35-06:03:44
There are many biological, psychological, and socio-environmental factors that impact the lived experience of any human on Earth.


06:03:44-06:03:49
There will always be factors that differentiate our lived experience.


06:03:49-06:03:56
This is part of why a tool that works for one person with ADHD may not for another with the exact same support needs.


06:03:56-06:04:00
To use an innocuous example, let's consider a big white board.


06:04:00-06:04:02
Maybe it works great for one person.


06:04:02-06:04:05
For the other, maybe the smell of the markers is distracting.


06:04:05-06:04:08
Maybe the walls in their workplace don't have space for one.


06:04:08-06:04:22
This difference in lived experience would exist in our community, even if we all had the same access to resources, faced the same level of discrimination, had the same impairments, and experienced the same demand on our executive function.


06:04:22-06:04:28
We would have differences even if our ADHD symptoms and impairments remained constant over time.


06:04:28-06:04:30
But we do not, and they do not.


06:04:31-06:04:49
While ADHD impacts brain development and cognition in much the same ways across gender, race, class, and nationality, those aspects of our social identity, also called intersectionalities because they intersect, significantly affect the way we live our lives.


06:04:49-06:04:54
They can determine the level of disadvantage and discrimination we may face.


06:04:54-06:05:04
The concept of intersectionality was developed by Kimberly Crenshaw, an American lawyer, scholar, and activist who wanted to make it clear that different forms of oppression aren't separate, they're linked.


06:05:04-06:05:16
They can influence how our ADHD-related behaviors are perceived by those around us, how we get diagnosed, if we get diagnosed, and what kind of treatment we receive.


06:05:16-06:05:25
I was just beginning to understand this when I learned that ADHD is also a biopsychosocial disorder.


06:05:25-06:05:29
While ADHD is highly genetic, there are many, many genes involved.


06:05:29-06:05:38
And many factors can influence how our ADHD presents, the severity of our symptoms, and the extent to which our cognitive impairments actually impair us.


06:05:38-06:05:48
Our social identities and the level of discrimination we face as a result quite naturally play a role in this too, though they're not the only factors.


06:05:48-06:05:54
I was about to dive deeper into understanding and untangling the interplay when my mom died suddenly.


06:05:54-06:05:55
And I learned it the hard way.


06:05:55-06:06:00
I heard the news in a phone call from a coroner's office.


06:06:00-06:06:05
This happened on the same day I found out my divorce was final, in 2020.


06:06:05-06:06:07
I was not okay.


06:06:07-06:06:15
The grief I experienced was so big, so all-encompassing, that it overwhelmed my mental resources and coping strategies.


06:06:15-06:06:21
I often couldn't function well enough to use my tools, and many of them no longer worked.


06:06:21-06:06:25
My grieving brain functioned completely differently when it functioned at all.


06:06:25-06:06:33
At the insistence of a friend and with her support, I enrolled in a peer support program to begin processing my grief.


06:06:33-06:06:47
I learned I had experienced a traumatic loss, and that, like so many adult children from complicated backgrounds, the loss of my last living parent had triggered the reemergence of significant childhood trauma that needed to be processed.


06:06:47-06:06:49
I started trauma therapy as well.


06:06:50-06:06:56
As I made progress toward healing, I began noticing that my ADHD was beginning to impact me differently.


06:06:56-06:07:04
Turns out, symptoms I had for years attributed solely to my ADHD were influenced by other factors.


06:07:04-06:07:10
I began thinking of my life not just in terms of my ADHD, but in terms of my ADHD and.


06:07:10-06:07:12
Take ADHD and trauma.


06:07:12-06:07:17
Emotion dysregulation is a common experience for ADHD brains.


06:07:17-06:07:27
But even before my mom died, the trauma I'd experienced as a child and the trauma patterns I'd repeated throughout my life had exacerbated the way I personally experienced it.


06:07:27-06:07:28
My ADHD impulsivity?


06:07:28-06:07:35
In therapy, I learned that a lot of my impulsive actions resulted from my intolerance for uncertainty.


06:07:35-06:07:41
This, it turns out, is common among those who, like me, also have anxiety disorders.


06:07:41-06:07:48
The impulsive choices I made as a young adult with ADHD and anxiety may have cost me opportunities and friendships.


06:07:48-06:07:54
But making those choices eased the anxiety of not knowing how those things would have turned out.


06:07:54-06:07:56
Would the opportunity have led to failure?


06:07:56-06:07:58
Would that friend ultimately hurt me?


06:07:58-06:08:02
Would it be easier to impulsively blow it all up rather than living with that uncertainty?


06:08:02-06:08:03
Yes, yes it would.


06:08:03-06:08:17
I realized years of damage to my self-esteem hadn't been about the ADHD alone, but from constantly running up against both neurotypical and gendered expectations that my ADHD made it difficult to meet.


06:08:17-06:08:25
I felt ashamed and discouraged when my body, home, and relationships didn't look like they were quote unquote supposed to.


06:08:25-06:08:33
Never realizing that my idea of what things were supposed to look like had a lot to do with being ADHD and a white middle class cisgender woman in the United States.


06:08:33-06:08:39
Over time, I learned to see the nuance involved in my challenges.


06:08:39-06:08:41
ADHD wasn't the only factor.


06:08:41-06:08:44
In a way, this was good news.


06:08:44-06:08:45
I would always have ADHD.


06:08:45-06:08:48
I would always experience emotion dysregulation.


06:08:48-06:08:52
Just as there are other factors I can't control that sometimes impair me, like being short.


06:08:52-06:08:58
But I didn't have to rely on just managing my impairments with literal and figurative step stools.


06:08:58-06:09:03
I could start to heal my trauma in therapy.


06:09:03-06:09:09
I could recognize my anxiety, practice sitting mindfully with my uncertainty, and work on setting healthy boundaries.


06:09:10-06:09:15
I could strengthen my self-esteem by expanding my vision of womanhood beyond the shoulds and supposed tos I grew up with.


06:09:15-06:09:23
There were aspects of my experience I could shift and aspects I couldn't, but they all played a role in my experience of ADHD.


06:09:23-06:09:31
As I began to emerge from the fog with this new perspective, I was learning how it applied to the people in my community too.


06:09:31-06:09:39
We have ADHD to connect us, but there are so many other factors that make our experiences unique and complicated.


06:09:39-06:09:43
Members of my community are ADHD and so many other things.


06:09:43-06:09:52
Autistic, Australian, gifted, queer, unhoused, immigrants, physically disabled, Southeast Asian, dealing with chronic pain.


06:09:52-06:09:54
The list could go on for pages.


06:09:54-06:10:15
As I learned about the impact these coexisting conditions, social identities, and other biopsychosocial factors have on their individual experiences with ADHD, I also began to understand how bias against ADHD and other disabilities intersects with bias against other social identities to compound the discrimination we face.


06:10:15-06:10:16
More on that later this chapter.


06:10:16-06:10:27
As I began intentionally listening for the complexities of my community's experience, I felt that I was seeing a lot of my community, really seeing them for the first time.


06:10:27-06:10:39
I'll never forget a comment a black member of the How to ADHD community left on an episode called What It's Like to be ADHD in Black, which was the project I did right before my mom died.


06:10:39-06:10:43
It said, we've been here, you didn't see us?


06:10:43-06:10:47
It broke my heart because the answer was no.


06:10:47-06:10:54
I hadn't considered the individual differences in lived experience because I've been so focused on ADHD to the exclusion of everything else.


06:10:54-06:11:05
Doctors, specialists, and ADHD experts, including me, tend to zero in on their area of specialty and often miss the bigger picture.


06:11:05-06:11:08
We, I, can do better.


06:11:08-06:11:15
We owe it to those who look to us for resources to see them as whole, complex, inimitable human persons.


06:11:15-06:11:17
What I learned.


06:11:17-06:11:23
With ADHD, coexisting conditions are more often the rule than the exception.


06:11:23-06:11:29
There are also biological and socioeconomic factors that impact our lived experience.


06:11:29-06:11:44
While I am just beginning to understand the complexities of this interplay, I felt it was important to try to tackle it because all of the factors in this chapter can impact not only our lived experience and access to care as people with ADHD, but how our ADHD even shows up.


06:11:44-06:11:51
It's impossible to give each factor the space it deserves or to cover it comprehensively here.


06:11:51-06:11:59
Each one could be its own book, but I did my best to include the most common or most impactful factors and include experiences from the community in their own words.


06:11:59-06:12:03
Psychological factors.


06:12:03-06:12:11
An estimated 60 to 80% of adults with ADHD have at least one of the conditions I'm about to mention.


06:12:11-06:12:18
These conditions are complex alone, but I've included at least one common way they can interplay with ADHD.


06:12:18-06:12:22
If you have one of these conditions, or if you treat them, hopefully you will be inspired to dig deeper.


06:12:24-06:12:27
Neurodevelopmental coexisting conditions common with ADHD.


06:12:27-06:12:34
Neurodevelopmental conditions involve differences in the development and function of the nervous system.


06:12:34-06:12:39
These conditions affect how our brains work on a fundamental structural level.


06:12:39-06:12:44
Think of these as our brain's operating system, or even firmware.


06:12:44-06:12:59
A tech savvy community member has pointed out to me that the operating system metaphor I generally use is flawed, since operating systems are installed rather than built into the computer, and that a better metaphor would be firmware, since firmware is what is in there before any other software can be installed.


06:12:59-06:13:01
Use the metaphor that resonates most with you.


06:13:01-06:13:08
Our functional impairments can be lessened, but the core differences in how our brains work don't go away.


06:13:08-06:13:11
The symptoms of those differences can only be managed or masked.


06:13:11-06:13:22
ADHD is a neurodevelopmental condition, but having one neurodevelopmental condition doesn't preclude you from having another, including autism.


06:13:22-06:13:38
In brains with autism and ADHD, or ADHD, if you like a portmanteau, restricted repetitive interests, plus ADHD related difficulty focusing on things that don't interest you, equals great difficulty focusing outside a narrow range of interests.


06:13:38-06:13:46
An ADHD friend joked to me that when ADHD people first meet, they might as well just see if they have any special interests in common right away.


06:13:46-06:13:48
If not, cool, nice to meet you, moving on.


06:13:49-06:13:54
Those with ADHD miss and misunderstand social cues for a variety of reasons.


06:13:54-06:14:04
But when they are also autistic, they may also have significant difficulty recognizing or reading them in the first place, which can make social connection very confusing.


06:14:04-06:14:06
Tick disorders.


06:14:06-06:14:16
Hard to control verbal or motor impulses can worsen social difficulties due to ADHD and increase the pressure to mask one's neurodivergence.


06:14:16-06:14:20
Tick disorders occur in less than 10% of the ADHD population.


06:14:20-06:14:25
However, 60 to 80% of those with Tourette's Syndrome also have ADHD.


06:14:25-06:14:29
Learning disabilities.


06:14:29-06:14:38
The prevalence rate for learning disabilities among ADHD brains is 43 to 55%, compared with 5 to 15% in the general population.


06:14:38-06:14:44
Common learning disabilities include dyslexia, a learning disability that affects reading and related skills.


06:14:45-06:14:49
ADHD brains often have trouble regulating their attention while reading.


06:14:49-06:14:54
When the reading process itself is a struggle, academic difficulties are compounded.


06:14:54-06:15:00
Dyscalculia, a learning disability that affects math related skills.


06:15:00-06:15:03
This disability affects life well beyond school.


06:15:03-06:15:06
Money management is already a challenge for those with ADHD.


06:15:06-06:15:08
Dyscalculia can make it overwhelming.


06:15:08-06:15:14
Dysgraphia, a learning and fine motor disability that affects handwriting.


06:15:14-06:15:26
Students with dysgraphia have difficulty writing quickly and legibly, which combined with ADHD difficulties related to organization and note taking, can make traditional study practices all but impossible without accommodations.


06:15:26-06:15:29
Giftedness.


06:15:29-06:15:36
Intellectually gifted students with ADHD may struggle to access support for either or both of these neurodifferences.


06:15:36-06:15:44
Attention and executive function challenges related to ADHD may prevent the twice exceptional student from having their intelligence recognized and challenged.


06:15:44-06:15:53
Likewise, rapidly acquired skills in a 2E student may impress teachers who then miss the opportunities to identify and support challenges in other areas.


06:15:53-06:16:01
The conditions I just described are the result of brains that develop in a way that diverges from neurotypical development.


06:16:01-06:16:06
This is why the term neurodivergent has come to be a preferred way to describe brains like ours.


06:16:06-06:16:13
Although not everyone likes using this term, since it centers neurotypicality as the norm, it helps people in our community find one another.


06:16:13-06:16:18
Personally, I like the incidental nod to the fact that we are often good at divergent thinking.


06:16:18-06:16:19
It's one of our strengths.


06:16:19-06:16:27
These differences in development mean that the symptoms of our conditions are written into the very structure of our brains.


06:16:27-06:16:32
Non-neurodevelopmental coexisting conditions common with ADHD.


06:16:32-06:16:39
Conversely, non-neurodevelopmental conditions are not a result of differences in brain development.


06:16:39-06:16:43
Think of them as software or malware.


06:16:43-06:16:50
They can change an individual's perceptions and thought processes, which can interact with our ADHD in profound and challenging ways.


06:16:50-06:16:59
Unlike the symptoms of neurodevelopmental conditions, the symptoms of these conditions can change with treatment, situation, or time.


06:16:59-06:17:05
In some brains, conditions like these may develop as secondary to the ADHD.


06:17:05-06:17:08
In others, they would occur regardless of whether the person has ADHD.


06:17:08-06:17:11
Depression.


06:17:11-06:17:18
One of the most common symptoms of depression is anhedonia, which is defined as markedly diminished interest or pleasure.


06:17:18-06:17:28
When ADHD motivation relies on interest and on us being able to reward ourselves for doing things that aren't interesting, anhedonia can be significantly disabling.


06:17:28-06:17:34
Depression can worsen the self-esteem issues that many ADHDers have.


06:17:34-06:17:38
Depression tells us we are useless, a failure, a burden.


06:17:39-06:17:47
Intrusive, judgmental thoughts like these make it easier to believe the negative labels and assessments we too often receive from the outside world.


06:17:47-06:17:49
Anxiety disorders.


06:17:49-06:17:59
Anxious rumination, basically thoughts cycling like a broken record of badness, is a common result of anxiety in ADHD.


06:17:59-06:18:08
Anxious thoughts keep us stuck in our heads, making it paradoxically more likely that we'll miss important information, which can lead to more errors, which leads to more anxiety.


06:18:08-06:18:11
Sometimes anxiety can lead to hypervigilance.


06:18:11-06:18:20
Because anxiety is convinced that it's helping us survive, anxious watchfulness is reinforced when it catches something we might otherwise have missed due to our ADHD.


06:18:20-06:18:27
Also, ADHD brains are motivated by urgency, and anxiety is great at creating a sense of urgency.


06:18:27-06:18:32
This can cause issues in ADHD brains who are treated for anxiety, but not ADHD.


06:18:32-06:18:36
They often find themselves struggling to be productive without the anxiety to propel them.


06:18:36-06:18:40
Oppositional Defiant Disorder, ODD.


06:18:40-06:18:52
In a clinic sample of adults with ADHD, 47% had been diagnosed with ODD, significantly higher than in the general population, where 4% received the diagnosis.


06:18:52-06:19:00
Many experts challenge the validity of ODD as a distinct disorder, particularly in people who also have ADHD.


06:19:00-06:19:08
Some see it as a collection of symptoms resulting from years of being compelled to do things in ways that don't work for their brain.


06:19:08-06:19:16
According to Dr. Sharon Salin, it's important to recognize that those who are oppositional aren't oppositional with everyone or every task.


06:19:16-06:19:21
Oppositional behavior is a relationship issue, and it is relationship dependent.


06:19:21-06:19:24
Sometimes the relationship issue is with the person.


06:19:24-06:19:27
Sometimes the issue is related to our relationship with the task.


06:19:27-06:19:30
Trauma-related disorders.


06:19:30-06:19:34
10% of adults with ADHD have post-traumatic stress disorder.


06:19:34-06:19:35
PTSD.


06:19:35-06:19:40
The incidence of PTSD in the general population is only 1%.


06:19:40-06:19:48
Some research implies that ADHD may put male veterans at a higher risk of developing PTSD than those without ADHD.


06:19:48-06:19:58
With both ADHD and trauma, people have intense reactions to specific triggers that can cause emotional flooding, which tanks our executive function.


06:19:58-06:20:08
The presence of trauma triggers worsens emotion dysregulation, and ADHD impulsivity can make us more likely to react to those triggers when they occur.


06:20:08-06:20:10
Substance use disorders.


06:20:10-06:20:16
Self-medicating is common with ADHD, particularly untreated ADHD.


06:20:16-06:20:26
Current alcohol and drug dependence is much greater in adults with ADHD than in neurotypical adults, 25% versus 13%, according to one study.


06:20:26-06:20:32
One common concern with prescribing stimulant medication is the potential for misuse and abuse.


06:20:32-06:20:38
Those with a history of substance use may not be given the option of stimulant medication at all.


06:20:38-06:20:45
The unfortunate irony is treatment with stimulant medication can actually decrease the risk for substance abuse in those with ADHD.


06:20:45-06:20:51
For the record, as prescribed, stimulant medication is not addictive.


06:20:51-06:20:55
As my community often jokes, if our meds are so addictive, why do we keep forgetting to take them?


06:20:55-06:21:05
When stimulant medication is abused, however, and taken at much higher doses, as it often is by those who don't need it and/or are using it recreationally, it can be.


06:21:05-06:21:09
Let's talk about eating issues.


06:21:09-06:21:19
According to Dr. Carolyn Lynch Parcells, a board certified pediatrician who works with adolescents with ADHD and eating disorders, there are some food issues that tend to be more common in ADHD.


06:21:19-06:21:22
Bulimia and binge eating disorders.


06:21:22-06:21:28
Binging and purging behaviors increase dopamine release in the brain.


06:21:28-06:21:32
For some people, this can help them cope maladaptively with their ADHD.


06:21:32-06:21:43
In addition to binging and purging behaviors, those who restrict and/or exercise excessively may also be using the resulting dopamine boosts to cope with underlying untreated ADHD.


06:21:43-06:21:48
Avoidant restrictive food intake disorder, or ARFID.


06:21:48-06:21:59
With this disorder, a person severely restricts the amount of nutrition they're taking in due to fear of either the food itself or what will happen as a result of eating the food.


06:21:59-06:22:06
ADHD related sensory issues and negative experiences around food can lead to the kind of anxious restricting typical of ARFID.


06:22:06-06:22:08
Disordered eating.


06:22:08-06:22:15
While a minority of our community develop eating disorders, a lot of us will experience disordered eating.


06:22:15-06:22:22
Eating or thinking about food in a way that negatively impacts our lives but does not meet the criteria for an eating disorder.


06:22:23-06:22:36
Disordered eating can be influenced by body image issues, but it can also be due to sensory issues, interoception issues, and/or executive function deficits, which make it difficult for us to meal plan, shop, and prep.


06:22:36-06:22:39
Why do we wanna eat so much when we go off our meds?


06:22:39-06:22:46
Dr. Lynch Parcells also answers this question that many people in our community ask.


06:22:46-06:22:53
Stimulant medication may suppress our appetite during the day, so we might not eat enough to keep our functions functioning.


06:22:53-06:22:57
When the meds wear off, our brain might say to us, dude, we need more calories, go get some.


06:22:57-06:23:13
Conversely, if we are eating sufficiently but then go off our meds and nom our way through the fridge like Ms. Pac-Man, there could be a couple of reasons why, including your ADHD impulsivity is no longer being treated, so when you see food, you eat food.


06:23:13-06:23:21
Your brain might not be getting adequate levels of dopamine after your meds have worn off, so it is not happy that it's back on the struggle bus.


06:23:21-06:23:23
I need dopamine, dude.


06:23:23-06:23:24
I know what gives me dopamine.


06:23:24-06:23:25
Where are the cookies?


06:23:25-06:23:27
Biological factors.


06:23:27-06:23:34
Sometimes with all our talk about brains, it's easy to forget that, yo, we also have bodies.


06:23:34-06:23:39
Even our brains themselves are just one part of a whole nervous system that affects every part of us.


06:23:39-06:23:42
Let's talk about how our brains and bodies cooperate with each other, or don't.


06:23:42-06:23:45
Hormones.


06:23:45-06:23:52
ADHD is often detected during or right after puberty, due to the rapid hormonal changes involved.


06:23:52-06:24:01
Higher levels of testosterone, which increases sevenfold during male puberty, have been linked to more impulsive decisions and greater hyperactivity.


06:24:01-06:24:05
Estrogen, on the other hand, is associated with dopamine production.


06:24:05-06:24:09
When estrogen levels rise, dopamine levels rise too.


06:24:09-06:24:18
Estrogen levels fluctuate throughout the menstrual cycle, which can worsen ADHD symptoms during the premenstrual phase, the days or week leading up to our periods.


06:24:18-06:24:21
And they drop significantly post-pregnancy and during menopause.


06:24:21-06:24:23
Biological sex.


06:24:23-06:24:32
Cisgender boys tend to display more of the hyperactive traits of ADHD, while cisgender girls tend to have more of the inattentive ones.


06:24:32-06:24:45
According to research, girls are less likely to be referred for diagnosis, less likely to be diagnosed with ADHD if they are referred, and less likely to be treated for ADHD if they are diagnosed.


06:24:45-06:24:51
However, ADHD can also be missed in boys and men who have a more internalized presentation too.


06:24:51-06:24:53
Age.


06:24:53-06:24:56
ADHD presentation changes with age.


06:24:56-06:25:00
As we get older, our likelihood to present inattentively goes up.


06:25:00-06:25:07
Similarly, impulsivity and hyperactivity symptoms evolve over time, especially as demands change.


06:25:07-06:25:14
While an impulsive child might run into the street, an impulsive adult might quit a job or say yes to an extra project, or five.


06:25:14-06:25:21
Executive function develops more with age, and we learn coping skills, both adaptive and maladaptive, as we go.


06:25:21-06:25:24
Pregnancy.


06:25:24-06:25:30
Existing research on the impact of stimulant medications taken during pregnancy suggests the risk of complications is minimal to none.


06:25:30-06:25:39
However, the limited nature of this research means that some doctors are not comfortable allowing their patients to continue those medications once they become pregnant.


06:25:40-06:25:50
Meanwhile, as my own psychiatrist explained to me, ADHD significantly impacts our ability to handle stress, which we know negatively affects a growing fetus.


06:25:50-06:25:56
And recent research has highlighted the negative impact of discontinuing meds due to pregnancy on parents-to-be.


06:25:56-06:25:58
Migraines.


06:25:58-06:26:02
Migraine disorders are common in the ADHD community.


06:26:02-06:26:08
In one study, men with ADHD were twice as likely to experience migraines compared to non-ADHD men.


06:26:08-06:26:14
Migraines don't always involve pain, as I discovered when I began experiencing migraines with aura.


06:26:14-06:26:18
I could have an amazing brain day but be unable to write because I couldn't see the screen.


06:26:18-06:26:20
Chronic pain.


06:26:20-06:26:29
Those with chronic pain deal with having good, moderate, or bad pain days, which get more complicated when combined with good, moderate, or bad brain days.


06:26:29-06:26:36
Pain at any level is distracting, and ADHD brains aren't great at filtering out sensory distractions.


06:26:36-06:26:41
Chronic pain can also make us avoid activities that support our mental and physical health.


06:26:41-06:26:47
Doing things we love increases dopamine, which boosts our mood and decreases our experience of pain.


06:26:47-06:26:55
Avoidance of activities that boost our dopamine due to bad pain days can lead to more bad brain days because we aren't getting the dopamine needed for us to focus.


06:26:55-06:27:07
It's important to note that any chronic condition can be more serious and difficult to treat in the presence of ADHD, because it may require extensive follow-up or complicated medication management.


06:27:07-06:27:11
Treatment compliance is difficult when we have trouble complying with life.


06:27:11-06:27:21
Rather than denying treatment to those who struggle with compliance, providers should collaborate with patients to find treatment plans that work with their patients' brains as well as their bodies.


06:27:21-06:27:26
Similarly, ADHD brains struggle with regular preventative healthcare.


06:27:26-06:27:35
For many of us, this involves navigating a minefield of executive function traps, complicated healthcare systems, scheduling, and insurance issues.


06:27:35-06:27:42
That doesn't even cover the everyday challenges of showing up to appointments on time and filling out the correct paperwork correctly.


06:27:42-06:27:51
Plus, since the nature of preventative healthcare is that it's not urgent, it's much harder for us to follow through on annual exams, screenings, dental cleanings, and so on.


06:27:51-06:27:55
Stigma, biases, and discrimination.


06:27:55-06:27:59
There is significant stigma around ADHD.


06:27:59-06:28:04
Misconceptions and stereotypes about those who have it are rampant.


06:28:04-06:28:07
The resulting biases lead to prejudice and discrimination.


06:28:07-06:28:10
More specifically, ableism.


06:28:10-06:28:24
Ableism is discrimination and social prejudice against people with disabilities, including ADHD, based on the belief that neurotypical abilities or those with neurotypical abilities are inherently better or more valuable.


06:28:24-06:28:30
You can find examples of ableism on display in many environments, even a kindergarten classroom.


06:28:30-06:28:34
Posters often outline the following or similar rules for good listening.


06:28:34-06:28:41
Eyes are watching, ears are listening, lips are closed, hands are still, feet are quiet.


06:28:41-06:28:47
We're not only expected to follow these rules, some of which can hurt our ability to focus, but we're often punished when we can't.


06:28:47-06:28:59
Those with ADHD receive regular messages throughout our lives that it is socially unacceptable and even unsafe to exhibit ADHD or other neurodivergent traits.


06:28:59-06:29:05
Or to engage in many of the coping mechanisms we use to navigate social situations and meet expectations.


06:29:05-06:29:10
We experience overt and covert discrimination on a routine basis.


06:29:10-06:29:20
The bias that leads to discrimination against those with ADHD often intersects with the bias against other marginalized identities, amplifying the discrimination.


06:29:20-06:29:31
For instance, bias against the LGBTQIA+ population can result in homophobia and transphobia, which can compound the ableism those with ADHD experience.


06:29:31-06:29:47
In the United States, black children who are disruptive in class are more likely to be punished than similarly disruptive white children due to the role of racial bias in painting school adults' views of African American youth as less innocent, older, and more aggressive than their white peers.


06:29:48-06:29:53
In pretty much any society, people face discrimination for many possible reasons.


06:29:53-06:30:02
Gender, religion, national origin, ethnicity, sexual orientation, medical or disability status, and more.


06:30:02-06:30:07
Each one of these intersectionalities can affect any and all of the factors we'll be talking about in a moment.


06:30:07-06:30:14
All of this is rooted in stigma, the belief that some identities or ways of being aren't acceptable and don't belong.


06:30:16-06:30:19
Socioevironmental factors.


06:30:19-06:30:22
We have our brains, which are integrated into our bodies.


06:30:22-06:30:31
These bodies are also integrated into complex cultural, economic, and environmental systems that span the whole globe and possibly space.


06:30:31-06:30:38
The way we interact with these systems and the way these systems interact with us can deeply affect our lived experience as ADHDers.


06:30:38-06:30:41
Culture.


06:30:41-06:30:49
ADHD exists in the same presentations across cultures, but not every culture acknowledges, views, and supports it in the same way.


06:30:49-06:30:56
Some cultures are more accepting and supportive of mental health challenges than others, and there are cultural differences in treatment approaches as well.


06:30:56-06:31:13
While studies have found significant differences in reported worldwide prevalence of ADHD, a systematic review of those studies found that these differences could be attributed to cultural differences in the diagnostic criteria used, as well as in how impairment is evaluated.


06:31:13-06:31:19
In other words, cultural context seems to be what is driving these differences, not variability in the existence of ADHD.


06:31:19-06:31:27
In addition, values differ among cultures, and some may align more with our brains' tendencies than others.


06:31:27-06:31:34
For example, some cultures are more relaxed about time, which makes it easier for ADHD brains with time-nearsightedness to function.


06:31:34-06:31:39
Socialization/societal expectations.


06:31:39-06:31:44
Those with ADHD often find themselves out of step with societal expectations.


06:31:44-06:31:47
ADHDers are more often gender nonconforming.


06:31:47-06:31:55
There are many reasons for this, one of them being that we commonly have difficulties meeting often gender-based societal expectations.


06:31:55-06:32:00
Societal expectations vary depending on the culture you come from and exist within.


06:32:00-06:32:06
And we often contend with multiple subcultures that each demand different things from us.


06:32:06-06:32:17
However, meeting any expectations from work, friends, family, society, and our political or ideological communities requires executive function.


06:32:17-06:32:23
And trying to meet all of these expectations often overloads our capacity to cope with competing demands.


06:32:23-06:32:25
Religion.


06:32:25-06:32:34
Many people, including those with ADHD, turn to religion to experience a sense of hope, comfort, belonging, and tangible social support.


06:32:34-06:32:42
Unfortunately, ADHD-related cognitive impairments can be perceived as a moral issue in some traditions.


06:32:42-06:32:50
And those struggling may be expected to turn toward religious tenets or their higher powers for support, providence, or even to pray it away.


06:32:50-06:32:56
While religion offers many forms of support, it can't always provide the kind of support we need.


06:32:56-06:33:08
In some cases, those with ADHD can feel like a bad Catholic/Christian/Muslim, etc., because their impairments make it difficult to do things quote unquote right, which alienates them from their religion.


06:33:08-06:33:11
Ethnicity and race.


06:33:11-06:33:17
We know that diagnosis and treatment rates for ADHD vary widely by race as a result of many factors.


06:33:17-06:33:27
For example, one recent study in the US found that black adults were 77% less likely to receive an ADHD diagnosis than white adults.


06:33:27-06:33:33
But black youths were 24% more likely to receive a diagnosis compared to white youths.


06:33:33-06:33:45
Another study found that black and Latinx children have lower odds of being diagnosed with ADHD and of taking ADHD medication compared to white children when controlling for socioeconomic status.


06:33:45-06:33:50
Compared to white children, Asian children had the highest odds of receiving no treatment.


06:33:50-06:33:59
These numbers highlight a clear racial disparity in how ADHD is diagnosed and treated, which also impacts outcome.


06:34:00-06:34:02
Socioeconomic status.


06:34:02-06:34:17
ADHD is much less impairing when you can afford to replace the stuff you've lost or broken, take a day off without impacting your ability to pay the bills, order takeout when you can't figure out what to cook, and hire help for day-to-day tasks.


06:34:17-06:34:20
Most people with ADHD don't have room in their budgets for these accommodations.


06:34:20-06:34:24
Access to care.


06:34:24-06:34:29
Accessing appropriate care and treatment is not a universal given for ADHD brains around the world.


06:34:29-06:34:31
The obstacles are many.


06:34:31-06:34:35
As I write this, the United States is dealing with a stimulant medication shortage.


06:34:35-06:34:38
The NHS in the UK has a years-long waiting list for evaluation.


06:34:38-06:34:44
In other countries, such as Russia, stimulant medications are straight up illegal.


06:34:44-06:34:50
While there are wonderful therapists who specialize in adult ADHD, they are not nearly enough.


06:34:50-06:34:58
And when you add other conditions and identities, especially marginalized identities, into the mix, it becomes exponentially harder to find a good provider fit.


06:34:58-06:35:01
Changing demands.


06:35:01-06:35:12
Certain life events, moving, marriage, starting a family, changing careers, taking care of aging parents, come with significantly increased demands on executive function.


06:35:12-06:35:18
Someone who starts college, for example, may experience what researchers call a double deficit.


06:35:18-06:35:39
They may struggle simultaneously with increased demands on their executive function from tasks, such as registering for classes, navigating a new campus, accessing accommodations, feeding themselves, as well as the new social context and additional social pressures that come with making new friends, living with roommates, dating, and attending parties.


06:35:39-06:35:48
Many people don't seek a diagnosis and treatment for their ADHD until the demands on their executive function overwhelm their ability to cope.


06:35:48-06:35:53
Unfortunately, this means we're often already drowning by the time we seek help.


06:35:53-06:36:00
We are now faced with understanding and managing our ADHD for the first time, despite being overwhelmed already.


06:36:00-06:36:13
Too often, those in a position to offer support and treatment fail to realize that their newly diagnosed patients aren't always in a place to implement many new strategies due to these excessive demands.


06:36:13-06:36:16
There's interplay among all these factors, too.


06:36:16-06:36:20
Some factors can improve access to treatment and support.


06:36:20-06:36:24
Some factors can be simultaneously positive and detrimental.


06:36:24-06:36:26
Others are undeniably negative.


06:36:26-06:36:35
Regardless, it's important to understand that there is an interplay not just between these factors and our ADHD, but between all of these factors as well.


06:36:35-06:36:51
For example, some people may have a higher genetic predisposition to anxiety, but the genes don't get switched on and trigger the development of an anxiety disorder until the effects of certain socio-environmental factors combine with the effects of living with ADHD.


06:36:51-06:36:56
Our DNA is like a library full of books, and only the genes that get read are expressed.


06:36:56-06:37:02
Epigenetics, the study of how genes switch on and off based on environmental factors, is super cool.


06:37:02-06:37:04
Seriously, Google it.


06:37:04-06:37:10
Cultural differences, medical discrimination, and lack of access to care may lead to that anxiety being untreated.


06:37:10-06:37:16
And untreated anxiety can lead to depression, all of which can make the ADHD more difficult to manage.


06:37:16-06:37:25
