The pressure on students to excel academically and the pressure on parents to support their children's performance in certain cultures can be beneficial for students with ADHD.


06:37:25-06:37:33
For example, the parents of a student struggling in school may engage a tutor that works with their learning style, which can lead to a positive outcome.


06:37:33-06:37:43
However, that same cultural emphasis on academic achievement can cause parents to feel stress and shame about their child's difficulties.


06:37:43-06:37:48
It may incentivize the parents to stay up until 2 a.m. every night to help their children with homework.


06:37:48-06:37:55
It may also discourage them from seeking mental health or learning support for their child because they think they just aren't doing enough.


06:37:55-06:38:03
In my case, my mom's death pushed me to face and process my childhood trauma, which improved my mental health overall.


06:38:03-06:38:14
But this was possible only because I had a support system that helped me find a trauma therapist, as well as a flexible schedule and supportive team that allowed me to schedule 3 p.m. sessions.


06:38:14-06:38:23
Differences in outcome can't be reduced to effort, virtue, or even the specific diagnoses that someone has.


06:38:23-06:38:29
There is interplay between many other factors, and they all impact our lived experience of ADHD.


06:38:29-06:38:41
Attempting to pick apart the interplay of various factors can be overwhelming, but acknowledging them is critical to effectively supporting someone with ADHD, especially if that person is yourself.


06:38:41-06:38:43
It's not ADHD alone.


06:38:43-06:38:46
It's ADHD and.


06:38:46-06:38:55
I asked my community to share how the interplay between ADHD and other factors, including their intersectionalities, affects their everyday life.


06:38:55-06:39:06
I read story after story, days on end, and was left with a greater understanding of how webbed, wonderful, and often woeful our individual experiences are.


06:39:06-06:39:08
I want to share a bit of that with you.


06:39:08-06:39:18
Heads up, some of these stories include frank discussions of topics like medical conditions, racism, transphobia, homophobia, and disordered eating.


06:39:18-06:39:21
Tiger T, 47, Maryland.


06:39:21-06:39:25
Autism and ADHD often ends up in an interesting dichotomy in my head.


06:39:25-06:39:28
Routines are good, but then boring.


06:39:28-06:39:32
We need to be stimulated, but not too stimulated.


06:39:32-06:39:40
Burnout comes from both sides of the equation, and many systems that are really ADHD defense mechanisms were created by the autistic side of me.


06:39:40-06:39:43
Lee D, 39, Massachusetts.


06:39:43-06:39:48
Being gay and having ADHD is a double coming out process for me.


06:39:48-06:39:58
I worry I'll be judged for these factors that are beyond my control, and I'm always at least a little bit afraid of the consequences of telling someone who isn't understanding.


06:39:58-06:40:01
Seren S, 35, Canada.


06:40:01-06:40:11
If you have ADHD, you are much more likely than the non-ADHD population to also have hypermobility, or EDS, Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, and dysautonomia.


06:40:11-06:40:19
If you do, some behaviors or struggles that might have been attributed to your ADHD may be adaptive strategies for your body.


06:40:19-06:40:27
For example, always moving to mitigate joint pain, fidgeting, or sitting strangely to manage orthostatic intolerance.


06:40:27-06:40:32
I've also found that my executive functioning is significantly worsened by pain from my joint issues.


06:40:32-06:40:35
Tracy L, 38, California.


06:40:35-06:40:41
ADHD and fat phobia can both lead you to overcompensate to prove you're not lazy.


06:40:41-06:40:52
Rejection sensitivity is also worse in a world that is not built to accommodate your body, and constantly tells you that these feelings of exclusion are your fault or something you can control by being more disciplined.


06:40:52-06:40:56
NCM, 37, United Kingdom.


06:40:56-06:41:02
When you're born into a poor family with uneducated parents, finding out what's wrong with you is much harder.


06:41:02-06:41:09
There was no one to keep me accountable at home, and teachers just assumed I didn't want to do any schoolwork because I was poor.


06:41:09-06:41:11
This never gets talked about.


06:41:11-06:41:13
You only hear middle class perspectives.


06:41:13-06:41:17
Mark J, 63, California.


06:41:17-06:41:20
I lived with undiagnosed ADHD until my 40s.


06:41:20-06:41:25
I'm also a third culture kid who grew up outside the culture of my passport.


06:41:25-06:41:27
I was always wrong.


06:41:27-06:41:32
It got to the point in Scottish Catholic school where I would take the blame because the leather strap didn't hurt my hands anymore.


06:41:32-06:41:36
Ying Di, 26, Virginia.


06:41:36-06:41:41
Just because I might look organized from the outside, it doesn't mean that's the case in my brain.


06:41:41-06:41:47
Being overly on top of paperwork is necessary masking for my Asian first generation immigrant ADHD brain.


06:41:47-06:41:52
Rex M, 34, North Carolina.


06:41:52-06:41:54
I'm trans and ADHD.


06:41:54-06:41:59
I'm not comfortable leaving the house without a binder, but I'm also not comfortable wearing a binder because of sensory issues with clothing.


06:42:00-06:42:03
This means I'm just not comfortable leaving the house.


06:42:03-06:42:07
Cecilia C, 37, Mexico.


06:42:07-06:42:13
Having ADHD and hypothyroidism makes you extra forgetful and extra depressive even when in treatment.


06:42:13-06:42:19
Hypothyroidism also drains your energy in the already difficult circumstances of not getting enough dopamine to start a task.


06:42:19-06:42:23
Emerus H, 32, California.


06:42:23-06:42:27
My disordered eating started because I was bad at managing money.


06:42:27-06:42:29
Hello, ADHD.


06:42:29-06:42:32
I still remember the first time I completely ran out of food.


06:42:32-06:42:40
It's difficult to fully describe the nightmare of starving for days, not by any choice, but because I'd entirely run out of food.


06:42:40-06:42:47
When I got my hands on food, I quickly learned that if I ate it all, I would have nothing left and would be just as tortured as I had been before.


06:42:47-06:42:52
If I only ate a little, I would still have food later and wouldn't suffer from having nothing at all.


06:42:52-06:42:59
In this way, restricting became associated with safety and ironically with the avoidance of total starvation.


06:42:59-06:43:02
It became ingrained and instinctive.


06:43:02-06:43:09
Even now, during periods of stress like finals, I have to actively override the instinct to restrict my intake.


06:43:09-06:43:12
Genova L, 34, Michigan.


06:43:12-06:43:20
I have congenital nystagmus and because my eyes are of different strengths, I developed a head tilt when looking at things.


06:43:20-06:43:28
This, along with my ADHD ditziness, were dismissed as cute little girl things until I reached an age where they were more annoying than endearing to those around me.


06:43:28-06:43:40
It took me reaching sixth grade for my visual impairment to be diagnosed and to receive an IEP, individualized education plan, for classroom accommodations.


06:43:40-06:43:43
My teachers largely proceeded to ignore them unless I fought for them.


06:43:43-06:43:47
Julia F, 38, New Zealand.


06:43:47-06:43:52
The intersection between ADHD and socioeconomic status is rough.


06:43:52-06:43:55
So many strategies that would help my executive dysfunction require money.


06:43:55-06:44:01
Getting assessed in the first place is so expensive that it's a barrier for many people.


06:44:01-06:44:11
It's common knowledge in the ADHD community here that there's no point going on the waiting list to be seen under the public system because they won't diagnose you unless your life is literally falling apart.


06:44:11-06:44:19
I was lucky enough to have the money to get assessed privately, but you need a follow up every two years to renew the special authority to get stimulants.


06:44:19-06:44:21
That appointment is expensive too.


06:44:21-06:44:27
There's also a prescription fee every month, not every three months as usual, because they are controlled drugs.


06:44:27-06:44:31
If you don't have money, you run into so many barriers along the way to get the help you need.


06:44:31-06:44:35
Ike A, 23, Illinois.


06:44:35-06:44:42
Part of the reason it's so difficult for black people to get treatment is because of medical racism and the distrust our community has for the medical system.


06:44:42-06:44:46
This means even if we are diagnosed, we're still not likely to get help.


06:44:46-06:44:49
Kiri S, 32, California.


06:44:50-06:44:57
I inadvertently masked my symptoms because I had to be, quote unquote, twice as good as a young black guy.


06:44:57-06:45:00
Many ADHD traits overlap with stereotypes.


06:45:00-06:45:07
Acting impulsively would have had a higher chance of being seen as deviant rather than, maybe he should get tested for ADHD.


06:45:07-06:45:10
The Toolbox.


06:45:10-06:45:25
I read through hundreds of quotes like these in awe of others' resilience, hopeful at the occasional protective factors, but more often, sobbing at how we have to fight so hard to navigate such complex struggles, largely on our own.


06:45:25-06:45:33
It would be impossible to offer targeted strategies for every interplay of factors, but there are some general principles that seem to hold true for many of us.


06:45:33-06:45:37
One, learn what you're dealing with.


06:45:37-06:45:46
For many, especially those of us with limited access to care, their journey towards seeking help starts with self-suspecting or even self-diagnosing.


06:45:46-06:45:53
If that's true for you, it can help to seek a professional diagnosis if and when you can to see what else may be going on.


06:45:53-06:46:02
There is an opportunity cost involved in not understanding all the factors you are dealing with as soon as possible, because there may be more effective ways to address them.


06:46:02-06:46:13
An accurate diagnosis or diagnoses can save years of wasted time and effort addressing a condition you don't have or one that is secondary to an underlying condition.


06:46:14-06:46:21
This is a heartbreaking but not uncommon experience, since the symptoms of ADHD resemble those of other conditions and vice versa.


06:46:21-06:46:30
A community member shared with me that they were diagnosed and treated unsuccessfully for depression for two decades before their ADHD was recognized.


06:46:30-06:46:34
And that treating the ADHD ultimately helped with the depression too.


06:46:34-06:46:42
Another community member and friend, a fierce, brilliant trans woman and coder, suspected she was autistic as well as ADHD.


06:46:42-06:46:55
When she was finally able to access a neuropsych evaluation, however, she was shocked to learn the rigidity she'd been attributing to her suspected autism was actually due to severe OCD, obsessive compulsive disorder.


06:46:55-06:47:04
Because she assumed it was due to autism, she didn't know to seek treatment for her OCD until her magical thinking and agoraphobia had gotten so bad that she couldn't leave the house.


06:47:04-06:47:10
While psychology is an imperfect science, there is still a science to it.


06:47:10-06:47:24
To maximize your chances of an accurate diagnosis, find a provider who has training in, and ideally specializes in, the conditions you suspect you have, as well as understands the cultural nuances that may impact your presentation slash experience.


06:47:24-06:47:40
Someone who specializes in your condition and population, women, LGBTQIA+, BIPOC, etc., will be most likely to accurately identify whether you fit the parameters of that condition, as well as educate you on your options for minimizing the impairments associated with it.


06:47:40-06:47:42
This may not always be possible.


06:47:42-06:47:52
If you feel as though a provider you do have access to is ruling out something prematurely, or that they've misdiagnosed you entirely, don't be afraid to ask, why are you ruling this out?


06:47:52-06:47:55
Or why this diagnosis and not that?


06:47:55-06:48:00
If their answer feels off to you, get a second opinion.


06:48:00-06:48:04
My first doctor told my mom, she can't have ADHD, she's too smart.


06:48:04-06:48:09
ADHD kids can also be gifted, they can even grow up to be authors.


06:48:09-06:48:15
Finally, even if you don't officially qualify for a diagnosis, you can still struggle with some of the symptoms of a condition.


06:48:15-06:48:19
Use strategies that work for you, regardless of what you're diagnosed with or not.


06:48:19-06:48:23
Two, think holistically.


06:48:23-06:48:28
When looking for tools and treatment, it's important to consider all the factors involved.


06:48:28-06:48:33
Effective treatment and support take the whole person into account, not just a single condition.


06:48:33-06:48:37
It's also important to consider what works from a variety of different sources.


06:48:37-06:48:40
You don't have to get all your tools from the same store.


06:48:40-06:48:42
Get curious.


06:48:42-06:48:47
When you're trying to support yourself, don't assume every struggle is rooted in ADHD alone.


06:48:47-06:48:50
Could it be ADHD and cultural expectations?


06:48:50-06:48:52
Anxiety?


06:48:52-06:48:53
Do you really have to pee?


06:48:53-06:48:59
Approaching a problem from another perspective might open your mind to tools and strategies you might not have considered otherwise.


06:48:59-06:49:04
Understand that you may need to treat other conditions first.


06:49:04-06:49:08
Maybe you have ADHD, but it's hard to tell through the fog of depression.


06:49:08-06:49:15
Or maybe you'd benefit from an ADHD coaching group, but you won't be able to participate effectively until you get support for your PTSD.


06:49:15-06:49:19
Keep other factors in mind when considering strategies.


06:49:19-06:49:25
It's true, using trendy, cool new system might help you with your time management issues, but can you afford it?


06:49:25-06:49:28
Is your anxiety going to be a barrier to using it?


06:49:28-06:49:32
Is it compatible with, wave's hand, the rest of your life?


06:49:32-06:49:36
On the flip side, what resources do you have access to that others do not?


06:49:36-06:49:40
Look for care that is informed about your intersectionalities.


06:49:40-06:49:49
Examples of this type of care include trauma-informed care, disability-informed practices, BIPOC and LGBTQIA+ affirming care.


06:49:49-06:49:59
Research shows that the therapeutic relationship is the greatest predictor of success, and the most productive therapeutic relationships are based on trust, support, and understanding.


06:49:59-06:50:06
While it might take longer or be more difficult or expensive to find such care, there is often a better return on your investment.


06:50:06-06:50:09
Make a plan for when things aren't OK.


06:50:09-06:50:14
My favorite example of this is a wellness recovery action plan, RAP.


06:50:14-06:50:21
This was designed by a group of people struggling with mental health challenges and has since been recognized as an evidence-based practice.


06:50:21-06:50:29
Creating a RAP plan can help you to identify the tools that keep you well and create action plans to put them into practice in everyday life.


06:50:29-06:50:34
You can find a link to learn more about this at howtoadhdbook.com.


06:50:34-06:50:41
Three, seek community with people who share your coexisting conditions, identities, and background.


06:50:41-06:50:48
There is robust literature showing that for ADHD brains, interacting with others who share your diagnosis can increase support and reduce stigma.


06:50:48-06:50:52
I've seen this again and again in the How to ADHD community.


06:50:52-06:50:57
Our community is intentionally built to be as inclusive to those who are neurodivergent as possible.


06:50:57-06:51:15
It includes brains and hearts from all around the world who have enthusiastic conversations about shared experiences, individual factors, and their individual intersectionalities, which serve to normalize those experiences, help us practice self-advocating, and increase compassion and understanding of other stigmatized differences.


06:51:15-06:51:22
Of course, there are going to be limitations to any community in terms of finding the understanding and social support you need.


06:51:22-06:51:34
For example, the main purpose of the How to ADHD community is to facilitate discussions about, well, ADHD, and to be accessible for ADHD brains and the hearts who love them, not just in the US, but internationally.


06:51:34-06:51:38
Our community guidelines are built with those goals in mind.


06:51:38-06:51:48
Each community serves a purpose, and even the most experienced community managers and moderators have their hands full trying to meet the needs of the group as a whole in service of that purpose.


06:51:48-06:51:55
Because of that, no one community is likely to meet all of our individual needs for understanding and belonging.


06:51:55-06:52:04
As social creatures, we all need and deserve understanding and belonging, not for one aspect of our identity, but for all of them.


06:52:04-06:52:08
If we have multiple stigmatized identities, we may need more than one community, too.


06:52:08-06:52:14
There are, however, also communities designed to support specific intersections of ADHD and.


06:52:14-06:52:18
You can find a list of the ones we are aware of at howtoadhdbook.com.


06:52:18-06:52:24
We don't vet these groups, but each one is contributed from someone in our community who has found it helpful for them.


06:52:24-06:52:29
I encourage people to, when they can, also find supports dedicated toward whatever else they're going through.


06:52:29-06:52:34
We have other life experiences that deserve specific connection and support, too.


06:52:34-06:52:40
Personally, I attend a peer support group for those who may need to use third-party donors to be able to conceive.


06:52:40-06:52:44
It's often worth finding more than one group when you're going through more than one thing.


06:52:44-06:52:47
This is especially true if you're seen as a leader in your group.


06:52:47-06:52:53
If people are used to leaning on you for support, they might not be able to switch to giving you the support you need.


06:52:53-06:52:56
It's good to have groups where you can just participate, too.


06:52:56-06:53:00
Connection with others who get it is healing.


06:53:00-06:53:03
Four, respect your own journey.


06:53:03-06:53:10
Again, to effectively manage our ADHD, we sometimes need to zoom out and consider the interplay of other factors involved.


06:53:10-06:53:13
We might also zoom out so we can look around and see what others are doing.


06:53:13-06:53:15
How have they made it work?


06:53:15-06:53:20
After that, though, we need to zoom in to check in with our inner compass.


06:53:20-06:53:22
Where do I want to go from here?


06:53:22-06:53:24
What makes sense for me?


06:53:24-06:53:33
Sometimes societal pressures to use or not use a particular strategy or treatment override our own sense of what we need or want to try first.


06:53:33-06:53:38
Or whether we even want to work on our stuff right now, maybe we need a break.


06:53:38-06:53:45
We can't make others agree or cooperate, but as adults, we can decide what to try or not try ourselves.


06:53:45-06:53:50
What we choose might look different from what research suggests.


06:53:50-06:53:52
It also might look different than others expect.


06:53:52-06:53:57
What others do or suggest is often based on their needs and experience and values.


06:53:57-06:54:01
It's important to do what makes sense for ours.


06:54:01-06:54:03
Labels.


06:54:03-06:54:05
I talk about a lot of labels in this chapter.


06:54:05-06:54:13
For some, these labels can get overwhelming, especially when it comes to communicating them to concerned family, friends, and colleagues.


06:54:13-06:54:17
A lot of people aren't even comfortable with the label ADHD.


06:54:17-06:54:23
I hear from parents who hesitate to get their kids diagnosed because they're afraid of their child being limited by labels.


06:54:23-06:54:26
There is an understandable fear there.


06:54:26-06:54:30
To those parents, I gently say, your child already has labels.


06:54:30-06:54:37
Teachers, peers, and family all apply labels to your child as an attempt to explain behaviors they don't understand.


06:54:37-06:54:50
Labels such as lazy, messy, spacey, and irresponsible are more stigmatizing, more shame inducing, less accurate, and much, much less helpful than any diagnostic term will be.


06:54:50-06:54:54
Having an accurate label for our experience helps us understand it.


06:54:54-06:54:56
It shows us what we can do about it.


06:54:56-06:55:00
It gives us access to treatment, accommodations, and other supports.


06:55:00-06:55:09
While the label itself may seem limiting, the understanding and treatment that we can access as a result can make us more functional and capable, not less.


06:55:09-06:55:14
It's important to understand how any piece of equipment we work with works.


06:55:14-06:55:18
And our brain is one we have to work with every day in everything we do.


06:55:18-06:55:28
In 2022, Renee Brooks, creator of Black Girl Lost Keys, gave the lived experience keynote address at the International ADHD Conference.


06:55:28-06:55:31
Those keynotes are typically 75 minutes long.


06:55:31-06:55:33
Renee's was only 10 minutes.


06:55:33-06:55:39
But in those 10 minutes, she gave a keynote so powerful it didn't need the other 65.


06:55:39-06:55:45
The title of her talk was Relabeling Yourself After an ADHD Diagnosis.


06:55:45-06:55:50
She told the audience that it was the label ADHD that had given her access to the label she wanted.


06:55:50-06:55:53
Good partner, good employee, good friend.


06:55:53-06:55:59
Too often, she said, people gatekeep those labels for us.


06:55:59-06:56:03
This label, ADHD, gives us access.


06:56:03-06:56:09
More than that, she said, it had given her access to a community of others who shared that label.


06:56:09-06:56:14
People she could call when she was struggling, isolated, or breaking down.


06:56:15-06:56:22
We were all crying by this point of the keynote, hugging each other, glancing knowingly across the room, connecting with one another.


06:56:22-06:56:28
People we'd never met, people who carried other labels we did not, but who shared this understanding.


06:56:28-06:56:32
We all knew what it was like to be denied access to the labels we wanted.


06:56:32-06:56:36
We knew how important it was to see and be seen by people who understood.


06:56:36-06:56:45
ADHD has a remarkable ability to connect people with different types of intersectionalities all around the world through a single label.


06:56:45-06:56:53
There is a kinship, empathy, and shared understanding that requires no words to describe, while also making space for way too many.


06:56:53-06:57:03
Stereotypical media portrayals may paint us all with one brush, but connecting with one another is opening our eyes not only to our own unique journey, but to the journeys of others.


06:57:03-06:57:11
After Renee's keynote, some of us recorded an episode about the experience for Brendan Mahan's podcast, ADHD Essentials.


06:57:11-06:57:17
What you might not notice in that episode is the way we truly saw one another for the first time.


06:57:17-06:57:24
We saw how lonely each of us had been despite our connection to one another, because we each had struggles that the others knew nothing of.


06:57:24-06:57:30
We hadn't felt it was appropriate to share them since we were at the conference to talk about ADHD.


06:57:30-06:57:37
But keeping them hidden had caused us individual pain and kept us from relating to each other as authentically as we might have otherwise.


06:57:38-06:57:44
We made a commitment to one another that we wouldn't have to be alone in our struggles, ADHD related or otherwise, not anymore.


06:57:44-06:57:54
Although recognizing the label we had in common had connected us, we had learned that true authenticity meant listening to the and in one another's stories.


06:57:54-06:58:02
Many of us still talk about our struggles beyond the ADHD, reach out when we think to, and respond when we can.


06:58:02-06:58:06
That doesn't mean it's always easy to connect.


06:58:06-06:58:14
In deeper discussions months later, Renee and I observed how some of the conditions I discussed in this chapter can decrease access to one another.


06:58:14-06:58:16
Anxiety can keep us from reaching out.


06:58:16-06:58:18
Trauma can trigger pain and distrust.


06:58:18-06:58:35
As Renee showed me in one brave and vulnerable conversation, labeling our experiences with terms such as anxiety, rejection, sensitivity, trauma, grief, just like labeling our emotions, can make them easier to understand.


06:58:35-06:58:43
And communicating those experiences can build trust and connection, restoring access to what humans need most, one another.


06:58:43-06:58:49
Chapter 12, How to Heart.


06:58:49-06:58:56
If it is true that there are as many minds as there are heads, then there are as many kinds of love as there are hearts.


06:58:56-06:58:59
Leo Tolstoy, Anna Karenina.


06:58:59-06:59:02
Why can't they just?


06:59:03-06:59:13
A few months after I started the channel, I began to realize it wasn't just ADHD brains who were watching, those who cared for them and wanted to better support them were too.


06:59:13-06:59:16
I nicknamed these people hearts because it was their heart that brought them to my videos.


06:59:16-06:59:27
I felt gratitude and awe that they would take the time to understand a brain that wasn't their own and one that seemingly contradicted all logic and reasoning on a regular basis.


06:59:27-06:59:28
I empathized with their frustration too.


06:59:28-06:59:33
I know what it's like to be in a relationship with someone with ADHD.


06:59:33-06:59:36
I've been in one my entire life.


06:59:36-06:59:39
My brain won't do what I want it to do a lot of the time.


06:59:39-06:59:42
It promises me it will remember to put the clothes in the dryer and then it doesn't.


06:59:42-06:59:48
It tells me I need to run back into the house for something real quick and an hour later, I still haven't left.


06:59:48-06:59:53
It insists that I need all the supplies for a new hobby, then gets bored of it in a week.


06:59:53-07:00:01
As much as I love how impulsive and creative and fun my brain can be, there have been many times when I wished it would just cooperate.


07:00:01-07:00:05
Thankfully, I've developed a much better relationship with my brain since learning how to work with it effectively.


07:00:05-07:00:15
When I started dating someone with ADHD, both ADHD and autism, and became a heart to him, I totally expected that knowledge to translate.


07:00:15-07:00:24
I saw my partner run into brain-based challenges and equipped with six years worth of knowledge about how to work with my own brain, I aggressively tried to help.


07:00:24-07:00:28
I mostly figured out how to work with my brain, he could too.


07:00:28-07:00:31
I patiently listened to his struggles and offered suggestions.


07:00:31-07:00:34
I sent him articles and put sticky note reminders all over the walls.


07:00:34-07:00:38
I tugged at his mask, encouraging him to be himself.


07:00:38-07:00:44
The idea of unmasking meanwhile terrified him because being himself had not gone well historically.


07:00:44-07:00:47
Showing his neurodivergent traits didn't feel safe.


07:00:47-07:00:50
It had led to being laughed or yelled at, it led to breakups.


07:00:50-07:00:55
I read articles about how to be a better partner and work on your relationship.


07:00:55-07:01:01
I learned about autism and alexithymia, trying to understand his brain the same way I'd done with mine.


07:01:01-07:01:06
I did all the things that felt as if they should work, though I never knew what would.


07:01:06-07:01:10
I felt as if it were throwing spaghetti against the wall and hoping something would stick.


07:01:10-07:01:14
I was determined to make it work, he was worth it.


07:01:14-07:01:23
I appreciated his comedic timing, his quirks, his enthusiasm for his hobbies, his sense of justice, and his way of cutting through the noise and speaking directly to the heart of a matter.


07:01:23-07:01:29
Our values were aligned, our hopes, our dreams, I loved him.


07:01:29-07:01:31
I also felt lonely.


07:01:31-07:01:38
Once the new relationship hyperfocus wore off, he'd get distracted while we talked, or interrupt me, or change the subject abruptly.


07:01:38-07:01:40
He'd miss my bids for attention.


07:01:40-07:01:43
He'd agree to do something and then completely forget about it.


07:01:43-07:01:50
I yearned for him to care for me as attentively as he did his espresso machine, hat collection, or cast iron pans.


07:01:50-07:01:59
Which in retrospect were not only his special interests, they were also activities he needed to engage in to cope with the stress of trying to meet relationship expectations.


07:01:59-07:02:02
I was special too, he just sometimes needed a break.


07:02:02-07:02:06
He wanted to do better, and I wanted to help.


07:02:06-07:02:09
But communication between us was a challenge.


07:02:09-07:02:13
I couldn't tell when he was listening and when he was lost in his own world.


07:02:13-07:02:15
I never knew what to expect from him.


07:02:15-07:02:19
Was what he was doing or promising to do actually what he wanted to do?


07:02:19-07:02:21
And could he do it sustainably?


07:02:21-07:02:25
Or was it what he thought he was expected to do and he couldn't keep up?


07:02:25-07:02:30
When I tried talking to him about my frustrations, his emotions ran high and I couldn't get through.


07:02:30-07:02:32
Mine escalated too.


07:02:32-07:02:39
I found myself biting my tongue, almost blurting out the comments that countless people have said to me and others with ADHD.


07:02:39-07:02:41
Why can't you just, what do you mean you forgot?


07:02:41-07:02:50
If you cared about me, you'd, I even found myself thinking, this relationship would be amazing if, yes, I heard the irony in that.


07:02:50-07:02:52
This relationship has so much potential.


07:02:52-07:02:56
Eventually we found ourselves trapped in a relationship death spiral.


07:02:56-07:03:01
He was terrified to mess up and spent all his energy contemplating what he should be doing.


07:03:01-07:03:04
And I'd grow frustrated about his apparent inaction.


07:03:04-07:03:10
I'd need something, but he wouldn't do it soon enough, consistently enough, or he'd forget or miss the part that mattered the most.


07:03:10-07:03:15
Because he'd feel bad about letting me down, the stakes felt higher the next time I asked him for something.


07:03:16-07:03:20
He'd already, quote, failed, and he was terrified of getting it wrong again.


07:03:20-07:03:26
I'd noticed his hesitance, and as much as I wanted to reassure him, my patience was wearing thin.


07:03:26-07:03:33
I'd tried all my neurodivergent friendly relationship hacks and communication tools, but the harder I tried, the less my needs were getting met.


07:03:33-07:03:40
I've seen these relationship issues play themselves out over and over in my community and in my own life.


07:03:40-07:03:52
Unmet expectations, frustration, trying to manage day to day life on behalf of your brain, trying to manage them, guilt and shame, loss of respect, or even the relationship.


07:03:52-07:03:59
My neurotypical mom took on more and more of the household, familial, and financial responsibilities and burned herself out.


07:03:59-07:04:06
I watched my dad, who likely at ADHD, get into intense and terrifying power struggles with my brother, who definitely does.


07:04:06-07:04:10
For the record, you can't punish away ADHD.


07:04:10-07:04:17
Resorting to punishment just made my brother more defiant, and it taught him that the way to respond to people who aren't doing what you want is to force them.


07:04:17-07:04:20
It didn't fix his mental health struggles, but worsened them.


07:04:20-07:04:30
I watched my dad fall into a depression too, painfully aware he wasn't contributing as much as my mom was, but not knowing what to do about it.


07:04:30-07:04:38
The more areas of responsibility she took over, the more he accepted her role as the caretaker of everything, and the more she had to do to keep everything from falling apart.


07:04:38-07:04:42
I've been on the receiving end of this type of support with executive function.


07:04:43-07:04:54
In past relationships, especially before I knew how to manage my ADHD, my partners ended up taking care of me, cleaning up my mess as I gave over my autonomy and passwords to them, ashamed I couldn't adult.


07:04:54-07:05:03
I hear all the time from community members who share how their partners treat them like a child, that their parents treat them like a problem to be solved.


07:05:03-07:05:07
I see their friends make jokes out of the struggles they face in ways that are hurtful, even cruel.


07:05:09-07:05:14
My partner had an ex who once asked point blank, why can't you just be normal?


07:05:14-07:05:17
We can't be normal, we can only be ourselves.


07:05:17-07:05:18
We wonder if we can ever be enough.


07:05:18-07:05:22
The hearts that love us wonder that too.


07:05:22-07:05:25
They wonder if they are doing too much for us or too little.


07:05:25-07:05:29
They wonder if they overvalue our struggles or undervalue our gifts.


07:05:29-07:05:33
Eventually, we all wonder what it is we're doing wrong.


07:05:33-07:05:35
Why can't we just make it work?


07:05:36-07:05:44
What I learned, every relationship, neurodiverse or not, runs into challenges with unmet expectations.


07:05:44-07:05:49
Either because they aren't expressed or because there's conflict in the ways each person is trying to get their needs met.


07:05:49-07:05:57
ADHD exacerbates this issue by interfering with our ability to meet expectations even once they're agreed upon.


07:05:57-07:06:04
In fact, relationship problems are one of the most common reasons why adults with ADHD seek treatment.


07:06:04-07:06:07
So brains, hearts, take a breath.


07:06:07-07:06:14
Whatever frustration, anger, guilt or shame you're feeling is normal if you and or your loved one has ADHD.


07:06:14-07:06:18
And your relationship challenges aren't ones that everyone goes through.


07:06:18-07:06:22
And it's not due to a lack of effort, it's because of ADHD.


07:06:22-07:06:27
Not only are there executive function challenges that make it difficult for us to do our part consistently.


07:06:27-07:06:32
There are also aspects about neurodivergence that look very different from the inside than they do from the outside.


07:06:34-07:06:40
This can make it look as though we don't even care, especially when we're pushing back against expectations we're struggling to meet.


07:06:40-07:06:43
We care way more than it looks.


07:06:43-07:06:51
When we continue to mess up even after apologizing for the same thing a hundred times, it might seem like we don't care.


07:06:51-07:06:55
After all, if we were so sorry, why wouldn't we change our behavior?


07:06:55-07:06:57
We are trying.


07:06:57-07:07:00
We are taught that accomplishment comes from effort.


07:07:00-07:07:03
If someone is not accomplishing something, they must not be putting enough effort.


07:07:04-07:07:06
This isn't true for those of us with ADHD.


07:07:06-07:07:12
We often must put in significantly more effort than our neurotypical peers to achieve the same results.


07:07:12-07:07:16
And frequently, our effort doesn't look very effortful.


07:07:16-07:07:18
Sometimes our effort isn't obvious.


07:07:18-07:07:24
We may have forgotten the instructions, misunderstood what was being asked, or put our effort toward the wrong task.


07:07:24-07:07:29
Much of our effort might not be visible at all because it is happening internally.


07:07:30-07:07:34
Improvement takes time, and progress is rarely linear.


07:07:34-07:07:39
It's not unreasonable to want the person you love to improve in the areas where they struggle.


07:07:39-07:07:41
They probably want that too.


07:07:41-07:07:50
What is often unreasonable, whether we're trying to change ourselves or support someone we love, is our expectations of the rate and magnitude of the change.


07:07:50-07:07:53
First of all, we can't change our symptoms.


07:07:53-07:08:00
Unlike conditions like anxiety or depression, which can improve or worsen over time, ADHD symptoms generally don't.


07:08:00-07:08:01
Our impairments can improve.


07:08:01-07:08:08
We can improve how functional we are given those symptoms, but we can't get rid of the symptoms themselves.


07:08:08-07:08:10
If it seems like we have, we're masking them.


07:08:10-07:08:15
Also, most people are only capable of one degree of change at a time.


07:08:15-07:08:26
Even if we can manage to change quickly and in multiple areas at once, which is often what's asked of us or what we ask of ourselves, it's generally impossible to sustain change that extensive.


07:08:28-07:08:30
Supports aren't designed with us in mind.


07:08:30-07:08:44
Many common solutions to relationship issues, from couples therapy to those how-to relationship articles on the Internet, are designed based on neuro-normative standards that assume typical cognitive function in most or all areas.


07:08:44-07:08:54
Not only do our executive function impairments make it challenging to implement any strategy, the strategies themselves, or many aspects of them, are often inaccessible due to the way our brains work.


07:08:56-07:09:03
In some cases, trying to make solutions or systems designed for neurotypical brains work for a neurodivergent one can backfire.


07:09:03-07:09:08
This can lead to a lot of frustration and can even be re-traumatizing for everyone involved.


07:09:08-07:09:13
This is why there's an entire industry designed specifically for those who are neurodivergent.


07:09:13-07:09:16
We need solutions that are designed to work the way our brains do.


07:09:16-07:09:19
We're not struggling on purpose.


07:09:19-07:09:25
This point might seem obvious, but it's easy to forget in the moment.


07:09:25-07:09:28
Struggling is not a choice or something we can opt out of.


07:09:28-07:09:29
It's a given.


07:09:29-07:09:34
If we didn't struggle with ADHD-related challenges, we wouldn't have been diagnosed with ADHD.


07:09:34-07:09:38
The same goes for any other coexisting conditions that we've been diagnosed with.


07:09:38-07:09:45
While we might struggle in ways that don't make sense from a neurotypical perspective, it's not weird that we're struggling.


07:09:45-07:09:47
Given our diagnosis, it would be weird if we didn't.


07:09:47-07:09:50
The double empathy problem.


07:09:51-07:10:02
In 2012, autism researcher Dr. Damian Milton created the double empathy theory as a way of understanding communication breakdowns between autistic, analistic, non-autistic people.


07:10:02-07:10:14
Previous theories proposed that people with autism struggled to communicate due to impaired empathy and theory of mind, a term used to describe the ability to imagine what might be going on in another person's head.


07:10:14-07:10:22
Milton posited that these difficulties resulted from lack of empathy and understanding on both sides.


07:10:22-07:10:33
In other words, because autistic and holistic brains function differently in many ways, an autistic and holistic person have trouble understanding what the other is feeling and thinking.


07:10:33-07:10:47
While the studies aren't specific to ADHD, there are traits that overlap between the conditions, and research has expanded to demonstrate how the double empathy problem may apply to autistic traits, regardless of specific diagnosis.


07:10:47-07:10:51
And we know that difficulties with empathy and understanding are complicated by differences in lived experience.


07:10:51-07:11:02
In neurodiverse relationships, missed connections, misunderstandings, and miscommunications are bound to happen, and the resulting stress on the relationship can be significant.


07:11:02-07:11:10
Thankfully, just being aware of this issue can be a good first step in learning how to interface together more effectively and help you look for supports.


07:11:10-07:11:14
There are reasons why we can't just.


07:11:15-07:11:21
Many of the expectations we place on those we love rely on the idea that they should be able to do the thing.


07:11:21-07:11:32
As a society, we tend to assume people's intelligence and capabilities based on characteristics such as their verbal ability, long term recall, or even the work they do for a living.


07:11:32-07:11:43
If my partner is a well-spoken psychologist who can name the exact title of season four, episode 11 of Star Trek The Next Generation, it's data's day, he just informed me.


07:11:44-07:11:47
He should be able to figure out how to buy me flowers, right?


07:11:47-07:11:48
Not necessarily.


07:11:48-07:11:53
Cognitive ability is actually cognitive abilities.


07:11:53-07:12:02
Tasks such as buying flowers require multiple cognitive abilities, and we need to use different combinations of these abilities to produce a given result.


07:12:02-07:12:07
We've gone over many of these in previous chapters already, so I'll just drop in a quick list.


07:12:07-07:12:19
Processing speed, working memory, pattern recognition, set shifting, response inhibition, verbal comprehension, visual spatial ability, perceptual reasoning.


07:12:19-07:12:27
Several of these cognitive abilities are significantly impaired in ADHD brains, at least relative to our areas of cognitive strength.


07:12:27-07:12:33
In addition, some combinations of abilities are more challenging for some brains than others.


07:12:33-07:12:37
For example, I might have no trouble understanding a recipe I'm reading.


07:12:37-07:12:46
My verbal comprehension is extremely high, but my poor response inhibition makes it hard for me to stay focused and in the kitchen long enough to prep and cook dinner.


07:12:46-07:12:53
Even if I stay focused, my relatively weak working memory means I will 100% forget the instructions two steps in.


07:12:53-07:12:58
My ADHD partner describes his experience in computer terms.


07:12:58-07:13:09
He might have a high capacity solid state hard drive and twin linked high end graphic cards, but a Pentium II processor and about half a gigabyte of RAM, random access memory.


07:13:10-07:13:19
He's capable of doing high level work with visual spatial processing or pattern recognition, but his processor is slow and he can only hold on to small chunks of new data at a time.


07:13:19-07:13:22
How does this affect our relationship?


07:13:22-07:13:38
The more I explain how important flowers are to me, describing how Susie's husband brought her flowers and wasn't that romantic, and running through all the tangential topics that come up for me while talking about flowers, the less likely he is able to process and remember the part that matters to me.


07:13:38-07:13:41
Hey, I think it's romantic when someone buys me flowers.


07:13:41-07:13:50
Because my own brain makes it hard to organize my thoughts, I also have trouble communicating this point succinctly without going off on tangents.


07:13:50-07:13:52
They sigh.


07:13:52-07:13:55
Inconsistency is part of the deal.


07:13:55-07:14:07
One of the most common frustrations for those with ADHD and those who love them is knowing we're capable of doing the thing and still for some reason aren't doing it when we're expected to.


07:14:07-07:14:12
Yes, just because we could take care of something yesterday doesn't mean that we can today.


07:14:12-07:14:17
Maybe we were hyper focusing for 12 hours yesterday and now our brains are too exhausted to focus.


07:14:17-07:14:22
Maybe we didn't sleep well and our emotional self-regulation went completely out the window.


07:14:22-07:14:24
Maybe there were more distractions today.


07:14:24-07:14:31
What helped me a lot in my own relationship was recognizing and embracing these inconsistencies.


07:14:31-07:14:40
I learned that just as sometimes I can focus and sometimes I can't, sometimes my ADHD partner could make the connections between what I was saying in the context of the situation.


07:14:40-07:14:41
And sometimes he couldn't.


07:14:41-07:14:44
Emotions get in our way.


07:14:44-07:14:52
Emotions like shame and anxiety can be powerful obstacles to completing even seemingly simple tasks.


07:14:52-07:14:59
On top of our struggle to do a task, we also have all the shame and negative emotions of past failures getting in our way.


07:14:59-07:15:02
Because we know the task isn't supposed to be that hard.


07:15:02-07:15:12
The fact that we care deeply about the task, know how important it is, and feel bad about the fact that we haven't done it yet and are having trouble getting ourselves to do it can add to the struggle.


07:15:12-07:15:16
ADHD traits are two sides of the same coin.


07:15:16-07:15:21
A lot of ADHD traits are strengths in the right situation.


07:15:21-07:15:31
For instance, the same emotional sensitivity that can make it difficult for us to have hard conversations also makes us quick to be enthusiastic, understanding, and generous.


07:15:31-07:15:37
As my partner tells me, many qualities of mind that frustrate him are in different situations, also the things he loves.


07:15:37-07:15:38
And honestly, same.


07:15:38-07:15:44
Here are some of the fantastic ways ADHD traits can show up in relationships with others.


07:15:44-07:15:58
Impulsivity, spontaneous romantic gestures, motivated by urgency, great in a crisis, divergent thinking, innovative problem solving skills, hyperactivity, infectious energy.


07:15:59-07:16:08
Shorter time horizons, ability to find joy in the moment, motivated by novelty, often trying, learning, and sharing something new.


07:16:08-07:16:10
The relationship will rarely get boring.


07:16:10-07:16:15
Emotionally sensitive, caring deeply, even when it doesn't seem like we do.


07:16:15-07:16:25
The frustrations that come with ADHD and other mental health struggles make it easier for the benefits of building a relationship with someone with ADHD to get lost.


07:16:25-07:16:30
But when we get support for the ways in which we struggle, the potential positives have a chance to truly shine.


07:16:30-07:16:35
That two-sided coin of ADHD traits can flip more often to the positive side than the negative one.


07:16:35-07:16:39
Amanda, 20s, USA.


07:16:39-07:16:45
When I was bored, I would turn to my husband for stimulation, which he ultimately could not keep up with.


07:16:45-07:16:50
He wouldn't respond with the adoration and interest I craved, so I felt lonely and unloved.


07:16:50-07:16:53
He felt overwhelmed and like a bad person for hurting me.


07:16:53-07:16:57
Chad M, 36, Texas.


07:16:57-07:16:59
ADHD almost ended my marriage.


07:16:59-07:17:02
My wife didn't know what she was getting into.


07:17:02-07:17:07
It became a perpetual cycle of me trying my best while she felt like I couldn't care less.


07:17:07-07:17:10
I finally got on meds and it changed everything.


07:17:10-07:17:15
My wife could see the sudden and significant change, which helped her see that it wasn't that I didn't care.


07:17:15-07:17:16
I just couldn't show it.


07:17:16-07:17:24
There was still a lot of pain to work through, and my ADHD is still a challenge for us, but we learned to differentiate actions from motives.


07:17:25-07:17:27
Amelia B, 35, Arizona.


07:17:27-07:17:37
My ADHD has caused a lot of little frictions in our day to day life, like when I repeatedly forget to put the keys back on the hook or put the wash in the dryer.


07:17:37-07:17:41
It's also caused a few major disasters that have definitely affected how my partner sees me, I think.


07:17:41-07:17:49
After making such big mistakes, it's like I have to prove again every single day that I can be a functioning, capable adult and partner.


07:17:49-07:17:52
Lee D, 39, Massachusetts.


07:17:54-07:18:01
I tend to get easily hyper fixated on another person when there are signs of good chemistry, a similar experience to what's been termed limerence.


07:18:01-07:18:07
If I don't keep it in check, it leads to broken boundaries, hurt feelings, and diminished or even ruined relationships.


07:18:07-07:18:10
The Toolbox.


07:18:10-07:18:17
One of the best ways to support those we love is to support their efforts to find treatment for their ADHD.


07:18:17-07:18:20
We don't expect other medical conditions to manage themselves without treatment.


07:18:20-07:18:23
Why would it be any different for ADHD?


07:18:23-07:18:28
Besides, most of us aren't trained to support others with their health conditions or mental health struggles.


07:18:28-07:18:33
And even if we are, we aren't objective enough to do so effectively for those we love.


07:18:33-07:18:39
That said, there are ways to strengthen your relationship or friendship with your brain that are appropriate for the role you already have.


07:18:39-07:18:42
One, engage in empathy.


07:18:42-07:18:48
So many of us with ADHD and those who love someone with ADHD feel alone in our struggles.


07:18:48-07:19:01
While you may never fully understand your brain's experience, you can certainly open your mind to learning about it, which can make empathizing easier and helps to dismantle the shame we've built up over a lifetime of feeling weird or wrong.


07:19:01-07:19:07
It also makes it safer and easier to talk about our brain's differences, so we can collaborate on solutions that are more likely to work.


07:19:07-07:19:15
And learning more about ADHD's impact on loved ones can normalize your experience too, so you too can feel less alone and better understand what you need.


07:19:15-07:19:18
Ask about their experience.


07:19:18-07:19:31
While those with ADHD often have very similar stories, strengths, and struggles, there are also profound differences in its presentation, how they feel about their diagnosis and challenges, what they've tried, and what works for them.


07:19:31-07:19:39
Getting curious about someone's individual experience and accepting their answers, even if they're surprising, is key to understanding them and helping them feel understood.


07:19:39-07:19:43
Ask about their inner world and thought processes.


07:19:43-07:19:53
You might think you know what your loved one with ADHD is thinking or why they did or didn't do a thing, but if their brain works very differently from yours, there's a decent chance you'll be wrong.


07:19:53-07:19:56
Remember the double empathy problem I spoke about before?


07:19:56-07:20:00
Instead of assuming, ask, and this goes both ways.


07:20:00-07:20:06
Asking about each other's thought processes and inner world can increase empathy and make it easier to connect.


07:20:06-07:20:08
It can also help us understand where their priorities are.


07:20:08-07:20:14
We often assume that what others want for themselves is the same as what we want for them, but that's not necessarily the case.


07:20:16-07:20:18
Be an advocate.


07:20:18-07:20:23
Some behaviors and actions aren't problems, they're strategies or coping mechanisms.


07:20:23-07:20:29
If your loved one suddenly leaves the room during a family dinner, see it as an opportunity to recognize and advocate for their needs.


07:20:29-07:20:31
Shrug and normalize it.


07:20:31-07:20:32
They get overwhelmed sometimes and need to step away.


07:20:32-07:20:39
Encourage them to do things in a way that works for them, even if it's weird, and have their back if they do it.


07:20:39-07:20:43
This models understanding and empathy so that others can engage in them too.


07:20:45-07:20:47
What if they don't wanna talk about it?


07:20:47-07:20:51
There are many reasons someone with ADHD may not wanna talk about their ADHD.


07:20:51-07:20:53
They've had it weaponized against them.


07:20:53-07:20:57
They don't understand their ADHD or don't know they have ADHD.


07:20:57-07:20:58
They don't wanna be fixed.


07:20:58-07:21:03
They don't feel safe talking about it with you, at least right now.


07:21:03-07:21:06
Talking about it brings up feelings they'd like to avoid.


07:21:06-07:21:10
If you wanna broach the subject, you can start the conversation with the help of these tips.


07:21:10-07:21:12
Start with respect.


07:21:12-07:21:15
Remember, we're not a broken version of normal.


07:21:15-07:21:17
We have a brain that works differently.


07:21:17-07:21:24
If you can respect the challenges we face as a result of trying to meet neurotypical expectations, it's easier to respect us too.


07:21:24-07:21:29
This mutual respect also makes it easier for us to trust that you're trying to support us, not fix us.


07:21:29-07:21:32
Be mindful of tone.


07:21:32-07:21:36
There's a big difference between a frustrated, seriously, why did you do it that way?


07:21:36-07:21:39
And a genuinely curious, seriously, why did you do it that way?


07:21:40-07:21:45
When you can, use a tone that expresses you're ready to listen, not to judge.


07:21:45-07:21:47
Then, listen.


07:21:47-07:21:49
There's often a reason we did something differently.


07:21:49-07:21:52
Try a different approach.


07:21:52-07:21:54
Conversations don't always have to be verbal.


07:21:54-07:21:59
Communication can happen by drawing or sharing videos or via emojis or gifs.


07:21:59-07:22:09
Asynchronous communication such as texting can be easier for many brains because it's easier to discuss important topics at a pace that lets us self-regulate and think through what we want to say.


07:22:10-07:22:13
Let us be done if we need to be done.


07:22:13-07:22:16
It's important not to force these conversations.


07:22:16-07:22:22
We all have our limits, especially when it comes to talking about aspects of ourselves that we may not love, ADHD or not.


07:22:22-07:22:28
It's also perfectly okay for you to set boundaries for yourself so the impact of unresolved issues doesn't fall unfairly on you.


07:22:28-07:22:32
Jessica H, 30, Ohio.


07:22:32-07:22:35
My partner and I were both diagnosed later in life.


07:22:35-07:22:41
Our struggle to moderate our emotions, combined with a processing disorder in one of us, means silly fights happen.


07:22:41-07:22:47
Being aware that there is something causing those emotions to get a little rowdy means we are more understanding of each other's struggles.


07:22:47-07:22:50
And because we think outside the box, we can find solutions where others might not.


07:22:50-07:22:54
Colleen H, 41, Pennsylvania.


07:22:54-07:23:00
Instead of pointing at things, I asked my now husband to gesture at the things he's talking about with his palm up.


07:23:00-07:23:07
I thought it was a silly request, but he immediately complied and we had zero confrontations about our then new cohabitation.


07:23:07-07:23:12
I start to panic if I'm afraid I've done something wrong and then I go into analysis paralysis.


07:23:12-07:23:15
Pointing, even conversationally, triggers feelings of no, what did I screw up?


07:23:15-07:23:20
Open palms let me know what he's talking about and it invites me in instead of calling me out.


07:23:20-07:23:24
Collin K, 35, Colorado.


07:23:24-07:23:28
When I told my dad that I was recently diagnosed with ADHD, he asked what it's like.


07:23:28-07:23:34
When I told him how a normal day, a good day, and a bad day was, he said he was sorry he never noticed.


07:23:34-07:23:36
It was nice that he just understood.


07:23:36-07:23:41
Even when I told him I was nervous about the meds cuz I didn't wanna lose my career, he told me, don't sweat it.


07:23:41-07:23:44
Being better is better and you'll find a new one.


07:23:44-07:23:45
That mattered a lot.


07:23:45-07:23:48
Two, choose your battles.


07:23:48-07:23:54
If we have ADHD, we'll struggle with many different things and in multiple areas of our lives.


07:23:54-07:23:57
And that's without adding other coexisting conditions into the mix.


07:23:57-07:24:02
We can't work on everything all at once, at least not effectively.


07:24:02-07:24:05
We'll need to decide what needs our focus now and what to set aside, at least for now.


07:24:05-07:24:08
Prioritize.


07:24:08-07:24:11
Those with ADHD often have trouble prioritizing.


07:24:11-07:24:18
If there are ten things to work on, we might choose one that's not as important, or the least taxing on our executive function.


07:24:18-07:24:21
We might get overwhelmed and not work on any of them at all.


07:24:21-07:24:27
Choosing one thing to focus on at a time, maybe two, improves the likelihood that we'll make progress on it.


07:24:27-07:24:31
What task, if left undone, is affecting the relationship the most?


07:24:31-07:24:34
What would create the most positive impact if it were to change?


07:24:35-07:24:37
Let us fail.


07:24:37-07:24:39
This one is especially important for parents.


07:24:39-07:24:43
Many parents are afraid to let us do things our way because they're worried we'll fail if they do.


07:24:43-07:24:50
We might, but having the space to try and fail while we have a safety net in place is important.


07:24:50-07:24:56
It gives us the opportunity to figure out what does work for us before we're off on our own and taking on even more.


07:24:56-07:24:58
It also builds our resilience.


07:24:58-07:25:03
Let us practice doing things on our own now that we'll need to do on our own when you're not around.


07:25:05-07:25:06
Let some stuff go.


07:25:06-07:25:10
Maybe we never get around to removing the bathroom wallpaper.


07:25:10-07:25:12
Whatever, it's kitsch.


07:25:12-07:25:20
My boyfriend learned to accept that if I'm making dinner, I'll need to use a recipe, which means we're probably going to have leftover fennel taking up space in the fridge.


07:25:20-07:25:26
As Dr. Ari Tuckman put it in an interview on my channel, yeah, I don't love this thing about my partner, but whatever.


07:25:26-07:25:28
There's a lot of things in my life I don't love.


07:25:28-07:25:32
Find other solutions with the milk carton rule.


07:25:32-07:25:36
The milk carton rule is inspired by the story of a real life couple.


07:25:36-07:25:39
The wife leaves the milk out after pouring some for her morning coffee.


07:25:39-07:25:44
And when the husband comes downstairs for breakfast, he has to use warm milk for his cereal.


07:25:44-07:25:48
The husband asks the wife to put the milk back in the fridge, but she keeps forgetting and leaving it out.


07:25:48-07:25:54
One way to resolve the issue is to keep reminding the wife to put the milk back.


07:25:54-07:25:58
But the husband has another option, buying his own carton of milk.


07:25:59-07:26:05
Many times, a problem can be solved in a way that does not depend on us being able to change our often ADHD related behavior.


07:26:05-07:26:09
Margaret, 46, Washington.


07:26:09-07:26:12
I wish this was something more parents supported each other with.


07:26:12-07:26:13
There's so much judgment.


07:26:13-07:26:16
Why did you let him take on so much when you knew he would likely struggle?


07:26:16-07:26:19
Or on the other hand, why are you still doing XYZ with them?


07:26:19-07:26:21
They should be able to do that independently by now.


07:26:21-07:26:25
I do a lot to support my neurodivergent kids and help them succeed.


07:26:25-07:26:29
But I've learned my kids will eventually tell me when they don't need or want that amount of support.


07:26:29-07:26:31
And they'll tell me if and when they need it again.


07:26:31-07:26:35
Rebecca C, 43, United Kingdom.


07:26:35-07:26:43
My partner swaps executive dysfunction jobs with me because we're more motivated to complete a task for one another than for ourselves.


07:26:43-07:26:48
For example, I find it hard to get myself to the post office and he finds it hard to pick up his prescriptions.


07:26:48-07:26:52
Jessica K, 28, Florida.


07:26:52-07:27:00
When my family members started working on not taking my quirks like leaving the refrigerator door open personally, it made me feel so much less ashamed.


07:27:00-07:27:03
Three, collaborate on solutions.


07:27:03-07:27:07
When it comes to ADHD related challenges, it's not you versus your loved one.


07:27:07-07:27:10
It's both of you versus the challenges.


07:27:10-07:27:11
Tackle them together.


07:27:11-07:27:17
The way in which you collaborate and the degree to which you do it will depend on the type of relationship you have with the person.


07:27:17-07:27:20
But the most effective solutions usually involve some level of collaboration.


07:27:20-07:27:24
Be clear on the goal.


07:27:24-07:27:29
Make sure everyone agrees on what the goal is and understands why you're working toward it.


07:27:29-07:27:31
This way, you're on the same team.


07:27:31-07:27:38
Defining together what success looks like ahead of time for a goal, week or outing, can also make it easier for everyone to recognize when it's been achieved.


07:27:38-07:27:45
This can build our confidence, which is critical for those of us who have internalized the idea that we aren't enough.


07:27:45-07:27:49
Allow us to achieve the goal in a way that works for us.


07:27:49-07:27:53
ADHD brains are capable of accomplishing a lot.


07:27:53-07:27:56
We might just go about it a bit differently.


07:27:56-07:28:06
Allowing for equifinality, achieving the same result through different means, can be the difference between us being able to accomplish tasks and leaving them undone, or you having to do them all yourself.


07:28:06-07:28:09
Look for the and.


07:28:09-07:28:12
I love this concept from the book Crucial Conversations.


07:28:12-07:28:15
Don't compromise if you can help it.


07:28:15-07:28:19
That can lead to resentment on both sides because both people are having to sacrifice something that matters to them.


07:28:20-07:28:24
Instead, look for a solution that fully meets your needs and theirs.


07:28:24-07:28:31
One of my favorite ands, instead of being soft or hard on yourself, you can be gentle and firm.


07:28:31-07:28:35
ADHD coach Dusty Chapura taught me this one from a book I totally don't remember the name of.


07:28:35-07:28:41
While we might struggle at executing, we often excel at ideating.


07:28:41-07:28:45
We can come up with lots of potential solutions, especially if we're given time to let them percolate.


07:28:45-07:28:47
You just might wanna help us narrow them down.


07:28:47-07:28:50
Stay in your lane.


07:28:50-07:28:58
At the end of the day, you are your brain's partner/friend/parent/roommate, not their coach or therapist.


07:28:58-07:29:01
It's incredibly easy to get caught up with someone's mental health struggles.


07:29:01-07:29:08
But being someone's main source of support inevitably comes at the expense of the role you already have.


07:29:08-07:29:10
Parenting a partner kills desire.


07:29:10-07:29:17
Playing therapist to a friend requires removing yourself from the equation so you can be objective, which comes at the expense of connection.


07:29:17-07:29:22
Rather than being someone's support system, support them in building one.


07:29:22-07:29:27
If they don't want to or can't, make it clear that their need for support can't fall entirely on you.


07:29:27-07:29:31
Lindell C, 30, Alberta.


07:29:31-07:29:35
One of the best things people have done to support my ADHD is feed me.


07:29:35-07:29:42
I eat supper with my parents twice a week, which helps free up the brain space that would have gone toward meal prep for other things.


07:29:42-07:29:49
An example, I had supper with my parents one evening, which meant I had enough energy and brain space to do all my own dishes later that night, three sink loads full.


07:29:49-07:29:53
Kristi H, 38, Colorado.


07:29:53-07:29:59
My partner will remind me I wanted to shower, turn it on, and wait with me while I get in, or even sit and talk with me while I'm in there.


07:29:59-07:30:01
He also sets easy food on my desk while I'm working.


07:30:01-07:30:05
Laura S, 37, Indianapolis.


07:30:05-07:30:09
My time blindness means I'm perpetually late to everything.


07:30:09-07:30:14
When I get ready, my partner checks in and gives me T-minus updates as our leave time approaches.


07:30:14-07:30:18
What's really special is there's no judgment or frustration in his tone.


07:30:18-07:30:19
He's just sharing information.


07:30:19-07:30:22
Four, look for the good.


07:30:22-07:30:31
Everyone's brains, by default, are designed to notice and remember what's wrong more often than what's right, making it easy to miss what's actually going well.


07:30:31-07:30:39
This can be discouraging in any relationship, but it's especially problematic in relationships where there's likely to be more going wrong.


07:30:39-07:30:44
Seeking out the good not only provides much needed encouragement for everyone involved.


07:30:44-07:30:47
It can also make it easier to enjoy the relationship.


07:30:47-07:30:50
Notice their effort.


07:30:50-07:30:53
Failing doesn't mean someone isn't trying.


07:30:53-07:30:56
In fact, it's often evidence that they are.


07:30:56-07:31:00
Yes, your roommate started the dishwasher without soap, but they started the dishwasher.


07:31:00-07:31:02
If you're not sure if they are making an effort, ask.


07:31:02-07:31:05
And if they tell you they are, believe them.


07:31:05-07:31:13
According to Dr. Tuchman's research, actively looking for and recognizing the ADHDer's effort is one of the most important things you can do to improve your relationship.


07:31:14-07:31:17
Brains, you can help by flagging your efforts for your hearts.


07:31:17-07:31:20
Flip the coin.


07:31:20-07:31:26
Remember, the things we love and the things that frustrate us are often two sides of the same ADHD trait coin.


07:31:26-07:31:32
Appreciating ADHD traits when they're enjoyable makes it a lot easier to accept those same traits when they happen to get in the way.


07:31:32-07:31:36
Look for the ways their impulsivity shows up in ways you love.


07:31:36-07:31:39
Assume best intent.


07:31:39-07:31:45
When you assume that someone with ADHD cares about an issue they agreed to address, you'll be right more often than wrong.


07:31:45-07:31:49
If you know they care but their actions are telling you they don't, it might be helpful to ask for clarity.


07:31:49-07:31:51
Hey, I know you care about this.


07:31:51-07:31:53
Can you help me understand why it's not happening?


07:31:53-07:31:54
I'm not seeing it.


07:31:54-07:31:59
Asking invites them to show you what you're missing or to explain what's getting in their way.


07:31:59-07:32:02
Celebrate their successes.


07:32:02-07:32:07
Specific immediate positive feedback is incredibly motivating to ADHD brains.


07:32:07-07:32:10
When we make progress on something important to us, give us a high five.


07:32:10-07:32:12
Throw a mini dance party.


07:32:12-07:32:14
Do whatever makes us feel good.


07:32:14-07:32:26
While we might get praised for going above and beyond on a project, coming up with a brilliant new idea or crushing it in sales, it's rare that we get celebrated for putting effort into the things that are hard for us because they seem easy or obvious to everyone else.


07:32:26-07:32:31
Scott D, 46, Washington.


07:32:31-07:32:35
My dad taught me the importance of looking at something you've done and appreciating it.


07:32:35-07:32:37
He called it admire time.


07:32:37-07:32:41
Mode lawn, clean car, new fence, etc.


07:32:41-07:32:44
After we finished a task, he would say, okay, admire time.


07:32:44-07:32:46
And we'd stand there and look at the thing we just did.


07:32:46-07:32:50
Ron W, 50, Detroit.


07:32:50-07:32:59
I often go through my day just trying to survive, putting out one figurative fire after another and desperately hoping I haven't missed anything that will cause everything to burn down.


07:32:59-07:33:06
When my wife says to me, hey, I really appreciate that you did X, or this thing that you built really makes things easier for me.


07:33:06-07:33:10
I suddenly feel truly seen and my feeling of self worth is revived.


07:33:10-07:33:12
Plus, it gives me renewed energy to keep going.


07:33:12-07:33:15
Five, take care of yourself.


07:33:15-07:33:21
Having a loved one who's struggling for any reason can be emotionally draining.


07:33:21-07:33:29
While almost no one with ADHD intends to make your life more difficult or make you feel neglected, intent isn't the only thing that matters.


07:33:29-07:33:31
How it's impacting you matters too.


07:33:31-07:33:37
Putting your own oxygen mask on first can help you be there for the person you care about in a sustainable way.


07:33:37-07:33:39
Set boundaries.


07:33:39-07:33:43
It's important to set limits that allow you to maintain your own peace.


07:33:43-07:33:50
It's okay to make requests of other people, but boundaries should focus on your actions, not the actions you want from someone else.


07:33:50-07:33:53
This is because we can't control other people's behavior.


07:33:53-07:34:01
If your boundaries involve someone else's behavior, such as yelling, a boundary that you might have is that you don't participate in a conversation while they are.


07:34:01-07:34:08
If you're concerned that expressing your boundaries could escalate the situation, please talk to a mental health professional before trying this.


07:34:08-07:34:19
While boundaries are important and can create a greater sense of safety in relationships for everyone involved, those who aren't used to boundaries can get angry and even lash out when others begin to communicate them.


07:34:19-07:34:32
Engage outside support, therapy, peer support, friends who are navigating similar situations, even articles about neurodiverse relationships can validate your experiences and help you process the emotions that come up.


07:34:32-07:34:35
They can also offer guidance so you don't have to figure it all out on your own.


07:34:37-07:34:38
Allow yourself to have feelings.


07:34:38-07:34:48
It might feel wrong to be upset about ADHD related behavior because it's unintentional, but you're allowed to have feelings, regardless of why something is happening.


07:34:48-07:34:52
There's a difference between understandable and acceptable.


07:34:52-07:34:55
You don't have to accept behavior that hurts you just because someone has ADHD.


07:34:55-07:34:59
Feel your anger, your hurt, your sadness.


07:34:59-07:35:05
Allowing yourself to feel can help you understand what you need, regulate those emotions, and communicate more effectively with your brain.


07:35:07-07:35:10
It helps you focus on your feelings and needs as opposed to their behavior.


07:35:10-07:35:18
Marshall Rosenberg, the creator of nonviolent communication, describes criticism as a tragic expression of an unmet need.


07:35:18-07:35:25
It's tragic because complaining about someone's behavior is much less likely to enact change than expressing your own feelings and needs.


07:35:25-07:35:28
The person we criticize often feels attacked and winds up on the defensive.


07:35:28-07:35:31
Take a break.


07:35:31-07:35:37
Make sure you're both getting time apart to pursue other social connections, hobbies, or even just to recharge.


07:35:37-07:35:44
Getting time away from each other is what many, even neurotypical couples, find important to the sustainability and happiness of their relationships.


07:35:44-07:35:48
You can also take a break from the relationship completely if you need to.


07:35:48-07:35:52
Just because someone has a disability doesn't mean you can't break up with them.


07:35:52-07:35:55
I ultimately had to take a break from my relationship.


07:35:55-07:35:58
We got back together after a year and lots of therapy.


07:35:58-07:36:01
It's unhealthy for you to feel chronically neglected.


07:36:01-07:36:05
It's also unhealthy for them to be in a relationship where they're constantly failing.


07:36:05-07:36:11
Sometimes the place you're both at right now means the relationship isn't serving you, even if you really love each other.


07:36:11-07:36:12
It's okay to step away.


07:36:12-07:36:17
Claudia B, 26, Mexico.


07:36:17-07:36:19
I'm a heart, my boyfriend's a brain.


07:36:19-07:36:29
We've been together almost nine years, and one of the things that has helped us both so much in our relationship is understanding the way we each communicate and reminding ourselves of our boundaries.


07:36:29-07:36:33
Sometimes he gets so excited, he just flat out interrupts me and goes off on his own.


07:36:33-07:36:35
So I firmly stop him and say, I'm not done.


07:36:35-07:36:41
At first it felt incredibly uncomfortable to have to do this because I didn't want to interrupt him.


07:36:41-07:36:44
But this is something he has asked me to do countless times.


07:36:44-07:36:49
And he doesn't take it personally because it's about our communication with each other and not so much about who is saying what.


07:36:49-07:36:54
Caster S, 24, United Kingdom.


07:36:54-07:36:58
When we are having trouble understanding each other, we change modes of communication.


07:36:58-07:37:02
We suggest things like, let's take turns and chat over messages.


07:37:02-07:37:05
I'm not upset, I don't think I'm portraying the emotion I'm feeling, and it is not your fault.


07:37:05-07:37:12
My partner and I have anxiety and ADHD, and using a tool to find space helps.


07:37:12-07:37:15
We've recently celebrated a decade of dating.


07:37:15-07:37:19
Being clear that we don't always show or interpret how we are feeling well is a linchpin.


07:37:19-07:37:22
What's the plan?


07:37:22-07:37:33
Those who live with and love those with ADHD often find themselves picking up after them, managing more and more of the responsibilities, and becoming hypervigilant about their mistakes.


07:37:33-07:37:42
Those with ADHD often find themselves feeling less than in the relationship, in constant fear of messing up, or not doing or being enough.


07:37:42-07:37:45
We slip into these patterns almost without realizing it.


07:37:45-07:37:51
It's not the choice either person would have made, but due to our executive function challenges, it is often what happens by default.


07:37:51-07:37:56
These arrangements work well for absolutely no one involved.


07:37:56-07:37:59
There is, I have found, a better way.


07:37:59-07:38:03
It starts by asking one simple question, what's the plan?


07:38:03-07:38:07
I knew I didn't want to repeat my parents' and grandparents' patterns.


07:38:07-07:38:09
I didn't want to parrot a partner.


07:38:09-07:38:11
I also didn't want to be parented by mine.


07:38:11-07:38:15
I wanted an actual partner, someone I could respect.


07:38:15-07:38:18
I also wanted to be respected and to be able to respect myself.


07:38:18-07:38:23
When I first moved in with my partner, we asked ourselves, what's the plan for housekeeping?


07:38:23-07:38:25
What do we care about maintaining and cleaning?


07:38:25-07:38:26
Who's doing what?


07:38:26-07:38:27
What's left?


07:38:27-07:38:29
Do we let that stuff go?


07:38:29-07:38:31
Do we get outside help?


07:38:31-07:38:36
This conversation helped us split up the mental and physical loads of housekeeping more evenly.


07:38:36-07:38:40
We checked in and tweaked the plan as needed, and it worked out wonderfully.


07:38:40-07:38:43
We now both knew who was doing what.


07:38:43-07:38:47
And if something didn't get done, we didn't feel the need to step in and do it for them.


07:38:47-07:38:50
We could just remind each other of what we'd agreed to.


07:38:50-07:38:53
We worked together as equals and split up the work equitably.


07:38:53-07:38:57
We both took pride in our contributions and appreciated the other person's efforts.


07:38:57-07:38:59
And we didn't stress about what we couldn't get to.


07:38:59-07:39:02
We outsourced or let it go.


07:39:02-07:39:06
It's unrealistic to expect partners to be able to split work exactly down the middle.


07:39:06-07:39:08
What if someone is working longer hours?


07:39:08-07:39:12
What if housework takes one of you twice as long due to a disability?


07:39:12-07:39:14
Don't aim for equality, aim for equity.


07:39:14-07:39:22
Instead of looking at how much each person is doing, consider how much free time or energy each person has to be able to pursue their interests, hobbies, etc.


07:39:22-07:39:24
How well are each person's needs getting met?


07:39:24-07:39:36
Three years later, somehow stuck in the relationship death spiral I mentioned earlier, I was humbled to realize I'd still been parenting my partner just in a different way than I'd anticipated.


07:39:36-07:39:41
Turns out the emotional parts are also a huge chunk of any romantic relationship.


07:39:41-07:39:44
And you might find yourself managing all of that for both of you.


07:39:44-07:39:48
I took on these responsibilities without realizing it.


07:39:48-07:39:55
To make sure our relationship was emotionally healthy, I tried to understand what he needed and accommodated my own ADHD so I could meet his needs.


07:39:55-07:40:00
I also tried to understand what I needed and accommodate his odd-HD so he could meet my needs.


07:40:00-07:40:07
When I told my partner how exhausted I was for my efforts to support his mental health and maintain our relationship, he replied, I didn't ask you to.


07:40:07-07:40:10
And you know what?


07:40:10-07:40:11
He was right.


07:40:11-07:40:16
I took it upon myself to do all of this because I thought it needed to be done.


07:40:16-07:40:20
And not only could he not consent to my efforts to support, he also couldn't appreciate them.


07:40:20-07:40:32
It turned out he was doing a bit of the same for me as well, reminding me to pick up my medication, picking up after me, trying to be present for me through grief, even when he was overloaded and needed time alone.


07:40:32-07:40:33
And I hadn't even realized it.


07:40:33-07:40:38
My partner and I started making time for relationship check-ins every week.


07:40:38-07:40:41
Again, we asked, what's the plan?


07:40:41-07:40:45
When are we spending time together, time apart, and time with friends?


07:40:45-07:40:46
What are we doing for date night?


07:40:46-07:40:48
Let's get that on our calendar.


07:40:48-07:40:50
What's going on with your mental health?


07:40:50-07:40:51
Do you need support?


07:40:51-07:40:53
And if so, how are you going to get that support?


07:40:53-07:40:54
What part do you want me to play?


07:40:54-07:41:02
It's naive to think ADHD and the coexisting conditions we have along with it won't impact a relationship.


07:41:02-07:41:06
While acceptance is lovely, it's a place to start, not a place to end.


07:41:06-07:41:17
Whether it's about managing housework, buying a car, raising a child, working on the relationship, or supporting someone's mental health, the path to true partnership is the same.


07:41:17-07:41:27
