If you make a plan together, you can discuss what's yours and what's theirs and decide what to do as adults and partners, not as someone who has to manage the other.


07:41:27-07:41:32
The plan won't always go perfectly, but things can only get so far off track in a week.


07:41:32-07:41:34
If it's not working for either person, it can be tweaked.


07:41:34-07:41:37
Also, write it down.


07:41:37-07:41:39
One or both of you have ADHD.


07:41:39-07:41:41
Someone will forget.


07:41:41-07:41:42
Don't worry, I can remember.


07:41:42-07:41:46
You might think, no, no you can't, especially when you're stressed or anxious.


07:41:46-07:41:50
Bring a notepad, have your calendar at the ready, come with pens in tow.


07:41:50-07:41:54
It's hard to change old habits.


07:41:54-07:42:01
It wasn't easy for us to learn, and we did have to learn, to communicate our needs instead of just offering solutions or complaints.


07:42:01-07:42:05
It wasn't easy to learn how to support each other's efforts instead of replacing them.


07:42:05-07:42:07
Did I mention we both have anxiety?


07:42:07-07:42:12
Having a shared plan can be helpful for keeping anxiety in check too.


07:42:12-07:42:20
Before, my anxiety kept trying to make new plans, and he kept wandering off somewhere to anxiously ruminate on potential solutions for eight hours.


07:42:20-07:42:22
At which point, I'd already come up with a better plan.


07:42:22-07:42:27
This, it turns out, was a large part of the cycle we'd fallen into.


07:42:27-07:42:29
We've now been together for four years.


07:42:29-07:42:35
Our relationship is better than it's ever been, and it's a much healthier relationship than either of us have ever had.


07:42:35-07:42:36
Do we actually meet every week?


07:42:36-07:42:39
No, we both have ADHD.


07:42:39-07:42:45
But we do check in with each other when we miss these meetings, and we have honest, non-judgmental conversations about how to get back on track.


07:42:45-07:42:55
Interestingly, when we plan together, it's actually less work for both of us, because the work we're each committing to is more helpful and meaningful to the other.


07:42:55-07:42:57
It's work that is working.


07:42:57-07:43:02
It helps us feel more optimistic, more empowered, and more appreciated for our efforts too.


07:43:02-07:43:09
Chapter 13, how to change the world.


07:43:10-07:43:14
We never touch people so lightly that we do not leave a trace.


07:43:14-07:43:15
Peggy Tabor-Millen.


07:43:15-07:43:18
A change of plans.


07:43:18-07:43:26
I started my YouTube channel and my journey towards self-empowerment with the premise that I could overcome my ADHD struggles.


07:43:26-07:43:29
I didn't think I wouldn't have ADHD anymore, exactly.


07:43:29-07:43:37
But I thought I'd keep the parts about it that I liked, figure out which impairments were getting in my way and what to do about them, and then I would be successful.


07:43:37-07:43:46
I would finally be the person I was supposed to be, someone who had friends I could remember to keep up with, a clean house and perfectly managed finances.


07:43:46-07:43:55
There are all kinds of suggestions for ways we might overcome the obstacles we face, reach our potential, be this person we're supposed to be.


07:43:55-07:43:57
Just do blank, they suggest.


07:43:57-07:44:01
Buy this planner, set a timer, make a list.


07:44:01-07:44:05
We can life hack our way over our hurdles and not struggle anymore.


07:44:05-07:44:07
So this was my plan.


07:44:07-07:44:12
I was willing to accept that if I was different, I might have to do things differently.


07:44:12-07:44:15
What I hadn't been willing to accept was the limits of this approach.


07:44:15-07:44:24
Turns out, even doing things in a way that works for our brains and equipping ourselves with tools that make the world more accessible to us isn't a panacea.


07:44:24-07:44:30
If anyone could have overcome their ADHD, it would have been me.


07:44:30-07:44:33
I had the time, energy and resources.


07:44:33-07:44:35
I made it my entire career.


07:44:35-07:44:38
Unfortunately, that is not how it works.


07:44:38-07:44:43
There is a limit to how many of these tools we can use at any given time.


07:44:43-07:44:47
And none of them amount to a get out of struggling free card.


07:44:47-07:44:54
Almost every tool we need to use costs us something, time, money, energy.


07:44:54-07:45:06
We have to find them, learn how to use them, adapt them so they work for us, defend our need to use them, reapply for them, pay for them, and, yeah, find ways to remind ourselves to use them.


07:45:06-07:45:21
And the time, energy and other resources we spend on tools that others don't have to use is time, energy and other resources we can't dedicate to going out with friends, enjoying our hobbies, or experiencing the other parts of life that we value.


07:45:21-07:45:24
And those with ADHD don't need just one tool.


07:45:24-07:45:28
Throughout this audiobook, I have mentioned hundreds of them.


07:45:28-07:45:32
Dozens of these tools may be crucial for any given person with ADHD.


07:45:32-07:45:36
And they may need to use them on a daily basis just to get by.


07:45:36-07:45:42
Because ADHD is a chronic condition, these aren't tools we need temporarily either.


07:45:42-07:45:45
I collected these simple tools one at a time.


07:45:45-07:45:50
But seeing them all laid out, it's clear managing ADHD is not a simple task.


07:45:50-07:45:52
Just do this and this.


07:45:52-07:45:54
And these 50 other things.


07:45:54-07:45:55
What, forever?


07:45:55-07:46:00
At the end of my journey, my toolbox was overflowing.


07:46:00-07:46:04
I understood how and when to use which tool and why.


07:46:04-07:46:09
I'd equipped myself with what I needed to be able to function reasonably well in a neurotypical world.


07:46:09-07:46:14
And I was incredibly fortunate to be able to afford many that used to be out of my budget.


07:46:14-07:46:22
But no matter how many tools you have access to, and no matter how good you get at using them, it doesn't cancel out the struggle.


07:46:22-07:46:23
Not entirely.


07:46:23-07:46:25
Ask anyone in a wheelchair.


07:46:26-07:46:31
No matter how great their wheelchair is, there are places that aren't wheelchair accessible.


07:46:31-07:46:36
There are also biases, stigmas, misconceptions, and moral judgments placed on those who use them.


07:46:36-07:46:40
Tools are not enough.


07:46:40-07:46:43
Our tools need to interface well with our environment.


07:46:43-07:46:46
We need to be able to access them, use them.


07:46:46-07:46:49
We need to feel safe enough to disclose that we need them.


07:46:49-07:46:53
In an ideal world, we don't even have to think about them.


07:46:53-07:47:01
The tools we need to function effectively would be built into our environments, available for anyone to use without having to explain why.


07:47:01-07:47:07
In the same way we have built services such as electricity, sidewalks, and Internet into our public infrastructure.


07:47:07-07:47:09
This is possible.


07:47:09-07:47:18
At the same time I was discovering the limits of my ability to overcome my ADHD and live the very neurotypical life I'd always imagined I would.


07:47:18-07:47:24
Just as I was realizing there is only so much a human can change about who they are and what they are capable of.


07:47:24-07:47:29
I realized that the world could change and the world could change fast.


07:47:29-07:47:37
The world is made of systems and systems are far more flexible and adaptable than people are.


07:47:37-07:47:40
They can be built, undone, rearranged.


07:47:40-07:47:46
For example, how many of us were suddenly able to work from home during the COVID-19 pandemic?


07:47:46-07:47:58
As I learned more about systemic issues, I came across the social model of disability, which argues that disability isn't inherent to a person's medical condition.


07:47:58-07:48:07
It results from living in a world that doesn't cater to their needs the way it does the needs of those who are non-disabled, where the systems aren't built with them in mind.


07:48:07-07:48:16
Systemic issues were, I realized, the reason why I even needed half the tools I was using, and I was not alone.


07:48:16-07:48:24
Those with ADHD turn to extensive and elaborate systems to help them be consistently productive, manage their time, and keep their house relatively livable.


07:48:24-07:48:28
Not because it's fun, but because we don't have a choice.


07:48:28-07:48:36
We can't opt out of trying to manage our ADHD no matter the cost, because the whole world is telling us we have to figure it out.


07:48:36-07:48:38
We aren't good enough if we don't.


07:48:38-07:48:40
We're not acceptable if we don't.


07:48:40-07:48:43
We're going to get fired if we don't.


07:48:43-07:48:45
The cost is even higher if we don't.


07:48:46-07:48:54
I realized that it was far easier to create an environment that accounted for neurodiversity than it was for people to not be neurodiverse.


07:48:54-07:48:56
So why weren't we starting there?


07:48:56-07:49:13
If I wanted to reach my potential and to help those in my community do the same, I realized I needed to help build a world in which we reasonably could, in which the entire cost of making a system accessible didn't fall to the person who was already struggling.


07:49:13-07:49:22
We're not only expected to provide our own wheelchairs, we're also expected to build our own individual ramps when we clearly aren't the only ones who need them.


07:49:22-07:49:29
This mental, emotional, and physical labor is costly, and not just to us, but to those we love, and to society as a whole.


07:49:29-07:49:33
Employers deal with high turnover rates and burnout.


07:49:33-07:49:38
Our prisons are disproportionately populated with those who have untreated ADHD.


07:49:38-07:49:52
Studies have shown ADHD costs society billions of dollars a year in the United States alone, while treatments and supports for ADHD are only a tiny fraction of that cost.


07:49:52-07:49:56
This plan isn't working out well for anybody.


07:49:56-07:50:03
Expecting those with ADHD to overcome their ADHD is hurting us.


07:50:03-07:50:06
It's hurting those we love, and it's hurting society as a whole.


07:50:06-07:50:14
We need a better plan, one that relies on change that is possible, sustainable, and good for everyone.


07:50:14-07:50:18
I decided to change course.


07:50:18-07:50:19
I set out to change the world.


07:50:19-07:50:22
What I learned.


07:50:22-07:50:30
Like many people who set out to change the world, I discovered change was already in progress.


07:50:30-07:50:34
The world was already a far cry from what it had been when I started the channel.


07:50:34-07:50:39
In the years since, it has become much more neurodivergent friendly.


07:50:39-07:50:48
I also learned how what I had already been doing had played a part in that change, and how the changes we needed were not only possible, but good for everyone.


07:50:48-07:50:54
Individual empowerment contributes to systemic change.


07:50:54-07:51:01
I didn't realize this at first, but I was trying to trade one simple solution in for another.


07:51:01-07:51:05
We can't change the fact that we have ADHD, so it's the world that needs to change.


07:51:05-07:51:06
What can I say?


07:51:06-07:51:08
Humans like simple solutions.


07:51:08-07:51:14
There is a limit to how much we can reasonably empower ourselves in a world that isn't built with our needs in mind.


07:51:14-07:51:17
But that didn't mean my journey of self discovery had been a waste of time.


07:51:17-07:51:22
Learning about ADHD helps close the self discrepancy gap.


07:51:22-07:51:33
Before I started learning about ADHD, there was an enormous gap between the person I was and the person I thought I should be, the person others thought I should be.


07:51:33-07:51:38
It was this gap, not the ADHD, that made me feel inadequate.


07:51:38-07:51:47
It's what led me to accept the judgments others placed on me when they told me I wasn't living up to my potential and run myself into the ground trying to.


07:51:47-07:51:50
I see this over and over again in our community too.


07:51:50-07:51:55
I learned there is a term for this gap, self discrepancy.


07:51:55-07:52:02
And it is responsible for a great deal of the emotional turmoil and negative mental health outcomes anyone can experience.


07:52:02-07:52:07
When who they want or feel they're supposed to be doesn't match up with the person they actually are.


07:52:07-07:52:09
It's not unique to ADHD.


07:52:09-07:52:14
But self discrepancy and those of us with ADHD can be a straight up chasm.


07:52:14-07:52:21
The expectations we and others have of us are often wildly inconsistent with our reality.


07:52:21-07:52:27
Sometimes we can perform on a very high level and sometimes we struggle with quote unquote simple tasks.


07:52:27-07:52:36
Our self discrepancy is also more profound because ADHD is an unseen disability that is not accommodated or even accounted for in most environments.


07:52:37-07:52:46
The tools and information I collected and shared didn't allow me to overcome my ADHD, but they did help me close this gap.


07:52:46-07:52:57
What I learned allowed me to let go of unrealistic expectations and understand that for example, the variability in my performance was normal given my ADHD.


07:52:57-07:53:01
My potential wasn't a set point, it was a range.


07:53:01-07:53:13
I now had tools that allowed me to perform at a higher level more consistently and understood that some of my ADHD behaviors like fidgeting were actually healthy coping mechanisms for me.


07:53:13-07:53:18
This changed how I interacted with the world and how I advocated for myself.


07:53:18-07:53:21
It helped me accomplish what I wanted to accomplish more often.


07:53:21-07:53:31
I was able to adjust my understanding of who I should be to something closer to the person I already am while still moving toward the version of myself I wanted to be.


07:53:31-07:53:36
Learning about ADHD has helped many in this community do the same.


07:53:36-07:53:43
It's changed how we interact with ourselves, how we speak to ourselves, how we allow ourselves to be spoken to.


07:53:43-07:53:48
Our mental health and self-esteem is better for it.


07:53:48-07:53:58
We're slowly beginning to let go of and refuse to listen to the internalized messages that we're not enough, not doing enough, not good enough, or not reaching our potential.


07:53:58-07:54:04
We're understanding and subsequently educating others that we already are who we're supposed to be.


07:54:06-07:54:08
Individual empowerment has ripple effects.


07:54:08-07:54:13
I thought setting out to change the world meant starting over on a new journey.


07:54:13-07:54:18
I thought that once again, I would have no idea what I was doing and would need to learn from scratch.


07:54:18-07:54:27
What I discovered was that what I had been doing all these years instead of pushing for systemic change had led to systemic change.


07:54:27-07:54:42
Turns out I was part of the system, the work I had done accepting and understanding my own ADHD, talking about it openly and sharing what helped had had ripple effects that expanded far beyond my immediate reach.


07:54:42-07:54:49
Maybe I had intended for people to watch my videos, learn about their brain, and create a toolbox of strategies for themselves.


07:54:49-07:54:53
But I didn't realize how many in my community would go on to create their own ripples.


07:54:53-07:54:56
I'd known about a few examples.


07:54:56-07:55:05
A Scottish MP sent me a video of himself at Scottish Parliament requesting action be taken regarding a dangerously misleading documentary about ADHD.


07:55:05-07:55:12
A middle school teacher told me about a weekly support group she'd started for her ADHD students.


07:55:12-07:55:17
Some brains who watched the channel became advocates and built their own platforms, even wrote their own books.


07:55:17-07:55:35
But when I started talking to my community about wanting to focus on systemic change, even more people reached out to share how they had started employee resource groups, worked with political leaders about initiatives that could benefit our community, or asked for accommodations that their boss was now providing to other ADHD employees.


07:55:35-07:55:44
It slowly dawned on me that for every ripple I learned about, there were many more I didn't, and all these ripples created others.


07:55:44-07:55:49
They didn't stop at the person they were intended to reach, they kept spreading.


07:55:49-07:55:53
I looked back at the ripples that had reached me.


07:55:53-07:55:55
I wasn't just influencing others.


07:55:55-07:56:00
I'd also been influenced by the work others had done and the work of those before them.


07:56:00-07:56:11
The researchers whose papers I read probably didn't expect me, a random person with ADHD who accessed their paper through a nursing student I knew, to present their work on YouTube.


07:56:11-07:56:12
But here we were.


07:56:12-07:56:20
Professional and grassroots efforts have long been making ripples in the world of ADHD, long before how to ADHD or ADHD TikTok was a thing.


07:56:21-07:56:25
Other advocacy groups and organizations and communities have been making ripples too.


07:56:25-07:56:33
The LGBTQIA+ community laid the groundwork for how people can disclose important parts of their identity safely.


07:56:33-07:56:40
Autistic advocates and other disability advocates have been initiating discussions about neurodiversity, ableism, and cognitive accessibility.


07:56:40-07:56:51
Researchers and mental health providers have been more open about their own conditions and pushing for greater collaboration and more effective support of the communities their work is meant to benefit.


07:56:51-07:57:00
As I looked around me, I realized all of these ripples were converging and turning into waves of significant change.


07:57:00-07:57:09
Open conversations about neurodiversity, improved care for adult ADHD, environments that account for neurodiversity and are more equitable for those who have it.


07:57:09-07:57:14
These changes don't apply to everyone yet.


07:57:14-07:57:22
As cognitive scientist Dr. Deirdre Kelly puts it, the future already exists in pockets around the world.


07:57:22-07:57:31
When we embrace this fact and learn to see the good, we can make decisions and take actions that bring us closer to who and where we need to be.


07:57:31-07:57:33
Look for what's possible and realize it.


07:57:33-07:57:46
In order to encourage more products and services to be designed in a way that's ADHD friendly and help those with ADHD connect with those that already are, we also collaborated with Dr. Deirdre Kelly to create a free accessibility rubric.


07:57:46-07:57:50
You can find a link to this resource on my website, howtoadhdbook.com.


07:57:50-07:57:52
Phases of acceptance.


07:57:52-07:58:05
Unfortunately, even after so much progress, you may still have people in your lives, family, friends, coworkers, even healthcare providers, who view your ADHD struggles with skepticism.


07:58:05-07:58:07
Maybe you still do so yourself.


07:58:07-07:58:14
Those working in the LGBTQIA+ community often refer to stages someone may go through when coming out.


07:58:14-07:58:21
These rely significantly on the original model developed by Dr. Vivian Cass in 1979.


07:58:21-07:58:25
It has expanded to apply to those who are navigating their journey of acceptance for others.


07:58:25-07:58:29
People navigate the stages at their own pace, often bouncing among them.


07:58:29-07:58:34
These stages can also apply to acceptance of stigmatized disabilities.


07:58:34-07:58:40
Here, I've adapted the acceptance model by Monica Ludkade to demonstrate the stages I've seen in the acceptance of ADHD.


07:58:40-07:58:43
Anti.


07:58:43-07:58:46
Is in denial about the existence of ADHD or of you having it.


07:58:46-07:58:48
It's just laziness and poor discipline.


07:58:48-07:58:51
Acknowledgement.


07:58:51-07:58:55
Recognizes ADHD is real, but still has misconceptions about the condition.


07:58:55-07:58:57
You don't seem ADHD.


07:58:57-07:58:59
ADHD doesn't have to limit you.


07:58:59-07:59:02
Why should she get insert accommodation here?


07:59:02-07:59:03
That gives her an unfair advantage.


07:59:03-07:59:06
Acceptance.


07:59:06-07:59:12
Recognizes that ADHD brains work differently from most and that those differences create real impairments.


07:59:12-07:59:13
Believes your experience of it.


07:59:13-07:59:19
Okay, I didn't realize emotion dysregulation was an aspect of ADHD.


07:59:19-07:59:20
That makes sense.


07:59:20-07:59:22
Affirming.


07:59:22-07:59:26
Asks you about your experience and listens to your responses.


07:59:26-07:59:30
Encourages you to get the support you need and offers accommodations.


07:59:30-07:59:35
Thank you for telling me that you have trouble prioritizing when multiple parts of a project are due at the same time.


07:59:35-07:59:39
Would it help to have separate deadlines so you can focus on one at a time?


07:59:40-07:59:44
Advocate. Speaks up for those with ADHD even when they're not in the room.


07:59:44-07:59:48
Offers support to those who have it without them having to ask.


07:59:48-07:59:51
Speaks up for themselves and what they need to others.


07:59:51-07:59:56
Class, we are moving through this material pretty quickly and some students might have trouble taking notes.


07:59:56-08:00:01
I will be emailing everyone the lecture outline and my TA has compiled notes you can refer to as well.


08:00:01-08:00:06
I imagine you can think of people in your lives who fit into many of these categories.


08:00:06-08:00:08
For better and for worse.


08:00:08-08:00:14
Someone at a different point in their journey doesn't mean they're a terrible person who will never support you.


08:00:14-08:00:17
If you're like me, you can think of times when you were in a different stage than you are now.


08:00:17-08:00:23
We also may be at a different stage for ourselves than we are for others.


08:00:23-08:00:25
It's fine if others need accommodations, but not me.


08:00:25-08:00:28
I should be able to do this.


08:00:28-08:00:30
A journey involves motion.


08:00:30-08:00:38
And even when people don't always move at the pace we would like them to, most can become more accepting and supportive with more information and experience.


08:00:38-08:00:47
I will never forget someone who commented angrily on my videos about how ADHD was fake and none of what I was saying was true.


08:00:47-08:00:50
Only to come back a few years later and apologize.


08:00:50-08:00:57
They shared how they had been diagnosed with ADHD and hadn't been ready to accept it and how my videos had helped them do so.


08:00:57-08:01:01
The changes we're creating benefit everyone.


08:01:02-08:01:08
Motivation matters whether we're trying to convince ourselves or someone else to make a change.


08:01:08-08:01:17
Thankfully, the changes we're creating have clear benefits for ADHD brains, the hearts who love and care for us, our employers, and society as a whole.


08:01:17-08:01:20
Open conversations about neurodiversity.


08:01:20-08:01:27
As we talk, we normalize and improve our understanding of one another and the diversity within neurodiversity.


08:01:27-08:01:35
And for the first time, many of us are starting to feel like we don't have to fit in, in more and more spaces, we can belong.


08:01:35-08:01:42
Coping mechanisms and tools that work for us are becoming more normalized and expected.


08:01:42-08:01:48
This allows us the ability to use strategies that help us effectively cope with our challenges rather than hiding them.


08:01:48-08:01:57
We're finding each other, offering each other support, and even inspiring those who didn't realize they had ADHD to seek diagnosis and treatment when what we're saying sounds really familiar.


08:01:59-08:02:07
Without these conversations, many more would go years or even decades longer without the support they need to speak up, get support, and thrive.


08:02:07-08:02:16
And as we speak up about our ADHD, our loved ones, employers, and friends can connect with each other about their role in supporting us too.


08:02:16-08:02:19
Improved care for ADHD adults.


08:02:19-08:02:27
Adults with ADHD have always existed, but have gone untreated, undiagnosed, and under supported.


08:02:27-08:02:34
In fact, as of this writing, there are currently no guidelines for treating ADHD in adults.


08:02:34-08:02:37
As a result, there is a wide discrepancy of care.


08:02:37-08:02:46
This makes it difficult for those of us who don't know what to expect when we go to a new provider, and leaves providers guessing at the best treatment options.


08:02:46-08:02:47
This is changing.


08:02:47-08:02:52
There is now a professional consensus that people do not typically grow out of ADHD.


08:02:54-08:03:03
And right now, the American Professional Society of ADHD and Related Disorders, APSARD, is working on guidelines for diagnosis and treatment.


08:03:03-08:03:11
Not only is this good for those who treat and need treatment for ADHD, it's good for society as a whole.


08:03:11-08:03:18
Better care for adults with ADHD can positively impact all kinds of societal change we'd like to see.


08:03:18-08:03:27
Better mental health outcomes, safer roads, decreases in obesity and addiction rates, and improved general life expectancy, to name a few.


08:03:27-08:03:32
Workplaces and higher education offer more supports for ADHD.


08:03:32-08:03:41
Those with ADHD feel more fulfilled in their jobs and in school when they can perform at their best and contribute in a meaningful way.


08:03:41-08:03:48
More and more colleges are offering programs to support those whose brains work differently and encouraging students to use the accommodations available to them.


08:03:49-08:03:55
This not only improves our sense of belonging and our mental health, but also graduation rates.


08:03:55-08:04:05
Many employers have initiatives in place to hire for neurodiversity and are learning how to effectively support their neurodivergent employees and set them up for success once they're hired.


08:04:05-08:04:16
Accommodations in the workplace are often a bargain compared with the high cost of absenteeism, presenteeism, burnout, missed deadlines, and employee turnover.


08:04:16-08:04:18
When effectively supported, many of us excel.


08:04:19-08:04:25
ADHDers are often characterized as doers, go-getters, and problem solvers.


08:04:25-08:04:30
We can be swift decision makers and pursue achievements without letting challenges or obstacles deter us.


08:04:30-08:04:35
In many cases, the harder a problem is, the more we want to explore and solve it.


08:04:35-08:04:48
When we can spend less time slogging through paperwork, we can spend more time inventing things, problem solving, collaborating, and enthusiastically taking on projects that would be daunting to others.


08:04:48-08:04:52
Increased accessibility for everyone.


08:04:52-08:05:05
In Canada, there's a push to have the tools we need in school readily available, not as accommodations we have to fight for and complete paperwork to access, but as standard features that are available to anyone who needs them.


08:05:05-08:05:12
This is an example of universal design, which takes into account the diversity in human capabilities.


08:05:13-08:05:26
Universal design, now, the design of a product, service, or environment to be as accessible as possible to as many people as possible without the need for adaptation or accommodations.


08:05:26-08:05:36
Universal design works well because what creates accessibility for one group often makes everyday life easier for everyone.


08:05:36-08:05:38
Consider curb cutouts.


08:05:38-08:05:49
They allow wheelchair users to navigate crosswalks, but they are also helpful for those who are traveling with luggage, delivering packages, or riding those dope little electric scooters.


08:05:49-08:05:53
With universal design, supports are seamlessly integrated into our environment.


08:05:53-08:05:58
We don't have to go out of our way or call attention to our challenges to obtain them.


08:05:58-08:06:01
It also offers end user choice.


08:06:01-08:06:10
Just as we can decide whether or not to turn on our desk lamp to work, we can decide whether to use a standing desk or to sit, and so can everyone else.


08:06:11-08:06:22
This also cuts down on judgment and jealousy for those who may misunderstand the accommodations we need as an unfair advantage, as opposed to creating the accessibility we need due to the impairments we face.


08:06:22-08:06:30
ADHD-friendly spaces often have whiteboards, notepads, and other office supplies readily available.


08:06:30-08:06:35
They're quiet spaces we can use to focus or step away from our work when we get overwhelmed.


08:06:35-08:06:41
The tools we need are placed at the point of performance, so we don't have to remember to bring them or to hunt them down.


08:06:41-08:06:47
And there are meeting free days when we can allow ourselves to get into flow without worrying our hyper focus will get us in trouble.


08:06:47-08:06:59
These are all examples of how universal design creates greater accessibility for those with ADHD and other cognitive challenges, while offering helpful tools for everyone else.


08:06:59-08:07:07
Everyone has limited working memory, loses and forgets stuff, and experiences moments when there's too much demand on their executive function.


08:07:07-08:07:18
As Microsoft's guidebook on cognitive inclusivity explains, for any task to be successful, motivation must equal or surpass cognitive load.


08:07:18-08:07:25
By reducing cognitive load, we make the bar for success lower, which makes it easier for everyone to clear.


08:07:25-08:07:28
The toolbox.


08:07:28-08:07:32
There's a joke kicking around the ADHD community that I hear often.


08:07:32-08:07:39
Those with ADHD could change the world if we could just remember what we wanted and organize ourselves well enough to go after it.


08:07:39-08:07:43
It's endearing, and every time I hear it, I laugh along with everyone else.


08:07:43-08:07:46
I truly appreciate how it conveys a loving acceptance of our limits.


08:07:46-08:07:53
I also call bullshit on the conclusion that we can't change the world just because we're too ADHD to do it.


08:07:53-08:07:59
We are changing the world, and we're doing it without overcoming our ADHD.


08:07:59-08:08:06
We're doing it while being distractible, while running out of meds or facing barriers to accessing them, and while dealing with chronic pain.


08:08:07-08:08:15
We're building a more ADHD-friendly world intermittently and perfectly, sometimes unintentionally, but we're doing it.


08:08:15-08:08:15
How?


08:08:15-08:08:21
By working with our brains, not against them, and by putting our efforts where we can create the most impact.


08:08:21-08:08:25
One, find your fuel.


08:08:25-08:08:28
Those with ADHD tend to get discouraged more easily.


08:08:28-08:08:32
I tried to change the world in five minutes, but it didn't work.


08:08:32-08:08:35
Change often happens over time.


08:08:35-08:08:41
It starts by planting seeds that take time to grow, or casting a stone and seeing where the ripples go.


08:08:41-08:08:46
We need to stock up on fuel for what can sometimes be a long and frustrating ride.


08:08:46-08:08:50
Keep an inspiration for a rainy day file.


08:08:50-08:08:59
When I started posting videos, I read every comment and saved the ones that made me feel appreciated or showed me I was making a difference.


08:08:59-08:09:03
Rereading them on days when I was exhausted or discouraged kept me going.


08:09:03-08:09:11
Making an inspiration or feel-good file can help you stay positive, as well as tap into your emotional why when you inevitably forget why you thought this was a good idea.


08:09:11-08:09:14
Be the person you needed.


08:09:14-08:09:23
Many advocates doing incredible work are able to sustain it because we're creating what we needed and didn't have.


08:09:23-08:09:31
If you create the kind of support you needed, chances are it will benefit others too, and you'll be able to tap into the memory of how important it was for you.


08:09:32-08:09:35
Spend some time ideating.


08:09:35-08:09:40
Our brains excel at being creative, generating new ideas, and thinking outside the box.


08:09:40-08:09:43
Dream up the future you'd like to help build.


08:09:43-08:09:44
What does it look like?


08:09:44-08:09:46
Who does it serve?


08:09:46-08:09:47
How so?


08:09:47-08:09:49
What is happening in other pockets of the world?


08:09:49-08:09:51
And what might make sense to implement in ours?


08:09:51-08:09:54
Conserve your fuel too.


08:09:54-08:10:00
Changing the world doesn't mean trying to change the world in all the ways it needs to change.


08:10:00-08:10:04
Many people in our community are exhausted just trying to keep their heads above water.


08:10:04-08:10:10
So our efforts are better spent in areas we can have the most impact.


08:10:10-08:10:19
Everyone has stuff they can control, stuff they can influence, and stuff outside their sphere of influence, places where only the ripples can reach.


08:10:19-08:10:25
As much as possible, put your energy into what's within your sphere of influence, and take breaks.


08:10:25-08:10:27
This is a marathon, not a sprint.


08:10:28-08:10:31
Nick K, 40s, New York.


08:10:31-08:10:33
My secret weapon is crowd sourcing.


08:10:33-08:10:39
I understand myself better through others who are like-minded and find their perspective helps me appreciate who I am.


08:10:39-08:10:45
Feeling a sense of community and connection can break away stigmas and redefine what were once considered cultural norms.


08:10:45-08:10:49
Emmanuel A, 30s, Georgia.


08:10:49-08:10:57
Support groups for neurodivergence, for example, Ada, did a lot to help me understand myself and my ADHD.


08:10:57-08:11:05
It helped bridge the gaps of loneliness and isolation, helping me to understand the power of having representation and telling my story.


08:11:05-08:11:09
Chris L, 37, Washington.


08:11:09-08:11:19
I wrote Soft Focuses, a game to help non-neurodivergent people better understand what those of us with ADHD experience on a daily basis.


08:11:19-08:11:23
A number of people have told me how much it has helped their loved ones understand them better.


08:11:23-08:11:27
It has even helped some people realize they have ADHD.


08:11:27-08:11:31
It's a very niche game, but I'm really proud of it and glad to have helped others with it.


08:11:31-08:11:36
Two, focus on what you want, not what you don't want.


08:11:36-08:11:43
When J2, our operations director, was learning how to ride a motorcycle, she received this advice.


08:11:43-08:11:46
Don't look where you don't want to go, you'll crash.


08:11:46-08:11:50
Same with horseback riding, according to our community manager, Harley.


08:11:50-08:11:55
There's robust research to support the same as true when pushing for change.


08:11:55-08:12:02
Looking where you want to go and encouraging people to go there is much more effective than trying to correct people on what you don't want.


08:12:02-08:12:05
Set constructive goals.


08:12:05-08:12:17
Without a positive goal to work toward, both small and big picture, it's easy to fall into the trap of trying to avoid negative outcomes, which can lead us to avoid taking the action that would lead to a positive one.


08:12:17-08:12:22
You can't make a basket if you're so afraid of missing the hoop you never shoot the ball.


08:12:22-08:12:25
Frame requests in the positive.


08:12:26-08:12:36
People are generally more willing and able to do something than to stop doing something, and most requests to stop doing something negative can actually be framed in the positive.


08:12:36-08:12:48
For example, if you want someone to stop being a judgmental jerk to the new ADHDer at the office, instead, ask them to use welcoming and encouraging language.


08:12:48-08:12:53
This framing gets better results because it helps them understand what successfully meeting your request looks like.


08:12:54-08:12:57
Respond to the people who add to the conversation.


08:12:57-08:13:02
I learned early on in my YouTube career that your attention is like a spotlight.


08:13:02-08:13:05
Shine a light on what you want seen.


08:13:05-08:13:15
Ignore, delete, or block comments that don't move a conversation forward, or respectfully contribute a different perspective, so you can focus on responding to and signal boosting those that do.


08:13:15-08:13:18
In other words, don't feed the trolls.


08:13:18-08:13:20
Vote.


08:13:20-08:13:25
You can vote by casting a ballot in an election, and you can also vote with your dollars.


08:13:25-08:13:33
Support ADHD friendly initiatives, products, and services, as well as businesses that prioritize mental health or are taking steps to do so.


08:13:33-08:13:38
You can even vote with your attention by curating what you listen to, what you share, and what you repeat.


08:13:38-08:13:40
Alexis M.


08:13:40-08:13:42
30, Oregon.


08:13:42-08:13:45
My favorite way to help is on a small scale.


08:13:45-08:13:49
I'm the oldest diagnosed ADHDer in certain branches of my family tree.


08:13:49-08:13:55
As such, I can advocate for the younger ones who are less eloquent and or less respected.


08:13:55-08:14:00
Sometimes I'm asked for advice, which I give with disclaimers and encouragement to listen to the kids.


08:14:00-08:14:03
Sometimes I can give context for a frustrating situation.


08:14:03-08:14:12
Sometimes I help avert a meltdown by identifying and addressing a need that the other adults may be too dysregulated and or naively neurotypical to address themselves.


08:14:12-08:14:15
I know these things bring peace in the moment.


08:14:15-08:14:19
I think they contribute to an understanding family culture.


08:14:19-08:14:23
And I hope they help counter the negativity I know these kids will experience as they grow.


08:14:23-08:14:25
Jacob K.


08:14:25-08:14:27
27, Australia.


08:14:27-08:14:33
Earlier in life, I was so adamant to prove what I could do and what I was capable of.


08:14:33-08:14:40
Now that I'm on the other side, I want people to understand that a lot of what I achieved was done at the expense of my own health.


08:14:40-08:14:44
And that a need for accommodation doesn't equate to a lack of value.


08:14:44-08:14:49
Going forward, I want to use my achievements as leverage to affect change.


08:14:49-08:14:51
You like what I can do now?


08:14:51-08:14:56
Well, imagine how much more I could achieve if I didn't have one hand tied behind my back.


08:14:56-08:15:02
If nothing else, I want to fight for people, no matter their needs, to be able to live up to their full potential.


08:15:02-08:15:06
Three, join forces with the people doing the work.


08:15:06-08:15:11
Someone may already be working toward the change you're hoping to see in the world.


08:15:11-08:15:22
Joining forces with them makes the work of advocates and organizations more efficient because they don't have to compete for the same resources or start their advocacy work from scratch.


08:15:22-08:15:27
It also helps you see where there might be gaps and how you can fill them with your own efforts.


08:15:27-08:15:30
There are also many ways you can support the work that is already underway.


08:15:30-08:15:34
Donate or fundraise.


08:15:34-08:15:37
This book would not exist if it weren't for my Patreon brains.


08:15:37-08:15:47
Their donations have allowed me to quit my day job to focus on how to ADHD full time, hire a team, and take time away to write this book.


08:15:47-08:15:58
Many organizations with which I've spoken stress that the best way to support a cause is to provide them with the resources they're requesting, money, supplies, volunteers, and let them take it from there.


08:15:58-08:16:00
Signal boost.


08:16:00-08:16:10
Commenting, sharing, reposting, and sharing a message with people and organization or advocate wouldn't otherwise reach is another way to make serious ripples.


08:16:10-08:16:18
It can be especially helpful to signal boost to others in the position to make a change, such as your employer, school board, or local representative.


08:16:18-08:16:22
Share resources.


08:16:22-08:16:24
Don't underestimate the power of sharing what you know.


08:16:24-08:16:30
You might connect someone with information that they in turn can use to inform and improve their own work.


08:16:30-08:16:33
When doing so, it can be helpful to vet your info.


08:16:33-08:16:37
At howtoadhdbook.com, I include a bonus tool on how to do that.


08:16:37-08:16:39
Collaborate.


08:16:39-08:16:47
In the ADHD and neurodiversity advocacy circles to which I belong, collaboration is welcomed and encouraged.


08:16:47-08:16:57
While we tackle ADHD from different angles and using different mediums, and we may not always agree on everything, we're all working toward the same goal, a better life for those who have it.


08:16:57-08:17:01
Todd H, 44, Australia.


08:17:01-08:17:08
I started a support group at work of just five people, which over 18 months grew to 35.


08:17:08-08:17:13
We share knowledge, advice, and experiences of all things, including diagnosis and treatment paths.


08:17:13-08:17:17
Claudia S, 32, Mexico.


08:17:17-08:17:29
I've had the opportunity to organize a couple of panels for PAX conventions, where we talk about intersectionality in video games and TTRPGs, tabletop role playing games.


08:17:29-08:17:34
It's been great for me and my fellow panelists, and I think we had the good luck of talking in front of open-minded audiences.


08:17:34-08:17:38
John Y, 75, Texas.


08:17:38-08:17:44
I work with teachers and counselors to help create more ADHD-friendly policies and spaces in our schools.


08:17:44-08:17:52
I have 35 years of classroom experience and 15 years as a journalist, and I now understand how to advocate for ADHD diversities.


08:17:52-08:17:55
So I write novels with ADHD characters.


08:17:55-08:17:56
I write op-eds.


08:17:56-08:18:00
I mentor reading programs and students in need or with neurodiverse conditions.


08:18:00-08:18:03
Four, share your story.


08:18:05-08:18:14
According to research on reducing stigma about mental health, telling stories is one of the most impactful ways to lay the groundwork for change.


08:18:14-08:18:17
Humans don't respond to data or how right you are.


08:18:17-08:18:21
They respond to emotion, to how something makes them feel.


08:18:21-08:18:36
By speaking to even one person about your ADHD or shining a light on one small positive action toward an ADHD accessible world, you may be creating ripples that will lead to change in someone you will never meet and in systems you will never even see.


08:18:36-08:18:40
Joseph, 22, Ohio.


08:18:40-08:18:44
One of the best decisions I ever made was to come out as neurodivergent.


08:18:44-08:18:53
I feel so much more like myself, and I've been able to connect with other people who share my experiences and even speak publicly about neurodivergence.


08:18:53-08:18:58
Being told that my words make a difference is why I continue to write and speak publicly about neurodivergence.


08:18:58-08:19:02
Julia F, 39, California.


08:19:03-08:19:09
I'm unapologetically open about my odd-HD in both my personal and professional life.


08:19:09-08:19:14
When the Adderall shortage hit me, I was up front with my boss and team.


08:19:14-08:19:16
I speak in open public work channels.


08:19:16-08:19:20
I'm raising my daughter to be proud of how her brain works, to never be ashamed.


08:19:20-08:19:25
Tawny F, 43, Colorado.


08:19:25-08:19:33
I create art that touches on mental health and societal divergence as an opening to have deeper conversations on what it means to be human.


08:19:33-08:19:37
I ask the question, who made the socially acceptable rules this way?


08:19:37-08:19:38
Why?


08:19:38-08:19:41
What if we chose not to follow them?


08:19:41-08:19:49
I believe it has helped the divergence feel less alone, with the added benefit of having conversations with typicals that may expand their understanding of others, as well as themselves.


08:19:49-08:19:53
Alana G, 30, Pennsylvania.


08:19:53-08:20:04
Whenever I require an accommodation, I try to openly state it's for ADHD, even when it's not necessary, to help let people know that this diagnosis requires accommodations in adulthood.


08:20:04-08:20:08
Jen R, 32, USA.


08:20:08-08:20:13
As difficult as it is, I disclose whenever I feel safe enough.


08:20:13-08:20:21
When people see ADHD in someone they interact with every day, in person or online, they see it doesn't make them a monster.


08:20:21-08:20:24
It's not just extremes or stereotypes.


08:20:24-08:20:27
There is power in demystifying other.


08:20:27-08:20:37
By existing as authentically as I can and being open and honest about my struggles and triumphs, I hopefully help return a human element to the cold and clinical.


08:20:37-08:20:39
Different is not less.


08:20:39-08:20:41
Perspective.


08:20:41-08:20:48
As you set out to change the world, know that you aren't approaching it with the same perspective as someone else.


08:20:48-08:20:58
People may be convinced change is impossible, want a different kind of change, want change to happen faster, or think things are fine as they are.


08:20:58-08:21:01
There are many reasons why you may perceive the world differently.


08:21:01-08:21:04
One is that brains are just glitchy.


08:21:04-08:21:07
We see the world skewed all of the time.


08:21:07-08:21:15
I learned this from The Skeptic's Guide to the Universe, but I was reminded of it by one of the quotes I collected while writing this book.


08:21:15-08:21:20
Brian, a grief counselor living in Washington, described how he lost a finger in an accident.


08:21:20-08:21:26
After I lost my finger, my brain couldn't accept that it was missing.


08:21:26-08:21:29
So it assumed there was something wrong with the world.


08:21:29-08:21:39
When I would rest my hand on a table, my brain perceived that there was a hole in the table where my finger should be, rather than recognizing that there was no finger anymore.


08:21:39-08:21:45
It took at least half a year before my brain started to accept that the world wasn't broken or missing, part of me was.


08:21:45-08:21:50
Our view of the world comes in through our senses.


08:21:50-08:21:57
Our brain encodes this information by comparing it to what we already know and fills in any gaps.


08:21:57-08:22:03
When the information coming in makes sense to us and fits with our current perspective, we're able to incorporate it.


08:22:03-08:22:10
When it threatens our self-esteem and self-concept, however, we often reject it.


08:22:10-08:22:15
This is important to understand, because what it means is this.


08:22:15-08:22:21
The way people change their minds isn't by replacing what they know with what we are telling them.


08:22:21-08:22:25
It's by incorporating it, when they can, into their existing perspective.


08:22:26-08:22:29
Perspective shifts can't be forced.


08:22:29-08:22:38
If you're sharing information with someone that contradicts what they currently believe, it can take time for their perspective to shift, if it happens at all.


08:22:38-08:22:43
This is the case even if the information is true, and you can show them evidence that it's true.


08:22:43-08:22:51
Sometimes people build fortresses around their perspectives and find evidence to reinforce them so that no one can kick them over.


08:22:52-08:22:56
What can make it easier for someone to see what we see is connection.


08:22:56-08:23:06
In an Italian poem called Quattro Occhi, the poet describes how when you're in love, you get to see the world through four eyes instead of two.


08:23:06-08:23:12
I adore this poem because it captures some of the main reasons why loving someone can be life changing.


08:23:12-08:23:23
We get to see ourselves through the perspective of someone who thinks we're beautiful or brilliant or brave, who can see our strengths, even when we may only see our struggles.


08:23:23-08:23:26
And we get to experience the rest of the world through their perspective too.


08:23:26-08:23:37
The danger here, of course, is that if we trust others more than we trust ourselves, we can still end up experiencing the world through only two eyes, theirs.


08:23:37-08:23:50
This can be especially common with an abusive relationship in which you are isolated, manipulated, or experiencing gaslighting, talked out of your own perspective and into one that benefits them to the point that you feel like you're losing your mind.


08:23:50-08:23:57
The same can occur if you spend a lot of time in a space where everyone hears and repeats the same perspective.


08:23:57-08:24:02
This is what happens for too many of us with ADHD.


08:24:02-08:24:13
We don't have the chance to view the world through our own two eyes because for most of our lives, we've experienced and internalized the neurotypical perspective on how we should exist in the world.


08:24:13-08:24:26
Back when I was trying to be an actress, I had all of these people, agents, managers, casting directors telling me how they saw me and what they thought I needed to change in order to be acceptable in their world.


08:24:26-08:24:37
When someone in the industry finally told me that it was okay to take up space as the shape that I am, I was able to begin to shift my perspective of who I was and who I had to be.


08:24:37-08:24:39
In turn, I've been able to do that for others.


08:24:39-08:24:49
How I have changed the world has not been through demanding that it change, but by sharing my perspective and listening to the perspectives of others.


08:24:49-08:24:52
By learning to see the world through four eyes instead of two.


08:24:52-08:25:01
Opening our minds to other perspectives when we feel safe enough to do so exposes us to new ideas and gives us new choices.


08:25:01-08:25:03
It can help us change the world.


08:25:03-08:25:10
We can share what we each know with one another, which gives us access to new information and new tools.


08:25:10-08:25:16
Plus, people can hear our perspective more clearly when we talk with them and try to understand theirs.


08:25:16-08:25:20
What this means though, is you can't just change the world.


08:25:20-08:25:24
You also have to be ready to be changed.


08:25:24-08:25:30
Having these conversations can expand your perspective, but it can sometimes threaten, even topple it.


08:25:30-08:25:36
My own perspective has shifted drastically many times in the process of listening to and learning from this community.


08:25:36-08:25:41
Research, other advocates, my team, and random strangers on the Internet.


08:25:42-08:25:50
While the shifts in our own perspective that may come from these conversations can be disorienting, I'm happy to report that they can also be empowering.


08:25:50-08:25:55
The more we share perspectives, the more complete ours becomes.


08:25:55-08:26:00
It becomes easier to connect with others and hear their perspectives without losing our own.


08:26:00-08:26:11
The new perspectives become less threatening to the worldview we are constructing, and our vision of the better world we work for becomes clearer and more achievable.


08:26:12-08:26:16
I'll leave you with a final perspective, one Deirdre shared with me.


08:26:16-08:26:21
If you want to change the world, you can, now, as you are.


08:26:21-08:26:36
If you want a world that is more accepting and understanding, accept and understand someone, and the world will already be a more accepting and understanding place, it's inevitable because you are part of the system.


08:26:36-08:26:37
We all are.


08:26:41-08:26:43
Stories and endings.


08:26:43-08:26:48
Two roads diverged in a yellow wood, and sorry I could not travel both.


08:26:48-08:26:55
And be one traveler, long I stood, and looked down one as far as I could, to where it bent in the undergrowth.


08:26:55-08:26:59
Robert Frost, The Road Not Taken.


08:26:59-08:27:03
I recently discovered I missed the most beautiful thing about this poem.


08:27:03-08:27:09
So do most people, which is why it's often mistakenly titled as The Road Less Taken.


08:27:09-08:27:13
The path the narrator took wasn't the road less taken.


08:27:13-08:27:18
As he points out earlier in the poem, both paths were worn the same.


08:27:18-08:27:28
But now that he's chosen one, he knows he'll someday tell a story of how he chose which road to take, and that he'll tell it in a way that gives meaning to his choice.


08:27:28-08:27:32
I took the one less traveled by, and that has made all the difference.


08:27:34-08:27:39
But and therefore, our lives are not stories on a page.


08:27:39-08:27:43
We don't get to determine what comes next or decide what the other characters will do.


08:27:43-08:27:47
Our lives are nonetheless full of story.


08:27:47-08:27:55
The stories we tell ourselves, the narratives we are trying to play out, the pages from the past we're trying to rewrite.


08:27:55-08:28:01
And just as in movies and books, the story paths in our lives aren't linear.


08:28:01-08:28:06
It's not we do this and then this and then we do this and this and this.


08:28:06-08:28:12
Like all good stories, we are faced with obstacles and choices.


08:28:12-08:28:14
A but and a therefore.


08:28:14-08:28:20
Our main character does this, but this happens, therefore, they make a new choice.


08:28:20-08:28:28
Whether the obstacle they faced was internal or external, this is a choice point for our hero.


08:28:28-08:28:32
They can attempt to continue the way they were going, or they can change direction.


08:28:32-08:28:35
Their choices may be limited.


08:28:35-08:28:38
They may not have any ideal choices.


08:28:38-08:28:44
They may not even have all the information they need to make the choice that seems obvious to those witnessing the story unfold.


08:28:44-08:28:48
But they do have a choice, and they make that choice.


08:28:48-08:28:58
As far as my own story goes, spending the last seven years learning how my brain worked has helped me recognize more of these choice points.


08:28:58-08:29:05
It helped me understand the buts, the invisible obstacles I kept running into, and gave me more effective options to choose from.


08:29:05-08:29:12
I went from this, I need to fill out this form, but I keep getting frustrated and overwhelmed.


08:29:12-08:29:19
Therefore, I'll avoid it and play video games while feeling like a terrible human who is incapable of adulting, to this.


08:29:19-08:29:25
I need to fill out this form, but I keep getting frustrated and overwhelmed.


08:29:25-08:29:28
Therefore, this might be a working memory issue.


08:29:28-08:29:37
I need to work one step at a time, have the instructions pulled up for reference, or maybe use an active body double who can read them to me as I fill this thing out.


08:29:37-08:29:45
I learned to let go of how I thought my efforts should go, and make choices based on how they did.


08:29:45-08:29:52
I made my invisible obstacles visible, which made it possible to navigate around them, sometimes.


08:29:52-08:29:54
I know what to do when I still run into them.


08:29:54-08:29:58
They slow me down, but they no longer stop me.


08:29:58-08:30:06
Because I learned how my brain works, I went from not respecting myself because of my failures, to respecting the challenges contributing to them.


08:30:06-08:30:09
And this ultimately helped me better respect myself.


08:30:09-08:30:22
As with the hero in the hero's journey, or the traveler in the road not taken, our power doesn't lie in deciding how the story goes, or even in predicting how it will.


08:30:23-08:30:28
Our power lies in evaluating information as it comes in, and in making new choices.


08:30:28-08:30:35
That choice can involve an action, or the meaning we assign to our experiences.


08:30:35-08:30:43
As Robert Frost perhaps hinted at in his poem, how we tell our stories is a choice we can make long after our path-choosing days are behind us.


08:30:43-08:30:52
Now, I'm learning to give myself choice, not just with how I'm navigating an obstacle on my path, but what path I'm even on.


08:30:53-08:30:59
There have been lots of warning signs that indicate a path isn't working out for me, or going to get me where I'm trying to go.


08:30:59-08:31:09
But no matter how many warning signs I pass, I still sometimes get stuck on following it, especially now that I have so many options for navigating the obstacles along the way.


08:31:09-08:31:15
In a way, I'm still trying harder, just with more ADHD friendly options in my toolbox.


08:31:15-08:31:23
If I want true empowerment, it's not enough to have choice in how I navigate the obstacles on my path.


08:31:23-08:31:25
I need choice as to what paths I follow, too.


08:31:25-08:31:29
I need choice in whether to continue down them.


08:31:29-08:31:34
With the support of a grief and loss counselor, I'm learning to give myself that choice.


08:31:34-08:31:45
As he gently points out to me, we never have complete choice, but learning to unattach from achieving or avoiding a specific outcome gives us greater access to the choices we do have.


08:31:47-08:31:50
I had no choice but to give myself choice.


08:31:50-08:31:55
Finishing this book represents, for me, the end of a seven year journey.


08:31:55-08:32:00
One where I put much of my life on pause so I could figure out how to work with my brain more effectively.


08:32:00-08:32:10
At the beginning of my journey, I set out to learn to work with my brain, not against it, and put everything I learned somewhere I could find it again.


08:32:10-08:32:15
Then, once I'd accomplished that, I could go back to my life, only this time successful.


08:32:16-08:32:22
I planned my journey the same way most people would plan to go to the store for the ingredients they needed to make dinner.


08:32:22-08:32:24
I'll just run out real quick, be back soon.


08:32:24-08:32:25
Can you pause the show?


08:32:25-08:32:33
It quickly became clear this was a much more complicated how to work with my brain recipe than I'd expected, with a lot of neuro spiciness involved.


08:32:33-08:32:40
And until I reached the very end of the path, I didn't totally believe I could get to this point.


08:32:40-08:32:44
I kept feeling that someone might realize I shouldn't be here and kick me out of the store.


08:32:44-08:32:48
What's a college dropout doing explaining research on the Internet?


08:32:48-08:32:50
Giving a TEDx talk, writing a book.


08:32:50-08:32:59
Also, I had never in my life finished a long term project before, no matter how often people told me I had the potential to do it.


08:32:59-08:33:04
I was worried I'd fail, or get bored, or burn out.


08:33:04-08:33:08
Sometimes I did, but I made it.


08:33:08-08:33:12
I basically put myself through neurodiversity university.


08:33:12-08:33:14
I turned in the manuscript for this book.


08:33:14-08:33:17
I reached the end of this path.


08:33:17-08:33:22
And the day I did, I realized I could finally go back to that life.


08:33:22-08:33:26
Excited, I scanned through the goals I'd put on pause.


08:33:26-08:33:30
Building the beautiful backyard, I was so close to finishing for my mom.


08:33:30-08:33:35
It had an optimistically large fire pit area that I could use to entertain new friends.


08:33:35-08:33:41
Planting the organic vegetable garden that was going to be the finishing touch to that backyard.


08:33:41-08:33:47
Being better about memorizing my lines before my acting class, so I didn't have to miss half the class trying to remember what they were.


08:33:47-08:33:52
Spending time with friends without panicking about what I was behind on.


08:33:52-08:33:59
Feeling more confident about attending the recurring pool parties at my co-workers building once I understood how to navigate social situations.


08:33:59-08:34:12
Impressing my agent and casting directors, and finally earning the leading roles my team said I had the potential to book so I could make my mom's life easier and make up for the ways in which I had made it so hard.


08:34:13-08:34:16
A lot of those goals didn't make sense anymore.


08:34:16-08:34:19
So much had changed since I set out on this journey.


08:34:19-08:34:21
My mom had died.


08:34:21-08:34:23
I had processed that much.


08:34:23-08:34:31
It was heartbreaking, the realization that there was nothing I could do to make her life easier anymore, when that's why I'd so badly wanted to succeed.


08:34:31-08:34:34
And why I went on this journey in the first place.


08:34:34-08:34:41
That loss of meaning was one of the main factors that led to the experience I shared earlier in how to feel.


08:34:41-08:34:45
I knew we'd sold that house and with it the backyard.


08:34:45-08:34:46
The place I live now doesn't have one.


08:34:46-08:34:49
I'd quit my job waiting tables.


08:34:49-08:34:52
There were no co-workers pool parties to attend.


08:34:52-08:34:55
I live in Seattle now and I don't know many people here yet.


08:34:55-08:34:57
Also, we don't really have pools.


08:34:57-08:35:04
I decided I liked what I do now more than acting so there was no point in going back to class.


08:35:04-08:35:07
Besides, it was 1,000 miles away.


08:35:07-08:35:12
I could go back to my life except it's been seven years.


08:35:12-08:35:15
I have nothing of that life left to go back to.


08:35:15-08:35:19
This realization hit me hard.


08:35:19-08:35:23
I'd been so attached to how my journey was supposed to end that I hadn't noticed.


08:35:23-08:35:24
It no longer could.


08:35:24-08:35:33
Obviously, I knew I had a new career, lived in a new city, and had a new partner, and I loved these life developments.


08:35:33-08:35:39
I'd known I was giving up some of the life I thought I'd be going back to, but all of it, I hadn't chosen that.


08:35:39-08:35:41
Would I have?


08:35:41-08:35:49
Would I have chosen to keep going on this path if I had recognized my ability to make a different choice as new information came in?


08:35:49-08:35:55
Would I have decided that I'd made enough progress on this path before the life I had known had all fallen away?


08:35:55-08:36:02
Would I have resumed some parts of my life if I hadn't been so stuck on finishing what I'd set out to do?


08:36:02-08:36:09
If I'd been mindful enough to notice the last bits of my past ambitions slowly disappearing while I was trying to become good enough to deserve them?


08:36:09-08:36:16
Maybe more importantly, was I okay with how this journey turned out?


08:36:16-08:36:25
Was I okay with the fact that I didn't have the chance to return to the life I'd wanted to live, that I was building out my life from the one I'd created along the way?


08:36:25-08:36:35
It is another truth in stories that what a character sets out in search of at the beginning of a story is not what they get in the end.


08:36:35-08:36:43
They might not wind up with a thing that is so important and powerfully motivating that they walk through fire and slay dragons to attain it.


08:36:43-08:36:47
Yet, we still have happy endings.


08:36:47-08:36:55
Why? By the end of their journey, they realize what they set out to pursue isn't what they actually needed.


08:36:55-08:36:58
I understand now that the same was true for me.


08:36:58-08:37:09
When I set out on this journey to learn about my brain, what I thought I needed were the tools and understanding to overcome my ADHD struggles and become the person I was supposed to be.


08:37:09-08:37:14
If I could use these tools to navigate around my invisible obstacles, I reasoned.


08:37:14-08:37:18
I could finally be happy, live a fulfilling life, and care for the people I love.


08:37:18-08:37:23
What I actually needed was to let go.


08:37:23-08:37:34
I needed to let go of rigid expectations of who I was supposed to be in order to be happy, because these rigid expectations were a large source of my unhappiness.


08:37:34-08:37:41
I needed to let go of the idea that I had to reach a certain level of functionality before I could enjoy my life and care for the people I love.


08:37:41-08:37:52
What I truly had to overcome was that biased perspective, so I could enjoy my life and care for the people I love in the ways I already could.


08:37:53-08:38:07
I needed to accept who I am, where I am, and what I had to give, and find joy and satisfaction in myself and my journey as is, even as I'm pursuing more, even as I'm trying to grow.


08:38:07-08:38:18
If my sense of worth hinged on being someone I'm not, or somewhere I'm not, I might spend my whole life chasing those things before I allow myself to actually live.


08:38:19-08:38:27
I might, and did, lose the life I wanted, even as I tried to make it to the starting line of being good/capable enough to live it.


08:38:27-08:38:43
I wish someone had told me I didn't need to be good at everything to be valuable, that I could be considered a good friend even though I forget to text back, or run a successful business without being able to successfully manage the contents of my car.


08:38:43-08:38:52
If anyone told me I was acceptable as is ADHD and all, I forgot, or I couldn't hear it through the noise of the whole world telling me otherwise.


08:38:52-08:38:55
So I'm saying it to you, for you.


08:38:55-08:38:58
You already are the person you're supposed to be.


08:38:58-08:39:05
You're already reaching the range of potential you have with the tools and skills and resources available to you.


08:39:05-08:39:10
That range may change over time, but this is how our brains work.


08:39:10-08:39:17
We'll work better on some days than others, and our level of focus will depend on how engaging a task is.


08:39:17-08:39:19
We'll get distracted.


08:39:19-08:39:23
We'll need things written down so we don't have to hold so much in our head.


08:39:23-08:39:27
We'll lose track of time and underestimate how long things will take.


08:39:27-08:39:31
We'll be impressively good at some things and epically bad at others.


08:39:31-08:39:35
We'll have a lot to give and have trouble consistently giving it.


08:39:35-08:39:43
And we are acceptable humans as is, not once we stop having ADHD.


08:39:43-08:39:47
You do not need to be fixed because you are not a broken version of normal.


08:39:47-08:39:52
Your brain works differently, and the goal can't be to fix that.


08:39:52-08:39:54
It needs to be given that.


08:39:54-08:39:55
What do we do?


08:39:55-08:39:57
What do we want to try?


08:39:57-08:39:58
What do we want to give?


08:39:58-08:40:00
What's worth doing?


08:40:00-08:40:03
What do I want to do and give?


08:40:03-08:40:05
It's kind of cool that now I get to choose.


08:40:05-08:40:15
Because I'm not going back to the life I was supposed to live, I can live a life more in line with my values, not with the values I thought I should have.


08:40:15-08:40:19
I can get curious about what my values are and what a life in line with them looks like.


08:40:19-08:40:23
I like that I'm getting to start from a relatively blank slate.


08:40:23-08:40:27
And yet, it would have been nice to have chosen that.


08:40:27-08:40:32
It took my brain just a couple of days to come up with a new hero's journey.


08:40:32-08:40:39
Once again, I'm setting out with a vision that I believe is worth walking through fire and battling dragons to achieve.


08:40:39-08:40:44
I want to be a good leader for my team and make space on my platform for voices besides mine.


08:40:44-08:40:51
The journey I'm personally on is no longer about self-improvement, but personal fulfillment.


08:40:51-08:40:54
Dedicating seven years to self-improvement was quite long enough.


08:40:54-08:41:00
Now that I understand how my brain works, I've chosen a new path to take.


08:41:00-08:41:09
To drop the compared to neurotypical stencil away from myself and start building toolbox in hand, a life that's based on the shape that I am.


08:41:09-08:41:14
I want to learn not just how to do things, but what's worth doing.


08:41:14-08:41:22
To answer a new question, not how do we reach our potential, but how do we live a fulfilling life as the person we already are?


08:41:22-08:41:28
This time around though, I'm also installing a few checkpoints.


08:41:28-08:41:31
I'm using what I learned about queued recall to help myself remember.


08:41:31-08:41:33
I get to choose my paths.


08:41:33-08:41:38
Every once in a while, I can look around to make sure that this is still the one I want.


08:41:38-08:41:46
And even though imagining the future motivates me, I'm trying to let go a bit of my expectations for how I think it will or should turn out.


08:41:46-08:41:49
Because I understand now that it may not go that way.


08:41:49-08:41:56
I've also learned not to put my whole life and personal fulfillment on hold until I reach a certain outcome.


08:41:56-08:41:59
Many times in my life I've done this.


08:41:59-08:42:04
In seven year journeys and in I need to finish this project first sprints.


08:42:04-08:42:09
But if there's anything I've learned, it's that there are no guarantees.


08:42:09-08:42:11
I won't always have time after.


08:42:11-08:42:15
The results I'm hoping to achieve won't always even be possible.


08:42:15-08:42:18
I'm never going to be someone who doesn't have ADHD.


08:42:18-08:42:23
Therefore, leading a life in line with my values can start now.


08:42:23-08:42:25
It could have started then.


08:42:25-08:42:27
Sure, I have limits.


08:42:27-08:42:32
But as fellow YouTuber and ADHDer Hannah Hart told me, creativity loves limits.


08:42:32-08:42:36
Hey look, a starting line.


08:42:36-08:42:38
Turns out we can draw our own.


08:42:38-08:42:44
You don't have to be who or where you want to be to begin enjoying yourself.


08:42:44-08:42:50
Accepting yourself, respecting yourself, caring for yourself.


08:42:50-08:42:52
To begin leading a life in line with your values.


08:42:52-08:42:56
And you don't have to be capable of all of the things to be valuable.


08:42:56-08:42:57
That's a lie we were taught.


08:42:57-08:43:02
You already are the person you're supposed to be.


08:43:02-08:43:04
And you, as you are, have plenty to give.


08:43:04-08:43:09
It doesn't mean you can't grow or that you can't pursue goals.


08:43:09-08:43:13
But it does mean you don't have to earn the right to enjoy your life.


08:43:13-08:43:20
Care for yourself, contribute your talents, or take breaks, or do whatever else you've put on hold.


08:43:20-08:43:27
You can do all that now, or just a little part of it, even if it's a smaller part than you would like.


08:43:27-08:43:35
I hope what you've found in this audiobook is not just information, but the ability to recognize more of the choice points in your own story.


08:43:35-08:43:43
Wherever you are along your journey, I hope that having listened to this audiobook, you can go back to a life that is better for us having traveled together.


08:43:43-08:43:50
I hope you can let go a little of the outcome you're counting on, of the expectations you've internalized.


08:43:50-08:43:55
I hope you can keep making progress in the areas that are important to you while you learn and grow.


08:43:55-08:43:59
And if you need more support, well, you know where you can find how to eat HD.


08:43:59-08:44:05
Seven years ago, I decided to put what I learned in a place that couldn't be lost.


08:44:05-08:44:07
Turns out, I put my brain and heart there too.


08:44:07-08:44:18
Whether you found us through a YouTube channel, a TEDx talk, or connected with us for the first time in this audiobook, know that you are never alone, you belong.


08:44:18-08:44:22
There's an entire community of us that exists all across the globe.


08:44:23-08:44:28
I hope as we walk our many paths, our brains and hearts keep finding one another.


08:44:28-08:44:33
Wait, one more thing.


08:44:33-08:44:35
This is important, I swear.


08:44:35-08:44:42
Me because I have ADHD and can't tell any story in order ever without leaving something important out, including apparently mine.


08:44:42-08:44:45
Lean into your strengths.


08:44:45-08:44:50
As I was finishing this book, I decided to get in touch with Dr. Ned Hallowell.


08:44:50-08:44:57
In his work as a psychiatrist, author, and ADHD advocate, he champions the strengths that come with ADHD.


08:44:57-08:45:01
He's always been so supportive of me and the work that I do.


08:45:01-08:45:07
And I was afraid he'd hate what I'd written because so much of my journey involved learning about my impairments.


08:45:07-08:45:10
I might have Hallowell's heart, but I have Barkley's research.


08:45:10-08:45:22
When it came to navigating the world and accomplishing goals that were meaningful to me, acceptance, reassurances, and platitudes about how my ADHD made me special hadn't helped me at all.


08:45:22-08:45:27
What had was understanding how my brain worked on an empirical and scientific level.


08:45:27-08:45:31
This knowledge gave me the ability to work with it effectively.


08:45:31-08:45:38
I'd found it empowering and validating to learn about my deficits and impairments in bits at a time.


08:45:38-08:45:42
But now I had collected all this information in one place.


08:45:42-08:45:46
I was worried that the picture of ADHD I painted might be too negative.


08:45:46-08:45:51
Would the alternate universe version of me who stumbled across this book find it empowering?


08:45:51-08:45:59
Or would seeing all of the tools and strategies we needed to function in the world feel overwhelming and discouraging?


08:45:59-08:46:00
Even hopeless.


08:46:00-08:46:04
I knew there were positives to having ADHD.


08:46:04-08:46:07
I saw them in my community and even in myself.


08:46:07-08:46:13
I just didn't know how to highlight them in a book that described the journey I had taken to learn about the impairments.


08:46:13-08:46:17
I couldn't say ADHD was a gift, as he so often does.


08:46:17-08:46:22
When it's clear from the research, it's not, at least not entirely.


08:46:22-08:46:28
Even our quote superpowers, like hyper focus, stem from difficulty regulating our attention.


08:46:28-08:46:30
And that's not always fun.


08:46:30-08:46:31
The struggle is real.


08:46:31-08:46:33
I asked Dr.


08:46:33-08:46:37
Halliwell if he had any research on the strengths so I could include it.


08:46:37-08:46:40
Research doesn't really focus on that, he said.


08:46:40-08:46:42
But people misquote me.


08:46:42-08:46:45
I don't say ADHD is a gift.


08:46:45-08:46:47
It's a potential gift.


08:46:47-08:46:48
It's also a potential disaster.


08:46:48-08:46:51
But tell them the stories.


08:46:51-08:46:55
Tell them about the stories of those who leaned into their strengths.


08:46:55-08:47:00
He launched into a long list of people who wielded their strengths to their advantage.


08:47:00-08:47:02
I nodded, took notes.


08:47:02-08:47:04
I mean, look at you, he observed.


08:47:04-08:47:07
Look at all you accomplished by leaning into your strengths.


08:47:07-08:47:08
I did?


08:47:08-08:47:10
I blinked.


08:47:10-08:47:14
We often don't metabolize our strengths, he explained.


08:47:14-08:47:15
But we need to.


08:47:15-08:47:21
This is why he points them out, because otherwise we don't see them.


08:47:21-08:47:26
A brilliant patient of his hadn't realized she was smart because she so often felt like she wasn't.


08:47:26-08:47:35
Dave Pilkey, the author of the Captain Underpants series, had been disciplined harshly for being disruptive in class and making the other students laugh.


08:47:36-08:47:41
But he leaned into that strength and because of that found success.


08:47:41-08:47:42
Now he makes a lot of people laugh.


08:47:42-08:47:59
I thought back to how my journey began before I understood anything about my ADHD, before I knew how to recognize the obstacles, before I had tools, before I had a team, before I had any of the language I'd learned over the last seven years.


08:47:59-08:47:59
What had I done?


08:47:59-08:48:06
What I could with the tools, traits, values, and skills I already had.


08:48:06-08:48:09
Many of which I'd never considered valuable.


08:48:09-08:48:13
I impulsively started a YouTube channel without thinking through other options.


08:48:13-08:48:16
It didn't occur to me that this was an outside the box idea.


08:48:16-08:48:19
I just knew I wouldn't lose YouTube.


08:48:19-08:48:31
I like helping others, so I decided to make those videos public and enthusiastically engaged with this community without considering the potential consequences of talking openly about my ADHD challenges on the Internet for anyone to see.


08:48:32-08:48:38
My naive willingness to trust people helped me be authentic and open and accept help from others.


08:48:38-08:48:50
My insatiable curiosity that led me to spend so much time Googling stuff that I was fired from my position as a volunteer receptionist at yoga studio served me well as I devoured information about ADHD.


08:48:50-08:49:01
When I struggled to figure out how to edit my videos because I went on so many tangents and kept forgetting what I was saying, I didn't try to get better at organizing my thoughts in real time.


08:49:01-08:49:05
I started writing scripts, which of course became its own challenge.


08:49:05-08:49:07
I have trouble memorizing lines.


08:49:07-08:49:09
Well, I love going to staples.


08:49:09-08:49:14
And showing up unprepared to all those acting classes had helped me get really good at cold reading.


08:49:14-08:49:20
I bought a poster board, printed out the script in 30 point font, read each line and then spoke them aloud to the camera.


08:49:20-08:49:29
The reason my videos punch in and out so much is to hide the cuts between each line, which ended up making them more engaging for the community too.


08:49:30-08:49:32
I also got an editor.


08:49:32-08:49:34
When I couldn't afford to hire one yet, I bartered.


08:49:34-08:49:36
Turns out I like doing other people's laundry.


08:49:36-08:49:43
The whole time, my passion, my obsession over something I cared about was fueling me.


08:49:43-08:49:47
As soon as I figured out how to navigate one challenge, another popped up.


08:49:47-08:49:52
But because there were no instructions to follow, I did things in ways that seemed easier to me.


08:49:52-08:50:01
Intuitively, I steered away from my areas of weakness and into my strengths, which were often the flip side of my challenges.


08:50:01-08:50:02
I hyper focused a lot.


08:50:02-08:50:12
With the help of my Patreon brains, we compiled a bingo board of strengths that those with ADHD often have and are valued for, which you can find at howtoadhdbook.com.


08:50:12-08:50:18
No one has all of them, but most of us have more than a few, and they're often related to our ADHD.


08:50:18-08:50:23
I also had strengths that weren't related to my challenges.


08:50:23-08:50:30
